[1] The Hacker News - Ruby Releases Fixes for 7 Regex and String Flaws, Warns 5 Have Proof-of-Concept RCE
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 14:12:11 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/ruby-releases-fixes-for-7-regex-and.html

[TAM METİN - 848 kelime]
Ruby contains seven memory-safety flaws in its regular expression engine and its string case-conversion code. For five of them, its maintainers say, proof-of-concept exploits, or PoCs, have shown that crafted input can run code on the machine.

"We are aware of PoCs that prove some of them can be used to trigger remote code execution," the maintainers said.

The project released fixed versions yesterday: Ruby 4.0.10, 3.4.13 and 3.3.14. Whether an application is at risk depends on whether an attacker can get crafted patterns or strings into the affected code, and the advisories report no attacks so far.

The other two flaws are weaker. The maintainers say one has proof-of-concept code that corrupts memory under an attacker's control but has not been shown to run code, and the other can only read memory it should not or crash the program.

The project set the seven out in a security announcement and a separate advisory for each. The advisories rate three flaws critical, three high, and one moderate, and say which carry the remote-code-execution PoCs. In order of severity:

Critical. GHSA-34m4-m9fh-gqp5, a one-byte heap overflow when Ruby changes the case of a string in certain single-byte encodings, such as ISO-8859-1 or Windows-1252. It has a remote-code-execution PoC. (GitHub advisory)

Critical. GHSA-7x2q-68qv-6p8x, a double free when Ruby compiles a crafted regular expression that uses a character-class intersection (&&). It has a remote-code-execution PoC. (GitHub advisory)

Critical. GHSA-3p8q-wmhx-vm2w, a heap overflow when the size of a compiled regular expression overflows during compilation. It has a remote-code-execution PoC. (GitHub advisory)

High. GHSA-87fm-7w84-j2px, a heap overflow when a case change produces a result larger than 4 GiB. It has a remote-code-execution PoC, though the size needed puts it out of reach for most requests. (GitHub advisory)

High. GHSA-7whj-cfh7-xgm4, a heap overflow when Ruby turns a crafted compiled regular expression back into text with Regexp#to_s. It has a remote-code-execution PoC. (GitHub advisory)

High. GHSA-wpqw-5qxg-m68r, an out-of-bounds read during case-insensitive matching. Its PoC shows attacker-controlled memory corruption, and the maintainers say remote code execution is possible but not shown. (GitHub advisory)

Moderate. GHSA-pvgc-g7gj-pv6h, an out-of-bounds read when Ruby matches a case-insensitive backreference against a crafted subject string. The advisory says it "may disclose memory contents or crash the process." (GitHub advisory)

The project credits Stephan Lipp of Ada Logics and Anthropic with four of the findings, and baba__yaga, aphantom, Hiroya Fujinami and Hiroshi SHIBATA with the other three.

Which Applications Are Exposed

The flaws are in Ruby's own code, so every application on an affected version carries them. Whether an attacker can reach one depends on how the application handles external input.

The regex flaws need an application that builds a regular expression from input an attacker controls and then compiles or runs it. Matching untrusted text against a pattern the developers wrote themselves does not pose the same risk, except in one case below.

The two case-conversion flaws do not involve regular expressions at all. They fire when the application changes the case of a crafted string, so code that runs case conversion over imported or outside text is worth checking.

The one case is the moderate read flaw, which fires when the application matches a crafted subject string against a fixed pattern that uses a case-insensitive backreference. The announcement and advisories do not include the proof-of-concept code.

The fixes are in Ruby 4.0.10, 3.4.13 and 3.3.14. The affected ranges are:

Ruby 4.0.0 through 4.0.9, fixed in 4.0.10.

Ruby 3.4.0 through 3.4.12, fixed in 3.4.13.

Ruby 3.3.13 and earlier, fixed in 3.3.14.

Ruby 3.2 and earlier, end of life and not fixed.

Those older versions will not get a fix, and the project recommends moving to a supported line. With 3.3.14, the maintainers renamed the Ruby 3.3 phase from security maintenance to legacy maintenance, and support still ends at the end of March 2027.

Because the fixes ship inside Ruby, updating an application's gems or framework does not replace a vulnerable interpreter. The version that matters is the Ruby running on application servers, background workers, and container images, which must be updated and then restarted or rebuilt.

The seven do not have CVE numbers yet. The project says it has requested them, and until they are assigned, the GitHub advisory IDs are the only way to track flaws in scanners and software inventories.

It is the second time in a month that the project has patched its regular expression engine. In the October fixes, released on October 23, it shipped Ruby 4.0.9, 3.4.12, and 3.3.13 to patch compilation bugs GitLab found, which sat behind four critical GitLab flaws.

One of those GitLab flaws drew an attempted attack on Alpine Linux's own GitLab server. CISA later added it to the KEV catalog, its list of Known Exploited Vulnerabilities, as actively exploited.

Those are different bugs from this week's seven, and the new advisories do not say that GitLab or any other named product can be attacked through them. What the October case showed is that a regular expression built from user input can become a real way onto a server, not just a theoretical one.

[2] The Hacker News - Researcher Demonstrates Database RCE Exploits Affecting PostgreSQL, MySQL and Oracle
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 13:44:00 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/researcher-demonstrates-database-rce.html

[TAM METİN - 521 kelime]
Low-privilege database accounts that cannot read a single application table can still be used to run code on the database server itself, a researcher has shown, by reaching memory-corruption bugs in the engine code behind ordinary SQL.

The exploits, published yesterday, are the researcher's proofs of concept against PostgreSQL, MySQL and ClickHouse, plus a separate Oracle chain that needs no login. None has been seen in a real attack.

The work is by Zheng Yu, a researcher at Depthfirst. A database account's SQL permissions control which tables and schemas it can access, the researcher argues, not the memory-unsafe C and C++ code the engine runs to read, plan, and execute a query.

Common grants give an account enough room to reach that code. PostgreSQL's default TEMPORARY privilege, or the right to create temporary tables in MySQL and ClickHouse, allows an account to create objects or send input that triggers a memory bug.

Four of the flaws now have vendor CVEs and fixes, but the full attack chains were not reproduced independently, and the research reports no sign that any of them has been used in an attack.

In PostgreSQL, the flaw CVE-2026-116331 allows an account that can create database objects to run code as the operating system user that runs the database.

It was fixed last week in a security advisory, and the CVE record credits the researcher for the report. The researcher's technical analysis shows a plain login role building the objects it needs in its own temporary schema, without touching application data, then turning a type-confusion bug into control of the server.

MySQL has a similar flaw in its query optimizer, CVE-2026-101786, in Server and Cluster versions 26.7.0 to 26.7.1. Oracle's MySQL October advisory rates it as needing high privileges.

The researcher reached code execution on the 26.7.0 image with an account that could only create temporary tables and could not read existing ones, a narrower kind of access than that rating suggests.

Oracle Database is the one case where an attacker needs no account at all, the researcher says. A bug in Oracle Text and a flaw in the GraphQL interface of Oracle REST Data Services, chained against an exposed web endpoint, reach the engine code and run commands before any login.

Oracle patched the Oracle Text bug, CVE-2026-114819, rated 9.9, earlier this week in its November update, and the GraphQL flaw, CVE-2026-91609, in the October update. On their own, the company rates neither as exploitable without a login.

ClickHouse is the fourth target. The researcher reports an integer-overflow write in how it reads a binary row format, reached by a user who sets a single option, format_binary_max_string_size, to zero, and reproduced a shell on version 26.7.5.10.

This one has no CVE and no confirmed fixed version, so operators should check with the vendor for the fix.

Beyond installing the updates, the other move is to review what accounts can do, not only what they can read, and to take away the TEMPORARY privilege and broad temporary-table rights from accounts that do not need them. That step does not close the Oracle path, which starts without any account.

[3] The Hacker News - PHP Warns of Critical Security Flaws Affecting All Supported Versions, Fixes Coming November 19
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 10:03:46 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/php-warns-of-critical-security-flaws.html

[TAM METİN - 343 kelime]
The maintainers of the PHP programming language on Wednesday issued an advisory announcing an upcoming security release that addresses critical flaws.

The release is scheduled for November 19, 2026, between 2 and 2:30 p.m. UTC.

"These releases are part of PHP's regular four-week release cycle and will address security vulnerabilities affecting all supported PHP versions," the maintainers said. "The highest severity is CRITICAL."

However, the project also noted that issues rated critical require either non-default configuration or uncommon setups. It did not disclose any additional specifics.

The update is expected to include CVE-2026-115560, a remote code execution flaw that succeeds only if the following conditions are met -

An application sets session.serialize_handler to php_binary or php_serialize in its configuration, and its code switches the handler to php with ini_set() before calling session_start().

Session upload progress is enabled, which is PHP's default.

As mitigations, users were urged to set session.upload_progress.enabled=0 until the next regular bugfix release and restart the PHP worker for the setting to be applied.

The development comes nearly a month after PHP released fixes for two critical vulnerabilities, CVE-2026-109618 and CVE-2026-109619, collectively tracked as PHP2Shell that enable unauthenticated remote code execution (RCE) via specially crafted HTTP requests.

The shortcomings have since come under limited active exploitation, targeting publicly-accessible Palo Alto Networks management interfaces and Check Point Security Gateways to launch attacks against Active Directory and deliver backdoors.

In a report published on October 30, 2026, Hunt.io said it identified 616 PHP2Shell-related files, spanning shared exploit kits, cloned proof-of-concepts (PoCs), custom heap exploits, scanners, and post-exploitation tooling, across multiple hosts, even as participants in underground forums have sought exploits, discussed prerequisites, and whether circulating PoCs worked.

"Exploit developers are unlikely to create a single universal exploit that works reliably across all affected PHP deployments," CrowdStrike said.

Last month, PHP also released patches to address a critical flaw in Brotli (CVE-2026-111497 aka Node2Shell or Broccoli2Shell), an out-of-bounds write vulnerability in the decoder library that could result in arbitrary code execution.

Users are advised to apply the updates as soon as they become available.

[4] The Hacker News - 10-Year-Old NGINX HTTP/2 Flaw Lets Unauthenticated Attackers Execute Code on Servers
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 00:01:17 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/10-year-old-nginx-http2-flaw-lets.html

[TAM METİN - 982 kelime]
A flaw in NGINX's HTTP/2 support can allow an attacker to run code on servers with HTTP/2 enabled, without needing to log in.

The decade-old flaw, CVE-2026-123007, dubbed "nginx2root," has been present in NGINX's open-source code since version 1.9.5, released on September 22, 2015. Fixed versions 1.31.9 and 1.30.7 came out today.

F5, the company behind NGINX, says attackers can run code where ASLR is turned off or can be bypassed. Security researchers at Wiz said they bypassed ASLR and ran code through the flaw in tests. Xint's researchers said they ran code through it with nothing more than a reachable HTTP/2 port.

Code run this way gets the permissions of an NGINX worker process. Getting root on the host takes a second way to raise privileges, according to CIRCL, the Computer Incident Response Center Luxembourg.

In Xint's tests, code running as a worker could get root on the host through a separate bug in the Linux kernel's SCTP code, even when NGINX ran inside a Docker container.

Neither the vendor nor the Wiz and Rapid7 researchers have reported any attacks exploiting the flaw so far. CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog did not list it as of today.

Proof-of-concept code for the flaw is publicly available on GitHub, but its notes state it is suitable only for demonstrating the attack against a freshly started Docker container on the same machine.

Every open-source release from 1.9.5 through 1.31.8 is vulnerable, according to NGINX's security advisories. That includes 1.31.8 from October 21, as well as the October 14 security releases 1.31.7 and 1.30.6.

NGINX Plus is affected too, in 37.x releases before the fixed builds and in R34 to R36, according to the vendor advisory.

Today's releases also fix four more flaws that the vendor says can lead to code execution under the same ASLR condition. It scores all five 9.2 out of 10 on CVSS 4.0.

Each flaw can be reached only in a particular setup:

HTTP/2 is on where a listen directive includes http2, or where http2 on is set. Running openssl s_client with -alpn h2 against a server shows whether it accepts HTTP/2: the output reads ALPN protocol: h2 if it does. Behind a CDN, test the NGINX origin itself, because an origin that accepts only HTTP/1.1 cannot be attacked through HTTP/2.

Fixes, Workarounds and Signs of Attack

These releases fix CVE-2026-123007, according to F5:

NGINX Open Source 1.31.0 to 1.31.8 (mainline): fixed in 1.31.9

NGINX Open Source 1.9.5 to 1.30.6, including older branches such as 1.28: fixed in 1.30.7

NGINX Plus 37.1.0 to 37.2.0.1: fixed in 37.3.0.1

NGINX Plus 37.0.0 to 37.0.7.1: fixed in 37.0.8.1

NGINX Plus R34 to R36: no fix listed

Versions 1.31.9 and 1.30.7 fix all five flaws, according to NGINX's release notes. On NGINX Plus, 37.3.0.1 and 37.0.8.1 fix four of the flaws, and the CVE-2026-123633 fix was introduced in the earlier builds 37.2.0.1 and 37.0.7.1.

After upgrading, restart NGINX fully or use its live binary upgrade. A reload alone leaves the old executable running, and existing connections can stay on old worker processes.

The official Docker images for NGINX had 1.31.9 and 1.30.7 tags as of today, including Alpine variants.

Ubuntu and Debian had not shipped fixed packages as of today. Ubuntu's CVE status page listed nginx as vulnerable on its releases from 16.04 to 26.10, and Debian's security tracker listed it as vulnerable in bookworm, trixie, forky and sid. A distribution package can carry a fix without changing the version number that nginx -v reports, so compare the full package version with the distribution's own advisory.

The vendor lists workarounds for only two of the five flaws. For the resolver flaw, CVE-2026-123634, avoid SSI and mirror directives, and do not build a proxied server name from variables. For the mail proxy flaw, CVE-2026-113425, limit listeners to trusted networks.

CIRCL's response guidance lists turning off HTTP/2 as a temporary mitigation until you can upgrade. That blocks CVE-2026-123007 and CVE-2026-123633, but not the other three flaws. Turning off HTTP/3 where it is used removes the setup that CVE-2026-121810 needs.

On NGINX 1.25.1 and later, set http2 off; in each affected server block and remove http2 from its listen directives. On 1.9.5 through 1.25.0, remove http2 from the listen directive, so listen 443 ssl http2; becomes listen 443 ssl;. Then restart NGINX where needed and rerun the openssl check to confirm the server no longer accepts HTTP/2.

Until the Linux kernel's SCTP bug is fixed, the same guidance advises that hosts not using SCTP, a network transport protocol like TCP, block its kernel module from loading. One way is to add the line install sctp /bin/false to a .conf file in /etc/modprobe.d/. That does not unload a module already loaded or turn off SCTP built into the kernel.

For signs of an attack, CIRCL suggests looking for NGINX workers that start unexpected programs, workers that exit abnormally or leave core dumps, long-lived HTTP/2 connections with heavy incoming traffic but few logged requests, and unusual outbound connections or DNS lookups. A clean access log does not rule out an attack, because HTTP/2 control frames do not normally show up there as requests. None of these signs is proof on its own.

Rapid7's version of the attack needs about 128 GB of HTTP/2 traffic, according to its technical analysis. The attack took two and a half minutes in its demonstration. On links slower than about 300 Mbit/s, the transfer would run past NGINX's default one-hour connection limit, and the attack would fail.

The method also depends on a glibc memory-copy routine that is chosen on x86-64 CPUs with the avx512f and avx512vl features.

Wiz ran its tests on official NGINX 1.31.7 and 1.31.8 builds, using HTTP/2 over TLS on 64-bit systems, according to its disclosure post.

Xint's attack worked against its test servers on AWS, GCP and Azure, including over a public IP address, according to its research report.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/10-year-old-nginx-http2-flaw-lets.html, thehackernews.com, 18.11.2026)

[5] The Hacker News - F5 Warns 6 Critical BIG-IP Flaws Can Crash Traffic in Specific Setups, May Allow RCE
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 23:45:39 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/f5-warns-6-critical-big-ip-flaws-can.html

[TAM METİN - 920 kelime]
Six critical vulnerabilities in F5 BIG-IP allow an attacker to crash the Traffic Management Microkernel (TMM) without logging in, disrupting traffic until the TMM restarts.

Each flaw needs a specific setup on a virtual server: an iRule that uses a particular command, or an APM policy that includes two particular kinds of agent.

F5, which makes BIG-IP, disclosed the flaws today and says they may also allow remote code execution. The company has released engineering hotfixes and lists no workaround.

The company rates all six 9.8 under CVSS v3.1 and 9.3 under CVSS v4.0. They are stack-based buffer overflows in BIG-IP's data plane, the part that processes almost all of its network traffic. The management side is not exposed, according to the company.

None of the company's advisories says the flaws have been exploited. WatchTowr researcher Sina Kheirkhah reported four, and the company found the other two itself.

Five of the flaws are in iRules, a BIG-IP feature that administrators use to manage traffic, and one is in BIG-IP's APM module. Some also affect BIG-IP Next for Kubernetes and BIG-IP CNF, part of the company's BIG-IP Cloud-Native Edition.

Each flaw needs the setup listed below on a virtual server:

CVE-2026-123637: an APM per-request policy that includes both the Client Information agent and Endpoint Security agents. Affects BIG-IP APM only.

CVE-2026-120292: an HTTP profile and an iRule that uses MR::message route. Affects BIG-IP (all modules) and BIG-IP Next for Kubernetes.

CVE-2026-120285: an HTTP profile and an iRule that uses PSC::ip_address. Affects BIG-IP (all modules), BIG-IP Next for Kubernetes and, as of today, BIG-IP CNF.

CVE-2026-116953: an iRule that uses GENERICMESSAGE::route on client traffic. Affects BIG-IP (all modules) and BIG-IP Next for Kubernetes.

CVE-2026-120722: an iRule that uses DNS::scrape on client traffic. Affects BIG-IP DNS and BIG-IP Next for Kubernetes. The company's advisory lists BIG-IP CNF as not vulnerable, but its CVE record, updated today, lists CNF as affected.

F5's security notification lists the 22 advisories it published today, including 10 for NGINX.

For the six critical flaws, the only fixes are engineering hotfixes, builds that the company issues between its normal software releases. No scheduled release is listed as a fix, and the advisories provide no workaround until a hotfix is installed.

The company warns that "engineering hotfixes do not undergo the extensive QA assessment of scheduled software releases." Its hotfix policy adds that they come with no warranty or promise that they will work.

A system on a custom engineering hotfix needs a new one from the company's support team.

BIG-IP Next for Kubernetes is fixed in BNK-2.4.2-EHF1, BNK-2.3.4-EHF1 and BNK-2.2.5-EHF1, for versions 2.4.0 to 2.4.2, 2.3.0 to 2.3.4 and 2.2.0 to 2.2.5. BIG-IP CNF, on the same version ranges, is fixed in CNF-2.4.2-EHF1, CNF-2.3.4-EHF1 and CNF-2.2.5-EHF1 for CVE-2026-121805 and CVE-2026-120285.

The company checks only versions that are still within technical support, so an older release missing from its tables has not been shown to be safe.

Today's flaws differ from CVE-2026-94127, an APM flaw that F5 disclosed on September 22 and that was later exploited. The U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) added it to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog the same day.

That flaw is a heap-based buffer overflow in APM setups that serve as OAuth authorization servers, allowing attackers to run code without logging in. Today's six are stack-based overflows that need different settings.

Six Other BIG-IP and BIG-IQ Flaws

Today's advisories also cover six lower-rated flaws in BIG-IP and BIG-IQ, each with its own fix. The scores below are CVSS v3.1:

CVE-2026-125274 (iControl SOAP, 8.8): A user with network access and at least the Guest role can send an unauthorized management request. The company says this may allow them to run system commands, create or delete files, or disable services. It is fixed in BIG-IP 21.1.0.3, 17.5.1.10 and 17.1.3.6, and in BIG-IQ 8.4.2.3. Until then, the SOAP advisory explains how to limit iControl SOAP access to trusted networks and warns that blocking it on self-IP addresses may break high-availability setups.

CVE-2026-120718 (iControl REST, 8.1): iControl REST tokens may not expire as expected. An attacker who already holds a token may be able to extend how long it stays valid unless its owner deletes it. It is fixed in BIG-IP 21.1.0.2, 17.5.1.10, and 17.1.3.6. As a workaround, scripts that create tokens can be changed to delete them when they are no longer needed.

CVE-2026-125272 (APM, 7.5): When an APM access profile on a virtual server has a per-request policy with a form-based Logon Page, an attacker can crash TMM without logging in. It is fixed in the same engineering hotfixes as the six critical flaws.

CVE-2026-126822 (APM SAML, 7.5): When an access profile is set up as a SAML Service Provider (SP), an attacker can crash TMM without logging in. It is fixed in the same engineering hotfixes. Until then, the company suggests removing the SAML Auth agent if SAML SP is not needed, and limiting access to that virtual server to trusted client networks.

CVE-2026-126336 (APM SAML, 7.5): A SAML SP agent with encrypted assertions turned on lets an attacker crash TMM without logging in. It is fixed in BIG-IP 21.1.0.3, 17.5.1.10, and 17.1.3.6, and the company recommends a high-availability setup to reduce the impact.

CVE-2026-121812 (APM and SSL Orchestrator, 7.5): A per-request policy with the tmm.access.policytrace database key turned on lets an attacker crash TMM. The key is off by default. APM is fixed in the same engineering hotfixes, and for SSL Orchestrator the advisory lists fixes on the 21.1 and 17.1 branches only.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/f5-warns-6-critical-big-ip-flaws-can.html, thehackernews.com, 18.11.2026)

[6] The Hacker News - Critical Apache Roller RCE Flaw Under Active Exploitation, No Authentication Required
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 22:40:45 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/critical-apache-roller-rce-flaw-under.html

[TAM METİN - 242 kelime]
A critical security flaw in Apache Roller, an open-source, Java-based blogging platform, has come under active exploitation in the wild, according to the Apache Software Foundation (ASF)

The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-129448 (CVSS score: 9.8), is a case of unauthenticated deserialization in the XML-RPC endpoint that could lead to remote code execution.

"Deserialization of Untrusted Data in Apache Roller versions 4.0.0 through 6.1.4 allows an unauthenticated remote attacker to execute arbitrary code," project maintainers said. "The XML-RPC endpoint accepts vendor extension types and deserializes them before authenticating the requester."

ASF also noted that the vulnerability is the same as CVE-2026-82384 (CVSS score: 9.8), which was patched in September 2026 and listed only Apache Roller 6.1.5 as affected.

"This issue is being actively exploited," it added. ASF did not share any details on how the vulnerability is being exploited, or who is being targeted as part of these efforts.

A security researcher named n0mi1k has been credited with finding the flaw, and Matt Raible has been acknowledged for reporting it.

Users are advised to upgrade to Apache Roller 6.1.6 or later, which disables the extension types and rejects XML-RPC requests when the XML-RPC feature is disabled.

If immediate patching is not an option, it's recommended to set "enabledForExtensions" to "false" in WEB-INF/web.xml, or remove the "/roller-services/xmlrpc" servlet mapping, and then restart Roller.

"Disabling XML-RPC in the administration UI is not enough, because older versions deserialize the request before checking that setting," ASF noted.

[7] The Hacker News - New EggStreme Malware Variant Goes Silent to Evade Security Tools in Government Attacks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 19:41:28 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/eggstreme-hackers-rebuild-malware-with.html

[TAM METİN - 1744 kelime]
The suspected Chinese advanced persistent threat (APT) group behind the sophisticated EggStreme malware has rebuilt its toolkit as it continues to target government organizations in the Philippines.

"When attackers are exposed, the cheap response is to rotate," Bitdefender said in a technical report shared with The Hacker News. "A file hash changes with every recompile, a domain costs a few dollars, and a new IP address costs less."

"The EggStreme operators did more than rotate – the agent we now see uses a different network protocol, has an enhanced command set, and comes in a passive variant that never beacons out. The infrastructure appears to be moving away from the one certificate authority that tied its older servers together."

The end result is that the changes allow it to bypass static signatures and heuristic detections built around the EggStreme malware framework when it was first exposed in September 2025.

Delivered via an as-yet-unknown initial access vector, EggStreme's defining characteristic is the use of DLL sideloading to execute payloads. It features multiple components as follows -

EggStremeLoader, which sets up persistence and decrypts and executes EggStremeReflectiveLoader

EggStremeReflectiveLoader, which executes EggStremeAgent

EggStremeAgent (aka Gorem RAT), the core component and a full-featured backdoor that enables system reconnaissance, lateral movement, data theft via an injected keylogger called EggStremeKeylogger, and reverse shell and file upload/download capabilities via a secondary backdoor called EggStremeWizard

It's worth noting that some components of the framework, such as EggStremeFuel and EggStremeLoader, have been put to use by another cluster tracked by Palo Alto Networks as CL-STA-1048 between March and September 2025.

Martin Zugec, technical solutions director at Bitdefender, told The Hacker News that CL-STA-1048's targeting of a Southeast Asian government entity and its associated tooling is "consistent with what we have observed" but emphasized that their research is more focused on the malware's capabilities and its evolution rather than attribution.

As is often the case with Chinese-aligned activity, the same tools can be used by more than one threat actor, Zugec noted, adding they have not compared infrastructure or victim data to determine if the two sets of activity constitute the same threat cluster as CL-STA-1048.

The latest findings from Bitdefender show a significant retooling of EggStreme's capabilities, including removing the keylogger from every agent build observed since then. Within two weeks after public disclosure, the threat actors are also said to have made other changes -

The method EggStremeFuel uses to protect its configuration

EggStremeLoader's cryptography and payload format, moving from RC4 to RC5 and to a custom cipher by the end of October 2025

Added commands in EggStremeAgent that facilitate Active Directory reconnaissance, Windows Management Instrumentation (WMI) querying, TCP traffic forwarding, and execution of PowerShell, Portable Executable (PE), .NET assembly payloads

Moving from a single certificate authority to campaign-specific certificate authorities

In addition, where lateral movement previously relied on Impacket-like tooling and on EggStremeAgent itself, newer incidents make use of an unmanaged system that sits outside of the organization's endpoint management but is still reachable by other workstations to stage the payloads and launch them via DLL sideloading using a Visual Basic Script (VBScript) file remotely run over Server Message Block (SMB).

Exactly how the operators gain access to the target is still unclear. "Our first investigation began with a logon script on a network share, and we could not determine how that script got there," Zugec said. "In the later activity we analyzed, the operators used valid credentials and a script staged on a host that the organization did not manage. That tells us how they moved once inside, not how they got in."

Some of the newer builds have been found to run in passive mode by opening a listening port and waiting for the operator to connect in as opposed to contacting an external command-and-control (C2) server.

Bitdefender also noted that there is no evidence at this stage to indicate EggStremeWizard has been discontinued by the attackers.

"The newer EggStremeAgent samples we analyzed do not show whether it is still being deployed because it is not part of the agent," Zugec said. "EggStremeWizard was an additional tool used alongside EggStremeAgent, not the primary implant, and the framework works without it. The keylogger is a separate component. Its absence from the newer agent builds reflects what the operators chose to compile into those builds, so we do not interpret its absence as evidence that the tool has been retired."

There is also some evidence that the attackers have also targeted Taiwan, but it has been treated with low to medium confidence.

"The speed of the loader changes says as much about the operators' maturity as about the cost of exposure," Bitdefender concluded. "The build-time design appears to be what makes rebuilds this fast. What they did not change is how they operate: sideloaded DLLs under system library names, staging through file shares, running from memory, and requiring mutual TLS."

Mustang Panda Targets Philippine Government Using ASEAN Summit Lures

As of late October 2026, Philippine government officials have also been targeted by Mustang Panda, a prolific China-aligned cyber espionage group, using ASEAN Summit lures to deliver PlugX using established tactics like DLL sideloading.

"The package impersonated a Laos–Philippines bilateral meeting agenda from the Lao Ministry of Foreign Affairs," Dream Research Labs said in a report published last week.

"It named five officials and referenced plausible diplomatic priorities, requesting that the reader provide confirmation before the summit. Behind it was a delivery chain that installed PlugX remote-access malware while displaying the decoy agenda."

Earth Alux Uses Stealthy Tradecraft to Strike Southeast Asia

The disclosure comes as a China-nexus threat actor tracked as Earth Alux (aka Ink Dragon, Jewelbug, REF7707, and UAT-11587) has been attributed to a fresh set of attacks targeting Southeast Asian government entities since at least July 2025. Palo Alto Networks Unit 42 has assigned the activity the designation TGR-STA-0049 (formerly CL-STA-0049).

"The defining signature of the threat actor behind the activity is the targeting of government entities, combined with the use of advanced custom payloads that are designed for high-stealth intelligence collection operations, with an emphasis on evading security detection tools," Unit 42 researcher Tom Fakterman said.

Attacks mounted by the group have historically deployed an advanced backdoor known as FINALDRAFT (aka Squidoor) since 2023. However, it has since expanded its malware arsenal to include two previously undocumented remote access trojans (RATs) called PerfectPioneer RAT (in July 2025) and KUKURAT (in March 2026).

Initial access to target networks is achieved via exploitation of vulnerable, unpatched Microsoft Exchange servers to deploy web shells like ASPXSpy and Godzilla, which are then used to deploy the RAT payloads. Alternatively, Earth Alux has leveraged phishing emails using decoy PDFs and lookalike domains to compromise victim hosts.

PerfectPioneer RAT is a Go-based malware that's launched via three different methods -

DLL sideloading to launch a malicious DLL that reads, decrypts and executes XOR-encrypted shellcode containing the RAT

Decoy installers for Palo Alto Networks GlobalProtect that installs the legitimate program but also writes the malware to disk and executes it in the background

Disguising executable files as PDF documents in phishing emails that, once launched, opens a decoy PDF document while the malware is launched in the background

The malware supports a number of features that enable the operator to gain remote control of the infected host. It facilitates file operations, shell execution, process management, keylogging, SOCKS5 proxy, screenshot capture, Window enumeration and manipulation, and CPU and memory utilization monitoring.

It also ensures a connection to the C2 server remains active at all times and systematically cleans up temporary files and runs garbage collection if memory usage exceeds 100 MB to minimize forensic traces.

Furthermore, select variants of the malware come with an "isDangerousCommand" feature to avoid running administrative commands that would be highly destructive or noisy, thereby allowing it to be detected by security programs installed on the host. These include rm, curl | bash, wget | bash, and powershell -e, among others.

KUKURAT, in contrast, functions as a modular implant that's executed in networks already compromised by PerfectPioneer RAT. Written in C++, the backdoor implements its functionality as a separate DLL module that's loaded dynamically at runtime directly from the C2 server.

This allows the malware to be highly evasive and adapt its capabilities on the fly in response to shifting operational objectives. It supports the following features -

Send system information and receive a configuration from the C2 server

Enumerate and terminate processes

Perform file operations

Execute shell commands via cmd.exe and PowerShell

Conduct lateral movement via PsExec

Carry out SMB credential brute-forcing

Run Beacon Object Files (BOF)

Facilitate local privilege elevation

Ensure the malware remains active by means of a standalone watchdog executable

What's more, KUKURAT initiates bogus, legitimate-looking activity on the endpoint to camouflage and cover up its tracks based on a command issued by the threat actor. This includes -

Generate 10 decoy HTTP requests per operational cycle to randomly selected domains that are extracted from a hard-coded list of 15 popular websites using randomly generated URL paths with an aim to blend C2 communications

Mimic legitimate Google Chrome browser behavior by writing random data to the registry path HKCU\Software\Google\Chrome\BrowserCrashRecovery, reading the data back, and deleting the key

Create temporary files with random names and randomized content inside the system %temp% directory, and delete them

"Both PerfectPioneer RAT and KUKURAT use HTTP/3 as the primary method for communicating with C2 infrastructure," Unit 42 said. "While the adoption of HTTP/3 is rare across the broader malware landscape, its deployment in this campaign reflects an emerging trend among advanced threat actors."

The attacks have also been found to leverage established tools like PsExec, WMI, and Windows Remote Management (WinRM) to move laterally across compromised enterprise networks; collect credentials using Mimikatz, ProcDump, and SharpDPAPI; compromise Domain Controllers to deploy KUKURAT on local endpoints; and exfiltrate sensitive data such as files and emails.

"Through the deployment of KUKURAT and the PerfectPioneer RAT, the attackers behind this activity are actively evolving to stay ahead of traditional network defenses by implementing protocol-level evasion via HTTP/3 and QUIC over UDP," Unit 42 concluded.

"What makes this group particularly evasive is the extensive effort invested in maintaining operational security and remaining hidden on compromised endpoints. The group demonstrates a mature understanding of modern defensive ecosystems. They have even gone so far as to embed hard-coded administrative safety switches within their binaries to prevent their own operators from running loud or destructive commands that might trigger security alerts."

[8] The Hacker News - New Attack Bypasses TPM Lockout to Crack Windows Hello and BitLocker PINs in Days, Not Years
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 17:58:30 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/flash-chip-attack-bypasses-tpm-lockout.html

[TAM METİN - 1546 kelime]
On AMD-based PCs and some Intel PCs, the TPM lockout for Windows Hello and BitLocker PINs can be reset by someone who opens the PC's case, researchers at Technische Universität Berlin say.

That leaves short PINs open to guessing. In lab tests, the researchers found a four-digit Hello PIN on an AMD laptop and a 6-digit BitLocker PIN on an Intel laptop this way.

Both PINs were set by the researchers and found within 236 tries. From the speeds they measured, they calculate a worst-case of 40 hours for a four-digit Hello PIN, compared to 69 days with the lockout working as documented. For a default six-digit BitLocker PIN, the worst-case time drops from 19 years to 20 days.

AMD confirmed yesterday that its processor firmware has a flaw that allows the attack on some PCs with its chips. It plans fixes for most of those chips between November and February 2027. For now, the researchers advise adding at least two digits to Hello and BitLocker PINs.

The TPM in these PCs is a firmware TPM, built into the processor. Unlike a separate TPM chip, it has no secure storage of its own, according to the research paper the researchers posted late last week. So it keeps its saved state, including the count of wrong guesses, encrypted on the mainboard's flash chip.

If someone copies that saved state and later writes the old copy back, the count goes back too, unless the PC detects it. Newer PCs try to catch this with counters inside the flash chip, called RPMC, that can only go up.

On AMD PCs that use these counters, the processor's firmware never verifies that a counter actually increased. A small board placed between the processor and the flash chip can block the update and report success, causing the PC to accept the old state.

RPMC chips from flash maker Winbond have a different weakness, the researchers found. A brief drop in the chip's power supply, called a voltage glitch, can cause the chip to reveal the secret key that signs its counter values. With that key, which took about a second to extract with their best settings, the board can forge any counter value.

On the Intel laptop they tested, one glitch was enough: the laptop's firmware feeds the chip the same value at every boot, so the leaked key kept working after each restart.

For the Hello test, the researchers attacked a locked HP OmniBook 3 with an AMD Ryzen AI 5 330 processor and a BitLocker-encrypted drive. Their setup typed PINs from 1000 upward and reached the correct one, 1235, after 236 tries in 57 minutes.

Windows still forced a restart after every 5 incorrect PIN attempts on that laptop, but no lockout delay was applied. Microsoft's lockout defaults lock the TPM after 32 wrong tries and forget one failure every 10 minutes.

The BitLocker test ran on a Framework Laptop 13 with an Intel Core Ultra 5 125H processor and Winbond flash. BitLocker's startup prompt adds no limit of its own, so each reset gave a fresh 32 tries. The setup found the PIN, 123457, after 236 tries in 6 minutes and 55 seconds.

In both tests, the researchers took the flash chip off the mainboard and wired in their own board. A script read the screen, typed PINs, and restarted the laptop through a KVM switch. They also changed firmware settings to slow each laptop's link to the flash chip, because their board could not keep up at full speed.

The researchers make two further claims that their tests did not cover. Either method could be used against either PIN. A recovered Hello PIN can also unlock keys tied to the device and enable Hello-based single sign-on at work, and, in some configurations, pass a multi-factor check.

The researchers have published their attack firmware, board designs, and scripts. They say reproducing the RPMC attacks is not plug-and-play and needs one of their boards. Neither the paper nor the vendor notices published so far describe attacks outside the lab.

Which PCs the Attack Works On and What Vendors Say

Whether the attack works depends on the processor and the flash chip:

AMD-based PCs with RPMC flash: open to the board that blocks counter updates. The chipmaker has confirmed the firmware flaw behind this and plans to fix it.

AMD-based PCs without RPMC flash: on two AMD desktops in the researchers' tests, writing an old copy back with an ordinary flash programmer reset the count, requiring no board. They conclude that AMD processors likely have no other protection against this, though two desktops may not be representative of all AMD systems.

PCs that rely on Winbond RPMC flash: the researchers say these are open to the leaked-key method whatever the processor, and that only a new chip design can fix it.

Intel PCs without RPMC-like flash: three Intel systems detected the same write-back in those tests.

The researchers expect the AMD firmware flaw to affect AMD PCs that use the Microsoft Pluton security core as their TPM, but did not show this.

On an AMD PC, the owner can boot Linux, for example from a live USB stick, and run the command grep . /sys/bus/pci/drivers/ccp/*/rpmc_* from the researchers' exposure check. The output indicates whether RPMC flash is present and enabled, which tells the owner which of the two methods applies. Missing files do not mean the PC lacks RPMC.

For Intel PCs, the researchers know of no such check. Owners would have to open the PC and look for a Winbond chip marked 25R or 74M.

AMD tracks the flaw as CVE-2026-43613 and assigns it a CVSS score of 5.1 in a security bulletin published yesterday.

The chipmaker says attacks requiring physical access fall outside its product security threat model, but that "RPMC is intended to stop this type of interposer attack." The researchers first told it about the flaw in February.

The chipmaker's fix targets run from November to February 2027 across Ryzen, Ryzen AI, Ryzen Threadripper, Athlon and EPYC chips, and one unnamed product has no date yet. These are planned dates for its own firmware, not updates already out for PCs.

The bulletin does not cover AMD PCs without RPMC flash. It also does not say whether the update will prevent an attacker from installing older, unfixed firmware on the PC, which the researchers say a fix must prevent.

Intel, whose processor was in the BitLocker test laptop, said in a security announcement earlier this week that the research "did not demonstrate a vulnerability in an Intel component". That matches the tests, in which the attack on the Intel laptop needed the Winbond chip's flaw.

According to the researchers, Intel's security team called the reused value a weakness rather than a vulnerability.

The researchers told Microsoft in April. According to them, the company rated the Windows Hello report as "moderate severity" and below its bar for an immediate fix, issued no CVE or bounty, and committed to raising its recommended minimum Hello PIN length to six digits.

At the company's request, the researchers filed the BitLocker finding as a separate report for a different team. The company declined to give them any further written statement for the paper.

Separately, a Windows 11 test build released late last week makes six digits the minimum for personal-account Hello PINs, with an option to use four. The release notes give no reason, and features in that test channel may never reach regular releases.

According to the researchers, Winbond was first told about its chips' weakness to voltage glitches in January 2025 and received a second report in April showing that a glitch also leaks the key. The flash maker is studying the issue, possibly including a new chip design, but has not committed to a fix or a date.

What Owners and Admins Can Do

Two extra digits make a large difference in the researchers' figures:

* BitLocker accepts a four-digit PIN only if an admin lowers the six-digit minimum. The times are worst cases calculated from speeds measured on one laptop each.

In May, Microsoft advised moving BitLocker from TPM-only to TPM+PIN to block the YellowKey bypass, as part of its YellowKey mitigation. The same TPM lockout guards that PIN.

Steps for each setup:

Windows Hello for Business: raise the Minimum PIN length policy, under Computer Configuration > Administrative Templates > System > PIN Complexity. If it is not set, Windows requires six digits, a worst case of 168 days in the table above, and admins can set it as low as four. The PIN policy settings page lists it on the PIN settings tab.

BitLocker: raise the Configure minimum PIN length for startup policy, which allows 4 to 20 digits and requires six by default. Turning on "Allow enhanced PINs for startup" lets letters and symbols, which the BitLocker PIN guidance recommends for older hardware.

AMD-based PCs: install the firmware update with the chipmaker's fix when one is available for your PC. Until then, the chipmaker advises keeping PCs under physical control and watching for signs of tampering.

PCs that rely on Winbond RPMC flash: the flash maker has not committed to a fix, according to the researchers. Intel advises its customers to ask their PC maker about updates.

[9] The Hacker News - AI Pentesting Checklist for Web Apps: 15 Questions to Ask Before Choosing a Vendor
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 17:45:00 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/ai-pentesting-checklist-for-web-apps-15.html

[TAM METİN - 753 kelime]
Attackers move first. On average, exploitation now starts before a patch exists (Mandiant M-Trends 2026). In 1H 2026, 23% of vulnerabilities VulnCheck added to its exploited list were hit on or before disclosure day.

AI pentesting vendors aren't interchangeable. Most look convincing in a demo. The gaps show up in your logged-in flows, your coverage, and your production safety.

Make them prove it on your own web apps. Score each vendor on context, reach, validated findings, guardrails, and retests.

Choosing an AI pentesting vendor? Get 15 questions, red flags, and a scorecard to test what vendors deliver, not just what they demo. Download the free checklist →

AI is speeding attackers up

For the first time, Google's Threat Intelligence Group identified a threat actor using a zero-day exploit it believes was developed with AI: a 2FA bypass rooted in a logic flaw that conventional fuzzing and static analysis can easily miss (GTIG).

Agentic pentesting can close that gap, and 87% of US security leaders surveyed are planning, piloting, or using it. Choosing the agent is the hard part.

Every Demo Looks Good. Your App Is the Real Test.

Picture three agents that all demo well. The first finds real flaws but stops at your login page. The second navigates your app but can't answer a basic question: coverage of what? The third exploits real issues but has no hard limits for production.

Each looks impressive on the vendor's target. The difference shows up on yours. A structured bake-off surfaces it before you sign.

Ask What the Agent Knows Before It Attacks

Every tester has to learn your site before the first payload. That recon is real attack work. The cost is redoing it from scratch on every engagement, when most of the map hasn't changed. In CyCognito's model, recon and analysis take 20 to 30 of a manual pentest's 63 to 95 hours.

Agents differ here. Some start from a blank URL and rebuild the map on every run. Others start from an existing model of your site and keep it current between runs. Ask which one you're buying, what carries over between runs, and whether the agent keeps discovering beyond its starting map. A starting map should be a head start, not a boundary.

Without context, an AI agent behaves like a smart scanner. With context, it can reason about your application. That changes what it finds, not just how fast. When a security researcher hunted for flaws in Apache Airflow, their AI assistant called the search a dead end. Testing how the deployment’s parts trust each other, instead of reading the code, turned up an unauthenticated RCE (CVE-2026-68982).

Ask for Proof, Not Suspicion

A suspicious pattern is not an exploitable vulnerability. Ask whether the agent exploits what it suspects, reproduces its findings, and hands over evidence your team can verify. A list of suspected issues is not a validated pentest.

Here is what proof looks like. During an authorized assessment of a major insurer's production website, an AI pentesting agent found that the one-time passcode request could send login codes to any email address. It proved the flaw by logging in with a code sent to its own inbox, and guardrails then stopped the test. The insurer said it had pentested the site about six months earlier (Reflectiz case study on The Hacker News).

Three Questions That Expose a Weak AI Pentesting Vendor

A demo can look convincing without proving that the agent reaches your logged-in workflows, validates what it finds, or confirms your fixes. Here are three of the 15 checks to run before you choose.

The other 12, covering starting context, coverage proof, attack chaining, access across roles, data handling, and guardrails, are in the full checklist.

Run a Bake-Off, Not a Demo

Demos run on the vendor's target. Bake-offs run on yours.

Same target, same week. Same app, test accounts, and scope for every vendor.

Log three timestamps. Access granted, discovery complete, first test.

Score every check. The checklist's scoring sheet weights what matters and gates on safety.

What's Inside the Checklist

Checks for attack chaining, roles, guardrails, and retesting

A 36-point scorecard and a per-check scoring sheet

The best AI pentesting agent isn't the one with the best demo. It's the one that can show what it tested, prove what it found, and run safely on your application. Use these 15 questions to find it.

[10] The Hacker News - Quality Isn’t The Main Worry About AI Pentesting — Data Privacy and Production Safety Are
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 16:33:57 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/quality-isnt-main-worry-about-ai.html

[TAM METİN - 1036 kelime]
AI is speeding up how software is built, which means more code, more changes, and more chances for vulnerabilities to reach production. It's speeding up how attackers find and exploit them too, with time-to-exploit now down to a single day. Security testing has to keep up with both.

AI pentesting has emerged as a way to run in-depth tests whenever teams need them, instead of waiting for the next scheduled pentest. To see how far adoption has come and what security teams need from it, Intruder surveyed 500 security and engineering leaders across the US and UK. Read on for the top findings.

Only 4% of security leaders believe their current testing frequency gives them sufficient coverage

68% of organizations deploy code to production at least daily, and 37% deploy multiple times a day. Understandably, pentesting runs on a much slower cycle. Nearly half (47%) go more than a month between full pentests on their web applications and APIs. Those figures are for each organization's most frequently tested application, so the rest of their estate is tested less often still.

Running a human-led pentest as often as code ships has never been realistic, largely because of the cost. But it means a vulnerability introduced in one release can go weeks or months without a pentest, and only 4% of security leaders believe their current testing frequency gives them sufficient coverage.

42% say they accept the risk and ship code without additional security checks between pentests

Most teams cover the gap between pentests with a mix of approaches, but 42% accept the risk and ship code without additional security checks in between. Automated scanning in the build pipeline, more frequent manual pentests, internal AppSec teams, and AI-driven pentesting are each used by around half.

Concern about the gap is widespread. 88% of leaders running scheduled pentests are worried about unmapped risk introduced between tests, and they split almost evenly on how serious it is. Half call it one of their largest unmanaged cyber risks, while the other half describe it as a known gap they're covering with basic perimeter controls.

Fewer than 1% have no plans to try AI pentesting

55% of organizations already use AI pentesting across their environment, a further 36% have tried it but not yet adopted it at scale, and fewer than 1% have no plans to try it. For a category that has only emerged in the past two years, that's fast uptake.

The largest organizations surveyed (5,000 to 10,000 employees) are furthest along, with 71% using AI pentesting across their environment. Midmarket organizations have tried it at a similar rate but scaled it less so far, at 54%.

Only a quarter see AI testing quality as a top concern

Security leaders need confidence that AI pentesting is safe to run on sensitive systems, and a way to check its work. The quality of AI pentesting isn't the main concern, with only a quarter naming it as one of their biggest barriers to adoption. The top barriers relate to how AI tools operate inside sensitive environments: data privacy (40%), safety on production systems (36%), and whether auditors and regulators will accept AI pentest reports (28%).

Asked what would most damage their trust in AI pentesting, nearly half (46%) point to a lack of visibility into what was tested (25%) or a lack of reproducible proof of exploitability (21%).

DevSecOps leads, who work most directly with the findings security tools produce, are the most skeptical — only 5% say nothing would damage their trust, compared with 19% of CTOs.

56% of teams spend six or more hours a week triaging false positives

More than half of teams (56%) spend six hours or more a week validating findings from their security tooling. Working out whether a finding is real is the biggest remediation bottleneck, named by 44% of leaders, and one in five teams spend 11 hours or more a week on it, more than a full working day.

Confirming fixes takes time too. Fewer than two in five organizations independently retest a fix in production as soon as it ships, and nearly half take up to two weeks. Until then, there's no way to know the vulnerability has actually been closed.

This is the work AI-enabled validation is designed to cut, by confirming findings are exploitable before they reach the team and retesting a fix as soon as it ships.

90% agree the traditional annual pentest is dead

Nine in ten security and engineering leaders agree that the traditional annual pentest is dead and that continuous AI pentesting is now required to keep pace. For most, it already is. Only 6% still rely on a single annual pentest.

Looking three to five years ahead, most (54%) expect AI to handle the bulk of testing, and only one in six expect manual pentests to remain a primary method of security validation. 28% expect to keep manual pentests mainly to satisfy auditors, regardless of their technical value.

What this means for security teams

Until now, teams have relied on two kinds of testing, each with a trade-off. Scanning and DAST run often but are generic by design, so they miss flaws specific to how an application works. A pentest goes deep, but it's a snapshot of how the application looked weeks or months ago. AI pentesting brings the depth of a pentest, on demand.

That changes what's possible across the testing cycle. Releases can be tested as they ship, instead of waiting for the next scheduled pentest. With findings validated before they're reported, teams spend their time on issues that are real, and fixes can be retested straight away.

For teams looking to adopt it with confidence, a few steps help. Start in a staging environment that mirrors production. Run an AI pentest alongside your next manual pentest and compare the results. Look for findings that come with proof-of-concept code and a record of what was tested. And check how your code and data will be handled before you give it access.

With AI speeding up how software is built and how it's attacked, AI pentesting makes it possible to keep pace with both. Get the full report or learn more about Intruder’s AI pentesting.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/quality-isnt-main-worry-about-ai.html, thehackernews.com, 18.11.2026)

[11] The Hacker News - Hackers Exploiting Critical Puppet RCE Flaw Affecting Enterprise Management Servers
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 13:54:31 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/hackers-exploiting-critical-puppet-rce.html

[TAM METİN - 471 kelime]
The U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) on Tuesday added a critical security flaw impacting Perforce Puppet, an enterprise infrastructure-as-code (IaC) and configuration management platform, to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog, citing reports of active exploitation.

The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-115731 (CVSS score: 9.4), is a case of deserialization of untrusted data that could result in remote code execution.

"Perforce Puppet Enterprise contains a deserialization of untrusted data vulnerability in Puppet's report-processing code that allows a client with any CA-signed agent certificate to execute arbitrary code on the Puppet Server as the service user by submitting a report containing a crafted rich-data type through a PUT request," CISA said.

"If the certificate authority automatically signs requests without strong verification, an unauthenticated network attacker can obtain a valid certificate and exploit the vulnerability."

The shortcoming impacts the following versions -

Puppet Enterprise 2026.0.0 before 2026.0.1

Puppet Enterprise 2025.0.0 before 2025.12.1

Puppet Enterprise 2023.8.0 before 2023.8.13

Puppet Core 9.0.0 before 9.1.3

Puppet Core 8.x before 8.22.2

It was addressed by Perforce Software on November 3, 2026, in the following versions -

Puppet Enterprise 2026.0.1 (Puppet Core 9.1.3)

Puppet Enterprise 2025.12.1 (Puppet Core 8.22.2)

Puppet Enterprise 2023.8.13 (Puppet Core 8.22.2)

It's worth noting that the issue also affects OpenVox, an open-source implementation of Puppet, and has been resolved in the following versions -

OpenVox Agent 9.3.0 and 8.32.0

OpenVox Server 9.2.0 and 8.18.0

Alain Rödel, a security researcher at Neodyme AG, has been credited with discovering and reporting CVE-2026-115731 along with CVE-2026-115732 (CVSS score: 9.0), a critical certificate-name authorization bypass affecting both Puppet and OpenVox. The issues were discovered with artificial intelligence (AI) assistance and precise steering, the German cybersecurity company added.

"The impact highly depends on the Puppet server configuration and the orchestration protocols used, if any are enabled at all," Neodyme said in a report published this month. "Independent of the configuration, an attacker with RCE on a Puppet server can grab the CA signing keys and impersonate other hosts."

"Puppet is a powerful software, often with full access to a fleet of systems in a corporate network. Remote Code Execution on such a central and important system can lead to worst-case scenarios: Deployment of ransomware, mass data exfiltration, and potentially lateral movement to other parts of the network."

As of writing, there are no details on how the vulnerability is being weaponized in the wild. However, a Reddit user named transdimensia noted in a November 4 post that it was being exploited shortly after public disclosure.

"After finding a severely misconfigured CA in my fleet, I traced the actions backwards on a [CVE-2026-]115731 intrusion," the user said.

Federal Civilian Executive Branch (FCEB) agencies are advised to apply the fixes by December 1, 2026, to secure their networks under Binding Operational Directive (BOD) 26-04: Prioritizing Security Updates Based on Risk.

[12] The Hacker News - CISA Urges Immediate Patching of Java4Shell Flaw That Could Allow Remote Code Execution
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 13:54:23 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/cisa-urges-immediate-patching-of.html

[TAM METİN - 912 kelime]
The U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) has urged organizations to immediately patch a newly disclosed critical security flaw impacting Oracle Java.

The flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-114938 (CVSS score: 8.1), refers to a case of out-of-bounds write that can lead to denial-of-service (DoS) or remote code execution under certain conditions.

"This critical memory-corruption vulnerability, known as 'Java4Shell,' affects the Base64 decoder of the x86-64 HotSpot Java Virtual Machine (JVM), which is part of the Java Development Kit (JDK)," CISA said. "Specially crafted Base64 input could crash the JVM and allow for unauthenticated remote code execution."

Patches for the flaw have been released by Oracle and OpenJDK. As of writing, there is no evidence the vulnerability has been exploited in the wild.

As reported previously by The Hacker News, Java4Shell can enable attackers without an account to execute arbitrary code on Keycloak, Atlassian Jira, Atlassian Confluence, JFrog Artifactory, Jenkins, and PingFederate, and with a login on Apache Kafka Broker, Apache Solr, Elasticsearch, Gerrit, and Spinnaker, under default settings.

Advisories for the flaw have been released by numerous security and software vendors, including Aikido, Amazon Linux, Blackpoint Cyber, Chainguard, Cisco Talos, Cycode, Datadog, Dynatrace, Endor Labs, Field Effect, Hadrian, Horizon3.ai, Miggo, Orca Security, Qualys, Rapid7, Red Hat, SentinelOne, Sophos, Tanium, Tenable, Upwind, VulnCheck, watchTowr, and Wiz.

In an update shared on November 18, 2026, the Keycloak team said an instance can be considered at risk if the following conditions apply -

It runs an unpatched OpenJDK build from 17.0.6 onward, including unpatched OpenJDK 21 and 25 builds

Its x86-64 host or virtual machine exposes the avx512vbmi CPU feature to Java

The running JVM has Base64 intrinsics enabled and has not been limited to AVX2 with -XX:UseAVX=2

"The x86-64 Keycloak container images tagged 26.8.2-1 and 26.8.2-2 bundle an affected OpenJDK 21.0.13 build," the team said. "These image tags alone do not establish whether your deployment is exploitable: check the CPU feature and effective JVM flags."

"A Keycloak ZIP or TAR.GZ distribution started directly on a host can also be affected; the conditions depend on its Java runtime and CPU, not on whether it runs in a container."

Keycloak is also recommending that users run the below command to check the CPU feature on a host or virtual machine -

If the output of the command returns nothing, it indicates that the CPU does not expose AVX-512 VBMI and is not known to be affected by the flaw. Keycloak has released temporary mitigations that users can apply until it releases updated Keycloak and Keycloak Operator container images once patched OpenJDK builds are available for them.

"The vulnerability could be reached through a local Spring Boot application using Spring Security," Blackpoint Cyber researchers Sam Decker and Nevan Beal said.

"In this environment, approximately 28,000 unauthenticated HTTP requests carrying a malformed Base64 authentication value caused the JVM to terminate after roughly 20 seconds of sustained requests. Additional testing confirmed corruption of nearby Java objects and changes to live application data without necessarily causing an immediate crash."

CISA is recommending that organizations take the following steps -

Identify affected Java installations.

Review software inventory for JDKs and Java runtimes, including those bundled with third-party applications or included in container images and appliances.

Apply relevant Java and vendor patches as soon as they are available.

"If immediate patching is not feasible, implement temporary compensating controls, such as restricting and validating Base64 input at the application boundary, enforcing input-size limits, and limiting unauthenticated access," CISA said.

In a statement shared with The Hacker News, watchTowr said it's observing initial attempts to exploit the flaw against its sensors.

"We are already seeing attackers spray the internet and hit our global honeypot network with requests decorated in 'java4shell-recon' User-Agents, alongside blind exploitation attempts using correctly shaped requests," Jake Knott, head of threat intelligence at watchTowr, said.

"Even though this latest vulnerability is far trickier to exploit, and requires specific prerequisites to be in use, where a Java-based application must be running on a particular CPU and JDK, and be handling attacker-controlled Base64 -- this will not stop attackers from trying their luck."

Organizations are advised to identify internet-facing Java applications for patching and remediation. If immediate update is not possible, it's recommended to consider restricting access or implementing additional mitigating controls, like disabling Java's use of AVX-512.

Advisories and write-ups have also been published by a number of companies over the past 24 hours -

In a follow-up analysis, Aikido said it traced the vulnerable Base64 implementation back to a change that merged into OpenJDK on June 25, 2021.

"AI has reduced the bar for finding and exploiting vulnerabilities in the wild, causing mass panic every week with a new Something2Shell dropping," OX Security's Moshe Siman Tov Bustan and Nir Zadok said.

"And while some are actually relevant and exploitable, or just very cool (but impractical) to learn about, (like the *sshole OpenSSH vulnerability), the mass panic causes more harm than good. Security teams fight alert fatigue and the constant feeling of 'everything is important,' but in reality, exploitation of Java4Shell is more like Java4Shell_WITH_X86_AND_Base64_AND_OPENJDK – which isn’t as cool as just saying Java4Shell, right?"

"Teams that really want to keep their systems safe must first check the basics – Are we internet exposed? Is this connected to a real system, with valuable data? Do we meet all of the criteria for exploitation? – before they go into full-on panic mode."

(The story was updated after publication to include the latest insights.)

[13] BleepingComputer - Ukraine, FBI arrest ransomware gang members tied to $40M in ransoms
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 18:08:33 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/ukraine-fbi-arrest-ransomware-gang-members-tied-to-40m-in-ransoms/

[TAM METİN - 1046 kelime]
Ukrainian authorities, working with the FBI, have arrested four suspected members of an international ransomware organization linked to more than $40.5 million in ransom payments from at least 28 companies worldwide.

The arrests come after a two-year investigation into a cybercrime organization comprising at least 27 individuals from Ukraine, Kazakhstan, Russia, and other countries.

Investigators say the organization was connected to a larger cybercrime network of more than 60 members operating across eight jurisdictions, including former members of the notorious Conti ransomware gang.

According to Ukraine's National Police, the group targeted organizations in the oil and gas, pharmaceutical, manufacturing, logistics, and financial sectors, as well as critical infrastructure.

The attackers researched their victims' financial standing, assets, employees, and IT vulnerabilities before launching attacks designed to steal corporate data, encrypt systems, and extort cryptocurrency payments.

Speaking in an exclusive interview with Ukrainian news outlet Censor.NET, acting National Police chief Maksym Tsutskiridze said investigators had identified at least 28 companies that paid more than $40.5 million in ransoms.

"We have identified at least 28 companies that transferred more than $40.5 million in ransom payments to the hackers, equivalent to approximately UAH 1.8 billion. This is money that has already been paid. We are still establishing the full extent of the damage," Tsutskiridze said.

In one attack, the hackers stole 1.4 terabytes of data from a U.S. company and expanded their attack to other companies within the same corporate group.

The attackers initially demanded $10 million but ultimately received $5 million after negotiations.

As first reported by French cybersecurity news outlet LeMagIT, Ukrainian court records identify several victims of the criminal organization, including Warren Oil Company, Maxxis International-USA, Newpark Resources, and Quantum Health.

The court documents identify Warren Oil Company as the victim of an August 2024 attack in which hackers copied 1.4 TB of data before receiving an 84.5 BTC ransom payment, equivalent to approximately $5 million at the time.

The records also allege that Newpark Resources paid $1.5 million in December 2024, while Quantum Health paid $5 million in June 2026 following an initial $15 million ransom demand.

LeMagIT also noted that the BlackSuit ransomware gang claimed Maxxis International as a victim in November 2024, while Ukrainian court records identify Chaos as the ransomware used by the criminal organization.

The Conti ransomware operation shut down in May 2022, with its members dispersing into smaller ransomware gangs and other cybercrime operations.

One of the groups linked to former Conti members was BlackSuit, which initially operated as Quantum ransomware in January 2022 before becoming Royal.

In June 2023, Royal began operating under the BlackSuit name after testing a new encryptor and carrying out high-profile attacks, including one against the City of Dallas, Texas.

However, Ukrainian authorities have not identified the four arrested suspects as former Conti members or confirmed their individual affiliations with BlackSuit or Chaos.

AI-powered IT support impersonation

To breach corporate networks, the cybercriminals impersonated IT support personnel and used a custom-developed platform with AI-generated voice messages to automate fraudulent phone calls.

During these calls, they tricked employees into disclosing login credentials or installing remote-access software, allowing the attackers to access internal corporate systems.

In one documented incident, an attacker kept an employee on the phone while another attacker accessed the company's systems, with the call ending only after the attackers confirmed the intrusion was successful.

"They created their own platform featuring voice messages generated by artificial intelligence. They called company employees, posing as IT support specialists. Under the pretext of providing technical assistance, they persuaded employees to disclose their login credentials or install remote access software. This allowed them to gain access to corporate networks," Tsutskiridze explained.

The Ukrainian court records provide additional insight into these attacks, describing how the hackers used phishing pages impersonating corporate VPN services, spoofed phone numbers, and remote-access tools such as TeamViewer and AnyDesk.

During the Warren Oil Company attack, the records allege that the attackers tricked an employee into entering credentials on a phishing page, then obtained a two-factor authentication code that granted them remote access to the corporate network.

Once inside the network, the attackers stole sensitive information and encrypted files using ransomware, disrupting the victim's operations.

The gang then demanded cryptocurrency payments in exchange for restoring access to encrypted systems while threatening to publicly release stolen corporate data.

"They would deprive a company of access to its own data and then demand millions of dollars in exchange for decrypting it. At the same time, they threatened to publish the stolen information. Victims were put under pressure from both sides, forcing them to pay the ransom in cryptocurrency," Tsutskiridze said.

Ukrainian authorities conducted 66 simultaneous searches across Kyiv and 11 regions of Ukraine as part of the joint operation with U.S. law enforcement. Four suspected key members of the organization were detained, although authorities did not disclose their exact arrest locations.

During these searches, investigators seized computers, mobile phones, bank cards, nearly $370,000 in cash, and €20,000.

Four alleged key members of the organization were formally notified of suspicion and are currently in custody.

The suspects face charges related to participating in a criminal organization and unauthorized interference with information systems under Ukrainian law. If convicted, they face up to 15 years in prison.

Ukrainian authorities say the investigation is ongoing as law enforcement works to identify additional members of the international network and determine their involvement in other cyberattacks.

The National Police also released a video showing footage from the investigation, including law enforcement raids, seized cash, interviews with investigators, and a suspect being questioned.

The video also contains audio from an alleged ransomware extortion call demanding $15 million after claiming to have stolen more than 1.5 TB of corporate data.
