[14] BleepingComputer - Coinbase blocks $4.7 million in transfers to Elon Musk deepfake scam
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 15:49:32 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/coinbase-blocks-47-million-in-transfers-to-elon-musk-deepfake-scam/

[TAM METİN - 597 kelime]
Coinbase says it blocked $4.7 million in cryptocurrency transfers to scammers promoting a fake giveaway using an AI-generated deepfake of Elon Musk during a Tesla livestream that attracted more than 500,000 viewers.

The deepfake took place during Tesla's "flying car" demonstration, when scammers created a fake YouTube livestream that appeared to broadcast the event.

The fraudulent stream featured an AI-generated video of Musk, cryptocurrency wallet addresses, and a QR code, promising viewers that any cryptocurrency they sent would be doubled.

According to Coinbase, hundreds of its customers attempted to send millions of dollars to the scammers over about two hours, including many longtime users of the exchange.

However, the company says its AI-powered fraud-prevention systems detected the scam and blocked about 91% of the attempted transfers, totaling $4.7 million.

Unlike traditional cryptocurrency theft involving compromised accounts or stolen credentials, victims in this campaign voluntarily made the crypto transfer after believing the livestream was real.

Coinbase explained that this makes scams like these difficult to detect because the transactions are created and approved by legitimate account holders.

"Most fraud defenses are built to spot activity the customer didn't authorize," Coinbase explained.

"That's not what happened here. The customer was logged in, the customer pressed confirm, and the customer believed it was the opportunity of a lifetime."

AI agents intervene to stop transfers

Coinbase says its scam prevention system uses machine learning models to analyze outgoing cryptocurrency transfers in real time, identifying suspicious activity associated with giveaway scams, brand impersonation, and newly created wallet addresses.

When a suspicious transfer is detected, an AI agent engages the customer in a short conversation, explaining the characteristics of the suspected scam and asking questions intended to help them recognize the fraud.

The system also assigns confidence scores to the agent's responses before presenting them to customers, allowing it to determine when additional safeguards may be necessary.

Coinbase says its AI agent had never encountered this particular campaign before but recognized the underlying scam techniques, including promises to multiply cryptocurrency, time pressure tied to a live event, and irreversible transfers.

The company claims the entire detection and intervention process operated automatically, without human involvement.

"What's notable is that the agent had never seen this scam. Deepfake giveaways tied to a live product launch weren't in its training," Coinbase said.

BleepingComputer contacted Coinbase to ask how many transfers reached the scammers and how much cryptocurrency was stolen, but has not yet received a response.

Elon Musk and his companies have long been popular lures for cryptocurrency scams, with fraudsters hijacking verified Twitter accounts, sending fake giveaway invitations via email, and flooding TikTok with fraudulent promotions.

Scammers have also used deepfake videos of Musk to promote fraudulent cryptocurrency investment platforms, while a separate campaign involving compromised verified Twitter accounts stole more than $580,000 in cryptocurrency in just one week.

To avoid falling victim to these types of scam campaigns, users should independently verify cryptocurrency promotions through official websites and social media accounts rather than trusting livestreams or QR codes displayed in videos.

[15] BleepingComputer - Data breach impacts 40 Irish credit unions in reported IT provider hack
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 14:39:33 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/data-breach-impacts-40-irish-credit-unions-in-reported-it-provider-hack/

[TAM METİN - 697 kelime]
A reported cyberattack on Wellington IT has exposed sensitive information belonging to members of 50 credit unions, the majority of which are located in Ireland.

Wellington IT is a Belfast-based supplier whose services are used by about 50 credit unions, including 43 in Ireland.

While the company has not publicly disclosed a security incident, Minister of State Robert Troy confirmed to RTÉ that Wellington IT is the third-party service provider linked to the cybersecurity incident involving credit unions, resulting in the exposure of data from about 40 credit unions in Ireland.

The company reportedly notified affected organizations of a data breach that posed a risk of financial fraud, impersonation, and targeted phishing to their members.

According to the Irish Examiner, which saw the notices, Wellington IT informed customers that an automated script made five million requests to exploit an authorization flaw in the company's infrastructure.

The attack unfolded between October 2 and 4, 2026, and the attacker accessed private records using valid credentials.

However, the firm said it lacked activity logs that would specify which member data was compromised and therefore cannot verify the impact on individual payees.

Minister R. Troy characterized the massive delay in Wellington IT notifying the credit unions as "not acceptable."

At this time, the total number of impacted individuals is unknown.

Sensitive information exposed

At least six credit unions have independently disclosed impact linked to the incident, including Capital Credit Union, Phoenix Credit Union, St. Canice's Credit Union, First South Credit Union, Malahide Credit Union, and Limerick & District Credit Union.

The announcements confirmed unauthorized access to payee information, with some noting that members' login credentials and funds haven't been affected, while others are working to determine exactly what was exposed.

The notices list the following payee records saved in online banking platforms as potentially exposed:

Payee name or name associated with the payee's bank account

Nickname the member used to save the payee, if provided

International Bank Account Number (IBAN)

Bank Identifier Code (BIC)

Payee address information

Payment reference information entered for the saved payee

The records could also reveal which credit union member saved a particular payee.

Account passwords, PINs, payment card details, and online banking credentials were unaffected, but union members are advised to remain vigilant for phishing and impersonation attempts.

BleepingComputer has contacted Wellington IT to learn more about the incident, and we will update this post with their response once we receive it.

Update 19/11 - Wellington IT CEO Colin Mitchell sent BleepingComputer the below statement:

“Wellington IT recently notified its credit union customers of a cyber security incident potentially involving certain information maintained within its CU Online platform. As soon as we confirmed the potential risk to customer data, we alerted our customers.

We have taken steps to address the issue and engaged external cybersecurity experts to assist with our investigation and response. This investigation remains ongoing as we work to confirm the nature and scope of the incident, including any information that may have been involved.

We are responding to customer inquiries as quickly as possible and we are collaborating fully with both regulators and law enforcement including the Data Protection Commissioner (DPC), the National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC), and the Garda National Cyber Crime Bureau (GNCCB).

We are directly supporting our customers in their assessment of the potential impact on affected individuals and to help them meet any applicable regulatory obligations.

We recognise the importance of this matter to our customers and their members and are treating it with the utmost seriousness. We remain focused on completing a thorough investigation and communicating directly with our customers as the investigation progresses.”

[16] BleepingComputer - From Deepfakes to Helpdesk Impersonation: Why Stronger Verification Matters
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 10:00:10 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/from-deepfakes-to-helpdesk-impersonation-why-stronger-verification-matters/

[TAM METİN - 1084 kelime]
In 2024, an employee at engineering firm Arup was tricked into transferring $25 million after attending a fake video conference featuring deepfake versions of their colleagues.

A year later, Marks & Spencer (M&S) suffered a major cyberattack after attackers used social engineering to gain access through a third-party helpdesk.

More recently, attackers have impersonated IT support staff to convince employees to hand over remote access to their devices.

While the tactics differ, these incidents raise an important question for IT and security teams: how can organizations be confident that someone requesting access or authorizing a sensitive action is who they claim to be?

We examine these attacks and explore how stronger identity verification and more secure workflows can help organizations defend against similar threats.

The Arup Deepfake Fraud

A finance employee at engineering firm Arup's Hong Kong office received an email in January 2024 that appeared to come from the company's UK-based CFO, requesting a series of transactions. Initially suspicious, the employee was convinced the request was genuine after joining a video conference featuring what appeared to be the CFO and several colleagues.

However, the meeting was a deepfake. According to Hong Kong police, the attackers had used publicly available video and audio to create a convincing pre-recorded conference.

The employee subsequently made multiple transfers, losing approximately HK$200 million (US$25.6 million).

In April 2025, British retailer Marks & Spencer (M&S) suffered a major cyberattack that disrupted operations for weeks. Online orders were suspended, contactless payments and Click & Collect services were affected, and customer data was stolen.

M&S initially estimated the incident would reduce its 2025/26 operating profit by around £300 million before mitigation.

The attack was linked to the Scattered Spider cybercriminal group, known for using social engineering to gain access to corporate networks.

M&S later confirmed that attackers had gained entry by deceiving employees at a third-party contractor, rather than exploiting a technical vulnerability in its systems.

Secure your Active Directory passwords with Specops Password Policy

Verizon’s Data Breach Investigation Report found stolen credentials are involved in 44.7% of breaches. 
 
Effortlessly secure Active Directory with compliant password policies, blocking 4+ billion compromised passwords, boosting security, and slashing support hassles!

Microsoft recently reported a cyberattack campaign in which threat actors impersonated IT support staff through Microsoft Teams, tricking employees into handing over remote access to their devices.

The attackers contacted employees through external Teams messages or calls, posing as internal IT personnel. Using convincing pretexts such as urgent technical issues, they persuaded employees to grant remote control through screen sharing or legitimate support tools like Quick Assist.

Once connected, the attackers used PowerShell to install malicious software, establish persistent access and gather information about the compromised network.

Microsoft also observed attempts to move laterally towards critical infrastructure, including domain controllers and certificate authorities.

The Common Thread: Trust Replacing Verification

Impersonation Exploits Familiar Trust Signals

Social engineering has long been a key technique in attackers’ arsenals. They don't need sophisticated malware or technical exploits to compromise an organization if they can convince someone that they're a trusted colleague.

Deepfake technology makes this particularly concerning, as familiar faces and voices can be recreated.

But even without AI, attackers can use convincing pretexts and trusted communication platforms to make fraudulent requests appear legitimate.

Trusted Processes Can Become Attack Vectors

Password resets and remote IT support are routine activities, but they can also give attackers opportunities to gain access if requests aren't properly verified.

Third-party providers expand the potential attack surface; organizations may have strong controls over their own systems but less visibility into how external support teams verify identities and authorize sensitive actions.

Employees Can't Be Expected to Spot Every Impersonation Attempt

Security awareness training helps employees recognize suspicious requests, but even well-trained staff can be deceived by convincing impersonation attempts.

Attackers will carry out reconnaissance beforehand to gather information that makes their social engineering attempts appear like routine requests.

Organizations need reliable verification processes that support employees when they're asked to make security-sensitive decisions, rather than leaving them to judge whether someone can be trusted.

How Can Organizations Mitigate These Risks?

Strengthen Identity Verification at the Service Desk

Rather than relying on security questions or personal information that attackers may have obtained, service desk teams should use trusted authentication methods to confirm callers' identities.

Specops Secure Service Desk helps organizations build this verification into their service desk processes. Agents can verify callers using more than 15 MFA factors, including options that don't require a phone, with integrations for existing identity platforms such as Duo and Okta.

This helps ensure that sensitive account recovery actions are supported by reliable identity checks, reducing opportunities for attackers to exploit service desk procedures.

To see it in action, explore our interactive walkthrough and discover how agents can securely verify callers before carrying out sensitive account actions.

Build Verification Into Business Workflows

As organizations introduce self-service portals, internal chatbots and automated IT processes, it's important that these systems follow the same verification standards as traditional support channels.

A password reset request made through an internal chatbot, for example, should still require strong identity verification before the request is processed.

It’s here that solutions like the Specops Authentication API can help, by allowing organizations to add secure workforce authentication and identity verification to their existing applications, automated processes and business workflows.

By integrating verification directly into these processes, organizations can apply consistent security checks at critical points without relying entirely on manual intervention.

Independently Verify Sensitive Requests

For financial transactions, privileged access, and other sensitive requests, organizations should require independent confirmation through trusted channels.

This might include contacting the requester using previously verified details or introducing additional authentication into approval workflows.

Verify IT Support Requests Before Granting Access

Where support processes are managed through internal applications or automated workflows, additional identity verification can help ensure that sensitive requests are made by authorized users.

These measures should be supported by broader security controls like phishing-resistant MFA and least-privilege access, while monitoring for suspicious account activity.

Make Strong Verification Part of Everyday Security

As social engineering tactics become more convincing, organizations can't rely on employees recognizing every fraudulent request. Strong identity verification, independent approval processes and clear security procedures can help reduce the risks.

By building these checks into service desks, applications and everyday workflows, organizations make it harder for attackers to turn a convincing impersonation into a successful compromise.

[TAM METİN - 457 kelime]
The Alliance for Creativity and Entertainment (ACE) has announced the shutdown of Dizigom, one of Turkey's largest piracy sites, after a settlement agreement with the site's operators.

Before being taken offline, the illegal TV and movie streaming service's websites had roughly 21 million visitors each month.

The investigation into Dizigom's operations began three years ago, in 2023, with Turkish law enforcement authorities helping identify the platform's operators and their assets.

Dizigom's domains now redirect visitors to ACE's "Watch Legally" portal, where they can find out more about legitimate streaming sources.

​ACE is backed by more than 50 major television networks and film studios, including Disney, Warner Bros., Netflix, Paramount, Sony Pictures, and Universal Pictures, and it focuses on taking down illegal streaming services through civil litigation, criminal referrals, and cease-and-desist operations.

In recent years, the coalition targeted a string of large-scale illegal streaming networks in joint operations with various law enforcement organizations worldwide, including Europol, Interpol, and the US Department of Justice.

"By identifying the people and infrastructure behind piracy services, we can disrupt the networks that allow them to operate and reemerge," added Larissa Knapp, Motion Picture Association's Executive Vice President and Chief Content Protection Officer, on Tuesday.

"This case reflects the value of our partnership with Turkish authorities and the sustained investigative work required to hold operators accountable."

Earlier this year, ACE took down AnimePlay, a major anime streaming platform with over 5 million users, and dismantled a piracy ecosystem centered on the CINEMAGOAL app, which provided access to various streaming platforms, including Netflix, Disney+, and Spotify.

More recently, it also announced the shutdown of a major sports piracy ring linked to the illegal PirloTV streaming platform, including 44 domains that generated more than 950 million visits every year.

[18] BleepingComputer - Apache warns of Roller RCE vulnerability exploited in attacks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 05:35:05 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/apache-warns-of-roller-rce-vulnerability-exploited-in-attacks/

[TAM METİN - 424 kelime]
Apache has released security updates to address a critical Roller flaw that threat actors are exploiting to gain code execution on vulnerable servers.

Apache Roller is an open-source, Java-based group-blogging server with a much smaller market share than modern content management systems and blogging platforms.

However, while this is a niche product, Apache has flagged the flaw (tracked as CVE-2026-129448) as actively exploited in the wild.

Apache has not yet provided details about these ongoing attacks, including who is responsible and how many servers have already been compromised.

"Deserialization of Untrusted Data in Apache Roller versions 4.0.0 through 6.1.4 allows an unauthenticated remote attacker to execute arbitrary code. The XML-RPC endpoint accepts vendor extension types and deserializes them before authenticating the requester," Apache explained in a Tuesday security advisory.

"This is the same vulnerability as CVE-2026-82384, which listed only Apache Roller 6.1.5 as affected. This issue is being actively exploited."

To block ongoing attacks, Roller users should immediately upgrade to Apache Roller 6.1.6 or later, which disables the extension types and rejects malicious XML-RPC requests after turning off the XML-RPC feature.

Admins who can't immediately upgrade can also temporarily mitigate CVE-2026-129448 by setting "enabledForExtensions" to "false" in WEB-INF/web.xml, removing the "/roller-services/xmlrpc" servlet mapping, and restarting Roller.

According to Apache, disabling XML-RPC in the administration user interface is not enough to block attacks because older versions deserialize the request before checking whether that setting is toggled on.

In April, the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) also flagged a 13-year-old Apache ActiveMQ vulnerability (CVE-2026-34197) as exploited in attacks and ordered federal agencies to secure their systems within two weeks.

More recently, earlier this month, Apache fixed a critical vulnerability in the HTTP/2 module of Apache HTTP Server that an unauthenticated attacker could exploit to gain remote code execution on unpatched servers.

Since November 2021, CISA has added 41 Apache flaws to its catalog of vulnerabilities exploited in the wild, eight of them also abused by ransomware gangs.

[19] BleepingComputer - Microsoft announces Windows Server 2028 with hardened security
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 04:24:31 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/microsoft/microsoft-announces-windows-server-2028-with-hardened-security/

[TAM METİN - 568 kelime]
Microsoft has announced Windows Server 2028, the next major release of its server operating system, at the Ignite 2026 conference on Tuesday.

This new Windows Server version ships with a hardened Server Core configuration by default and a new security baseline that allows admins to relax specific settings.

"A hardened Server Core configuration by default and a new security baseline help reduce the work of establishing a secure baseline from day 1," said Neil Hinnant, Director of Windows Server & I/O Fabrics PM.

"Trusted Launch extends protection to virtual machines, guaranteeing boot integrity across your VM fleet, and Confidential VM support begins a journey to support integrity-protected runtimes on supported CVM hardware."

The list of hardened Server Core settings is extensive and includes:

Identity and authentication: Credentials are hard to steal and hard to reuse: Kerberos over NTLM, LSASS protected, strong passwords and account lockout, no consumer accounts.

Network exposure: The server talks only over signed, encrypted, authenticated channels: SMB signing and encryption, the firewall on in every profile, legacy name resolution and unauthenticated RPC off.

Cryptography: TLS is limited to modern cipher suites, key exchange is hybrid post-quantum (ML-KEM), and stored keys are strongly protected.

Privilege and admin access: Admins approve elevation, powerful rights stay with Administrators, Guests cannot sign in, and installers cannot elevate on their own.

Platform and code integrity: The sign-in process runs only Microsoft-signed code. Signature validation is stricter. The paging file is encrypted.

Remote access and observability: RDP with NLA and TLS, WinRM without Basic, Digest, or unencrypted traffic, advanced audit with command lines, PowerShell script block logging, and larger event logs.

However, the company said it's still working on adding NTLM blocking, hardware-backed platform protection, print and RPC hardening, and more to the hundreds of security settings enabled from first boot.

Windows Server 2028 also offers simplified administration and management at scale through Intune management for Windows Servers, bringing endpoint-management workflows for configuration, compliance, applications, and updates to eligible, persistent servers.

It also improves performance with native NVMe storage enabled by default, NVMe over TCP and RDMA transports, and the ability to boot from ReFS.

Last but not least, it introduces new AI accelerators, including the ability to deploy and configure inference environments, manage accelerated hardware, and support open-weight and custom models (including Windows ML, Foundry Local, vLLM, and Llama.cpp).

Admins who want to test Windows Server 2028 can register for the Windows Insider Program for Windows Server or apply to enroll in the Windows Server Technology Adoption Program (TAP) for earlier access.

"This major release raises the security and access bar, accelerates enterprise infrastructure, and simplifies management at scale," Hinnant added. "It also begins our long term investments extending Microsoft Foundry to bring AI inference to fleets of local token factories, giving customers more choices to put the right intelligence in the right place with the right cost structure."

[20] Security Affairs - Apache Roller RCE Flaw CVE-2026-129448 Under Active Attack
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 20:11:35 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203419/security/apache-roller-rce-flaw-cve-2026-129448-under-active-attack.html

[TAM METİN - 612 kelime]
Apache Roller RCE Flaw CVE-2026-129448 Under Active Attack

Attackers are exploiting a critical Apache Roller flaw that allows unauthenticated remote code execution, prompting urgent security updates.

A critical vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-129448 (CVSS score of 9.8), in Apache Roller could allow an unauthenticated attacker to execute arbitrary code on a vulnerable server. Apache Roller is an open-source blogging platform written in Java. According to the Apache Software Foundation (ASF) the flaw is being actively exploited in attacks in the wild.

The issue affects Apache Roller versions 4.0.0 through 6.1.4. It stems from unsafe deserialization in the platform’s XML-RPC endpoint, which processes certain data before checking whether the requester is authenticated. An attacker doesn’t need valid credentials to reach this vulnerable code path.

“Deserialization of Untrusted Data in Apache Roller versions 4.0.0 through 6.1.4 allows an unauthenticated remote attacker to execute arbitrary code. The XML-RPC endpoint accepts vendor extension types and deserializes them before authenticating the requester.” reads the security notice published on the Apache mailing list. “This is the same vulnerability as CVE-2026-82384, which listed only Apache Roller 6.1.5 as affected.”

The Apache project described the problem in its security notice published on the Apache mailing list:

The maintainers explained that the XML-RPC endpoint accepts vendor extension types and deserializes them before authenticating the requester. This order of operations is the core security problem: the application processes attacker-controlled data before establishing whether the request should be trusted.

The ASF also confirmed that the vulnerability is the same issue previously tracked as CVE-2026-82384. That earlier advisory, published in September 2026, listed only Apache Roller 6.1.5 as affected. The new notice expands the affected version range to include releases from 4.0.0 through 6.1.4, making it important for administrators not to rely solely on the scope of the earlier advisory.

The project has explicitly confirmed exploitation in the wild, although it hasn’t disclosed technical details about the attacks, the threat actors involved, or the organizations being targeted.

“This issue is being actively exploited.” continues the notice.

Security researcher n0mi1k is credited with discovering the vulnerability, while Matt Raible reported it to the project. The ASF recommends upgrading to Apache Roller 6.1.6 or later. This release disables the vendor extension types and rejects XML-RPC requests when the XML-RPC feature is disabled.

Organizations that can’t install the update immediately should apply one of the workarounds provided by the project. They can set enabledForExtensions to false in WEB-INF/web.xml, or remove the /roller-services/xmlrpc servlet mapping. In either case, administrators must restart Apache Roller for the change to take effect.

The experts warn that simply disabling XML-RPC through the administration interface doesn’t fully address the problem in older releases. The vulnerable code can deserialize the request before checking that configuration setting, leaving the application exposed even when administrators believe they have turned the feature off.

The confirmed exploitation makes this more than a routine patching recommendation. Organizations running Apache Roller should identify every affected instance, check whether the XML-RPC endpoint is reachable, and prioritize the upgrade or the recommended workaround. Internet-facing systems deserve particular attention, but internal installations should not be overlooked: a server doesn’t need to be publicly accessible to become a useful target after an attacker gains access to an organization’s network.

The ASF has also warned that disabling the feature through the administration interface alone won’t protect older installations:

“Users who cannot upgrade right away should set “enabledForExtensions” to “false” in WEB-INF/web.xml, or remove the “/roller-services/xmlrpc” servlet mapping, and restart Roller.” concludes the advisory. “Disabling XML-RPC in the administration UI is not enough, because older versions deserialize the request before checking that setting.”

[21] Security Affairs - Scattered Spider Member Gets Six Years for Phishing and SIM-Swapping Attacks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 14:46:34 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203408/cyber-crime/scattered-spider-member-gets-six-years-for-phishing-and-sim-swapping-attacks.html

[TAM METİN - 795 kelime]
Scattered Spider Member Gets Six Years for Phishing and SIM-Swapping Attacks

Scottish hacker gets six years for phishing attacks, SIM swapping and stealing $11.6 million in cryptocurrency from US victims.

Tyler Robert Buchanan (24) of Dundee, Scotland, was sentenced to 72 months in federal prison. The Justice Department says he conspired to hack at least a dozen companies through text message phishing and stole over $11.6 million in virtual currency from individuals across the US. He pleaded guilty in April to conspiracy to commit wire fraud and aggravated identity theft, and he’s the fifth and final defendant sentenced in this case.

Employees who typed their credentials into those pages handed over account access, personal information and company data. The conspirators built a phishing kit that captured those logins and piped them straight to a Telegram channel Buchanan ran with another member. When police searched his home in Scotland in April 2023, they found files tied to numerous victim companies.

Spanish police arrested the British national who was suspected of being a key member of the cybercrime group Scattered Spider (also known as UNC3944, 0ktapus). The man was arrested in Palma de Mallorca while attempting to fly to Italy. During the arrest, police confiscated a laptop and a mobile phone. The arrest resulted from a joint operation conducted by the U.S. Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) and the Spanish Police.

Spanish police tracked the suspect to Mallorca after he entered Spain via Barcelona in late May 2024.

Buchanan has been in U.S. custody since April 2025. Between 2021 and 2023, he and his group targeted companies and individuals across several sectors. They sent large SMS phishing campaigns that mimicked trusted services and tricked employees into sharing credentials and personal data. Using this access, they broke into corporate systems and stole sensitive information, including intellectual property and user data.

“The SMS phishing messages contained links to phishing websites designed to look like legitimate websites of a victim company or a contracted IT or BPO supplier. The websites then lured the recipient into providing confidential information, including personal identifying information (PII), account usernames, and passwords.” reads the press release published by DoJ.

“As part of the scheme, the conspirators created a phishing kit that captured login credentials entered the fraudulent phishing websites by a victim company’s employees. The stolen credentials were then transmitted to an online Telegram channel administered by Buchanan and another co-conspirator.”

Buchanan admitted he kept files linked to many victim companies at his home in Scotland. He and his accomplices used stolen data from corporate breaches to target individuals and access their cryptocurrency accounts and wallets, stealing millions. To do this, they broke into online accounts and carried out SIM swap attacks, taking control of victims’ phone numbers and intercepting two-factor authentication codes sent via SMS or calls. This let them bypass security protections and fully access accounts. Investigators also found sensitive data on his devices, including names, addresses, login credentials, and even cryptocurrency seed phrases tied to victims’ wallets, showing how the group combined corporate intrusions and personal targeting to maximize profits.

“Buchanan further admitted that he and several co-conspirators used the information stolen from company intrusions to identify and gain access to virtual currency accounts and wallets belonging to individual victims to steal millions of dollars’ worth of virtual currency.” continues press release.

Noah Michael Urban, also known as “Sosa” and “Elijah,” received a 10-year prison sentence and must pay $13 million in restitution after pleading guilty to fraud charges. Ahmed Hossam Eldin Elbadawy and Evans Onyeaka Osiebo each got 45 months, and Joel Martin Evans got two years.

The cybercrime group Scattered Spider is suspected of hacking into hundreds of organizations over the past two years, including Twilio, LastPass, DoorDash, and Mailchimp.

Scattered Spider members are part of a broader cybercriminal community called “The Com,” where hackers brag about high-profile cyber thefts, typically initiated through social engineering tactics like phone, email, or SMS scams to gain access to corporate networks.

“Those thefts had severe, life-altering consequences for many victims and underscore both the seriousness and far-reaching impact of this conspiracy,” prosecutors argued in a sentencing memorandum.

A restitution hearing is set for 11 December.

The investigation pulled in the FBI, the US Attorney’s Office for the Eastern District of North Carolina, the Justice Department’s Office of International Affairs, Police Scotland, Spain’s Cuerpo Nacional de Policía, and FBI field offices in four cities. SIM swapping defeats SMS-based two-factor authentication specifically, so an authenticator app or a hardware key closes that door even when a text message doesn’t look suspicious. The case is a reminder that your phone number is a password too, and one you can’t change as easily.

[22] Security Affairs - Norwegian Gas Giant Equinor Says It’s Under Cyberattack. Production Keeps Running
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 11:36:00 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203394/hacking/norwegian-gas-giant-equinor-says-its-under-cyberattack-production-keeps-running.html

[TAM METİN - 374 kelime]
Norwegian Gas Giant Equinor Says It’s Under Cyberattack. Production Keeps Running

Norwegian Gas Giant Equinor confirms cyberattack on IT systems, with no impact on production or confirmed data theft.

Equinor, Europe’s largest gas supplier, said on 17 November it had detected an ongoing cyberattack against some IT systems. The Norwegian company said the attack had not affected operations and production, and it had taken steps to neutralise it.

“Equinor has detected an ongoing cyber-attack against certain IT systems. Relevant resources have been mobilised to neutralise the attack and regain full integrity.” reads the statement published by the company. “As this is not a virus attack, there is no risk of the attack spreading. At present, there is no indication that the attack has consequences for employees, partners or suppliers. Production remains unaffected.”

The energy giant said there was also no indication data had been stolen. A company spokesperson declined to give further details.

The disruption reached everyday work, not the wells. Norwegian news agency NTB reported, citing the company, that IT applications and internal systems were affected, and a person at Equinor told Reuters the internal email system was down.

Norway’s National Security Authority (NSM) said it was aware of the attack and was ready to provide support if needed. Last month, the agency raised its cyber threat level to four on a five-level scale. It warned that cyberattacks could disrupt critical services such as water and electricity. NSM also reported that successful cyberattacks against Norwegian companies had tripled in the previous six months.

“The incident currently being ⁠experienced ​by Equinor confirms that this risk ​is indeed high,” the NSM spokesperson said on Tuesday.

This isn’t Norway’s first brush with this kind of pressure this year. In August, a pro-Russian group claimed a cyberattack that disrupted multiple Norwegian government digital services for several days. Separate claims from that period don’t establish who’s behind this week’s attack on Equinor, and nobody has named a group yet.

Treat the gap between claim and evidence as the story for now. A company confirming an attack and ruling out operational impact is a fact; who did it and why is not, until someone shows proof.

[23] Security Affairs - 65 Characters to RCE: Inside the Java4Shell Vulnerability
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 10:45:57 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203387/hacking/65-characters-to-rce-inside-the-java4shell-vulnerability.html

[TAM METİN - 992 kelime]
65 Characters to RCE: Inside the Java4Shell Vulnerability

Calif disclosed Java4Shell, a critical OpenJDK Base64 bug enabling pre-auth RCE in Jenkins, Jira, Keycloak and more. Patches are out. Update now.

Calif researchers disclosed Java4Shell, a memory-corruption bug, tracked as CVE-2026-114938 (CVSS score of 9.2), in OpenJDK’s Base64 decoder. OpenJDK is the world’s most common Java runtime. OpenJDK (Open Java Development Kit) is the open-source implementation of the Java platform. It provides the tools and components needed to develop and run Java applications, including the Java Virtual Machine (JVM), libraries and development tools. It is widely used in enterprise systems, servers and cloud environments.

Base64 shows up everywhere: authentication, file uploads, network messages. To decode it fast, OpenJDK uses special x86-64 instructions that process many bytes at once, and the bug lives in that hand-written machine code. A single malformed string triggers it: 64 “A” characters followed by one equals sign, or 128 on older OpenJDK 17.

The flaw sits in how the decoder handles padding at the end of a Base64 string. When the trailing characters are “=”, “==” or similar, the code can compute a negative output length, and that underflow turns into a write of all 64 bytes instead of the few that should go out. For a 65-character trigger, OpenJDK allocates a 50-byte buffer and ends up writing 62 bytes past its end.

“At runtime, the decoded buffer length is calculated as (ceil(inp_len / 4) * 3) - padding. When the bug is triggered, with 65 bytes ([64 bytes] + '=') as input, that calculation allocates a 50-byte buffer. During decoding the bug makes output_mask == -1 for evmovdqub, so 64 bytes are written starting at offset 48, an out-of-bounds write past the end of the buffer.” reads the report published by Calif. “On OpenJDK 17, the trigger input is 'A' * 128 + '=' instead of 'A' * 64 + '=', because the length threshold to reach the code path is higher.”

Calif demonstrated that attackers could execute code remotely, without authentication, on Keycloak, Atlassian Jira, Atlassian Confluence, JFrog Artifactory, Jenkins and PingFederate, all using their default settings. Remote code execution was also possible after authentication on Apache Kafka Broker, Apache Solr, Elasticsearch, Gerrit and Spinnaker. Applications built with Struts and Spring Boot were also vulnerable. A client-side attack could even target a Java program simply by making it connect to a malicious server over TLS.

The attacker can initially control only where data is written, while most of the data itself is fixed. Calif bypassed this limitation using a technique often seen in browser attacks: corrupting the length of a nearby array so it can store attacker-controlled data. This turns a limited write operation into a much more powerful one. By applying the technique to another array, Calif also achieved the ability to read memory. This allows attackers to expose sensitive data from the Java process before executing code, a technique Calif calls JavaBleed.

Getting two objects to land next to each other in memory took real engineering. Calif used a timing trick over the network, holding back parts of an HTTP request until earlier data was acknowledged, so it could control when the server allocated memory. They even rented a VM in the same region as the target, because a stable, low-latency connection matters more than clever TCP tuning.

“For the current attack path to achieve optimal stability and reliability in exploitation, Calif turned to physics and utilized money to solve the problem, rather than experimenting with TCP/IP stack configuration.” continues the report. “In other words, Calif rented another VM in the same region as our target.”

An AI coding assistant found the bug. Researcher Quang Luong pointed OpenAI’s Codex at OpenJDK’s internals and told it to audit the JIT compiler, intrinsics and cryptography, and most early results were useless.

“It took our own Quang Luong an hour to discover the vulnerability, and two more hours to develop the first working exploit.” Calif states. “The total token cost was less than $200.”

Total AI cost: under $200, which buys you a serious headache for a lot of enterprise software.

There’s no universal exploit here. Every target needs its own delivery path, a writer, a reader and an application action, so a patch for one product doesn’t tell you about the next. Calif published a safe proof of concept that overwrites bytes inside a harmless array, and is holding back the weaponized chains until more systems are patched.

Java4Shell affects OpenJDK 17.0.6 and later, plus Oracle Java SE, Amazon Corretto, Eclipse Temurin, Microsoft Build of OpenJDK, Azul Zulu, BellSoft Liberica, SAP SapMachine and Red Hat OpenJDK. It also needs specific hardware: an x86-64 CPU with AVX-512 VBMI, found in Intel Xeon chips from Ice Lake onward and AMD Zen 4 or newer. ARM64 systems are safe from this one.

Oracle shipped a fix on 17 November as part of its November Critical Patch Update, which covers 655 vulnerabilities total. Update your Java runtime now. If you can’t patch immediately, start OpenJDK with -XX:-UseBASE64Intrinsics to disable the accelerated decoder, or -XX:UseAVX=2 on x86-64 hosts to turn off AVX-512, but treat both as a stopgap, not a fix.

Calif frames the bigger problem as one of unused complexity: features built for a few applications ship to everyone, and everyone inherits the risk.

“This is where AI can help to make that work practical at a scale we could not previously afford.

The same centaur hacker that discovered PHP2Shell and Java4Shell can comb a large codebase and flag dead code, bloated features, and needless optimizations. We call this vibe-uncoding: identify the parts a system actually needs, then remove everything else to reduce its attack surface.” the report concludes.

“With AI, everyone is asking, “What can we build?” But maybe the right question for security is, “What can we delete?””

[25] SANS ISC - Treat Your Honeypot Analysis Like Code: Git, Pull Requests and a Human Gate for AI &#x5b;Guest Diary&#x5d;, (Wed, Nov 18th)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 01:53:48 GMT
Link: https://isc.sans.edu/diary/rss/33534

[TAM METİN - 1058 kelime]
Treat Your Honeypot Analysis Like Code: Git, Pull Requests and a Human Gate for AI [Guest Diary]

[This is a Guest Diary by Kevin Truong, an ISC intern as part of the SANS.edu BACS program]

Introduction
When conducting analysis of honeypot activity, it's easy for it to start as a pile of terminal output. The process is manual; screenshots of the terminal, plus a text file that gets edited in a common word processing application until it is "done". There is no say whether a certain number came from a certain query, what changed between drafts, or who decided to drop a caveat.

While hosting my own honeypot sensor, I wrote up three attacks:

An SSH brute force that logged in as mysql/mysql [1]

A bot looking for Telegram Desktop sessions and SMS modems [2]

A month of RedTail exploits against PHP plus one oversized multipart POST that matches the shape of PHP2Shell (CVE-2026-109618) [3].

Instead of a document folder though, I kept every report in a public git repository [4] and changed it only through pull requests. Supporting me in my analysis was an AI coding agent (Claude Code), which also drafted reports as a separate GitHub machine account. I wanted to give my Claude Code sessions a tangible identity in the code submission and review process, where a dedicated GitHub account could propose changes but never approve or merge them.

Even though I drove every part of the analysis process, the code review of each pull request marked a deliberate step in my workflow which acted as both a gate and a spot-check of the content I was producing.

This diary describes that setup, what the git history shows about how well it worked, and what I would change.

The question this post answers are: can version control and a mandatory human review make AI-drafted security analysis trustworthy enough to publish?

It matters for three reasons:

Reproducibility. An indicator list or a timeline is only as good as the query behind it. If the query and its output are stored next to the claim, anyone can check it.

Public safety. Honeypot reports contain things that must not be published: the sensor's own address, its hostname, its cloud account, and sometimes attacker material such as private keys. Once something reaches a public git history, deleting the file does not remove it [4, SECURITY.md].

AI accountability. Language models are fluent and are confident; but fluency and confidence don't mean accuracy. If an AI is part of the workflow, there needs to be a record of what it wrote, what a human changed, and who approved it.

The repository has one folder per attack. Each folder holds the summary, every piece of evidence it cites, and a hash list:

Every exhibit in a report's appendix shows the exact jq query that produced it, for example:

Before anything leaves the sensor, output is passed through a filter that replaces the sensor's address with <sensor-ip>, and then hashed. Anyone who clones the repository can confirm the evidence has not changed since it was committed:

The workflow around those files is shown in Figure 1.

The important pieces are:

A separate identity for the AI. Pull requests from the machine account are written by Claude Code sessions. It is a machine account I operate, and the README says so publicly [4].

The AI can write but not merge. A GitHub ruleset on main requires a pull request with one approval, blocks force-pushes and branch deletion and has no bypass actors. The AI account has written access, but GitHub does not let an author approve their own pull request, so it can never get a change into main alone.

A public-safety checklist on every PR. The pull request template asks the author to confirm that they read the full diff, that the secret scan is clean, that every IP and account is a placeholder (attacker indicators excepted), that there are no hostnames or local paths, and that attachments were checked by hand. The AI fills this in and I check it.

A secret scanner before every commit. A pre-commit hook runs gitleaks locally.

Options to Close Gaps

Comparing the ruleset with my own design notes [4, homelab/change-control.md] showed where the live settings fall short:

Fortunately, none of these needs’ new tools. These are repository settings and habits, which is the point. The controls that are deployed are the ones enforced by the server (GitHub), not ones written into a file the AI could edit on its own branch.

Students and new analysts get a structure that forces each claim to point at evidence, and a history that shows a reviewer how their analysis improved. This works as a solo project, but the benefits grow exponentially with multiple analysts and reviewers.

Honeypot operators and DShield contributors can publish findings with less risk of leaking their sensor's identity, because the scrubbing and checklist happen before a merge, not after.

Teams adopting AI assistants get a pattern they can copy: a separate identity for the AI, server-side merge controls, and a checklist that the AI must fill in honestly. The record shows exactly where the AI's work ended and a human's began.

Readers of the reports can verify them. Every indicator traces to a query, and every query's output has a hash.

Version control is not new to security teams, where it's commonly seen in areas like detection rules and infrastructure code. That said though, treating something like honeypot/log analysis like code gave me reproducible evidence, a reviewable history, and a safe way to use an AI drafter: it proposed, I reviewed, and only I could merge. The history also kept me honest about the parts that did not work. Those gaps are visible because the work was in git, and each has a simple fix in the repository settings.

[1] Honeypot Attack Summary: SSH Brute Force from 77.239.124.204. https://github.com/kevtruo/security/tree/main/analysis/2026-10-18-ssh-brute-force-mysql-default-creds
[2] Honeypot Attack Summary: Telegram Session and SMS Modem Reconnaissance Bot. https://github.com/kevtruo/security/tree/main/analysis/2026-10-19-telegram-sms-recon-bot
[3] Honeypot Attack Summary: PHP Vulnerability Hunt, RedTail and a PHP2Shell-Pattern Request. https://github.com/kevtruo/security/tree/main/analysis/2026-10-03-php-vulnerability-hunt
[4] kevtruo/security repository (README, SECURITY.md, pull request template,homelab/change-control.md). https://github.com/kevtruo/security
[5] GitHub Docs, "About rulesets." https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-rulesets/about-rulesets
[6] GitHub Docs, "About push protection." https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/secret-scanning/introduction/about-push-protection

[26] SANS ISC - Java4Shell, NGINX2Shell and more: Why we are not at "Yellow", (Wed, Nov 18th)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 19:54:50 GMT
Link: https://isc.sans.edu/diary/rss/33532

[TAM METİN - 790 kelime]
Java4Shell, NGINX2Shell and more: Why we are not at "Yellow"

About a month ago, we went to Infocon Yellow for the first time in a few years. We decided to address the php2shell vulnerability and alert our readers to its significant impact. Following php2shell, we had a number of vulnerabilities and events that may also have scored high enough to trigger an Infocon "Yellow" [infocon]. In the last few days, we had a major Microsoft 365 outage, a critical vulnerability in Java, and a critical vulnerability in NGINX, which was disclosed today.

My plan today was to write a bit about attacks we are seeing against one of our Java-specific honeypots. But the results can be summarized in one sentence: We are seeing many attacks, but none that exploit Java4Shell (yet). Will it be an important issue? Probably. Should you patch? Sure. Will the world as we know it end? Maybe.

So why not go to "Yellow"? This is not a new question. Often people ask why we are on "Green" if they are seeing hundreds or thousands of attacks hitting their system each day. The Internet is a dangerous place. Shouldn't we be on "Yellow" (or "Orange") to reflect that danger? My answer has been: "Green" means the Internet is broken as usual. As long as the status is "Green", you should expect a normal "9-to-5" day. I have heard from organizations that have used our infocon to justify staffing and overtime, and I am well aware of the impact it may have.

The reason behind our conservative Infocon is that you can't be alert and "on guard" all the time. An alert status that is "high" all the time is meaningless and counterproductive. You must not treat these recent vulnerabilities as "emergencies". They are the "new normal". Treat them as such. You will not be able to patch nginx2shell across a large organization before the exploit is released. Instead of trying to do the impossible and fail, think beyond the vulnerability of the day. A new reality requires new procedures and techniques. Here are a few ideas:

Automate patching as much as possible, in particular for less critical systems. Traditionally, automatic patching had two significant drawbacks. First, you may crash a system because of a failed patch. Secondly, the patch may not be applied correctly, and you treat a vulnerable system as "patched".
 Select less critical systems that would be at the end of your patch priority list. I'd rather take a system down myself than have ransomware do it for me. Having a system possibly patched is better than being sure the system isn't patched. And of course, there should be some testing, but that can wait a bit longer. This responds well to the faster development of exploits.

Reduce your exposure. This should have been a priority years ago. But there are still too many systems connected to the internet that shouldn't be connected. Build a defensible architecture. The same is true for individual applications: reduce the "blast radius" by restricting which data and resources each application can access. And reduce your supply chain exposure. Do you get enough value from tools like Optimizely to justify the risk of them compromising your application?

Avoid chasing squirrels. Do not change your plan and drop what you are doing just because the world is going to end. The world may end, in which case, patching one more system is probably not going to make a difference. Whether the world ends or not, at least you will be well-rested to deal with it.

During my classes, I am exposed to security professionals from a wide range of backgrounds, working in different industries and countries worldwide. The one thing they all have in common is that they do not have enough time, tools, and sometimes skills to do their job. Making the job you can't do right now any more urgent won't help. Work on tooling and resources, and develop a plan for applying best practices at a sustainable pace. Embrace AI tooling. It is immensely useful for automation. It is even more amazing at helping you get organized. Many partitioners are afraid that AI will do more harm than good, and they are not wrong. But use it where it helps you most. Are you great at writing little scripts but horrible at tracking and inventorying them? Give AI the job you suck at and develop a rapport with your tools. But embracing automation does include embracing AI tooling.

Finally, I got a lot of positive feedback on the final "conclusion" of yesterday's podcast, so I am going to link to it directly here:

[php2shell] https://isc.sans.edu/diary/33440
[infocon] https://isc.sans.edu/infocon.html

--
Johannes B. Ullrich, Ph.D. , Dean of Research, SANS.edu

[27] Recorded Future - Recorded Future Launches New Automation Capabilities for Tech Stack Watch List
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 00:00:00 GMT
Link: https://www.recordedfuture.com/blog/tech-stack-automation

[TAM METİN - 738 kelime]
Recorded Future Launches New Automation Capabilities for Tech Stack Watch List

Recorded Future Launches the Tech Stack Watch List Connector

Today, Recorded Future is announcing the Tech Stack Watch List Connector, an automation that helps to keep a customer’s Tech Stack Watch List in sync with the products running in their environment. The connector pulls from security tools customers already use, including Tenable Vulnerability Management, Recorded Future Attack Surface Intelligence, and coming soon for Microsoft Defender XDR.

A Tech Stack Watch List that keeps pace with your environment

Watch Lists are how security teams tell Recorded Future what matters to them. The more accurate the list, the more relevant the intelligence can be that comes back. Accuracy depends on keeping the list current, and the Tech Stack Watch List has the most moving parts. Software is installed, patched, and retired constantly.

The information needed to track those changes already lives in the tools security teams run every day. Moving it into a Watch List by hand works, but exports and CSV uploads take time analysts could spend on the intelligence itself.

Introducing the Tech Stack Watch List Connector

The Tech Stack Watch List Connector is designed to put that data to work. It draws on software discovery data from tools a team has already deployed, populates the Tech Stack Watch List from it, and keeps the list in sync on a schedule the team sets. That means less manual upkeep.

The connector adds to Recorded Future’s Watch List automation capabilities. Customers can already automate their IP, Domain, and Vulnerability Watch Lists, including with the Watch List Connector for Tenable Vulnerability Management, and the Tech Stack Watch List now joins them. Together, these connectors help teams get more from their Watch Lists with less manual effort.

What this means for security teams

When the Tech Stack Watch List already holds the products in the environment, Recorded Future can surface early warning on at-risk products at disclosure, rather than after a detection ships and a scan completes. For vulnerability management leads and security operations analysts, that may shorten the window in which a team is exposed without knowing it.

New software can show up in the Tech Stack Watch List without a ticket or a spreadsheet, so analysts spend more of their time on the intelligence instead of the list that feeds it.

The connectors also pair well with the Vulnerability Watch List. For customers running both, one feeds in the vulnerabilities the scanner found and the other the products it saw. Used side by side, they tie more of the internal environment to the external threat picture.

In the portal, navigate to the Integration Center, find the connector for your tool, enable the connector, and choose how often to sync. After the first sync, review the Tech Stack Watch List to confirm it reflects your environment.

Learn more about Recorded Future’s Cyber Operations, browse our integrations, or request a demo.

Frequently Asked Questions

Who gets access to the Tech Stack Watch List Connector, and when is it available?

Customers with the Vulnerability Intelligence Module, Threat Intelligence Module, or Cyber Operations solution can use the connector as part of their current pricing and packaging at no additional cost. Availability varies by integration as connectors roll out, so see the support article for the current list.

How is this different from the Vulnerability Watch List connectors?

Vulnerability Watch List connectors add the CVEs your scanner has detected. The Tech Stack Watch List Connector adds the products and software versions in your environment, giving Vulnerability Intelligence and Threat Intelligence another way to report on what matters to you.

If my Tech Stack Watch List is already populated, will the connector update what’s there?

Yes. The connector can update your existing Tech Stack Watch List to reflect what’s currently live in your environment as reported by the connected integration. It adds new entities without creating duplicates.

Can I still add items manually?

Yes. You can still add items manually whenever you want. Manually added entities carry a manual tag, and entities added by the connector carry the name of the connector tag, so you can always see where each entry came from.

How often does it sync?

You choose the sync frequency when you configure the connector. We recommend aligning it with how often your tools refresh discovery data.

Where can I find more information?

For more detail contact us with any questions.

[28] Cyberscoop - Consensus mounts that tech outages were fueled by patching of AI-discovered vulnerabilities
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 20:56:55 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/tech-outages-ai-vulnerability-patching/

[TAM METİN - 689 kelime]
Consensus mounts that tech outages were fueled by patching of AI-discovered vulnerabilities

Cyberattacks almost certainly aren’t responsible for the procession of major tech outages over the past week, Trump administration officials say  — but there’s a growing sense that patching fueled by a race to close vulnerabilities uncovered by artificial intelligence was a contributing factor.

The top Democrats on the House and Senate intelligence committees are among those observing that the rise in vulnerability discoveries resulting from AI are at least partially to blame for tech service interruptions like those ranging from the aviation ground stop to incidents with major communications and banking systems, rather than the direct result of hacking.

“What appears to be happening instead is a consequence of how quickly artificial intelligence is changing the cybersecurity landscape,” Sen. Mark Warner, D-Va., said Tuesday. “AI is making it possible to identify vulnerabilities across enormous numbers of systems at unprecedented speed, creating enormous pressure on companies and government agencies to patch those vulnerabilities immediately, often all at once, putting tremendous strain on systems that weren’t built to handle that pace of change.”

And Rep. Jim Himes, D-Conn., said Wednesday that the trend is likely to continue.

“It is possible that the increased pace of vulnerability discovery and patching could cause more frequent outages, but the breakneck pace of patching we have seen in recent months is vital to shore up the security of all IT infrastructure,” he said. “Disruptions in networks, whether through internal errors or outside attack[s], are a fact of life, but we can do much more to be prepared and resilient.”

At a House Homeland Security Committee hearing Wednesday, Trump administration witnesses repeatedly cited patching, rather than hacking, as a cause of the outages, although they didn’t specify that the patches were a result of AI unearthing vulnerabilities.

“We are in an environment where the rate of patching, the rate of upgrading of systems, is increasing,” said Jay Clayton, director of national intelligence. “That is a result of the advancements in compute, and in applications.”

But lawmakers were more explicit. The Washington Sun reported that Wednesday that during Hill briefings, industry and government officials cited patching in response to AI-discovered vulnerabilities as the cause. Rep. James Walkinshaw, D-Va., responded at Wednesday’s hearing to Clayton testifying that the incidents were “unrelated” to one another.

“The recent tech outages are not coincidences, and they are connected to cybersecurity,” Walkinshaw said. As cyberattacks are “exploding,” everyone is “frantically patching systems.”

Walkinshaw noted that as attacks have been on the rise, the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency has had its personnel and budget dramatically cut.

Department of Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said that despite the cuts, CISA is “able to meet our mission right now because of new technology that has come in,” but he didn’t say what technology he meant.

Mullin also said he didn’t have any update for lawmakers about the pace of CISA hiring, as committee members from both sides of the aisle pressed him to increase personnel at the agency.

Clayton and Mullin, as well as FBI Deputy Director Christopher Raia, repeatedly said that so far, there remains no evidence that malicious cyberattacks had anything to do with the outages. They also said that there was no sign that cyber adversaries were seizing on the time necessary to implement patching to conduct attacks.

[29] Cyberscoop - Senate Republicans advance FCC nominee, slate of cyber bills
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 19:16:11 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/danielle-thumann-severs-fcc-nomination-tech-bills/

[TAM METİN - 908 kelime]
Senate Republicans advance FCC nominee, slate of cyber bills

Republicans in the Senate Commerce Committee advanced the nomination of Danielle Thumann Severs to serve as a commissioner at the Federal Communications Commission.

Alongside Severs’ nomination, the GOP majority also voted favorably on a slew of tech and cybersecurity bills. But Democrats – who will wield the majority gavel in January – boycotted the hearing, throwing the fate of the bills into question, even as many had bipartisan sponsorship.

If confirmed, Thumann Severs would give Republicans and chair Brendan Carr a 3-1 majority on the commission, with Biden administration holdover Anna Gomez the only remaining Democrat. The Trump administration has not moved to fire Gomez as it did two Democratic commissioners on the Federal Trade Commission last year. But he also broke with precedent by declining to name a Democratic-appointee alongside Thumann Severs to round out the five-member commission.

In a hearing room that sat half-empty on the Democratic side, committee chair Sen. Ted Cruz, R-Texas defended the decision to move forward with Thumann Severs’ nomination after resounding losses in the midterm elections.

“What it should tell us is that the Democrats have zero intention of processing any of the president’s nominees,” Cruz said. “We are heading into two years of total destruction, total war, partisan warfare is what the Democrats are promising us.”

He argued President Donald Trump is not legally required to nominate FCC commissioners in pairs, and is merely to elect no more than three members of the same party. The nomination of Thumann Severs, he claimed, is “consistent with that requirement.”

He also suggested that President Trump had demonstrated a desire for the commission to remain bipartisan by declining to fire Gomez after the Supreme Court upheld his right to do so to FTC commissioners last year.

CyberScoop has reached out to Democrats on the Senate Commerce Committee for comment. On Tuesday committee member Sen. Ben Ray Luján, D-N.M., accused Thumann Severs of not being forthcoming about her role in support of Carr’s media bias investigations.

Republicans on the committee also advanced multiple tech and cybersecurity-related bills.

The COLLUDE Act, sponsored by Sen. Eric Schmitt, R-Mo., would create a private right to sue social media companies that make content moderation decisions based on government requests. Specifically, the bill would strip liability protections under Section 230 of the Communications Decency Act for social media companies, opening them to private lawsuits from individuals if their account or post bans

As Missouri Attorney General, Schmitt led a lawsuit over the Biden administration’s information sharing with social media companies around COVID-19 and election disinformation. That case eventually reached the Supreme Court, where the majority concluded that the communications were non-coercive and that social media companies have their own, constitutionally-protected incentives for moderating speech on their platforms.

Under the Trump administration and Carr, the FCC has gone considerably further than the Biden administration in attempting to police political speech over the public airwaves, opening investigations into broadcast media organizations that have aired interviews with Democratic opponents and at times likening their perceived disagreement with the federal government as evidence of broadcasting undue ideological bias. Thumann Severs served as Carr’s senior counsel prior to being nominated as FCC commissioner.

When moving forward Thumann Severs’ nomination earlier in the meeting, Cruz and other Republicans gave only verbal commitments to hold the FCC accountable for its mission to protect free speech.

Another bill, the Telecommunications Cybersecurity and Resilience Act, sponsored by Cruz and Sen. Mark Warner, D-Va., would establish a telecommunications cybersecurity working group at the Department of Commerce that would be charged with developing industry best practices around cybersecurity and develop criteria for independent third-party cybersecurity certifications and assessors.

The bill comes at the end of the first congressional session since U.S. policymakers were notified that a Chinese hacking group called Salt Typhoon had systematically infiltrated U.S. telecommunications infrastructure and spied on high level U.S. officials, including then-incoming Vice President J.D. Vance.

But both Congress and the FCC opted against new legislation or regulation to improve the status quo, instead touting voluntary partnerships that they claim have led to material improvements in cybersecurity across the sector since the Salt Typhoon hacks. Earlier this year, the FCC rescinded new regulatory requirements that were put in place during the final months of the Biden administration.

The Chip EQUIP Act, introduced by Sen. Mark Kelly, D-Ariz., and Marsha Blackburn, R-Tenn.,  would require companies that receive federal funding under the 2022 CHIPS Act to sign a ten year agreement prohibiting the money from being used to procure, install or use of semiconductor manufacturing equipment produced by companies owned or controlled by China, Russia, Iran or North Korea. U.S. chipmaker NVidia  is currently embroiled in an investigation over more than $12 billion in alleged chip sales, some of which may have been diverted to China.

[30] Cyberscoop - It’s NGINX’s turn to cause vulnpocalypse headaches
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 18:54:12 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/nginx-critical-vulnerabilities-exploits/

[TAM METİN - 860 kelime]
It’s NGINX’s turn to cause vulnpocalypse headaches

Security researchers disclosed five vulnerabilities Tuesday in NGINX, the web server software that handles traffic for roughly a third of all websites, each with a working exploit. The most severe allows an unauthenticated attacker to run code on the server from anywhere on the internet, and in tests the researchers chained it with a separate Linux kernel flaw to take over the machine hosting the server entirely.

F5, the company behind NGINX, released fixes Tuesday and published an advisory classifying the worst of the flaws, an integer underflow tracked as CVE-2026-123007, as critical, scoring 9.2 out of 10 on the CVSS scale.

The problem sits in HTTP/2, the protocol NGINX uses to carry many requests over a single connection. HTTP/2 uses a credit system: the client grants the server permission to send a fixed amount of data. When a client cancels a request while its response data is still waiting to be sent, NGINX hands back that unused credit without checking whether the total now exceeds the protocol’s maximum. A later check subtracts the inflated number from the maximum using unsigned arithmetic, which cannot go negative, so the calculation wraps into an enormous value. That broken number is eventually treated as a negative response length, producing a memory buffer whose end pointer sits before its start, and a memory copy that spills outside its boundaries.

Rapid7 researcher Stephen Fewer, credited in F5’s advisory along with Wiz Research, Xint.io and Ikotas Labs, built a working exploit against a NGINX 1.31.8 container with HTTP/2 and TLS enabled.

The attack has practical limits: it requires roughly 128 gigabytes of HTTP/2 traffic, most of it control frames carrying no actual data. The demonstration took two-and-a-half minutes on a fast local network, but would need about two minutes over a 10-gigabit link and 17 minutes over 1 gigabit. Because NGINX closes connections after an hour by default, a network slower than about 300 megabits per second cannot finish the attack in time. The exploit also leans on a specific optimized memory-copy routine in the GNU C Library, selected on x86-64 chips with certain AVX-512 features. Rapid7 said other exploit methods may exist but none are currently known.

The other flaws affect different NGINX features, including reverse proxying over HTTP/2 and HTTP/3, combinations of the resolver and mirror directives, and mail proxying. Wiz Research, working with Ikotas Labs, Metnir and Rapid7, independently found two of the same flaws and documented one of them, a use-after-free bug, in reverse-proxy setups where connection slots run low.

Xint, which found four of the five flaws with an automated testing harness and no human guidance, went furthest. A researcher pointed the harness at about 50,000 lines of NGINX code; 90 minutes later it produced high-severity findings, and a working exploit followed within 10 minutes. The team then chained the HTTP/2 bug with a separate flaw in the Linux kernel’s SCTP networking code to reach root, the highest privilege level on the host. F5 said its cloud services are still being evaluated while BIG-IP and F5OS products were found not vulnerable.

“When we used Xint to scan NGINX, we didn’t know what to expect, since it’s a mature, widely deployed codebase that’s undergone years of scrutiny,” Brian Pak, co-founder and CEO of Xint, said in a statement to CyberScoop. “Others have targeted NGINX with AI pentesting, but none found this critical bug, which is a testament to our team and the engineering expertise that’s gone into our AI harnesses. We’re grateful for the collaboration with project maintainers to get a patch released quickly.”

Xint’s Shodan snapshot found 98.9% of 11.94 million version-identifiable NGINX hosts running affected releases, though that counts versions, not confirmed exposure, since it does not show whether each server accepts HTTP/2 or has received a backported fix. Wiz, which validated its findings with AI models, connected the convergence of separate teams on the same long-hidden bugs to AI’s improving ability to trace memory-safety flaws, a capability available to defenders and attackers alike.

The findings come as cybersecurity researchers continue to find critical bugs in foundational software used to power internet-connected software. Just 24 hours ago, researchers disclosed a critical flaw in Java, one of the world’s most used programming languages, that could let an attacker take over servers without a password. Last month, researchers found a host of similar vulnerabilities, leading to some labeling the month as “Black October.”

Senate Republicans advance FCC nominee, slate of cyber bills
