[31] Cyberscoop - Government tech debt is a national security risk
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 11:00:00 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/government-tech-debt-national-security-risk-op-ed/

[TAM METİN - 714 kelime]
Government tech debt is a national security risk

In August, the Federal Bureau of Investigation announced that it detected and disrupted another Chinese state-sponsored cyberattack targeting U.S. critical infrastructure — the latest in a series that includes the 2025 PlugX malware incident, the 2024 Flax Typhoon hack and the 2023 Volt Typhoon operation. The severity and frequency of these attacks should raise alarms because they show a pattern of America’s adversaries looking for any possible weakness in our sensitive systems to exploit, and in many cases cooperating together to do so.

These attacks are often aimed at the federal government’s own networks, and they are all too often successful. The incoming House and Senate cyber leaders will need to assess and address this vulnerability fast.

The math isn’t on the government’s side. Cyber adversaries only have to be right once, whereas defenders must be right across every system, program and vendor. Unfortunately, the very way that the federal government contracts and manages critical systems makes the job of its cyber defenders much more difficult.

Federal systems are often cobbled together over decades, often with unpredictable budgets, shifting missions and procurement rules that favor incremental fixes over true modernization. The outdated technology is often easy to identify. The more dangerous aspect of the problem is the complexity embedded in federal management efforts, ensuring that addressing the challenge will be hard.

Under the guidance of the Office of Management and Budget and the General Services Administration, federal agencies routinely run three or four generations of systems simultaneously — legacy databases sitting beneath cloud environments, with managed service providers and internal teams each touching different layers, often without a shared map of what connects what. Security is often added later, after missions have changed, the platforms expanded, and new compliance requirements emerged.

Federal information technology spending exceeds $100 billion annually, according to a 2025 R Street Institute report, with as much as 80 percent dedicated to operating and maintaining existing systems. The government spends this $80 billion a year simply to keep old systems secure while adversaries invest in new technologies and techniques to infiltrate them. Adversaries now have access to agentic AI systems to automate their reconnaissance efforts, accelerate exploitation of known vulnerabilities and move through supply chains before a patch cycle completes.

The fragmentation of federal networks and defenses makes it easier for foreign adversaries to penetrate systems, and it poses a significant strategic risk. The federal government may not have the network resilience to continue to operate in a crisis or contingency with China or Russia. Congress is starting to pay attention, but because cyber investment is less tangible than a new fighter jet or missile system and is spread across dozens of authorization and appropriation subcommittees in each chamber, it often takes a back seat.

Congress should treat federal technology modernization as a security imperative with the same urgency it has applied to traditional Defense Department weapons acquisition.

Agencies need procurement pathways that allow trusted security technologies to be adopted without multiyear acquisition cycles. Vendor contracts should define accountability clearly enough that, after a breach, the party responsible can be identified in hours rather than weeks. Platform duplication should also be reduced where it expands the attack surface without adding operational capability.

Weapons acquisition reform is focusing on delivering capabilities to warfighters faster and at lower cost. Federal cybersecurity infrastructure acquisition should be held to the same standard. Faster modernization would make government harder to penetrate and quicker to recover. Government tech debt is a strategic vulnerability, and our adversaries are already exploiting it.

[32] The Register - Get ready for another critical PHP security release to drop
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 23:38:12 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/patches/2026/11/18/get-ready-for-another-critical-php-security-release-to-drop/5310911

[TAM METİN - 551 kelime]
Get ready for another critical PHP security release to drop

At least one dangerous RCE bug will get a fix on Thursday

AI found more critical bugs in another widely used technology running 225M websites  
11 hours ago

California tells Waymo to take the next exit after freeway mishaps
14 hours ago

Microsoft's official Python agent framework hijacked to distribute malware
15 hours ago

Cyber insurers now want proof you can actually recover
16 hours ago

AI-powered cyber threats are changing the rules of enterprise recovery
17 hours ago

Apologies in advance to all patch managers and security teams that thought they might get a break this week. The PHP Foundation will release a “critical” security update on Thursday between 14:00 and 14:30 UTC.

While we don’t have many details about the latest vulnerabilities – these haven’t been leaked like last month’s critical PHP2Shell flaws – a Wednesday advisory said the highest-severity bugs in the upcoming release are rated critical.

“The issues rated critical require either non-default configuration or uncommon setups,” the foundation said, urging PHP users to “prepare to upgrade promptly once the releases become available.”

We do know that one of the vulnerabilities is another remote code execution (RCE) flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-115560.

In October, the PHP Foundation told The Register that it had reserved this CVE identifier for another RCE bug, but added it would not get an emergency fix like the earlier two PHP2Shell vulnerabilities because the third one doesn't pose the same risk.

CVE-2026-115560 “requires a very unlikely combination of configuration and specific PHP application code, which we do not expect to occur in practical use of PHP,” Elizabeth Barron, executive director of the PHP Foundation, told The Register at the time. “The issue can be mitigated with a configuration setting. So for the moment the PHP Security Team has only published an informational advisory on the PHP website, explaining exactly this situation.”

The earlier informational advisory urged users to “set session.upload_progress.enabled=0 until the next regular bugfix release, to fully protect from an unlikely RCE vector.”

These unlikely RCE attacks require an application to set “session.serialize_handler to php_binary or php_serialize in its configuration and then switch it to php at runtime with ini_set() before calling session_start().”

Additionally, the session upload progress feature must be enabled, which the PHP Foundation said is very unlikely in real-world applications.

The November security release follows a hectic October emergency PHP patch-a-thon, with security teams racing to apply fixes for CVE-2026-109618 and CVE-2026-109619, collectively called PHP2Shell, before attackers could abuse the memory-safety flaws in the open source programming language running on about 70 percent of websites.

While it took miscreants longer to reverse engineer the PHP2Shell fixes than some researchers had anticipated – and the entire internet didn’t burn down in the meantime – the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) did add the two PHP vulns to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog on October 26.

Suspected Chinese attackers chained PHP2Shell with a Linux kernel privilege-escalation exploit to root 25 servers, including Palo Alto Networks firewalls linked to US government-affiliated organizations, as early as October 23, according to Dream researchers.

The Israeli AI cybersecurity startup said the attackers targeted organizations across the US, Japan, Taiwan, and South Korea, and “likely still” have persistent, remote access to compromised organizations’ networks. ®

[33] The Register - AI found more critical bugs in another widely used technology running 225M websites
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 22:11:04 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/18/ai-found-more-critical-bugs-in-another-widely-used-technology-running-225m-websites/5310887

[TAM METİN - 862 kelime]
AI found more critical bugs in another widely used technology running 225M websites

Open source NGINX joins patchapalooza

South Korea tries to out exam cheats with Extreme Emergency smartphone alert
4 hours ago

Chinese web giant Tencent says new US GPU ban ‘sets everyone back in China’
7 hours ago

Get ready for another critical PHP security release to drop
9 hours ago

Anthropic decides to go head-to-head with GTA VI release, schedules IPO for the same day
10 hours ago

To rank the world's biggest supercomputers, we're gonna need a bigger bench
12 hours ago

Hot on the heels of serious vulnerabilities in seemingly every other widely used software powering the entire internet, F5 today released emergency patches for 10 bugs in open source NGINX.

These include critical remote code execution flaws that let attackers hijack NGINX servers with HTTP/2 enabled, as well as the millions of websites running NGINX, to steal sensitive data and disrupt services.

“At this time, we have found no evidence of active exploitation of any of these vulnerabilities in the wild,” and F5 spokesperson told The Register. “We continue to monitor closely and will share updates via myF5 as we learn more.”

As with all of the other recently disclosed vulnerabilities in foundational technologies like PHP, Apache, and Java, these critical NGINX bugs were uncovered by researchers using AI. One of them has been hiding in the code for at least 10 years.

Tomer Schwartz, Wiz’s chief technology officer of exposure management and AppSec, helped find the bug, along with researchers from Ikotas Labs, Metnir, Rapid7, and Xint working independently.

Schwartz told The Register that this wave of AI-discovered flaws “point to a new reality.”

“We need to treat every internet-connected system as potentially vulnerable,” Schwartz said. “AI has changed not only which vulnerabilities can be found, but how quickly longstanding risks can become real threats.”

NGINX is the world’s most widely used open source web server, handling HTTPS requests and routing incoming traffic to the right application or API. It powers about a third of all websites (around 225 million), and F5 maintains the open source version.

On Wednesday, F5 published an out-of-band security notification that addressed 10 CVEs in open source NGINIX. If you run NGINX, update to 1.31.9 as soon as possible.

CVE-2026-123633 and CVE-2026-123007

Two of the vulns are especially serious as they allow a remote attacker to execute code on the NGINX server without authentication. These two, tracked as CVE-2026-123633 and CVE-2026-123007, both received a critical, 9.2 CVSS rating, and CVE-2026-123007 affects versions dating back to NGINX 1.9.5 in 2015.  Both vulnerabilities require HTTP/2 to be enabled on the affected NGINX server.

All of the security shops listed earlier independently found at least one of these two CVEs - most, if not all, with an AI assist - and reported them to F5.

“Both vulnerabilities are memory-safety issues in how NGINX handles HTTP/2 traffic,” Schwartz and fellow Wiz kid Nir Ohfeld said in a Wednesday blog about the two bugs. “In CVE-2026-123633, a connection's memory is freed while a request still needs it, creating a use-after-free. In CVE-2026-123007, incorrect flow-control accounting leads to an invalid memory copy.”

Rapid7’s team also published a proof-of-concept to demonstrate leveraging CVE-2026-123007 for unauthenticated remote code execution against a vulnerable linux/amd64 Docker image running nginx version 1.31.8.

Xint found three other NGINX flaws, plus a separate vulnerability in the Linux kernel that can be chained with any of the five NGINX vulnerabilities to gain root privileges on the host. The research team dubbed the six bugs “nginx2root.”

The AI-powered offensive security firm also published videos demonstrating attacks using CVE-2026-123007 to execute commands as the NGINX worker inside a container, and then using the kernel vulnerability to escalate to root on the host. These attacks work on AWS, Microsoft Azure, Google Cloud Platform, and bare-metal servers.

AI-powered bug hunting for the win?

"While preparing for ZeroDay Cloud 2026, a Xint researcher found CVE-2026-123007," the company said in a Wednesday blog. "A few days later, a fully automated Xint scan found the other four without human guidance."

All of these newly-disclosed CVEs point to a larger security issue - especially in light of Tuesday’s Java4Shell, found by AI-bug-hunting-outfit Calif, PHP2Shell (Calif was one of the firms that also used AI to find and weaponize these critical vulns in PHP), and a critical Apache flaw under active exploitation .

“AI is also accelerating vulnerability discovery faster than existing infrastructure was designed to absorb,” Schwartz told us. “Even after a vendor ships a fix, security teams still face heavy manual work in terms of locating vulnerable versions and configurations, testing patches, deploying interim mitigations (such as disabling HTTP/2), and coordinating all of these rollouts.”

There is light at the end of the tunnel, he believes. “In the long run, AI-powered vulnerability research will harden foundational software.”

But in the meantime, it’s a nightmare for security teams, vulnerability managers, and system administrators, who are drowning in the near-daily deluge of CVEs to patch before attackers find and exploit these vulnerabilities.

“Continued collaboration between security researchers, maintainers, and defenders is crucial to absorb the speed and scale of these disclosures,” Schwartz said.®

[34] The Register - California tells Waymo to take the next exit after freeway mishaps
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 18:45:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/18/california-tells-waymo-to-take-the-next-exit-after-freeway-mishaps/5310841

[TAM METİN - 537 kelime]
California tells Waymo to take the next exit after freeway mishaps

Robotaxi operator suspends driverless rides on high-speed roads nationwide after construction zone incursions and a run-in with patrol vehicles

RIP to the world’s ugliest car
12 days ago

Uber says it alone can make robotaxis pay, hours after Waymo quit its app
14 days ago

Toyota shows off SoC that stores model weights and its OS on integrated memory
16 days ago

Google says Asian advertisers spending less due to Europe's customs changes
17 days ago

GM cracks the petty cash box for multistate driver tracking settlement
19 days ago

Waymo has suspended driverless freeway rides across the US after California regulators intervened over robotaxis entering construction zones and trying to bypass highway patrol vehicles.

California's DMV banned Waymo from offering driverless trips on its freeways on Wednesday, November 11. The same day, Waymo pulled the handbrake on its freeway services countrywide.

The move affects all areas in which Waymo operates on freeways, including San Francisco, Los Angeles, Miami, Phoenix, and Austin. The Alphabet-owned company has not indicated how long the suspension will last. Waymo says freeway testing will continue with trained specialists behind the wheel.

Jonathan Groveman, spokesperson for the California DMV, told the Associated Press that the organization imposed the ban following two incidents involving Waymo's robotaxis that posed an "imminent hazard to public safety."

On November 5, three of the company's driverless cars entered an active construction zone on US 101 near Burlingame, south of San Francisco.

The second incident occurred the following day, and in footage shared widely online, a Waymo vehicle can be seen trying to drive around highway patrol vehicles conducting a traffic break – a maneuver used to slow or stop traffic.

"The Department determined that Waymo's driverless freeway operations pose an imminent hazard to public safety due to recurring incursions by its vehicles into active construction zones where construction workers, vehicles and equipment are present," Groveman said in the statement.

Groveman said the DMV had already imposed restrictions and required corrective action following similar incidents on May 18.

Waymo subsequently recalled nearly 4,000 cars after some were caught ignoring road closure signs and cones designating construction zones in Phoenix and San Francisco.

A separate recall covered software affecting nearly 3,800 robotaxis over problems including slowing on high-speed roadways and driving into floodwater.

A Waymo spokesperson said that despite California's temporary ban, its robotaxis continue to operate on city streets across the US.

"Safety is at the foundation of everything we do," they said. "Following recent freeway construction zone encounters on US 101 in California that did not involve any injuries or collisions, the California DMV has implemented an operating restriction on freeway operations in the state.

"In alignment with our safety-first principles, we have also temporarily restricted freeway operations across our other operating markets to evaluate our performance. Our ride-hailing service continues to operate safely on surface streets, serving over half a million trips each week.

"We are working with the California DMV and other state officials diligently and responsibly to resume freeway operations."

Other nuisances and hazards caused by errant Waymos include clogging traffic in thick fog, driving into telephone poles, and killing cats and dogs. ®

[35] The Register - Microsoft's official Python agent framework hijacked to distribute malware
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 18:23:37 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/18/microsofts-official-python-agent-framework-hijacked-to-distribute-malware/5310858

[TAM METİN - 587 kelime]
Microsoft's official Python agent framework hijacked to distribute malware

In today's how-could-things-get-worse-for-Microsoft news, it seems someone's GitHub org admin creds have been compromised

Google continues model chemistry experiment with Neon
now

Chinese web giant Tencent says new US GPU ban ‘sets everyone back in China’
7 hours ago

Anthropic decides to go head-to-head with GTA VI release, schedules IPO for the same day
10 hours ago

The wrong setting for AI cache can cost you a lot of cash
16 hours ago

PowerShell 7 heading into Windows as 5.1 hits the end of the road
17 hours ago

Microsoft’s official agent framework package hosted on PyPI has been hijacked and now installs info-stealing malware, multiple security research firms have discovered.

CEO Satya Nadella may be on stage trying to wow investors and customers at his company’s annual Ignite conference, but somewhere in the bowels of a building in Redmond his open source team is likely panicking at what appears to be unauthorized access to an administrator account on Microsoft’s Github organization.

The problem package is Microsoft’s agent-framework, versions 1.26.1 and 1.26.2, researchers reported late yesterday and this morning. According to OX Security, version 1.26.1 was reverted to a safe version, but the attacker later uploaded 1.26.2 through Microsoft’s official PyPI publisher account, suggesting that, at least at the time of their report, the credentials hadn’t been locked down.

Safe to say, but if you find either version in your environment assume you have some account security work to do.

Microsoft agent-framework for Python boasts nearly 200,000 downloads a month and is used to build and deploy AI agents. As of those latest releases, it’s also being used to exfiltrate environment variables, credential files, and other secrets for Azure, Git, Docker, Hugging Face, and other sensitive developer services.

To make matters worse, the package never needs to be imported to do its dirty work – all it takes is a Python interpreter with the package installed to start up – the infected package still pulls the genuine 1.26.0 version of Microsoft’s agent-framework in order to keep itself running and looking legitimate, while in the background it gathers up credentials, encrypts them as a base64-encoded chunk of JSON, and sends them off to a webhook.site address hard coded into the malware.

It appears from researcher accounts that this is made to act fast, as the malware isn’t persistent and only runs as long as the malicious version of the agent-framework remains in a site-packages directory. What it does do, however, is not just look for credentials in the environment: It actually starts calling for new ones and collecting them, too.

In particular, the malware requests fresh identity tokens from GitHub Actions, Azure DevOps, and Azure itself, which are exfiltrated to the same webhook site.

“That combination of passive theft and active token requests makes it especially dangerous in cloud build environments, because even short-lived tokens are useful when they're exfiltrated right away,” said researchers at OpenSourceMalware.

The agent-framework GitHub repo is offline as of writing. It’s been pointed out online that Microsoft’s repo for ONNX Runtime, an AI accelerator, is also offline. Other Microsoft repos remain available, and neither Microsoft nor security researchers have linked the ONNX repo’s pull to the malware campaign.

“We have made some repositories private while we investigate malicious content,” a Microsoft spokesperson told The Register. “Our priority is to protect customers and the broader ecosystem. We are working to review and will take further action as needed.”®

Updated at 1805 with comment from Microsoft.

[36] The Register - Cyber insurers now want proof you can actually recover
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 17:00:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/18/partner-content-cyber-insurers-now-want-proof-you-can-actually-recover/5309325

[TAM METİN - 926 kelime]
Cyber insurers now want proof you can actually recover

PARTNER CONTENT: As carriers tighten underwriting, a backup job marked green is no longer enough. Here’s the evidence underwriters expect

Cyber insurance pricing has cooled. Marsh’s Global Insurance Market Index shows global cyber rates falling for a 13th consecutive quarter, with US rates recently stabilizing after the ransomware-driven spikes of 2020 and 2021.

Part of the reason rates are lowering is because carriers are imposing stricter requirements that determine if you will qualify at all. A major global carrier recently stopped writing law firms altogether because of claims volume, and brokers expect healthcare and managed service providers (MSPs) to be next.

The newest requirement is the one many MSPs are least ready for: proof that you can actually recover. This is what insurers really want to see, and meeting the bar is harder than it sounds.

Why insurers stopped taking backups on faith

In a recent webinar with cyber insurance provider Beltex, the familiar list of requirements came up first:

Multifactor authentication (MFA) on external access and privileged accounts

Multifactor authentication (MFA) on external access and privileged accounts

Endpoint detection and response (EDR) on every endpoint

Endpoint detection and response (EDR) on every endpoint

A staffed 24/7 team where managed detection and response (MDR) is required, and timely patching.

A staffed 24/7 team where managed detection and response (MDR) is required, and timely patching.

Backup is now joining that list, and for good reason. For years it was assumed that having backups and a green job status was good enough. Claims have shown otherwise.

Now, attackers go after backup infrastructure to make their ransomware more effective, and an untested restore can re-infect the production network. Beltex described an MSP that restored a client directly from backup without involving the insurer. The attackers were still in the environment, and the ransom reportedly doubled.

So, carriers are asking for proof. Some want evidence of a successful restore within the last six months, and some insurance applications must be signed by a C-level executive rather than the IT director to put ownership behind it.

Proof of speed matters as much as proof of recovery

Being able to recover is only half the question, because time is where claim costs balloon. Insurers, it turns out, don’t object to paying claims. They object to paying big ones.

Beltex’s favorite example involved an 85-person firm hit through a zero-day firewall flaw. Their MDR solution contained the intrusion in about 20 minutes, forensics confirmed two servers were touched, and clean backups had the firm fully functional within 36 hours. The claim came in at around $85,000.

Compare that with downtime and business interruption: a firm billing $10,000 a day that is offline for a week loses $70,000 in income before recovery costs and claim submission. Yet large servers can take four to 24 hours to restore. That is why underwriters care how fast you can come back and how successfully.

Where you recover is the other half

Speed raises a second problem: where do you restore to? Breach counsel treats the first 72 hours as the most critical window, and incident response (IR) teams need to isolate and investigate infected systems during it.

Restoring into production too early risks reintroducing the threat and disturbing the evidence investigators need. Waiting for a safe place has the clock running. Recovery that is fast but not safe doesn’t satisfy anyone.

Meeting all of this is harder than it looks

Put the pieces together and the bar is high:

Backups you have actually tested

Backups you have actually tested

Recovery times you can state and meet

Recovery times you can state and meet

And a clean, separate place to recover to

And a clean, separate place to recover to

Underwriters want to know whether you exercised reasonable care, so a row of green checkmarks won’t do. Run a test restore. Confirm you’re protecting everything that matters, including the server someone labeled “not in production.” Have the business owner, not just IT, confirm the right systems came back within agreed recovery time objectives (RTO) and recovery point objectives (RPO), and keep the logs, screenshots and sign-off in your ticketing system. It doesn’t need to resemble a government audit; Beltex noted that this alone puts you ahead of most organizations.

Where cloud disaster recovery fits

Cloud-native disaster recovery changes the equation. Cove’s DRaaS solution lets teams stage a pre-recovered VM in the N-able cloud that can be failed over to in about 15 minutes, off the production network. That answers the speed question and the location question together. Forensic investigators can validate and hunt in the sandbox while the business keeps moving, with rollback to earlier points in time. Once everyone agrees the recovery point is clean, workloads can run from the cloud over VPN, and failback uses recovery agents to bring changes home.

Insurance is a financial failsafe, not a substitute for a bad data protection posture. Start by listing what the business couldn’t lose for 72 hours, set recovery targets, test them and document the results. Check the policy itself: a standalone cyber policy rather than a small add-on to a general one, with a limit of at least $1 million, and $2 million as the business scales. And if an incident does hit, disconnect affected machines from the network rather than powering them off, so forensic data survives.

N-able Cove Data Protection, recognized as a Champion in the Infotech 2026 Enterprise Backup Data Quadrant, provides cloud-native data protection for organizations. Learn more at n-able.com/products/cove-data-protection.

Contributed by N-able.

[37] The Register - AI-powered cyber threats are changing the rules of enterprise recovery
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 16:00:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/18/sponsored-ai-powered-cyber-threats-are-changing-the-rules-of-enterprise-recovery/5310515

[TAM METİN - 424 kelime]
AI-powered cyber threats are changing the rules of enterprise recovery

SPONSORED POST: As cyberattacks become more sophisticated, organizations need to look beyond prevention and ensure they can recover critical data quickly and reliably

Cybersecurity has long been a contest between attackers finding new ways in and defenders trying to keep them out. Artificial intelligence is making that contest more complicated, giving cybercriminals new ways to develop and adapt their attacks while putting additional pressure on conventional cyber defences.

For enterprise IT leaders, the challenge extends beyond preventing a breach. What happens when an attacker gets through? Can compromised data be identified, clean copies recovered, and essential systems brought back online without prolonged disruption?

These questions are becoming increasingly important as organizations confront both traditional threats and emerging AI-powered cyberattacks. A successful defense strategy must, therefore, account for what happens after an incident, not just the measures designed to prevent one.

Making recovery part of the defence

This is where cyber-resilient enterprise storage has an important role to play. Rather than relying solely on traditional backup processes, organizations need ways to protect critical data, detect potential compromise,  establish which copies can safely be recovered, and restore those copies as quickly as possible.

Infinidat addresses this challenge through its InfiniBox and InfiniBox SSA enterprise storage platforms, which incorporate the company’s InfiniSafe cyber resilience and recovery software suite.

InfiniSafe combines immutable snapshots, which cannot be altered or deleted during their protected retention period, with logical air-gapping to isolate the management plane from the data plane. Fenced forensic environments provide a separate space for validating data before recovery.

The technology also supports automated cyber protection, allowing security monitoring data center wide cyber security software applications or a Security Operations Center (SOC) to automatically talk to InfiniBoxes and trigger immutable snapshots when suspicious activity is detected. This helps reduce the window in which critical data might be exposed.

For organizations concerned about recovering compromised information, optional InfiniSafe Cyber Detection uses AI-based analysis to help identify cyber affected data and locate the last known good copy of the data for recovery.

Speed is another consideration. Infinidat guarantees recovery of immutable snapshots in one minute or less for InfiniBox and InfiniBox SSA, subject to the guarantee’s terms and conditions.

Ultimately, cyber resilience isn’t about assuming every cyberattack can be stopped. It’s about ensuring that when defences are breached, organizations have the tools and processes to recover trusted data and limit the operational consequences.

For enterprise IT teams, that makes recovery readiness an essential part of the cybersecurity conversation.

Sponsored by Infinidat

Microsoft's official Python agent framework hijacked to distribute malware
15 hours ago

Cyber insurers now want proof you can actually recover
16 hours ago

UK MPs want a watchdog with the power to pull the plug on dangerous AI models, rather than leave the likes of OpenAI and Anthropic to decide when they've gone too far.

In a report shared with The Register ahead of publication on Wednesday, a parliamentary committee is calling for an AI Safety and Security Bill that would give a watchdog powers to intervene while models are still being developed.

It could halt high-risk training and testing, block deployments, or suspend AI services if the risks can't be contained. LLMs are trained on billions of learned parameters and so developers can't always predict what a model will do in every situation.

The recommendations come from Parliament's Business, Innovation, Science and Trade Committee, which questioned researchers, former AI employees, and representatives from OpenAI, Google DeepMind, Anthropic, and Meta.

All four companies assured MPs they wouldn't release models they considered unsafe. The trouble is, they don't use the same yardstick for safety, and UK law doesn't generally require them to get an independent opinion before releasing anything.

"We cannot give today’s AI leaders unfettered freedom to behave like modern-day Dr Frankensteins, creating dangers beyond anyone’s control," said committee chair Liam Byrne MP.

Just as with aviation, pharma or nuclear safety, UK government want to rely on manufacturers' assurance alone, and want regulation.

"Our hearings exposed gaps in independent scrutiny, incident reporting and powers to stop unsafe systems. Once some models are released, they cannot reliably be recalled," added Byrne. "Protection must begin while systems are being built and tested."

The committee's report, Guardrails for Growth: Making AI Safe, identifies ten holes in Britain's approach to AI safety, including the lack of mandatory incident reporting, limited access for independent testers, and inadequate protections for whistleblowers.

The UK already has an AI Security Institute (AISI), which has made a name for itself testing some of the world's most powerful models, but its work depends on voluntary cooperation, and it has no authority to stop a company releasing a model that raises safety concerns.

MPs want AISI to continue its testing work while a separate regulator takes responsibility for enforcement. Developers of designated high-risk systems would have to provide evidence supporting their safety claims and give independent researchers access to examine them. Significant changes to a model's capabilities or safeguards would trigger further scrutiny.

Former AI employees also told MPs that raising safety concerns can come at a price. Restrictive contracts, commercial pressures, and financial disincentives can all discourage staff from speaking out, the committee heard. MPs want stronger legal protections for whistleblowers and compulsory reporting of AI incidents and near misses.

There's a fair bit of money riding on this, too. Using OECD productivity estimates, the committee calculates that AI could leave Britain's economy up to £400 billion larger over the next decade. It also sees an opportunity for the UK to make money from independently testing AI models, building on the expertise already established at AISI. MPs argue that stronger safeguards could encourage businesses, particularly smaller firms, to adopt the technology.

"AI firms must be required to substantiate their safety claims, independent experts must be able to scrutinize them, and a regulator must have the power to intervene when risks cannot be controlled," Byrne said. "Ministers, accountable to Parliament, must decide what risks the public should be asked to bear."

MPs want the new laws on the books in the next parliamentary session, and say Britain should use its turn at the G20 helm in 2027 to get other countries on board with tougher rules for AI testing, incident reporting, and risky model releases.

Whether ministers are prepared to hand a watchdog the power to stop AI development is another matter. The committee's recommendations aren't binding, and the government has yet to commit to the proposed legislation. ®

[39] TechCrunch Security - Palmer Luckey doesn’t deny that he’s a prepper
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 22:04:03 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/18/palmer-luckey-doesnt-deny-that-hes-a-prepper/

[TAM METİN - 748 kelime]
Defense mogul Palmer Luckey — whose $90 billion drone and weapons startup Anduril is flush with government contracts — is still the kind of guy who prepares for the worst.

During Luckey’s conversation with Andrew Ross Sorkin at The New York Times’ DealBook Summit on Wednesday, the billionaire implied that Americans should batten down the hatches in case there are rough times ahead. Luckey himself has taken steps to prepare for a potential cataclysmic event, he said.

The conversation took place against the backdrop of a series of recent large-scale outages — at Apple, at Microsoft, at the NSA, and at the Federal Aviation Administration — that have led to concerns that America may be under attack by cyberthreat actors. The government has said that this is not the case, but Luckey cryptically teased that there may be more to the story.

“Look, all the 4chan schizo posters are like 12 hours ahead of the mainstream media on this. You guys are going to find out what’s going on soon enough,” he said.

Sorkin also asked Luckey to expound on comments made backstage “about what might be a cyber war that seems to be going on around us.”

Luckey described a “mismatch” between the sophisticated AI-powered cyberattacks that exist today and “old, outdated systems that are not able to adopt technology as quickly as it is developed.” He added: “I think people are going to have to pick up the pace. It’s time to start running.”

“What does that mean? Are you a prepper?” Sorkin asked.

Luckey didn’t deny the characterization. “I own a missile base 200 feet underground,” he answered, to audience laughter. “It’s a funny joke, but a missile base is actually a pretty bad way to run from the apocalypse. Everyone knows exactly where you are, and they [bases] are designed to take a hit from a nuclear weapon, a blast overpressure event.”

However, he had some qualifications on the prepper label. “The point is, like everyone should have three days of water. Everyone should have three days of dry food. If you have prescription medication, don’t go refill it when you run out. Make sure you refill it 72 hours before you run out.”

“People used to be taught this in middle school, and now everyone who remembers that is treated like a nut,” he added.

It should be pointed out that preparing for the apocalypse is not necessarily a new interest for Luckey. In 2017, a person familiar with the billionaire described him as a “prepper” and the bunker that was broached Wednesday is one of several decommissioned Cold War era underground bases that Luckey has purchased over the years. In 2024, he revealed that his massive video game collection is housed in one of them.

[40] TechCrunch Security - Palmer Luckey warns AI-powered cyberattacks are outpacing defenses: ‘It’s time to start running’
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 21:34:13 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/18/palmer-luckey-warns-ai-powered-cyberattacks-are-outpacing-defenses-its-time-to-start-running/

[TAM METİN - 662 kelime]
AI-powered cyberattacks are moving faster than outdated technology systems can defend against them, Palmer Luckey warned at The New York Times DealBook Summit on Wednesday. Addressing the recent spate of tech outages, the Oculus founder and Anduril Industries co-founder argued that older systems are struggling to keep pace with the new hacking capabilities enabled by AI and warned it was time to quicken the pace of securing our defenses.

“We’re seeing the natural effects of a lot of these advanced AI tools allowing for the creation of various exploits,” he said.

While government officials largely attributed the latest set of technical issues to companies rushing to patch their systems to protect against the new vulnerabilities AI has discovered, Luckey focused on the underlying security challenges facing organizations using outdated technology.

Luckey explained that U.S. adversaries are currently exploiting the mismatch between new attack capabilities and our defense capabilities. These attacks are being carried out for both strategic and financial reasons, and North Korea was particularly involved, he added.

The interviewer, Andrew Ross Sorkin, noted that U.S. Vice President J.D. Vance had canceled his appearance at the Times’ conference this week because of the recent tech outages, saying he now had to attend meetings about the matter.

Given the situation, Sorkin asked Luckey how worried we should be about all this.

“It depends on how well prepared you are,” was Luckey’s answer. (He then ominously warned, “You guys are going to find out what’s going on soon enough.”)

The tech executive argued that AI could ultimately give defenders the upper hand, but they’d have to move more quickly.

“In the long run, the advantages are going to accrue to the side that is most effectively using AI for defense,” Luckey said. “With the people who say that everything can be hacked — they are right, for now. That actually may be the case with certain new techniques that are coming along. But as of right now, there’s a mismatch between the attackers who are very fast-moving and using technology and old, outdated systems that are not able to adopt technology as quickly as it is developed,” Luckey continued.

“And I think … people are going to have to pick up the pace,” he said. “It’s time to start running.”

[41] TechCrunch Security - More banks say customers using AI agents are on their own — even if they get hacked
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 17:32:10 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/18/more-banks-says-customers-using-ai-agents-are-on-their-own-even-if-they-get-hacked/

[TAM METİN - 809 kelime]
Citibank, the third largest commercial bank in the United States, changed its terms of service this week, telling customers who use third-party AI tools for personal finance that anything that goes wrong is their problem — even if they get hacked.

AI agents, ranging from frontier lab models to purpose-built personal finance tools, can be given the credentials to your bank account and other online financial portals, with the idea that they can analyze spending and investment decisions, offer advice, and even handle transactions and bill payments.

But financial institutions aren’t thrilled with the unreliable AI actors in their systems, and are telling customers that they won’t be held responsible for reversing an intentional or unintentional transaction made by automated assistants.

“As third-party AI tools become more widely available, we want customers to understand the risks associated with sharing banking credentials or authorizing those tools to access their accounts,” a Citi spokesperson told TechCrunch. “We recently updated our Online User Agreement to provide greater clarity about those risks and to reinforce that customers who choose to grant account access to third-party AI agents are responsible for activities conducted through those agents.”

JPMorgan and Bank of America, the two largest banks in the country, announced similar policies last month. And PNC, the eighth largest bank, changed its terms today.

Citi’s update goes beyond telling customers that they are responsible for anything their agents may do; it also informs them that if the AI tool leaks their credentials or is hacked, the customer will be responsible for anything hackers do with their account. Banking giant Citizens similarly emailed its customers this week, warning that they are also on the hook for anything their AI assistant does, according to an email seen by TechCrunch.

As AI startups look for more revenue, breaking into personal finance presents an attractive opportunity for companies to add value for customers and wet their beaks in the stream of fees generated by the sector. But if consumers are already leery of sharing their email or chat records with an AI agent, bank and brokerage accounts offer a new trust challenge.

Anthropic’s approach is telling: The personal finance tool it launched earlier this month is explicitly billed as “read-only” and “can’t move, manage, or invest your money.”

Beyond cybersecurity concerns, banks also view efforts to enter their franchise as a competitive threat. Markets shuddered briefly in reaction to the prospect of AI tools making it easier for consumers to avoid unnecessary fees or low-interest deposit accounts that pad bank margins. But if AI agents become an important way to interact with financial decisions, banks will fight to control that pathway. Citi’s terms note that AI agents it provides can be used by customers without worrying about additional liability.

The reaction by banks is part of a broader trend among businesses of all stripes: AI labs promise that their agents will revolutionize the way people engage with a variety of service providers, then the service providers say “hold on a minute.” We’ve seen it in everything from car-hailing to food service, and how these relationships play out will help determine which AI investments pay off.

[42] TechCrunch Security - British hackers face prison for GTA 6 data breach and crypto thefts
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 14:55:13 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/18/british-hackers-face-prison-for-gta-6-data-breach-and-crypto-thefts/

[TAM METİN - 709 kelime]
Two British hackers are facing lengthy prison sentences for separate hacks as part of their roles in long-running, prolific cybercriminal hacking groups. The two are accused of hacks that stole millions of dollars in cryptocurrency and leaked pre-release footage from Grand Theft Auto VI.

Tyler Buchanan, 24, from Dundee, Scotland, has been sentenced to six years by a U.S. court after he was extradited to the United States in 2025. Buchanan was accused of stealing at least $11 million in cryptocurrency from dozens of companies as part of a phishing scheme. The attacks would impersonate IT helpdesks and call up unsuspecting employees and trick them into giving over their credentials, which the hackers would use to break in and steal cryptocurrency.

Meanwhile, Arion Kurtaj, 21, from Oxford, England, has pleaded guilty to several cyberattacks that he carried out while a teenager. One hack included the theft of data and extortion of Rockstar Games, the maker of Grand Theft Auto VI, which was released Wednesday after several delays.

Kurtaj was previously ordered by a U.K. court to be detained indefinitely after deeming him unable to stand trial due to his autism diagnosis and his perceived high hacking risk to the public. But U.K. authorities, tasked with charging Kurtaj with cybercrime offenses, confirmed Monday that he had admitted to several hacks, including leaking footage of the long-awaited video game, and would subsequently face a public trial.

U.K. authorities confirmed earlier reporting that Kurtaj hacked several companies, including while he was confined to a hotel amid police protection from rival criminal gangs. The authorities said Kurtaj used an Amazon Fire TV stick to hack the companies, which breached his bail conditions.

Both Buchanan and Kurtaj were members of overlapping cybercriminal groups, dubbed 0ktapus, Lapsus$, and later Scattered Spider. These prolific hacking gangs mostly consist of teenagers and young male adults, lending to the term “advanced persistent teenagers,” whose focus and motivations were to steal as much money as possible through various cyberattacks.

Over the years, the hackers were collectively responsible for hundreds of cyberattacks targeting big companies, including Coinbase, DoorDash, Mailchimp, and Nvidia.

By late 2024, both U.K. and U.S. authorities say they had indicted five of the hackers whom they believed were key members of the hacking groups, including Buchanan. The U.S. Department of Justice said this week that Buchanan is the fifth and final defendant to be sentenced in this case.

[43] CSO Online - Vulnerability management: The cybersecurity industry is still solving the wrong problem
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 09:00:00 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4250261/vulnerability-management-the-cybersecurity-industry-is-still-solving-the-wrong-problem.html

[TAM METİN - 1681 kelime]
AI can accelerate vulnerability discovery, but fixing flaws remains a leadership and governance challenge that technology alone cannot solve.

We have never been short of vulnerabilities. We have always struggled with remedial work.

For decades, the cybersecurity industry has approached vulnerability management from the wrong end. The emphasis has consistently been on finding vulnerabilities, cataloguing them, scoring them and, more recently, prioritising them through increasingly sophisticated risk models.

An entire industry has developed around vulnerability scanning, exposure management, threat intelligence and remediation prioritization. Artificial intelligence is now promising to take those capabilities to another level.

But there is something fundamentally wrong with that approach.

The real challenge in vulnerability management has never been identifying what needs fixing. It has always been getting the remedial work done.

Most large organizations already know they have significant numbers of vulnerabilities across their technology estates. They have known for years. They have dashboards, reports, inventories, risk ratings and remediation backlogs.

What they frequently lack is the organizational machinery to translate that knowledge into effective, sustained remedial action.

And that is not primarily a technology problem.

The uncomfortable reality behind remediation backlogs

Consider what happens when a large enterprise identifies a critical vulnerability.

The security team assesses its severity and potential impact. It raises a ticket, assigns a priority, identifies an owner and requests remediation.

On paper, the process looks straightforward.

In practice, that is often where the real problems begin.

The infrastructure team may be managing ageing systems with complex dependencies. The development team may have committed to a demanding release schedule. Production operations may be unwilling to accept additional downtime. Business stakeholders may resist disruption to revenue-generating services.

Some systems may have very limited maintenance windows. Others may require extensive regression testing before deploying patches. Legacy applications may depend on components that cannot easily be upgraded.

And ownership itself may be disputed.

Security teams frequently have the responsibility to identify and report vulnerabilities but lack the authority to enforce remediation. Technology teams control the systems but may have competing priorities and insufficient resources.

The result is familiar: escalating tickets, repeated exceptions, missed deadlines, unresolved disputes and growing remediation backlogs.

None of these problems can be solved simply by producing a better vulnerability score.

Prioritization is necessary, but it is not the answer

The industry has increasingly recognized that organizations cannot remediate everything.

That has driven the development of risk-based vulnerability management, exploitability intelligence, asset criticality models and sophisticated exposure prioritization platforms.

These capabilities have genuine value: There is an obvious difference between a vulnerability that is actively exploited on an internet-facing critical system and one that exists on an isolated, low-value asset.

But prioritization is only the beginning of a management process. It is not its outcome: A perfectly prioritized remediation backlog remains a backlog.

In many organizations, the difficulty is not deciding which vulnerabilities matter most. It is securing agreement on who will fix them, when the work will take place and who has the authority to resolve conflicts or accept the risk.

These are matters of governance, accountability and business priorities.

The cybersecurity industry remains disproportionately focused on the what, when the real challenge lies in the how and the who.

AI is about to make the problem considerably worse

Artificial intelligence is fundamentally changing the economics of vulnerability discovery.

AI-assisted tools can analyse code, identify weaknesses and accelerate vulnerability research on a scale and at a speed that would previously have required substantial human effort.

According to Google Threat Intelligence Group, monthly vulnerability disclosures more than doubled between January and August 2026, while observed exploitation also increased significantly. AI is contributing to that acceleration, although changes in disclosure practices also influence the figures.

The consequences for large organizations could be significant.

We are moving towards a world where the identification of vulnerabilities becomes increasingly automated, increasingly inexpensive and potentially continuous.

But what happens when the volume of identified vulnerabilities increases dramatically while the organization’s capacity to remediate them remains essentially unchanged?

The answer is predictable: Larger backlogs. More pressure on technology teams. More exceptions. More disputes over priorities. And potentially greater exposure, despite unprecedented visibility into technical weaknesses.

The Cloud Security Alliance has already highlighted this growing disconnect between accelerated vulnerability discovery and the capacity of organizations to absorb and remediate findings.

AI is industrialising vulnerability discovery while remediation remains trapped in organizational structures designed for another era.

That is the real emerging challenge.

AI cannot negotiate a production maintenance window

Of course, AI can also contribute to remediation.

It can establish proofs of concept, generate and test fixes, propose configuration changes, analyse dependencies and automate certain deployment activities.

These are important developments, and organizations should embrace them where appropriate.

But their practical impact must not be overstated.

AI cannot independently decide that a critical production system should be taken offline during a peak business period. It cannot assume accountability for disrupting customer services or resolve competing priorities between a business unit, an infrastructure team and the security function.

It cannot simply override legacy dependencies, contractual obligations or regulatory constraints.

Even where AI can technically generate a suitable fix, somebody still needs to validate it, approve its deployment, ensure that the fix has not broken something else and verify that remediation has been successful.

Some of those steps can be automated. Others require judgement, authority and organizational coordination.

The more powerful AI becomes, the more visible those remaining management bottlenecks will be.

And this is where the traditional cybersecurity approach is increasingly inadequate.

Vulnerability management needs an operating model, not another dashboard

What large organizations need is not simply better vulnerability management technology.

They need a remediation operating model that establishes clear accountability across security, development, infrastructure, operations and business management.

That model should address several fundamental questions.

Who owns the remediation of vulnerabilities affecting shared platforms or complex applications? Who allocates the necessary resources? Who can authorize emergency changes? Who resolves disagreements when remediation conflicts with business priorities?

How are maintenance windows negotiated and secured? How are persistent exceptions challenged? Who accepts the residual business risk when remediation cannot be completed within the expected timeframe?

And, crucially, who is responsible for ensuring that agreed actions happen?

These responsibilities cannot be left to informal relationships between technical teams or to the persistence of individual security managers.

They need to be embedded in management processes, supported by executive authority and reinforced through meaningful performance measures.

Security should provide risk intelligence, challenge and assurance. Technology teams should own the delivery of remediation within their environments. Business management should participate in decisions where operational disruption or investment trade-offs are unavoidable.

The CISO’s role is to help establish and oversee that machinery, not to become the organization’s chief ticket chaser.

Measuring remediation effectiveness, not just reporting activity

There is also a fundamental problem with how vulnerability management performance is measured.

Too many organizations remain focused on the number of vulnerabilities identified, severity distributions, compliance with remediation deadlines and the volume of outstanding findings.

Those indicators have their place, but they can encourage an administrative approach to vulnerability management: Closing tickets becomes a proxy for reducing risk.

What matters is whether material exposures are being eliminated within acceptable timeframes and whether the organization can demonstrate that remediation has worked.

Management should pay particular attention to recurring remediation failures, ageing high-risk exposures, operational bottlenecks, the effectiveness of exception handling and the time required to implement verified fixes.

The distinction between a vulnerability being reported as resolved and genuinely being remediated is essential.

AI can help generate more accurate and timely evidence. But it cannot substitute for accountability over the results.

Auditability and regulatory obligations add another critical dimension to this challenge.

Regulators increasingly expect organizations not only to identify and assess vulnerabilities, but also to demonstrate that appropriate remedial actions have been taken within reasonable timeframes.

This requires clear ownership, documented decisions, traceable exceptions periodically reviewed and reliable evidence that remediation has been completed.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery and potentially automates parts of the remediation process, maintaining an auditable chain of accountability becomes even more important.

Ultimately, compliance cannot rest on the ability to identify problems: It needs to rest on the ability to demonstrate that they have been effectively addressed.

The real opportunity for AI is to transform execution

This is where I believe the cybersecurity industry should concentrate its attention.

Rather than simply applying AI to discover more vulnerabilities and refine their prioritization or their treatment, vendors and practitioners should focus on how AI can accelerate the entire remediation lifecycle.

That means understanding dependencies, identifying suitable deployment opportunities, preparing changes, automating testing, coordinating workflows, validating results and providing management with reliable evidence of progress across the whole cycle.

In highly automated environments, AI agents may eventually execute substantial portions of that process under clearly defined controls.

But the objective should not be automation for its own sake: It should be to reduce the friction between identifying a problem and resolving it.

And that requires technology to be designed around a functioning governance model, rather than expecting governance to emerge from the technology.

The technology will change. The management problem will not.

I have argued for years that cybersecurity is fundamentally a leadership and management challenge supported by technology.

Vulnerability management provides perhaps one of the clearest demonstrations of that principle.

The same structural problems that prevented organizations from patching critical systems fifteen years ago remain visible today: fragmented responsibilities, competing priorities, operational constraints, weak governance and insufficient management follow-through.

AI will not make those problems disappear.

On the contrary, by accelerating vulnerability discovery and shortening the time available to respond, it is exposing them more brutally than ever.

The organizations that succeed will not necessarily be those with the most sophisticated vulnerability discovery capabilities.

They will be those that have built the governance, accountability and operational discipline required to translate technical intelligence into effective remedial action.

The future of vulnerability management will not be determined by how quickly we can find what is wrong. It will be determined by how effectively organizations can get things fixed.

And that has always been a management problem.

[44] CSO Online - AI unearths ‘internet breaker’ flaws in Nginx web server
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 23:36:40 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4250460/ai-unearths-internet-breaker-flaws-in-nginx-web-server.html

[TAM METİN - 957 kelime]
The vulnerabilities — including a 10-year-old RCE flaw — add to the recent spate of AI-discovered cracks in foundational internet technologies.

Nginx, the web server estimated to power a third of the internet, has shipped fixes for multiple vulnerabilities, including a critical unauthenticated remote code execution flaw that puts millions of websites at risk and has existed in the code for over a decade. Researchers from several security firms independently found some of the same flaws by using AI agents, adding to a string of recent AI-discovered vulnerabilities in widely used internet technologies such as PHP, Node, OpenSSH, and Java.

The most severe flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-123007, lets an attacker manipulate flow-control windows in the HTTP/2 protocol implementation to trigger an out-of-bounds memory copy that researchers from Xint.io turned into command execution with Nginx worker privileges. The vulnerability affects every official Nginx release for more than 10 years since version 1.9.5, which originally added HTTP/2 support in 2015.

A second HTTP/2 flaw, CVE-2026-123633, is a use-after-free vulnerability affecting releases 1.19.7 through 1.31.8. Both are fixed in Nginx 1.31.9 and 1.30.7, which F5 released along with fixes for eight other vulnerabilities.

Xint’s researchers, who used the company’s own autonomous AI harness, found five of the flaws and discovered a separate Linux kernel vulnerability in the SCTP subsystem that can be chained with any of those flaws to gain root privileges on the server. The company has dubbed the collection nginx2root.

In their tests the exploit chain escaped a Docker container running Nginx and obtained root on the underlying host. The kernel flaw has existed since Linux 4.15, released in 2018, and has no CVE or patch so the company is withholding details until distributions can ship a fix.

The exposure of web servers on the internet is vast with the Shodan search engine showing 56.7 million hosts running Nginx, and 98.9% of those that disclose their version report one in the vulnerable range. F5 recommends updating immediately or disabling HTTP/2 on affected ports, which blocks the two most severe flaws but not Xint’s other findings — CVE-2026-123634, CVE-2026-121810, and CVE-2026-113425 — which are in other components.

The CVE-2026-123007 flaw was reported to F5 by researchers from Rapid7, Wiz, Xint, and Ikotas Labs, while CVE-2026-123633, dubbed Drainbreak, was discovered and reported by researchers from Metnir, Wiz, and Xint. Only Rapid7 senior principal security researcher Stephen Fewer doesn’t mention AI in his write-up. The other four teams used AI agents and harnesses, with Xint’s CEO Brian Pak saying that his team’s platform scanned “50,000 lines of code in just 90 minutes” to surface “a critical vulnerability that gave an unauthenticated user a RCE path — essentially an internet breaker.”

Exploitation is likely

No one has exploited either flaw in the wild yet, according to the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency’s assessment of both CVEs, but every research team built working exploits, and two describe the flaws as readily exploitable.

Xint called CVE-2026-123007 highly exploitable, saying an externally reachable TLS/HTTP/2 port was enough to corrupt the send window, disclose addresses, and execute commands, and validated the attack over public IPs on AWS, GCP, and Azure under concurrent traffic.

Metnir ran its exploit for CVE-2026-123633 with ASLR enabled, taking about 10 minutes, nearly all of it spent on defeating memory layout randomization, and reproduced the use-after-free in OpenResty, crashing the shipped binaries of Kong, Apache APISIX, NGINX Gateway Fabric, and community ingress-nginx, all of which enable HTTP/2 by default.

Rapid7’s Fewer calculated that running his exploit requires transmitting roughly 128GB of HTTP/2 data to the target. That takes about two minutes on a 10Gbit/s network and 17 minutes on 1Gbit/s. The technique also relies on a memory-copy routine in glibc that is used only on CPUs with AVX-512 extensions, but these are common on servers.“Exploitation is by no means a walk in the park, as the vulnerable systems must be directly exposed to the internet with no WAF or CDN terminating HTTP/2,” Yordan Ganchev, principal threat intelligence specialist at watchTowr, told CSO. “Memory corruption can also be tricky, as an effective exploit must properly groom and shape the memory heap and handle different NGINX versions, builds, and configuration variants. And when the system is internet-facing and under load, exploitation can cause more crashes than successful code execution.”

Patch and check what you’re actually running

Nginx Plus users should also update to 37.3.0.1 on the newer release line or 37.0.8.1 on the 37.0 line. The older R33 through R36 release line will never receive a fix because those versions have reached the end of technical support.

Disabling HTTP/2 comes with costs, as services that depend on it, such as native gRPC, stop working. Xint’s other three flaws require specific configurations. The resolver flaw affects setups that pick their backend per request and use the mirror directive, the HTTP/3 flaw affects QUIC listeners, and the mail proxy flaw affects deployments that use proxy on with auth_http. Xint published per-flaw mitigations in its technical write-up.For the accompanying Linux flaw, until a kernel patch is released, administrators who don’t use SCTP can block the module from loading by adding install sctp /bin/false to a file under /etc/modprobe.d.Administrators should also investigate unexpected Nginx worker crashes or commands launched by Nginx workers, Wiz noted.

“The real risk with this nginx bug isn’t the 11 years it went unnoticed, it’s how many teams will think they’ve patched when they haven’t,” Daniel Shechter, co-founder and CEO of Miggo Security, told CSO. “The version number can be wrong because distros backport fixes without changing it, a reload doesn’t swap out the running code, and the attack happens in HTTP/2 traffic that WAFs and access logs never see. If you can’t prove the fix is actually running, assume you’re still exposed.”

[45] CSO Online - A new critical Java hole illustrates the enterprise IT visibility problem
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 20:39:16 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4250390/a-new-critical-java-hole-illustrates-the-enterprise-it-visibility-problem.html

[TAM METİN - 1436 kelime]
Java is so ubiquitous in enterprises that identifying every instance is almost impossible. But if even a few instances remain unpatched, the risk is extreme.

When cybersecurity research firm Calif published details Tuesday of a potentially major Java security flaw, dubbed Java4Shell, it described an unauthenticated attacker’s ability to read Java heap memory, steal private keys, tokens, and session secrets, and reach remote code execution (RCE).

With Java being all-but-ubiquitous in today’s enterprise, coupled with the inability of CISOs to have visibility into every instance of it in their estate, this means that the bug could be a big problem for IT.

Java, which has had its fair share of cybersecurity issues lately, is well-known for safely handling memory. That is why a Java bug affecting memory management is so dangerous. Java4Shell, tracked as CVE-2026-114938, is a vulnerability in the OpenJDK x86-64 Base64 decoder that scores 9.2 in severity (Critical) under CVSS 4.0, Calif said. Oracle assigned it a lower rating of 8.1 (High) using CVSS 3.1.

Calif explained in its post describing the issue that Base64 is a common way of representing binary data as text. “It is used in authentication protocols, to encode file uploads, and to deliver network messages,” it wrote. “To decode Base64 faster, OpenJDK uses specialized x86-64 CPU instructions to process many bytes at once. We discovered a bug in optimized machine-level code that lets malformed input overwrite neighboring memory. With careful exploitation, the corruption can reveal sensitive memory, as Heartbleed did, or lead to remote code execution.”

It said that the corruption can be triggered by a string of just 64 “A” characters followed by a single equals sign (=); in older OpenJDK 17 versions, 128 “A” characters are required before the equals sign.

“Memory corruption is the most common class of vulnerability on the internet,” Calif said. “But one of Java’s biggest selling points is memory safety. Java manages memory on developers’ behalf and checks memory accesses, protecting applications from the memory-corruption bugs common in other software. But like PHP and many other technologies, much of Java itself is written in low-level languages like C, C++, and, in this case, assembly, where memory corruption is still a part of life.”

Assessing the bug’s potential for exploit is complicated because triggering it requires a hardware accelerated decoding function that’s used on some x86-64 processors supporting AVX-512 VBMI instructions. And fixing it requires patches from a lengthy list of vendors; just installing Tuesday’s patch from Oracle, which owns Java, is not going to do the trick.

Potential damage ‘extreme’

Noah Kenney, principal consultant at Digital 520, said the potential damage from an unpatched instance of exposed Java is extreme.

“The researchers were able to read Java heap memory before they achieved code execution, so an attacker may be able to steal tokens, credentials, or keys from those systems. That access can remain useful even after the vulnerable server has been patched,” Kenney said. “Any unexplained crashes on an exposed system should lead to a review of what secrets were in memory and whether they need to be rotated.”

But not every instance is vulnerable to this flaw.

“The same Java application and version can be vulnerable on a newer x86 server and unaffected on older hardware because the faulty decoder only runs when AVX-512 is available,” he noted. “That makes a normal software inventory less useful than it usually is. CISOs need to know the Java distribution and version, the processor underneath it, and whether untrusted Base64 reaches the decoder. Installing a newer processor can expose an application that was safe on the server it replaced, without changing the application at all.”

A further complication is that enterprises will also be running Java from a massive array of specialized vendors.

“Oracle released a fix for its Java builds, but enterprises also run Java from Amazon, Eclipse, Microsoft, Red Hat, and Azul, along with runtimes buried inside commercial products and containers,” Kenney pointed out. “CISOs should start with identity systems and software-delivery infrastructure, identify the runtime each one is actually using, apply the appropriate vendor fix, and restart or rebuild the workload.”

He also said that outbound connections need to be evaluated. “Researchers demonstrated that a Java program could be compromised by connecting to a malicious TLS server, even when it had no service exposed to the internet.”

Aman Mahapatra, chief strategy officer for New York City-based technology consulting firm Tribeca Softech, agreed, pointing out, “the client-side exploit puts any Java process that connects to a malicious TLS server at risk: integrations, connectors, crawlers and batch jobs. Patch scopes built around internet-facing listeners will miss them.”

Lack of visibility a big problem

Mahapatra also stressed that many Java implementations can be hard to locate.

“Your SBOM [software bill of materials] can’t see the real exposure. Whether a service is vulnerable depends on the silicon underneath as much as the JDK version,” he said. “The same container image is exploitable on an Ice Lake or Zen 4 node and safe on ARM or older hardware, and autoscaling can move it between the two. For once, the organizations that refreshed their hardware are the exposed ones.”

But it is the lack of visibility that concerns practitioners the most.

The Java hole “exposes a blind spot in how enterprises identify, prioritize and validate vulnerabilities,” said Raj Annaladasu, staff customer success engineer at Palo Alto Networks. “Enterprise teams invest heavily in application code review, dependency scanning, and secure coding practices. Java4Shell is a reminder that vulnerabilities can originate below the application layer, inside the managed runtime itself.”

“This is an important distinction: Java’s memory-safety abstractions do not protect an application from memory corruption originating inside a vulnerable native runtime implementation,” he said. “Applications may be using a standard Java API correctly while the underlying runtime implementation is vulnerable. This shifts the security boundary beyond application code and third-party dependencies to include the runtime, its implementation, and the hardware capabilities that influence execution.”

This means, he said, that exploitability depends on multiple conditions coming together: an affected Java runtime, the relevant CPU instruction capabilities, the vulnerable execution path being enabled, and attacker-controlled input reaching the decoder.

But, he pointed out, “one of the bigger challenges is discovering where Java is actually running. Runtime installations are often bundled inside third-party products, container images, appliances, custom runtime distributions, and application-specific directories. They may not appear as distinct entries in conventional software inventories. A clean application dependency scan or CMDB does not establish that the environment is free of vulnerable Java runtimes.”

Java hides everywhere

Given that there are going to be so many Java vendors, and so many hidden apps, at issue, the risk of a CISO missing a few is massive, noted Frank Dickson, principal analyst at Dickson Research.

“Java4Shell is a big deal because a single missed server with Java is all an attacker needs, allowing someone who never logs in to read its memory, including personal data, private keys, and tokens,” he said. “From there, the attacker can run code on the server. If that server is an identity system like Keycloak or PingFederate, the attacker now owns you.”

And, he said, it’s highly unlikely that enterprise CISOs will catch all or most Java instances.

“Will a CISO miss one? The odds say yes. Java hides everywhere. Each Java instance needs its own fix,” Dickson said.

Its popularity is Java’s greatest weakness

Brian Levine, a partner with consulting firm Control Risks, said Java’s popularity is its biggest weakness.

“Java runs the back office of corporate America: logins, code pipelines, ticketing, internal wikis,” he said. “Java4Shell lets an attacker who hasn’t even logged in take over some of the most sensitive of those systems by sending a short string of text. If your company runs Java on modern servers, and almost every large company does, you’re exposed until you patch.”

He enumerated some examples: “Keycloak and PingFederate control who gets into your company. Jenkins and Artifactory build and store the software you ship. Jira and Confluence hold your roadmaps, incident notes and plenty of passwords people shouldn’t have pasted there,” he said. “Take over those and you aren’t breaking into one room. You’ve stolen the master key, the blueprints and the security guard’s notebook in one move.”

He added, “Java programs that simply connect out to the wrong server can be compromised too. That last one means firewalls and perimeter controls don’t fully protect you.”

However, he pointed out, “there’s one small mercy: the bug only fires on certain newer Intel and AMD chips. The catch is that those chips fill modern data centers and cloud fleets.”

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.csoonline.com/article/4250390/a-new-critical-java-hole-illustrates-the-enterprise-it-visibility-problem.html, csoonline.com, 18.11.2026)
