[46] CSO Online - OX Security Launches OX Radar and Response Center to Show Companies Which Cyber Threats Actually Affect Them
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 14:54:07 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4250228/ox-security-launches-ox-radar-and-response-center-to-show-companies-which-cyber-threats-actually-affect-them-2.html

[TAM METİN - 624 kelime]
New technology monitors critical vulnerabilities and supply chain threats, automatically assessing customer exposure and guiding remediation

OX Security, a leader in AI-native application protection, today announced the launch of OX Radar and Response Center, giving security teams one place to track high-priority cyber threats, determine whether their organizations are affected, and manage remediation.

OX Radar continuously monitors emerging vulnerabilities, compromised software dependencies, and actively exploited threats. Integrated with OX Security’s application security platform, the Response Center automatically checks supported threats against customer environments, identifying affected assets and providing the context and guidance needed to respond. Separately, a publicly accessible OX Radar threat feed provides broader security research and findings across the software and AI supply chain.

When a major vulnerability or supply chain attack emerges, security teams often spend hours searching advisories, checking inventories, and correlating findings across tools to answer the question that matters most: Are we affected? As new software and AI components proliferate, investigating every potential threat manually is becoming increasingly impractical.

“The question isn’t just whether a new threat is dangerous. It’s whether it’s dangerous to you,” said Neatsun Ziv, CEO and co-founder of OX Security. “AI is accelerating software development, expanding the supply chain, and generating more security alerts than teams can realistically investigate. Security teams need to know immediately which threats affect their organization, which don’t, and what action is required.”

From Breaking Threats to Customer Action

To help security teams stay ahead of threats requiring urgent attention, OX Radar scans CVE databases, known-exploited vulnerability catalogs, security advisories, and vendor research approximately every 10 minutes. The customer-facing capabilities focus on high-priority threats, including compromised or malicious dependencies, exploited vulnerabilities, and critical-severity CVEs.

The Response Center connects supported OX Radar threats to each customer’s environment, checking whether vulnerable components are present and assessing their exploitability. It combines intelligence from the U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) and the European Union Agency for Cybersecurity (ENISA) Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalogs, EPSS exploit predictions, and VEX exploitability status with OX Security’s AI-generated exploit checks and runtime visibility to determine whether vulnerable libraries are affected.

Through the Response Center, security teams can:

Track threats: Follow high-priority incidents as new intelligence and findings emerge.

Assess exposure: Identify affected assets and examine supporting evidence.

Prioritize risk: Evaluate severity, exploitation, code usage, and reachability.

Manage remediation: Access safe versions and guidance, and create or track tickets and fix pull requests.

Document response: Maintain records of findings and remediation activity.

For each supported incident, the Response Center indicates whether a vulnerability has been detected in the customer’s environment, provides evidence of its potential exploitability, and incorporates available VEX information to help teams determine whether action is required. Teams can also initiate investigations using a CVE identifier, GitHub Security Advisory identifier, or advisory URL.

Expanding Threat Visibility Through Public Research

Alongside the customer-facing capabilities, OX Security is introducing a publicly accessible OX Radar threat feed with a broader catalog of security findings. Powered by OX Security’s independent research and scanning, the feed analyzes newly published npm and PyPI packages, AI skills, and Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers.

OX Security will continue expanding the range of threats assessed against customer environments, bringing more findings from its public research into the Response Center over time.

OX Radar and Response Center are available to OX Security customers as part of OX Security’s AI-Native Application Protection Platform (AINAPP). The broader OX Radar research feed is publicly accessible through the OX Security website.

OX Security is the first AI-Native Application Protection Platform built to secure software from prompt to production runtime. Its platform includes VibeSec, OX Code, OX Cloud, and OX Agentic Pentester, giving security teams a unified system for securing AI-driven development, applications, and cloud environments.

Head of Communications

jillian.jones@ox.security

[47] CSO Online - The resilience gap: Why cybersecurity investment alone is not enough
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 09:00:00 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4249721/the-resilience-gap-why-cybersecurity-investment-alone-is-not-enough.html

[TAM METİN - 1314 kelime]
You can spend millions on cybersecurity and still be fragile. The real test is whether your organization can recover when prevention fails.

Death, taxes and cyber incidents. It’s not quite the original saying, but for today’s CISO, all three carry a certain inevitability, and most organizations have come to accept that reality.

Protiviti’s 2027 CISO Top Risks and Opportunities study confirms that cybersecurity remains the top investment priority for CISOs. Yet even well-funded security programs can struggle when something goes wrong.

Why? The cause is rarely missing plans or controls; it’s silos. Service owners; security, recovery and crisis teams; and key providers too often plan and operate apart. As a result, organizations are beginning to see that investment and resilience are not the same thing.

Ultimately, the investment didn’t fail; the expectation did. Technology alone can’t deliver an outcome that also depends on people, process, governance and coordination. The lesson is clear: A well-funded cybersecurity program can still be operationally fragile.

The findings in Protiviti’s 2027 CISO Top Risks and Opportunities study illustrate the problem. Cybersecurity remains the leading strategic investment priority, selected by 48% of CISOs. Yet only 41% report high or very high confidence in their organizations’ ability to protect enterprise value, while just 29% express that level of confidence in responding with agility. Another 54% describe their confidence in their organizations’ ability to respond with agility as moderate.

That gap should concern every CISO. It suggests organizations have built security capabilities without fully developing the enterprise operating model needed during disruption.

The real test begins when prevention ends

Security investment matters. But you aren’t buying away cyber risk. You’re buying it down. Even the most mature organizations will eventually face an event they cannot prevent, often involving a third party, technology failure or circumstance outside their control.

What happens next separates resilient organizations from everyone else. Security works to contain the threat while infrastructure and application teams decide what can safely go offline. Business leaders push to restore critical services, sometimes with competing priorities. Legal weighs disclosure obligations with incomplete information. Communications prepares messages for employees, customers and regulators.

Meanwhile, a cloud, software-as-a-service or other technology provider may control part of the environment the company needs to recover.

That’s where resilience usually breaks down: not within any one team, but in the handoffs and decisions between them. In the exercises we run, the hardest questions are often surprisingly basic:

Who has the authority to shut down a revenue-producing system?

If two critical services are down, who decides which comes back first?

If Microsoft 365 or another primary collaboration platform is unavailable, how does the crisis team communicate?

If a critical provider is also down, do its recovery priorities and timelines align with yours?

The useful output of an exercise is not a reassuring score. It is the list of things that didn’t work: unclear authority, gaps in escalation paths, inaccessible backups and dependencies that teams incorrectly assumed had been tested. Consider Quantum Dawn VIII, SIFMA’s sector-wide exercise from November 2025. The polycrisis scenario included an undersea cable cut, and roughly two-thirds of participants said they had either never tested that scenario or weren’t sure they had. That is exactly the kind of blind spot an exercise is designed to expose. Better to uncover it in an exercise than during an actual disruption.

And the event you plan for is seldom the event you get. That’s why resilience cannot depend on following a script. Build a framework that enables collaboration and increases the speed of informed decisions, regardless of what actually happens.

Cyber, AI and third-party risks no longer stay in their lanes

Risks don’t arrive one at a time. A cyberattack can quickly become a privacy issue, a regulatory matter and a business interruption. A third-party outage becomes your outage. And AI can act as a force multiplier, allowing a single issue to move faster and spread across more data, systems and business processes.

The findings support this. Cyber threats remain the highest-rated near-term concern in the Top Risks survey, but privacy, third-party risk, AI, resilience and regulatory uncertainty are now clustered closely behind.

AI doesn’t change the fundamental resilience questions. It raises the stakes. An agent may read customer data, query an ERP system, act through a service account or trigger a workflow without a person clicking the button. So, start with an old question: What can it access? Then ask whose permissions or delegated authority it is using, what actions it can take, where those actions are logged and how quickly you can stop it when something goes wrong.

CISOs appear to recognize that challenge. In our 2027 Top Risks study, 51% identified improving AI governance and accountability as a leading priority, while 45% pointed to workforce readiness. That’s telling. Sustainable adoption requires some basic discipline: maintain an inventory of agents and their owners, map the data and tools each can reach, restrict service accounts and delegated permissions, monitor actions and make sure access can be revoked quickly.

Third parties deserve the same scrutiny. A third-party assessment can tell you a lot about a provider’s controls at a point in time. What it rarely tells you is how that provider will perform on a bad day. Most assessments still rely heavily on documentation, discussion and self-reporting. Those assessments may assure a provider’s security posture, but they do not prove that a critical service will remain available, that the provider’s recovery sequence aligns with yours or that the appropriate people on both sides know how to disconnect, communicate and restore service together.

CISOs should be able to identify the external relationships most critical to business operations, understand the services and data that depend on them, and test what will happen if those relationships are interrupted.

This broader responsibility is increasingly part of the CISO mandate. CSO’s examination of how CISOs can rise to the business resilience challenge describes the role’s evolution from a primarily preventive function toward greater responsibility for response, recovery and business resilience.

Build resilience into the investments already underway

If cyber risk increasingly travels through interconnected systems and infrastructure, resilience has to be part of how those environments are modernized in the first place.

The percentage of CISOs selecting cybersecurity slipped from 53% to 48%, while the percentage selecting infrastructure modernization rose from 21% to 33%. Taken together, those shifts make sense. Security and modernization are increasingly intertwined. A cloud migration, enterprise resource planning (ERP) replacement, identity redesign or data-platform upgrade can retire unsupported systems and standing privileges. It can also create new concentration risk, recovery dependencies and failure points if resilience is not designed in from the start.

CISOs should use these major transformation programs to improve resilience, not complicate it. Questions to ask include:

Are we retiring legacy platforms and connections, or simply putting a new layer on top of them?

Are old accounts, interfaces, backup jobs and vendor dependencies actually being removed?

Can the order-to-cash, payments, customer service or other critical processes be restored faster after the investment?

Are recovery, isolation and alternate-processing requirements in the architecture and acceptance criteria from the start?

Have we tested the new platform’s recovery path, or are we assuming modernization made it more resilient?

Closing the resilience gap

The point isn’t to spend less on cybersecurity. It’s to expect more from the investment. The 2027 Top Risks study shows CISOs balancing cyber threats with AI, third-party risk, resilience and modernization. Those may look like separate priorities on a survey. In practice, they increasingly collide in the same incident.

We aren’t going to prevent every cyber incident. The question is what happens when the next one gets through. Does the organization know what matters most, who makes the hard calls and how to recover? Or does it figure those things out during the crisis?

That gap is the resilience gap. And it is where CISOs should be looking next.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.csoonline.com/article/4249721/the-resilience-gap-why-cybersecurity-investment-alone-is-not-enough.html, csoonline.com, 18.11.2026)

[48] Dark Reading - Africa Retakes Top Spot as Most Attacked Region, Led by Nigeria Surge
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 08:00:00 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/africa-top-spot-most-attacked-region-nigeria-surge

[TAM METİN - 912 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
4 Min Read

![Image 1: A digital map of Africa.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/bltcfa70452a477c780/6afe245fab384927766a0238/africa-da-kuk-Getty-1386416241.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: da-kuk via Getty Images

Organizations in Africa faced an average of 3,834 cyberattacks per week in October, a jump of 38% from a year earlier, making the continent the most attacked region for the first time in more than a year.

Nigeria, Angola, and Kenya were among the top-5 most targeted countries, with Nigeria topping the list with nearly 6,700 attacks targeting organizations in the country every week; that marks a 140% increase over October 2025, according to data published by Check Point Research earlier this month. The nations are among those rapidly pushing technological transformation of their infrastructure, expanding connectivity, investing in digital services, and putting sensitive data in network-connected applications and storage.

The fast transformation is exposing more opportunities for attackers, says Shayimamba Conco, chief technology officer and security evangelist in the Office of the CTO at Check Point Software.

Related:[Chinese AI Project Scouts Spies in Taiwan Chip Industry](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/chinese-ai-project-scouts-spies-taiwan-chip-industry)

"All this rapid digital expansion is now happening within the region, but then, at the same time, this [expansion] is outpacing improvements in security maturity," he says, adding that security improvements are uneven across the continent. "Yes, some areas — let's say for example, South Africa — it's adopting cybersecurity at a much quicker rate than the rest of the Africa region, but there will be those regions which are still lagging behind a bit."

The significant increase in average weekly attacks pushed African organizations past those in Latin America as the favored targets of cyberattackers. In the first quarter of 2026, Latin America averaged more than 3,000 attacks per week, [surpassing the approximate 2,700 attacks targeting African organizations](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/african-organizations-see-easing-of-cyberattacks). In September, the two regions essentially tied in terms of attacks, with Africa once again becoming the most popular target in October.

## Global Attack Traffic Rising

Globally, organizations experienced an average of 2,550 weekly cyberattacks in October, down 9% from September but up a third from the previous year. While Africa encountered the most weekly attacks, both Latin America and Asia-Pacific regions saw a greater-than-average number — 3,483 and 3,436, respectively.

Globally, cyberattacks focused heavily on the [education sector](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/byod-qr-codes-education-attacks), with the average academic institution suffering more than 5,900 attacks per week, up a third from the same month in 2025. The education sector has topped the global list of popular targets every month, with organizations made attractive to attackers because their large data sets and reliance on legacy systems, says Conco.

Related:[Malicious Linux Implants Mimic Asian Mail Security Products](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/malicious-linux-implants-mimic-asian-mail-security)

"There is a lack of budget when it comes to, not just IT, but specifically cybersecurity," he says. "A junior IT engineer has to manage these vast environments, which are hard to lock down by nature, because you've got all these students who need access to all these applications and resources."

![Image 2: Clustered bar chart showing the most attacked industries globally by month.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt99147de41709f6e1/6afcccaa6a1d0d7a930c0e3e/most-attacked-industries-Aug-Oct-Check_Point_Research.jpg?width=1400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

In Africa, however, cyberattackers most often targeted financial services, government agencies, and the energy sector, according to Check Point's data.

The countries are racing to improve their cybersecurity postures and address persistent threats. Nigeria's National Information Technology Development Agency (NITDA) continues to [work on the details of the nation's cybersecurity and cloud-sovereignty policies](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/nigeria-cybersecurity-efforts-cybercriminals-profits). Last month, Operation Serengeti 3.0 — a joint task force between Interpol and 20 African countries — led to [the arrest of more than 1,500 suspects](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/third-serengeti-sweep-1400-arrests-investigations-mature) and the recovery of $105 million in stolen funds. In August, Operation Jackal IV, [targeted West African crime syndicates](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/interpols-jackal-iv-west-african-crime-infrastructure), leading to more than 200 arrests.

## Poor AI Privacy Also Poses Issues

Check Point also flagged the use of AI in companies as a potential weak point, finding that about 2.9% of prompts to generative AI and [large language models](https://www.techtarget.com/news/large-language-model-LLM/871881/) (LLMs) posed a high risk of leaking sensitive information. The risk is steadily increasing over time, from 2.3% in August to 2.5% in September.

Related:[UAE, Saudi Arabia Face Onslaught of Increasingly Complex Cyberattacks](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/uae-saudi-arabia-face-onslaught-of-increasingly-sophisticated-automated-cyberattacks)

For privacy-breaking prompts, Latin American organizations shouldered the highest risk, with 3.8% of prompts posing leak risks.

Interpol has also observed that attackers are increasingly using AI in a way that has impacted African nations. In its African Cybercrime Assessment Report 2026, Interpol found that 55% of reported cybercrimes across Africa used AI to some degree.

"Cybercrime has emerged as one of the most significant criminal threats to the region," Neal Jetton, Director of Interpol’s Cybercrime unit said in [a statement in August](https://www.interpol.int/en/News-and-Events/News/2026/INTERPOL-report-finds-AI-linked-to-more-than-half-of-cybercrime-in-Africa). "AI is automating every stage of a cyberattack from reconnaissance and phishing to extortion and evasion. However, we see that when countries work together, cybercriminal infrastructure can be identified, disrupted and dismantled."

## About the Author

[![Image 3: Robert Lemos](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/bltfac4d821bdd7e9f1/6a0c6d499384132f848610d6/robertlemos2024.jpg?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/robert-lemos)

Contributing Writer

Rob is an award-winning, veteran technology journalist of more than 30 years, reporting on global cybersecurity issues, the latest offensive and defensive technologies, malware incidents, cyber conflict, and AI's impact on software and cybersecurity.

A former research engineer, Rob has written for more than two dozen publications, including CNET News.com, Dark Reading, MIT's Technology Review, Popular Science, and Wired News. He has received five awards for journalism, including Best Deadline Journalism (Online) in 2003 for his coverage of the Blaster worm. Rob also analyzes data on various trends using Python and R for both his reporting and his clients. Recent reports include analyses of the shortage in cybersecurity workers, annual vulnerability trends, and annual threat reports.

Rob holds degrees from Cornell University in Electrical Engineering and Computer Science (double major).

[49] Dark Reading - Russia's Cold Relic APT Turns One Cloud Breach Into Another
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 21:59:46 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/cold-relic-turns-one-cloud-breach-into-another

[TAM METİN - 1017 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
4 Min Read

![Image 1: Russia digital technology flag cyber on on computer CPU.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt0cf0a0b934852050/6afe1492ac3412795c5370fe/russia_Skorzewiak_shutterstoc.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: Skorzewiak via Shutterstock

The Russian cyberespionage group known as Cold Relic is turning cloud breaches into a springboard for targeting a victim organization's wider network of trusted relationships.

It's a tactic that researchers at Google Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) observed the attacker deploy with clinical efficiency in two separate compromises involving US organizations over the past year.

In the first intrusion, late last year, the attackers gained control of a cloud administrator account and harvested internal communications, employee details, and other organizational records. Cold Relic actors later used that material to impersonate an employee of the first organization for a successful [social engineering attack](https://www.darkreading.com/cloud-security/unc6692-social-engineering-malware-cloud-abuse) on an individual at a second organization.

The activity, GTIG researchers said, shows how traditional social engineering is becoming more closely tied to attacks on [cloud identities](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/cloud-security-identity-attacks-reliaquest/804621/). Once inside an organization's cloud environment, an attacker can gather the kind of internal information that makes a message from a supposedly trusted colleague or business contact much harder to spot as a scam.

Related:[China-Nexus Actors Breach 10 Organizations in AI-Driven Attacks](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/china-nexus-actors-breach-10-organizations-ai-driven-attacks)

The campaigns "demonstrate the convergence of human-centric [social engineering](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/social-engineering-campaigns-highlight-the-ability-to-exploit-human-behavio/760747/) and cloud identity abuse," GTIG researchers wrote in a [blog post](https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/cold-relic-a-case-study-in-impersonation-and-compromise). "By obtaining access to strategic personnel rosters and operational documents, Russian intelligence services turn legitimate business communications into attack vectors against trusted partners."

## A Tale of Two Breaches

Cold Relic (aka also known as [Star Blizzard](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/russia-star-blizzard-apt-ditches-clickfix-widen-phishing-net), Callisto, and Gossamer Bear) is a [cyberespionage](https://www.techtarget.com/news/Microsoft-Nation-state-activity-blurring-with-cybercrime/836920/) group that Google researchers have been tracking — and disrupting — since 2015. The US government has assessed the outfit as working within Russia's Federal Security Service (FSB) and tasked with collecting information of political and strategic interest to Moscow.

Though low-profile, Cold Relic has notched up numerous high-value targets including defense contractors, government officials, and NGOs across North America and Europe. One of its favorite post-compromise activities, according to GTIG, is to steal emails and data that can help it establish "authentic pretexts" and convincing impersonations when reaching out to other potential targets.

In the first intrusion in late 2025, Cold Relic used stolen credentials for a super admin account to access a US organization's cloud environment. The attackers routed their activity through a residential proxy located near the organization, so their connections appeared to originate from a familiar geographic area. The attacker repeatedly kept sending the actual account holder multifactor authentication (MFA) push notifications until the [overwhelmed user approved one](https://www.darkreading.com/cloud-security/mfa-bombing-attacks-target-apple-iphone-users).

Related:[Inside an AI Attack Against a US Retailer](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/inside-ai-attack-us-retailer)

After gaining access, Cold Relic actors registered their own device on the network and established additional ways to authenticate to it, including via passkeys, backup MFA codes, and additional rogue MFA devices. The threat group used the stolen Super Admin user privileges to exfiltrate the organization's cloud storage, including full employee lists, email conversations, and organizational directories and contact lists. The threat actors then initiated several anti-forensics measures that allowed it to maintain access to the compromised network for several months.

In the second attack, earlier this year, Cold Relic impersonated an employee of the first company to contact an individual at a second organization through an encrypted messaging app. The threat actors sent the unsuspecting target phishing links disguised as documents relevant to their ongoing conversation.

After stealing the victim's Microsoft Entra ID credentials and authentication tokens, Cold Relic used them to access the organization's Google Workspace environment through its federated [single sign-on system](https://www.techtarget.com/news/What-is-Single-Sign-On-SSO-Definition-How-It-Works-Benefits/836522/). The attackers then helped themselves to files from the compromised user's cloud drive while also seeking out email archives, chat records, credentials, personnel information, and other data of potential intelligence value.

Related:[Artex: The AI Pen-Test Tool Hackers Turned Into a Weapon](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/artex-ai-pen-test-tool-hackers-weapon)

## A Cold Relic Lesson for Organizations

"Cold Relic is highly adept at leveraging social networks and impersonation of key individuals to achieve a realistic pretense for reaching out to the targeted individual or organization," Wes Shields, security engineer at GTIG, tells Dark Reading. "Once they have achieved their initial access goal, they move quickly to maintain persistence and exfiltrate data."

The lesson for organizations is to train employees to be wary of unexpected messages, particularly when they arrive through communication channels a supposed colleague or business contact doesn't normally use. If a message seems unusual, employees should verify the sender's identity through a separate, previously established channel before responding or acting on it, Shields advises.

"In today's hyper-connected world, it is impossible to have employees not be reachable via multiple channels, and attackers are taking advantage of this," he says.

Joseph Spero, principal incident response consultant at Google, recommends other measures to limit the damage from identity-based attacks. These include enforcing phishing-resistant MFA, restricting and auditing passkey registrations, [hardening MFA](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/mfa-oauth-consent-abuse) registration procedures, and auditing Oauth grants.

"Short-lived sessions and principle of least privilege will limit the potential scope of access when credentials are compromised," he says. "When a compromise is suspected, mandate session revocation in addition to resetting passwords and MFA mechanisms."

## About the Author

[![Image 2: Jai Vijayan](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt1a674355ce57f27b/64f14f228727730c7f926217/Jai-Vijayan.jpeg?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/jai-vijayan)

Contributing Writer

Illinois-based Jai Vijayan is a veteran, award-winning technology journalist with more than 25 years of experience covering cybersecurity. His information security reporting has explored everything from ransomware, nation-state threats, and identity security to AI risk, critical infrastructure protection, software supply chain security, cloud security and emerging enterprise technologies.

Over the course of his career, Jai has written news stories, feature articles, survey reports, white papers, and e-books for enterprise and technology audiences. He has also moderated panel discussions and executive roundtables featuring CISOs, security researchers, and industry leaders.

Jai previously served as senior editor at Computerworld, where he covered information security and data-privacy issues. His work has also appeared in CSO Online, InformationWeek, The Christian Science Monitor Passcode, The Economic Times, and other publications.

His work has earned multiple industry honors, including a Joint ASBPE Excellence Award for Best Coverage of Government IT, and a Joint Jesse H. Neal Award for wireless LAN security coverage. Jai holds a Master’s degree in statistics from Bangalore University, and studied broadcasting and electronic communication at Marquette University in Milwaukee.

# Strike Graph Launches Integration AI to Automate Continuous Compliance Evidence Using Any Existing Tool

New agentic AI solution leverages APIs to collect compliance evidence from systems and applies it to over 30 frameworks with no duplicate work.

November 18, 2026

3 Min Read

![Image 5: Dark Reading logo in a gray background | Dark Reading](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/bltc94608acf452fd67/655cf371ab171e040a838b2a/329050_DR23_Graphics_Website_V5_Default_Image_v1.png?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

[](https://www.linkedin.com/sharing/share-offsite/?url=https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/strike-graph-launches-integration-ai-to-automate-continuous-compliance-evidence-using-any-existing-tool)[](http://www.facebook.com/sharer/sharer.php?u=https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/strike-graph-launches-integration-ai-to-automate-continuous-compliance-evidence-using-any-existing-tool)[](http://www.twitter.com/intent/tweet?url=https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/strike-graph-launches-integration-ai-to-automate-continuous-compliance-evidence-using-any-existing-tool)[](https://www.reddit.com/submit?url=https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/strike-graph-launches-integration-ai-to-automate-continuous-compliance-evidence-using-any-existing-tool&title=Strike%20Graph%20Launches%20Integration%20AI%20to%20Automate%20Continuous%20Compliance%20Evidence%20Using%20Any%20Existing%20Tool)[](https://bsky.app/intent/compose?text=Strike%20Graph%20Launches%20Integration%20AI%20to%20Automate%20Continuous%20Compliance%20Evidence%20Using%20Any%20Existing%20Tool%20-%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.darkreading.com%2Fcyber-risk%2Fstrike-graph-launches-integration-ai-to-automate-continuous-compliance-evidence-using-any-existing-tool)[](mailto:?subject=Strike%20Graph%20Launches%20Integration%20AI%20to%20Automate%20Continuous%20Compliance%20Evidence%20Using%20Any%20Existing%20Tool&body=I%20thought%20the%20following%20from%20Dark%20Reading%20might%20interest%20you.%0D%0A%0D%0A%20Strike%20Graph%20Launches%20Integration%20AI%20to%20Automate%20Continuous%20Compliance%20Evidence%20Using%20Any%20Existing%20Tool%0D%0Ahttps%3A%2F%2Fwww.darkreading.com%2Fcyber-risk%2Fstrike-graph-launches-integration-ai-to-automate-continuous-compliance-evidence-using-any-existing-tool)

PRESS RELEASE

SEATTLE, November 18, 2026 — [Strike Graph](https://www.strikegraph.com/), the enterprise AI-native governance, risk, and compliance (GRC) management platform, today announced a new integration solution that seamlessly connects to any software API to automatically collect compliance evidence from it. With Integration AI, organizations using the Strike Graph platform are no longer limited to a fixed integration catalog. They simply indicate the system they are using and Strike Graph builds a native integration that pulls compliance evidence from it automatically.

Once connected via an API, the Strike Graph platform employs an AI research agent to read the vendor’s public API documentation and draft a build specification. Concurrently, a second AI agent builds the complete integration across Strike Graph’s backend, user interface, and evidence pipeline. Integrations connect API-to-API, and customers never install agents or software inside their own infrastructure, which is critical for maintaining enterprise-grade security.

Related:[Optimizely Malware Attacks Highlight Third-Party Vendor Risks](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/optimizely-malware-attacks-third-party-vendor-risks)

“For compliance to be successful, access to evidence must be quick, easy, and continuous, but integration with existing tools often presents a challenge,” said Justin Beals, CEO and founder of Strike Graph. “Integration AI has empowered Strike Graph to overcome those roadblocks and accomplish all three of those requirements at once. If it has an API, Strike Graph can collect evidence from it. Furthermore, since Strike Graph’s evidence model is graph-based, evidence from a newly built integration automatically counts toward every framework a customer pursues, including SOC 2, ISO 27001, HIPAA, and CMMC, with no duplicate work.”

# L o a d i n g...

## Read-Only and Strict Separation by Design

As with all Strike Graph evidence collection integrations, Integration AI builds secure integrations that are restricted to read-only operations by default. Each one carries an endpoint allowlist that ratifies against the vendor’s real API documentation, which guarantees evidence collection can observe a customer’s systems but never change or impact them.

Strike Graph keeps strict separation where credentials are never touched by Integration AI.The build process works outside of the core governance, risk, and compliance (GRC) program itself, building the connection with no access to any customer secrets. Credentials and other sensitive data live in a securely managed secrets vault, and the build spec is scanned and automatically rejected if any credentials are detected in it.

# L o a d i n g...

## Built with Defense Against Attacks on AI

With Integration AI, vendor documentation is treated strictly as data and never as instructions, which blocks prompt-injection attempts hidden in third-party content. The AI build environment holds no publishing rights, and a separate locked-down step does the publishing.

Related:[NIST Finalizes Post-Quantum Cryptography Guidance, GAO Offers a Reality Check](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/nist-finalizes-post-quantum-cryptography-guidance-gao-offers-a-reality-check)

Builds sit behind an approver allowlist and a kill switch, and humans are always in the loop to review the research plan before build starts, after code has been written, and before anything is deployed to production. The AI can only propose changes, not ship them, and every integration requires a human engineering review.

Once built, Integration AI runs evidence scripts in self-contained isolated sandbox environments with no shared code paths, so a flaw in one integration can’t spread to another.

Availability

Integration AI is available to Strike Graph customers today. For more information, visit[www.strikegraph.com](http://www.strikegraph.com/).

About Strike Graph  
Strike Graph is an AI-native GRC company empowering organizations to eliminate redundant compliance work, accelerate audits, and achieve trust. Strike Graph's next-generation platform transforms GRC through its purpose-built graph-based architecture, patent-pending agentic evidence validation technology, Verify AI, intelligent recommendation engine, Security Assistant, and dynamic mapping across 30+ compliance frameworks. Built with privacy-first principles, Strike Graph hosts its own AI models rather than relying on third-party services, ensuring customer data remains secure and siloed. Founded in 2020 by technologist and serial entrepreneur Justin Beals and backed by top-tier investors, Strike Graph has helped hundreds of organizations reduce compliance timelines by more than 86%. Learn more at[strikegraph.com](http://strikegraph.com/).

Related:[AI Raises the Stakes for Cyber Resilience](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/ai-raises-stakes-cyber-resilience)

[51] Dark Reading - AttackIQ Research Reveals How Iranian Cyber Groups Hand Off Network Access to Enable Destructive Attacks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 20:38:42 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/attackiq-research-reveals-iranian-cyber-groups

[TAM METİN - 836 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
PRESS RELEASE

ANTA CLARA, Calif. — ([BUSINESS WIRE](https://www.businesswire.com/)) —[AttackIQ](https://cts.businesswire.com/ct/CT?id=smartlink&url=https://AttackIQ.com&esheet=54642571&newsitemid=20261117842327&lan=en-US&anchor=AttackIQ&index=1&md5=50a2fd86ecf6c02cfb7662aaca4ff6ac), the leader in threat-informed Continuous Threat Exposure Management (CTEM), today released new research that connects previously fragmented reporting on individual Iranian cyber campaigns to map the relationships among state-sponsored operators and the institutions supporting them. The findings show Iranian espionage groups turn compromised networks into launchpads for other operators, transforming intelligence-gathering operations into destructive attacks.

Threat reporting on Iran typically covers one campaign at a time, and vendors track the same groups under different names. Helix Kitten alone appears under eight other names, including OilRig, APT34 and Scarred Manticore. AttackIQ’s Adversary Research Team consolidated that reporting into W eighing Cyber Power: Evaluating the Strategic and Operational Significance of Iran’s State-Sponsored Cyber Capability, a single map of 20 Iranian adversaries, the government institutions they are linked to and the contractors that supply their personnel, tooling and infrastructure.

Related:[Ransomware Attacks Increase Behind AI, but Payments Decline](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/ransomware-attacks-increase-ai-payments-decline)

“The value of threat research is measured by the decisions it informs,” said Paul Reid, VP of Adversary Research at AttackIQ. “Understanding which Iranian groups work together, and how confident we can be in each relationship, gives defenders a clear starting point for emulation and a realistic picture of what could follow an initial compromise. It turns a single detection into a forecast of what comes next.”

## How an Intrusion Changes Hands

The report traces how a single intrusion can pass between groups. In one documented case, Helix Kitten (Scarred Manticore), conducted prolonged intelligence collection inside a network before handing off the network access to Banished Kitten (Void Manticore), which pursued destructive objectives. In a second case, Static Kitten (Mango Sandstorm) established access and passed it to Storm-1084, a destructive operator. In a separate campaign against the Albanian government, four separately tracked groups contributed to the same operation, which spanned reconnaissance, initial access, data theft and the deployment of destructive tooling.

When the damage arrives, it is frequently disguised. The research also found Iranian groups staging destructive attacks as ransomware or hacktivism, obscuring the intent behind the attacks. Pioneer Kitten provided criminal ransomware affiliates with access to compromised networks.

The organizations hit first may be a route to other targets. Several groups can gain access to more heavily defended organizations, including in the defense industrial base, through their subsidiaries, contractors, and suppliers.

Related:[Chinese AI Project Scouts Spies in Taiwan Chip Industry](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/chinese-ai-project-scouts-spies-taiwan-chip-industry)

## Lower Weight Class Does Not Mean Lower Impact

The analysis classifies 20 Iranian adversaries into three weight classes based on their organizational significance, operational relevance, and demonstrated capabilities. The classifications underscore that even smaller, specialized groups can play critical roles in larger cyber campaigns.

*   Heavyweight: Established groups with sustained espionage operations, internally developed tooling and a central role in Iran’s cyber strategy. 
*   Lightweight: Groups with more focused missions, including specialized espionage, access facilitation and destructive operations. 
*   Featherweight: Groups with narrower or less consistently documented activity, some relying on support from more established adversaries. 

“Iran isn’t as technically sophisticated as China, Russia or North Korea, but what it lacks in sophistication it makes up for in cadence,” said Francis Guibernau, Senior Adversary Research Engineer at AttackIQ. “They target anything and everything, on every operating system and in industrial environments. That volume, combined with how freely these groups share access, is what makes the ecosystem dangerous.”

Related:[Shadowy AI Relay Disables OpenAI Codex, Claude Code Sandboxes](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/ai-relay-service-openai-codex-claude-code-sandboxes)

The research draws on more than 200 references and consolidates more than 2,600 procedures, 1,300 file indicators, and 2,300 network indicators. The analysis was also validated against threat intelligence and real-world activity observed by several Fortune 150 companies actively targeted by Iranian threat actors. The research team also received input from National Computer Incident Response Teams (CIRTs) and members of the intelligence community.

## From Research to Validation

To help organizations put the research into practice, AttackIQ will be releasing adversary emulations based on the documented tactics, techniques, and procedures of Iranian threat actors over the coming weeks. These emulations will enable security teams to safely test their defenses against relevant adversary behaviors, identify gaps in detection and prevention, and prioritize improvements based on real-world threat intelligence.

[Read the full report.](https://www.attackiq.com/resources/reports/weighing-cyber-power/research-report/)

For a closer look at the findings with AttackIQ’s adversary research team, join the December 10 webinar, [Breaking Down Iran’s Most Dangerous Cyber Tactics.](https://cts.businesswire.com/ct/CT?id=smartlink&url=https://www.attackiq.com/resources/webinars/irans-most-dangerous-cyber-tactics/&esheet=54642571&newsitemid=20261117842327&lan=en-US&anchor=Breaking+Down+Iran&#8217;s+Most+Dangerous+Cyber+Tactics.&index=3&md5=f6d8d401dcfc30b4899ddb40a9a9c00e)

About AttackIQ

AttackIQ is the leader in threat-informed Continuous Threat Exposure Management (CTEM), helping organizations continuously validate defenses, break attack paths, and reduce threat debt through evidence-based security operations.

Through AVA Agentic OS, AttackIQ has introduced the industry’s first agentic operating system purpose-built for CTEM, enabling organizations to execute Continuous Threat Exposure Management autonomously at enterprise scale.

By orchestrating specialized AI agents across threat intelligence, adversary exposure validation, security validation, detection engineering, control optimization, threat debt reduction, and AI security validation, AttackIQ transforms fragmented security activities into continuous cyber resilience.

Trusted by the world’s largest enterprises, government agencies, and managed security providers, AttackIQ empowers organizations to continuously discover, validate, and reduce cyber risk with confidence.

CTEM Runs on AVA.

[52] Dark Reading - Java4Shell Flaw Threatens Global Enterprises, Java Application Ecosystem
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 16:54:52 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/java4shell-flaw-threatens-global-java-application-ecosystem

[TAM METİN - 1302 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
![Image 1: A cup of coffee on a saucer with a skull and crossbones formed in the milk foam](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt99cd31a80daa3833/6afdaa35fe696e77159c89b6/dangerous_coffee_cup_Nikreats_Alamy.png?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: Nikreats via Alamy Stock Photo

A critical flaw in an open source runtime at the heart of the Java programming language allows unauthenticated attackers to perform remote code execution (RCE) on myriad Java applications used globally across enterprise organizations.

The memory corruption vulnerability exists in versions of [OpenJDK](https://www.techtarget.com/news/Oracle-moves-OpenJDK-to-Git-and-GitHub/863571/), a popular open source runtime. Researchers at security startup Calif demonstrated a general proof-of-concept (PoC) exploit for the flaw, dubbed "Java4Shell" and discovered with the help of artificial intelligence (AI), according [to a report](https://calif.io/java4shell) published Tuesday.

However, they are holding back the release of "weaponized chains for individual products" to give organizations time to upgrade to patch the vulnerability, officially tracked as CVE-2026-114938 and rated as 9.2 under [CVSS](https://www.techtarget.com/news/Common-Vulnerability-Scoring-System-CVSS/837194/) 4.0, in affected applications, Calif security engineer Quang Luong wrote in the post.

Java4Shell affects the following releases: Java 17: 17.0.6 to 17.0.21; Java 21: up to 21.0.13 affected; Java 25: up to 25.0.5; Java 27: up to 27.0.1; Java 8, 8u511b33-perf, its Performance Pack build. Java 11 and Java 17.0.0 to 17.0.5 remain unaffected.

Related:[Major Brazilian Signature Apps Enable RCE & Fraud](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/major-brazilian-signature-app-rce-fraud)

OpenJDK published [a security advisory](https://openjdk.org/groups/vulnerability/advisories/2026-11-17) on the flaw, and Oracle released [a patch](https://www.oracle.com/security-alerts/cspunov2026.html) yesterday to fix the issue in the affected versions of Java. "All Java users should update immediately," Luong recommended.

## Issue Lies in Base64 Decoding

The flaw exists because of how OpenJDK [decodes Base64](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/apache-tomcat-rce-vulnerability-exploit), a common way of representing binary data as text in authentication protocols, in file uploads, and to deliver network messages.

"To decode Base64 faster, OpenJDK uses specialized x86-64 CPU instructions to process many bytes at once," Luong explained. "We discovered a bug in that optimized machine-level code that lets malformed input overwrite neighboring memory."

Specifically, OpenJDK uses specialized AVX-512 VBMI instructions on supported x86-64 processors, he explained. The bug is present in the hand-rolled assembly code that handles the final few characters.

"For the 65-character input above, OpenJDK allocates a 50-byte result array," Luong wrote. "After decoding the first 64 characters, the routine mishandles the stray = and calculates a negative output length. That invalid length becomes a full 64-byte write, sending 62 bytes past the end of the array."

Java eventually rejects the input, but by then neighboring objects may already be corrupted, he added. Thus, attackers can exploit the bug to reveal sensitive memory, as the highly critical OpenSSL flaw [Heartbleed](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/heartbleed-when-is-it-good-to-name-a-vulnerability) did in 2014, or to perform RCE. "The corruption itself can be triggered by just 64 A characters followed by a single =:," Luong wrote.

Related:[Google Project Zero Slashes Bug Disclosure Deadlines, Splitting Experts](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/google-project-zero-bug-disclosure-deadlines)

## Java4Shell and Ghosts of Flaws Past

Much like previous high-profile flaws before it, including not only Heartbleed but also [PHP2Shell](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/no-safe-exploit-you-can-still-validate-php2shell) and Log4Shell, the flaw has potential to create a security quagmire across entire technology ecosystems because of how widespread the use of the vulnerable software component is. Indeed, [Java](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/what-the-ecdsa-flaw-in-java-means-for-enterprises) "is the beating heart of the modern economy" and the third most popular programming language, powering 90% of Fortune 500 companies, Luong wrote.

[Log4Shell](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/one-year-later-log4shell-exposed-attack), disclosed in 2021, affected Apache Log4j, a commonly used Java logging library, and enabled attackers to execute malicious code remotely. More recently, PHP2Shell, disclosed in October 2026, exposed critical vulnerabilities in the PHP runtime that could allow unauthenticated attackers to execute code on vulnerable servers.

Both flaws highlighted the difficulty of identifying [vulnerable components](https://www.darkreading.com/cloud-security/regresshion-bug-threatens-takeover-of-millions-of-linux-systems) hidden within applications and third-party software used across organizations; Java4Shell is similar in this respect because it gives attackers a direct route to enterprise environments through otherwise trusted apps.

"Java4Shell is a reminder that the software underneath your applications is also your attack surface," observes Dom Glavach, CISO at application security solutions provider Black Duck.

Related:['Sleeper Agent' Tactic Hides Payloads in Open-Weight AI Models](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/sleeper-agent-tactic-payloads-open-weight-ai-models)

The practical problem now for organizations "is that exposure depends on the Java build, the processor and how an application handles input, and many teams cannot answer those questions quickly, especially for JDKs bundled inside applications and containers," he says.

## AI Gets the Assist on Java4Shell Discovery

Because flaws like these are [so deeply embedded](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/php2shell-exploited-chained-attacks-check-point/874751/), they may go undiscovered and remain critical security gaps. In this case, the use of AI to uncover Java4Shell potentially may have helped the industry avert disaster, giving organizations time to patch before it's been widely exploited, notes James Matchett, head of security at Cloudsmith.

"Its discovery shows how AI can uncover weaknesses in widely used software that might otherwise have gone undetected for years," he says.

Indeed, Java4Shell's discovery iillustrates how AI can speed up [vulnerability discovery](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/vulnerability-gap-why-discovery-is-outrunning-repair).

"An AI coding assistant found this bug in about an hour and a first working exploit took two more, for under $200 in tokens," Waseem Ahmed, head of engineering for cybersecurity firm Secure.com, tells Dark Reading. "The flaw sat in one of the most-audited runtimes there is. That is what discovery looks like now: cheap, fast, and available to defenders and attackers alike."

However, Matchett says the flip side is it also can help attackers move more quickly to weaponize security flaws, noting that organizations are under intense pressure to find and remediate vulnerabilities before attackers can exploit them. As Ahmed puts it, "defenders now have less time between a bug being found and being used."

## RCE Across Multiple Platforms

There is little doubt that defenders should take the Java4Shell seriously. Calif researchers demonstrated RCE in a laundry list of widely deployed Java applications in their default configurations, noting that they, too, used AI to develop exploits, Luong wrote.

The team achieved pre-authorization [RCE](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/swift-banking-govt-middleware-rce) in the following: Keycloak, Atlassian Jira, Atlassian Confluence, JFrog Artifactory, Jenkins, and PingFederate. Meanwhile, Apache Kafka Broker, Apache Solr, Elasticsearch, Gerrit, and Spinnaker are vulnerable to post-auth RCE, they said.

"The lists are non-exhaustive," Luong wrote. "We also demonstrated RCE in sample applications built on widely used frameworks, including [Apache Struts](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/actively-exploited-bug-struts-2) and Spring Boot. Ordinary features such as authentication and file uploads can expose the vulnerable Base64 decoder, extending the potential impact far beyond the products we tested."

An independent researcher also shared with Calif a client-side exploit that demonstrates how "a Java program that opens a TLS connection to a malicious server can be compromised, even if it exposes no service of its own," Luong wrote.

## Immediate Patching, Mitigation Recommended

Organizations also can mitigate Java4Shell by starting OpenJDK with one of the following flags: -XX:-UseBASE64Intrinsics to disable hardware-accelerated Base64 encoding and decoding, or -XX:UseAVX=2 to disable AVX-512, Luong advised.

The second should only be applied on x86-64 hosts, as [OpenJDK](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/oracle-delays-java-8-to-improve-java-7-security) on ARM does not recognize it and thus won't start the system, he added.

Black Duck's Glavach recommends that organizations start their mitigation process with conducting an inventory, patching [Internet-facing identity](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/fritzfrog-botnet-exploits-log4shell-overlooked-internal-hosts) and build systems first, and staying clear of unverified fixes and fake exploits circulating online.

Secure.com's Ahmed was even more specific, advising that organizations "find every server running Java 17.0.6 or later, patch the ones open to the Internet first, and turn off the Base64 intrinsic where you can't patch yet."

## About the Author

[![Image 2: Elizabeth Montalbano](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/bltd6f16d37895d5bfd/6a06cc5deb9f4ad4d2785f3f/LizHeadshot2026.png?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/elizabeth-montalbano)

Contributing Writer

Elizabeth Montalbano is freelance writer, editor, and journalist with 30 years of professional experience and a master's degree from Arizona State University. Her areas of expertise include enterprise technology, cybersecurity, business, and culture. During her long career, Elizabeth has lived and worked as a full-time journalist in Phoenix, San Francisco, and New York City. She specializes in news coverage and analysis, using her years of experience to look at the current state of cybersecurity with a critical gaze. She currently resides in a village on the southwest coast of Portugal, where in her free time she enjoys surfing, hiking with her dogs, growing plants, and playing and performing as a singer and musician.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/java4shell-flaw-threatens-global-java-application-ecosystem, darkreading.com, 18.11.2026)

[53] Dark Reading - Major Brazilian Signature Apps Enable RCE &amp; Fraud
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 14:00:00 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/major-brazilian-signature-app-rce-fraud

[TAM METİN - 1021 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
4 Min Read

![Image 1: A close-up of a Brazilian driver's license.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/bltd3d76d438281428b/6afb51c99f6beb04c5e535c4/Brazil_CNH-RafaPress-Getty.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: RafaPress via Getty Images

Brazil's digital certificate system was recently found to be rife with vulnerabilities in browser extensions used by millions of government agencies and legal, medical, and tax professionals.

That's the assessment of researchers at Bay Area Labs, who this week flagged five particularly popular digital extensions that allow Brazilian citizens and institutions to not only sign official documents, but sign them with the greatest possible degree of trust issued by their government. All five of them possessed vulnerabilities which could've allowed attacker-controlled websites to run arbitrary code on users' machines.

One can only hope that the researchers, who [disclosed](https://amibeingpwned.com/blog/how-we-hacked-brazil-869982c4b473e6b8cfa8605f4cd3f12d) the vulnerabilities this week, were the first to discover them. Remote code execution (RCE) in this context could have allowed fraudsters to sign legal documents in victims' names, and hackers to breach major government organizations (as well as professionals who manage large amounts of personal, financial, tax, and [health information](https://www.techtarget.com/searchhealthit/definition/personal-health-information) on behalf of their clients).

Related:[Java4Shell Flaw Threatens Global Enterprises, Java Application Ecosystem](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/java4shell-flaw-threatens-global-java-application-ecosystem)

## RCE in Brazil's Digital Signing Infrastructure

On Sept. 23, 2020, Brazil's National Congress enacted Federal Law No. 14,063. 14,063 laid the ground rules on how individuals and organizations could digitally sign documents when dealing with [public institutions](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/local-government-ransomware-spike/633095/). Among other things, it delineated three tiers of trust that an electronic signature could carry: "simple," "advanced," and "qualified." "Qualified" meant that a digital signature would be equivalent to a notarized, handwritten one. It would be sufficient for high-stakes legal proceedings, like real estate transfers and electronic tax processes. And any digital signature backed with an ICP-Brasil certificate — ICP-Brasil is the country's official national public key infrastructure (PKI) framework — would be considered "qualified."

Brazilian government institutions, tax professionals, and anyone in-between who wants to provide a qualified digital signature for a sensitive legal document online can use browser extensions like Lacuna Web PKI, Softplan Web Signer, Certisign WebSigner, Certisign CertiPlugin, and BRy Assinatura Digital. Each of these tools lets official government and vendor portals read ICP-Brasil certificates, to confirm that signers are who they say they are, and put pen to Web page.

These tools will also engage with non-official websites. So if an attacker can lure a victim to their own site — or, in some cases, plant a malicious ad or iframe on a legitimate site — they can engage that victim's authentication middleware. Only one of the five extensions, CertiPlugin, had any kind of allowlist, yet it included any domain ending in ".one," meaning that an attacker would simply need to purchase a domain with that common and freely available suffix in order to perform an attack.

Related:[Google Project Zero Slashes Bug Disclosure Deadlines, Splitting Experts](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/google-project-zero-bug-disclosure-deadlines)

The core issue endemic to each of these five extensions, and [others used by other national governments](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/belgium-eid-authentication-citizen-accounts-rce), is that they allow websites to dictate the dynamic link libraries (DLLs) they load. Loading cryptographic libraries is a normal part of the way these apps work. But if they don't properly check that a loaded file is trusted? In that case, an attacker that lures a victim to their site can trigger a malware download and then instruct the victim's signing software to load that malware as if it were a legitimate library.

All five extensions' RCE vulnerabilities earned "critical" 9.6 scores in the Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS). Each of them was patched as of mid-September, but lessons remain.

## Signing Tool Risks to High-Value Organizations

Lacuna Web PKI, Softplan Web Signer, Certisign WebSigner, Certisign CertiPlugin, and BRy Assinatura Digital have more than 10 million installs between them.

Among that user base are some of the most significant, high-value organizations and professionals in the country. On its website, [Lacuna](https://www.lacunasoftware.com/en/aboutus#clients) alone counts Brazil's Federal Senate, its National Council of Justice, its navy, and its stock exchange among its clients "who trust our work." These extensions are far from limited to major government agencies, of course. [BRy](https://www.bry.com.br/#:~:text=The%20digital%20trust%20behind%20major%20operations) advertises its work with the University of São Paulo, the São Paulo metro, and one of the country's few biggest banks, Caixa Econômica Federal.

Related:['Sleeper Agent' Tactic Hides Payloads in Open-Weight AI Models](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/sleeper-agent-tactic-payloads-open-weight-ai-models)

Attackers could have used the vulnerable authentication middleware to infect employees at major state institutions and, in Arnott's words, "cause mayhem." As an easier alternative, they could target middle class professionals: lawyers, doctors, accountants and so on. An accountant might manage digital signing credentials for hundreds of different individuals and businesses on their single computer. An attacker with that accountant's access could then impersonate those people and companies in legally significant transactions — forging agreements, submitting false tax filings ... the sky's the limit.

"It's really interesting how these signing tools always consistently have the same critical vulnerabilities," Arnott marvels, after having found characteristically similar vulnerabilities in extensions used by Belgium, [Sweden, and Qatar's governments](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/swift-banking-govt-middleware-rce) in recent months. "Since they are security tools, you'd expect them to be properly built. But they're so often making the same mistake, by allowing any site to pick which DLLs to load. I imagine it's to reduce friction so organizations can more easily load in the drivers for hardware security tokens and readers, because the driver DLLs aren't always known to the signing tool vendor or signed properly with Authenticode. It is insane to me how many independent vendors make this same mistake though."

## About the Author

[![Image 2: Nate Nelson](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt8edb70f1c73f8a5a/6978c3c26fdea754c9f2e2e9/Nate_Nelson_bio_pic_2-crop_2.jpg?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/nate-nelson)

Contributing Writer

Nate Nelson is a journalist and award-winning scriptwriter. In addition to Dark Reading he writes for Darknet Diaries, the most popular show in cybersecurity across all media.

He began his career as a freelancer, ghostwriting Forbes and CNBC op-eds for executives in tech and finance. Then he transitioned to journalism at Threatpost, where he covered cybersecurity news and trends. Throughout those years he co-created a cybersecurity podcast, Malicious Life, which in its day climbed into the Top 20 technology podcasts charts on Apple Podcasts and Spotify.

He holds degrees from New York University and Bard College. As a born and bred New Yorker, he enjoys a superiority complex, but is polite enough to keep it to himself.

[54] SecurityWeek - PHP Readies Critical Security Updates to Patch Another RCE Flaw
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 07:45:17 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/php-readies-critical-security-updates-to-patch-another-rce-flaw/

[TAM METİN - 2220 kelime]
PHP Readies Critical Security Updates to Patch Another RCE Flaw

Affecting the session upload progress, CVE-2026-115560 was publicly disclosed two days after PHP2Shell patches landed.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

PHP is readying a new batch of critical security updates, four weeks after patches for the PHP2Shell vulnerabilities landed.
Scheduled for November 19, between 14:00 and 14:30 UTC, the refreshes will fix critical-severity issues that can be triggered in non-default configurations or uncommon setups.



“These releases are part of PHP’s regular four-week release cycle and will address security vulnerabilities affecting all supported PHP versions. The highest severity is Critical,” PHP’s maintainers noted in a pre-release announcement.



One of the security defects expected to be resolved is CVE-2026-115560, a remote code execution (RCE) defect in the file-upload progress tracking functionality. The vulnerability was publicly disclosed two days after PHP2Shell made headlines.



PHP’s maintainers warned at the time that the security flaw can be triggered only under an unusual combination of configuration and code.



“An application must set session.serialize_handler to php_binary or php_serialize in its configuration and then switch it to php at runtime with ini_set() before calling session_start(). Session upload progress must also still be enabled,” PHP said last month.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Noting that patches would be released as part of the regular update cycle, PHP developers recommended disabling session upload, a setting also listed as a mitigation for CVE-2026-109619, one of the two RCE bugs tracked as PHP2Shell.



“Users are strongly encouraged to prepare to upgrade promptly once the releases become available. Technical details and fixed version numbers will be published alongside the releases,” PHP’s pre-release notice reads.



The PHP2Shell vulnerabilities have been widely exploited. Attacks began days before the patches were published.

[55] SecurityWeek - Palo Alto Networks to Buy AI Startup Applied Compute for $4.5 Billion
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 03:25:23 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/palo-alto-networks-to-buy-ai-startup-applied-compute-for-4-5-billion/

[TAM METİN - 1934 kelime]
Artificial Intelligence

Palo Alto Networks to Buy AI Startup Applied Compute for $4.5 Billion

The all-stock deal will give the cybersecurity company a platform for fine-tuning and running open-weight AI models in public and sovereign clouds.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Palo Alto Networks (NASDAQ: PANW) said Wednesday it has agreed to acquire AI infrastructure company Applied Compute in an all-stock transaction valued at $4.5 billion, subject to adjustments.
Applied Compute’s platform lets companies adapt existing open-weight AI models to their own data and run them in production. Palo Alto Networks plans to offer the platform to its security customers, with deployment options in public clouds and sovereign environments. Applied Compute will continue serving its existing customers. 



The acquisition gives Palo Alto Networks a role in how enterprises build and deploy AI, expanding its reach beyond the security tools those companies use to protect their deployments. Palo Alto CEO Nikesh Arora said he expects businesses to use a mix of open-weight and proprietary models. Palo Alto Networks evaluated Applied Compute as a prospective customer, and its Unit 42 team assessed the platform’s security, Arora said. 



Applied Compute co-founder and CEO Yash Patil will become co-chief AI officer at Palo Alto Networks after the deal closes. The startup’s research team will also join the company and help train proprietary AI models for use across its products. 



The deal is expected to close in its fiscal third quarter of 2027, pending regulatory clearance and other customary closing conditions. 



In September, Palo Alto Networks acquired Console, an AI-native platform designed to help organizations build agentic workflows and automate operational tasks using natural language.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Separately on Wednesday, Palo Alto Networks reported that revenue for its fiscal first quarter ended October 31 rose 39% year over year to $3.44 billion. Next-generation security annual recurring revenue climbed 66% to $9.73 billion. GAAP net income increased to $482 million, or $0.57 per diluted share, from $334 million, or $0.47 per share, a year earlier. 



The company also raised its full-year outlook and now expects fiscal 2027 revenue of $14.52 billion to $14.57 billion, up 26% to 27% year over year.

Artificial Intelligence

Vero Security Raises $11 Million for AI Security Engineering Platform

AI Security Engineering is a new and essential security discipline now being brought into focus by VeroSecurity.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Vero Security, founded in 2025 by IDF 8200 alumni Elad Tessler (CEO) and Daniel Meirov (CTO), has raised $11 million in a seed funding round led by TLV Partners. The firm is building a platform for AI Security Engineering (ASE) – the process of securing AI tools and agents – and will use the funding to advance the platform and grow its go-to-market presence in the United States.
AI Security Engineering is a new, and as yet ill-defined, security discipline. Its purpose is to secure the AI tools and agents that are increasingly being adopted, used, and modified in-house by company employees. This is happening at pace and volume – so the object of ASE is a moving target; and is a security requirement that is different to and separate from cybersecurity. 



ASE policy is defined by security teams. Vero turns these policy definitions into enforced controls, automatically updates them as the AI tools evolve, and refers only those enforcement decisions that require a human judgement to the security team. 



Vero’s idea is to bring a new approach to securing AI. “CISOs have seen enough dashboards and tables that tell their teams what’s wrong and leave the work to them. AI Security Engineering deserves a better operating model, designed for AI rather than borrowed from other areas of security,” commented Elad Tessler, Co-Founder and CEO of Vero Security. “We’re working alongside the first generation of AI security leaders and building Vero around what they actually need: a platform that does the work, keeps protections current as AI changes, and brings them only the decisions that require human judgment.”



The core technology within the platform is Vero’s AI Runtime Governor. It puts security decisions into effect as AI operates, enforcing approved policies and blocking agent actions that trigger unauthorized behavior. If a change requires a new risk decision, Vero brings the analysis and recommended action to the responsible security professional.



In parallel with developing its AI Security Engineering platform, Vero is establishing AISecurityEngineering.org, an open community and knowledge center featuring practical field guides, workshops and the ASE credential track. ASE is new, rapidly growing, and different to existing mainstream cybersecurity. Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


“Our own weekly count of open roles titled ‘AI Security Engineer’ went from 367 on 25 October to 451 on 14 November, up 23% in three weeks,” Tessler told SecurityWeek. “Demand is arriving before the discipline has been written down.”



He added, “Security disciplines that have lasted, like AppSec and detection engineering, grew a shared body of knowledge before they had standard job titles. AI Security Engineering needs that too, and right now!”



Vero is bringing order to the evolving and disorganized security requirement of AI Security Engineering. While the new open community will help to define and establish the discipline, Vero’s own platform will assist the ASE role to perform the operational work. 



“The Vero platform undertakes the impossible part of the ASE work, leaving the human engineer in situ to make judgement calls where necessary,” explained Tessler. The impossible part is the volume (dozens of tool changes every week, each one mapped to every control they touch), and tempo (agents act in seconds, so defenses must be in place before the action, not reviewed after it) of AI tool changes. “The human part is deciding what the business will accept, which exceptions make sense, and when a new tool is worth the risk.”



“We’re betting on more AI security engineers, not fewer,” said Tessler, “which is why we’re building AISecurityEngineering.org to train them. Without Vero, keeping up with AI in a large company takes a team. With it, one person, or a security leader taking it on alongside their job, can stay ahead.”



Vero is adding flesh to a newly emerging security requirement: AI Security Engineering. The Vero platform and AI Runtime Governor automates much of the mechanics of securing AI tools and agents used in-house, by continuously discovering, assessing and securing enterprise AI. Additionally, launching the separate AISecurityEngineering.org open community will help establish a body of subject knowledge to sustain and grow the new discipline.

“Our own weekly count of open roles titled ‘AI Security Engineer’ went from 367 on 25 October to 451 on 14 November, up 23% in three weeks,” Tessler told SecurityWeek. “Demand is arriving before the discipline has been written down.”



He added, “Security disciplines that have lasted, like AppSec and detection engineering, grew a shared body of knowledge before they had standard job titles. AI Security Engineering needs that too, and right now!”



Vero is bringing order to the evolving and disorganized security requirement of AI Security Engineering. While the new open community will help to define and establish the discipline, Vero’s own platform will assist the ASE role to perform the operational work. 



“The Vero platform undertakes the impossible part of the ASE work, leaving the human engineer in situ to make judgement calls where necessary,” explained Tessler. The impossible part is the volume (dozens of tool changes every week, each one mapped to every control they touch), and tempo (agents act in seconds, so defenses must be in place before the action, not reviewed after it) of AI tool changes. “The human part is deciding what the business will accept, which exceptions make sense, and when a new tool is worth the risk.”



“We’re betting on more AI security engineers, not fewer,” said Tessler, “which is why we’re building AISecurityEngineering.org to train them. Without Vero, keeping up with AI in a large company takes a team. With it, one person, or a security leader taking it on alongside their job, can stay ahead.”



Vero is adding flesh to a newly emerging security requirement: AI Security Engineering. The Vero platform and AI Runtime Governor automates much of the mechanics of securing AI tools and agents used in-house, by continuously discovering, assessing and securing enterprise AI. Additionally, launching the separate AISecurityEngineering.org open community will help establish a body of subject knowledge to sustain and grow the new discipline.
