================================================================================

[57] SecurityWeek - Russian APT Poses as Employee of Hacked Firm to Breach Another US Organization
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 13:33:47 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/russian-apt-poses-as-employee-of-hacked-firm-to-breach-another-us-organization/

[TAM METİN - 5919 kelime]
Russian APT Poses as Employee of Hacked Firm to Breach Another US Organization

Star Blizzard weaponized data and identities stolen in late 2025 to phish and compromise a US organization.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Russia-linked APT Star Blizzard hacked a US organization by impersonating an employee at another US organization it had previously compromised, Google Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) reports.
Also tracked as Callisto, Cold Relic, Cold River, UNC4057, and Gossamer Bear, and active for over a decade, Star Blizzard has been formally associated by the US with Russia’s intelligence service FSB.



The APT is known for mounting sophisticated phishing and malware campaigns to collect information relevant to the Russian government, and to use victims’ data in attacks on subsequent targets.



Star Blizzard “has been highly successful at compromising high-value targets (including government officials, defense contractors, and non-governmental organizations (NGOs) across Europe and North America) while maintaining a consistent operational tempo,” Google says.



In a recent attack against a US organization, Star Blizzard impersonated an employee of another organization it had breached months earlier. Google, which tracks the group as Cold Relic, believes the hackers likely used contact and relationship information obtained during the earlier intrusion to select and deceive their new target.



The first organization was hit in late 2025, using the compromised credentials of a super admin account. The APT leveraged a residential proxy network close to the victim and relied on multi-factor authentication (MFA) push notification fatigue to register a device to the victim’s tenant.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Next, the group downloaded the organization’s entire cloud storage drive, accessed the admin console to fingerprint defenses and remove evidence of the initial intrusion, exported full employee lists, and launched a domain-wide export.



According to Google, the group used passkeys, rogue MFA devices, and MFA backup codes to maintain persistent access to the victim organization for several months. It also exfiltrated emails and targeted cloud drive documents containing information of interest.



“When the threat actor was removed from the victim organization’s infrastructure, GTIG observed Cold Relic leveraging email accounts impersonating employees of the victim organization. These impersonated accounts were used to target both internal employees and associates within the same professional circles with renewed social engineering attempts,” Google says.



The APT impersonated an employee at the victim organization to establish communication with the second victim organization via a secure messaging application.



Ultimately, it shared phishing links designed to steal credentials and Entra ID authentication tokens, and used the secrets to register a new MFA device to the second organization’s SSO solution to retain access to the compromised account.



“By impersonating someone known in the same professional circles as the new target, Cold Relic increased their chance of phishing success. Ultimately, in this case, Cold Relic was able to obtain credentials to the organization and perform bulk download of files accessible in the compromised user’s cloud drive,” Google says.



To maintain persistence, the APT registered rogue MFA devices, passkeys, and MFA backup codes, conducted aggressive data collection and exfiltration, and searched for credentials, employee information, email archives, documents, and any other information it could use in subsequent spear-phishing attacks.



The threat actor also changed its profile name in the secure messaging application used in this intrusion, likely to reuse it in another attack.



“Cold Relic’s campaigns demonstrate the convergence of human-centric social engineering and cloud identity abuse. By obtaining access to strategic personnel rosters and operational documents, Russian intelligence services turn legitimate business communications into attack vectors against trusted partners,” Google notes.

[58] SecurityWeek - Last of Five Defendants Sentenced in US Scattered Spider Hacking Case
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 13:19:32 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/last-of-five-defendants-sentenced-in-us-scattered-spider-hacking-case/

[TAM METİN - 2532 kelime]
Last of Five Defendants Sentenced in US Scattered Spider Hacking Case

British national Tyler Robert Buchanan has been sentenced to 6 years in prison in the United States, the DOJ announced.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

A British Scattered Spider hacker will spend six years in a US federal prison for his part in a conspiracy to hack companies and take at least $11.6 million in cryptocurrency from individuals across the US.
Tyler Robert Buchanan, 24, from Dundee, Scotland, was arrested in Spain in May 2024 and has been held by US authorities since April 2025. He pleaded guilty in April 2026 to conspiracy to commit wire fraud and aggravated identity theft.



Between September 2021 and April 2023, the Scattered Spider group texted hundreds of phishing lures to staff at telecommunications, technology, interactive entertainment, cloud communications, and cryptocurrency companies.



Links in the texts pointed to phishing websites where recipients were tricked into entering credentials and personal information. Login details captured by the cybercriminals ended up in a Telegram channel managed by Buchanan and an accomplice.



With the stolen credentials, the hackers broke into employee accounts and company systems and took confidential information. That information helped them pinpoint and access cryptocurrency accounts and wallets owned by individuals.



To defeat two-factor authentication, they took over victims’ phone numbers through SIM swaps, moving the numbers to devices under their control.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


The theft total has grown from the $8 million cited at the time of Buchanan’s plea. The court will hold a restitution hearing on December 11.



Buchanan is the final defendant in the five-person case to receive a sentence. Ahmed Hossam Eldin Elbadawy and Evans Onyeaka Osiebo, both of Texas, were each given 45 months in September and August, respectively.



Noah Michael Urban is serving 10 years in federal prison, while Joel Martin Evans received a two-year term.



Earlier this year, two other Scattered Spider members, Thalha Jubair and Owen Flowers, were sentenced to jail in the United Kingdom for a cyberattack targeting Transport for London (TfL).



Related: Alleged Scattered Spider Hacker Extradited to US
Related: Ukrainian Conti Ransomware Developer Sentenced to 4 Years in US Prison
Related: Third US Security Expert Sentenced to Prison for Helping Ransomware Gang

[59] SecurityWeek - CISO Conversations: Patti Degnan – Part of the Job Is Deciding What Not to Do
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 12:00:00 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/ciso-conversations-patti-degnan-part-of-the-job-is-deciding-what-not-to-do/

[TAM METİN - 30564 kelime]
CISO Conversations: Patti Degnan – Part of the Job Is Deciding What Not to Do

Patti Degnan’s identity was stolen in 2006. It set her on a path to the CISO seat at endpoint security firm Glow.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Patti Degnan’s journey is unusual. She didn’t have an early plan to become a CISO; but perhaps life events made it inevitable.
Patti Degnan’s first encounter with security shaped her future interest: her identity was stolen in 2006. “That was my first introduction into safeguarding data. It piqued my interest in how to better protect my own information. I wanted to understand what had happened, so I tried to discover what information about me was held by different agencies. The intent was seeing if my personal data was safe – but the effect was learning the concepts of privacy and personal data protection.”



It wasn’t until joining Symantec in 2012 that this underlying interest began to rise to the surface. “I was in internal audit, focusing on the operational and IT perspective, and began to focus on data privacy.” This was pre-Snowden and years before GDPR. She was combining audit knowledge, PCI understanding and privacy knowledge at a time its importance was growing. 



Symantec had made several acquisitions. “We probably had close to 60 different backend infrastructure components that were all payment gateways. I worked hard trying to figure out what that complex environment looked like, and how we were deploying controls around it – and that was my first serious foray into security.”



She left Symantec in 2015, having become IT audit and compliance manager, and moved to LendingClub as senior manager in IT compliance and assurance. She was now seriously immersed in security. By 2018 she was senior director, information security GRC at Informatica. She then led security teams at Looker through its acquisition by Google, and by 2023 she was CISO at Notion. In 2025 she became an operating partner with the venture firm Andreessen Horowitz but returned to full-time cybersecurity in 2026 as CISO at Glow, an AI-powered endpoint security company that came out of stealth in July with $180 million in funding.



During this 20-year journey she evolved from a security victim to a security leader. Noticeably, however, it wasn’t a career plan to become a CISO – cybersecurity leadership simply grew from increasing interest and concern over personal privacy. Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Leadership is a complex subject. Not everyone can be a leader, but you must be a leader to be a CISO. Where does that ability come from? Are you born with it or without it, or is leadership a quality you can learn?



The most common suggestion is that you learn as you journey. Nobody leaves high school and jumps straight into being a CISO. The journey begins with excelling in a position within a small team, being promoted to lead that team, and progressing to lead a larger team. As time passes, you learn more leadership skills by leading teams. But this still doesn’t explain why some people can be excellent at their job, but don’t seem able to become a leader.



“Philosophically, I don’t think anyone cannot learn to be a leader. But I think you must want it. I think inherently everyone has the capability to become a leader in their own way,” says Degnan. The implication is that everyone is born with the potential to be a leader, but the potential can only be activated through individual desire to do so. If, for whatever reason, you want or need to be a leader, you can learn the skills necessary to be one.



Since Degnan’s early stolen identity, adversarial activity has grown from ‘simple’ identity theft to any credential theft that can be used as an entry point into enterprise networks. The threat is more sophisticated, and the risk is more damaging. It is no longer a simple threat to individuals but a threat to entire businesses. As this threat has widened, so have CISO responsibilities also widened – CISOs must now have increased business understanding to protect the business. Cybersecurity has become a business enabler rather than simply a threat preventer. The modern CISO needs to be a businessperson as well as a technical security expert; and the former is slowly increasing in importance over the latter. So, today, can a CISO be purely a businessperson without a background of technical expertise?



“I think you can,” she says, “although you must at least have some understanding of how technology works. But ultimately, it’s entirely dependent on the business you’re in. If you’re in a tech company, you should understand how technology works because you have to translate across different stakeholder groups. I’ve worked with a guy who has primarily worked in manufacturing, and his landscape of what he would have to secure is entirely different from what I’ve had to secure in my career; so yes, I do think you have to have some level of technical understanding. Do I think you need to be a hands-on keyboard developer to understand it? No.”



CISOs do not work alone. They either inherit an existing security team, add personnel to that team, or recruit a new one. So, what does Degnan look for when recruiting her own security team members? Are, for example, security certifications important? 




“To me, no. I know that’s controversial because some people spend their whole life getting a million certifications. I believe some people are great at applying theory to how things work in reality, but you don’t need to have a certification to do that. I’ve had multiple certifications over the years that I haven’t maintained, but they are a good foundation and a way to understand how things work in different areas of security, you know, in the different departments. But I don’t think it necessarily makes you a better practitioner.”

What she does look for, however, is proof of ownership and understanding of any claimed achievements. If a candidate’s resume claims to have built a specific process, she might ask for details. “Walk me through the thought process that got you there, and how did you do it?” she said. The purpose is not directly to challenge the candidate’s claim, but to discover how well they understand the cause of the need and the solution they created for it. 



This approach could but does not exclude keen newcomers who haven’t yet had any opportunity to build anything. “I would never exclude that type of person. But it’s not always possible to include them. It largely depends on the size and maturity of the team you’re building. Large teams can advocate for more junior people that you can grow and shape. Small teams tend to be super lean, and you need to concentrate on acquiring a candidate that can immediately shore up one or more of the domains you need to cover.”



Regardless of the method and reasoning behind recruiting individuals, she keeps one eye on the end product: a single team with a single culture of cooperation. “It is important,” she explained, “to have the right people on the team that are not simply curious and qualified for their own position but will also work together for the team. That’s very important because we’re all in the trenches together, pushing the same rock up the same hill.”



In this process, she doesn’t specifically seek diversity in team member selection. Diversity is likely to come naturally within a team built for functionality. Gender, background, and whether someone is neurodivergent or neurotypical are irrelevant to the purpose of building the best possible cooperative team, and she neither seeks nor rejects any candidate on diversity reasoning.



One issue that faces every security practitioner is ‘burnout’. The dictionary definition is ‘mental or physical collapse caused by overwork or stress’. Working in security embraces both causes: the work is always-on, crises occur at any hour of any day, and failure to remediate any crisis could adversely affect the company and everyone working for it. Burnout is often described as rampant within cybersecurity, with some surveys suggesting it affects between 50% to 70% of practitioners.



Recovery is possible, but it takes time. But then, what? A return to the same conditions that caused the burnout in the first place: overwork and stress? Preventing future or recurring burnout can only be achieved by improving the working environment of overwork and stress. Stress cannot be eliminated because it is caused by adversarial third parties over which we have no control. Overwork could be reduced by increasing the staffing levels; but it is rare for any CISO to have the budget to adequately do so. And, ironically, the more successful the CISO, the less likely they are to receive funding for more staff.





The cause of burnout is cybersecurity itself. The CISO cannot eliminate it. All a CISO can do is attempt to manage the symptoms as they become, or before they become, apparent. Today, AI can help. 



“I think burnout comes from everything being urgent,” explains Degnan. “Everything’s on fire all the time, and obviously in security, there’s an infinite amount of work. Part of the role of the CISO, or any leader, is deciding what not to do. So, I try to give very clear priorities. Automate the work you know machines should be doing in this day and age. Reserve humans for judgment and creativity in the problems where context really matters. And I’ve always been a big advocate for telling people to take time off. If you feel you need an extra day, take the day, especially now when we have the ability to build redundancy into workloads with AI. AI is pretty exciting. It gives you greater flexibility in reducing burnout.”



She goes further in time off. “My philosophy has always been that everybody should work wherever they’re going to do their best work. If someone is having a rough week and can’t come in to work, they shouldn’t. I would be happy to take the heat for it in that scenario.”



To be a successful CISO, she believes the two most important character traits are personal resiliency and communication skills. For the first, “In the face of the constantly evolving and changing concerns that are always there, you must be able to pick up the stick and keep walking every day.” That’s not just for yourself; it’s the image of strength you need to convey to your team.



Communication skills include the ability to receive information from one source and translate and deliver that information to a different destination. “You’re sitting between different levels of technical understanding among teams, executives, customers, and in some cases regulators. The board, if you report to it, is a prime example. You must have the communication skills able to translate the potential effect on the business of subtle and technical security issues into language the non- or less-technical business managers can really understand.”



For the CISOs with a planned career path, advice from mentors along the journey is common and important. Degnan had no visible path and received no specific advice. Instead, she learned from her own experiences. “The best lesson I ever learned was to never underestimate yourself,” she explained. “There will always be someone who can go deeper or be more technical in any domain, but you can still understand the strategy and the programs better than you credit yourself.”



But while she had no mentors, she is now a mentor for her security team. So, what advice would she give them in their career path? In specifics, it varies between individuals. “More generically, I have two pieces of advice. Firstly, always dive as deep as you can and know your domain, so you are comfortable when talking about it with other people. Secondly, understand the importance of communicating and partnering across the aisle. We’re not here just to look busy; we have to partner with other teams in the business to add the value of security understanding to what they are doing.” 



This is perhaps the single greatest lesson to be learned from Patti Degnan’s philosophy. Security is not an island – it is a partnership with the entirety of the business. Deep understanding of the domain, and constant cooperation within the security team, and from the team to the entirety of the business, is what leads to successful cybersecurity.



Related: CISO Conversations: Noopur Davis – The Accidental Global CISO at Comcast
Related: CISO Conversations: Chris Wheeler – Trust Is the Job, From the Navy to the C-Suite
Related: CISO Conversations: Nico Waisman – From Self-Taught Hacker to AI-Driven Offensive Security at XBOW
Related: CISO Conversations: Russ Kirby – Passion Is the Antidote to Burnout

In 2025 she became an operating partner with the venture firm Andreessen Horowitz but returned to full-time cybersecurity in 2026 as CISO at Glow, an AI-powered endpoint security company that came out of stealth in July with $180 million in funding.



During this 20-year journey she evolved from a security victim to a security leader. Noticeably, however, it wasn’t a career plan to become a CISO – cybersecurity leadership simply grew from increasing interest and concern over personal privacy. Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Leadership is a complex subject. Not everyone can be a leader, but you must be a leader to be a CISO. Where does that ability come from? Are you born with it or without it, or is leadership a quality you can learn?



The most common suggestion is that you learn as you journey. Nobody leaves high school and jumps straight into being a CISO. The journey begins with excelling in a position within a small team, being promoted to lead that team, and progressing to lead a larger team. As time passes, you learn more leadership skills by leading teams. But this still doesn’t explain why some people can be excellent at their job, but don’t seem able to become a leader.



“Philosophically, I don’t think anyone cannot learn to be a leader. But I think you must want it. I think inherently everyone has the capability to become a leader in their own way,” says Degnan. The implication is that everyone is born with the potential to be a leader, but the potential can only be activated through individual desire to do so. If, for whatever reason, you want or need to be a leader, you can learn the skills necessary to be one.



Since Degnan’s early stolen identity, adversarial activity has grown from ‘simple’ identity theft to any credential theft that can be used as an entry point into enterprise networks. The threat is more sophisticated, and the risk is more damaging. It is no longer a simple threat to individuals but a threat to entire businesses. As this threat has widened, so have CISO responsibilities also widened – CISOs must now have increased business understanding to protect the business. Cybersecurity has become a business enabler rather than simply a threat preventer. The modern CISO needs to be a businessperson as well as a technical security expert; and the former is slowly increasing in importance over the latter. So, today, can a CISO be purely a businessperson without a background of technical expertise?



“I think you can,” she says, “although you must at least have some understanding of how technology works. But ultimately, it’s entirely dependent on the business you’re in. If you’re in a tech company, you should understand how technology works because you have to translate across different stakeholder groups. I’ve worked with a guy who has primarily worked in manufacturing, and his landscape of what he would have to secure is entirely different from what I’ve had to secure in my career; so yes, I do think you have to have some level of technical understanding. Do I think you need to be a hands-on keyboard developer to understand it? No.”



CISOs do not work alone. They either inherit an existing security team, add personnel to that team, or recruit a new one. So, what does Degnan look for when recruiting her own security team members? Are, for example, security certifications important? 




“To me, no. I know that’s controversial because some people spend their whole life getting a million certifications. I believe some people are great at applying theory to how things work in reality, but you don’t need to have a certification to do that. I’ve had multiple certifications over the years that I haven’t maintained, but they are a good foundation and a way to understand how things work in different areas of security, you know, in the different departments. But I don’t think it necessarily makes you a better practitioner.”

What she does look for, however, is proof of ownership and understanding of any claimed achievements. If a candidate’s resume claims to have built a specific process, she might ask for details. “Walk me through the thought process that got you there, and how did you do it?” she said. The purpose is not directly to challenge the candidate’s claim, but to discover how well they understand the cause of the need and the solution they created for it. 



This approach could but does not exclude keen newcomers who haven’t yet had any opportunity to build anything. “I would never exclude that type of person. But it’s not always possible to include them. It largely depends on the size and maturity of the team you’re building. Large teams can advocate for more junior people that you can grow and shape. Small teams tend to be super lean, and you need to concentrate on acquiring a candidate that can immediately shore up one or more of the domains you need to cover.”



Regardless of the method and reasoning behind recruiting individuals, she keeps one eye on the end product: a single team with a single culture of cooperation. “It is important,” she explained, “to have the right people on the team that are not simply curious and qualified for their own position but will also work together for the team. That’s very important because we’re all in the trenches together, pushing the same rock up the same hill.”



In this process, she doesn’t specifically seek diversity in team member selection. Diversity is likely to come naturally within a team built for functionality. Gender, background, and whether someone is neurodivergent or neurotypical are irrelevant to the purpose of building the best possible cooperative team, and she neither seeks nor rejects any candidate on diversity reasoning.



One issue that faces every security practitioner is ‘burnout’. The dictionary definition is ‘mental or physical collapse caused by overwork or stress’. Working in security embraces both causes: the work is always-on, crises occur at any hour of any day, and failure to remediate any crisis could adversely affect the company and everyone working for it. Burnout is often described as rampant within cybersecurity, with some surveys suggesting it affects between 50% to 70% of practitioners.



Recovery is possible, but it takes time. But then, what? A return to the same conditions that caused the burnout in the first place: overwork and stress? Preventing future or recurring burnout can only be achieved by improving the working environment of overwork and stress. Stress cannot be eliminated because it is caused by adversarial third parties over which we have no control. Overwork could be reduced by increasing the staffing levels; but it is rare for any CISO to have the budget to adequately do so. And, ironically, the more successful the CISO, the less likely they are to receive funding for more staff.





The cause of burnout is cybersecurity itself. The CISO cannot eliminate it. All a CISO can do is attempt to manage the symptoms as they become, or before they become, apparent. Today, AI can help. 



“I think burnout comes from everything being urgent,” explains Degnan. “Everything’s on fire all the time, and obviously in security, there’s an infinite amount of work. Part of the role of the CISO, or any leader, is deciding what not to do. So, I try to give very clear priorities. Automate the work you know machines should be doing in this day and age. Reserve humans for judgment and creativity in the problems where context really matters. And I’ve always been a big advocate for telling people to take time off. If you feel you need an extra day, take the day, especially now when we have the ability to build redundancy into workloads with AI. AI is pretty exciting. It gives you greater flexibility in reducing burnout.”



She goes further in time off. “My philosophy has always been that everybody should work wherever they’re going to do their best work. If someone is having a rough week and can’t come in to work, they shouldn’t. I would be happy to take the heat for it in that scenario.”



To be a successful CISO, she believes the two most important character traits are personal resiliency and communication skills. For the first, “In the face of the constantly evolving and changing concerns that are always there, you must be able to pick up the stick and keep walking every day.” That’s not just for yourself; it’s the image of strength you need to convey to your team.



Communication skills include the ability to receive information from one source and translate and deliver that information to a different destination. “You’re sitting between different levels of technical understanding among teams, executives, customers, and in some cases regulators. The board, if you report to it, is a prime example. You must have the communication skills able to translate the potential effect on the business of subtle and technical security issues into language the non- or less-technical business managers can really understand.”



For the CISOs with a planned career path, advice from mentors along the journey is common and important. Degnan had no visible path and received no specific advice. Instead, she learned from her own experiences. “The best lesson I ever learned was to never underestimate yourself,” she explained. “There will always be someone who can go deeper or be more technical in any domain, but you can still understand the strategy and the programs better than you credit yourself.”



But while she had no mentors, she is now a mentor for her security team. So, what advice would she give them in their career path? In specifics, it varies between individuals. “More generically, I have two pieces of advice. Firstly, always dive as deep as you can and know your domain, so you are comfortable when talking about it with other people. Secondly, understand the importance of communicating and partnering across the aisle. We’re not here just to look busy; we have to partner with other teams in the business to add the value of security understanding to what they are doing.” 



This is perhaps the single greatest lesson to be learned from Patti Degnan’s philosophy. Security is not an island – it is a partnership with the entirety of the business. Deep understanding of the domain, and constant cooperation within the security team, and from the team to the entirety of the business, is what leads to successful cybersecurity.



Related: CISO Conversations: Noopur Davis – The Accidental Global CISO at Comcast
Related: CISO Conversations: Chris Wheeler – Trust Is the Job, From the Navy to the C-Suite
Related: CISO Conversations: Nico Waisman – From Self-Taught Hacker to AI-Driven Offensive Security at XBOW
Related: CISO Conversations: Russ Kirby – Passion Is the Antidote to Burnout

It wasn’t until joining Symantec in 2012 that this underlying interest began to rise to the surface. “I was in internal audit, focusing on the operational and IT perspective, and began to focus on data privacy.” This was pre-Snowden and years before GDPR. She was combining audit knowledge, PCI understanding and privacy knowledge at a time its importance was growing. 



Symantec had made several acquisitions. “We probably had close to 60 different backend infrastructure components that were all payment gateways. I worked hard trying to figure out what that complex environment looked like, and how we were deploying controls around it – and that was my first serious foray into security.”



She left Symantec in 2015, having become IT audit and compliance manager, and moved to LendingClub as senior manager in IT compliance and assurance. She was now seriously immersed in security. By 2018 she was senior director, information security GRC at Informatica. She then led security teams at Looker through its acquisition by Google, and by 2023 she was CISO at Notion. In 2025 she became an operating partner with the venture firm Andreessen Horowitz but returned to full-time cybersecurity in 2026 as CISO at Glow, an AI-powered endpoint security company that came out of stealth in July with $180 million in funding.



During this 20-year journey she evolved from a security victim to a security leader. Noticeably, however, it wasn’t a career plan to become a CISO – cybersecurity leadership simply grew from increasing interest and concern over personal privacy. Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.

Cybersecurity has become a business enabler rather than simply a threat preventer. The modern CISO needs to be a businessperson as well as a technical security expert; and the former is slowly increasing in importance over the latter. So, today, can a CISO be purely a businessperson without a background of technical expertise?



“I think you can,” she says, “although you must at least have some understanding of how technology works. But ultimately, it’s entirely dependent on the business you’re in. If you’re in a tech company, you should understand how technology works because you have to translate across different stakeholder groups. I’ve worked with a guy who has primarily worked in manufacturing, and his landscape of what he would have to secure is entirely different from what I’ve had to secure in my career; so yes, I do think you have to have some level of technical understanding. Do I think you need to be a hands-on keyboard developer to understand it? No.”



CISOs do not work alone. They either inherit an existing security team, add personnel to that team, or recruit a new one. So, what does Degnan look for when recruiting her own security team members? Are, for example, security certifications important? 




“To me, no. I know that’s controversial because some people spend their whole life getting a million certifications. I believe some people are great at applying theory to how things work in reality, but you don’t need to have a certification to do that. I’ve had multiple certifications over the years that I haven’t maintained, but they are a good foundation and a way to understand how things work in different areas of security, you know, in the different departments. But I don’t think it necessarily makes you a better practitioner.”

What she does look for, however, is proof of ownership and understanding of any claimed achievements. If a candidate’s resume claims to have built a specific process, she might ask for details. “Walk me through the thought process that got you there, and how did you do it?” she said. The purpose is not directly to challenge the candidate’s claim, but to discover how well they understand the cause of the need and the solution they created for it. 



This approach could but does not exclude keen newcomers who haven’t yet had any opportunity to build anything. “I would never exclude that type of person. But it’s not always possible to include them. It largely depends on the size and maturity of the team you’re building. Large teams can advocate for more junior people that you can grow and shape. Small teams tend to be super lean, and you need to concentrate on acquiring a candidate that can immediately shore up one or more of the domains you need to cover.”



Regardless of the method and reasoning behind recruiting individuals, she keeps one eye on the end product: a single team with a single culture of cooperation. “It is important,” she explained, “to have the right people on the team that are not simply curious and qualified for their own position but will also work together for the team. That’s very important because we’re all in the trenches together, pushing the same rock up the same hill.”



In this process, she doesn’t specifically seek diversity in team member selection. Diversity is likely to come naturally within a team built for functionality. Gender, background, and whether someone is neurodivergent or neurotypical are irrelevant to the purpose of building the best possible cooperative team, and she neither seeks nor rejects any candidate on diversity reasoning.



One issue that faces every security practitioner is ‘burnout’. The dictionary definition is ‘mental or physical collapse caused by overwork or stress’. Working in security embraces both causes: the work is always-on, crises occur at any hour of any day, and failure to remediate any crisis could adversely affect the company and everyone working for it. Burnout is often described as rampant within cybersecurity, with some surveys suggesting it affects between 50% to 70% of practitioners.



Recovery is possible, but it takes time. But then, what? A return to the same conditions that caused the burnout in the first place: overwork and stress? Preventing future or recurring burnout can only be achieved by improving the working environment of overwork and stress. Stress cannot be eliminated because it is caused by adversarial third parties over which we have no control. Overwork could be reduced by increasing the staffing levels; but it is rare for any CISO to have the budget to adequately do so. And, ironically, the more successful the CISO, the less likely they are to receive funding for more staff.





The cause of burnout is cybersecurity itself. The CISO cannot eliminate it. All a CISO can do is attempt to manage the symptoms as they become, or before they become, apparent. Today, AI can help. 



“I think burnout comes from everything being urgent,” explains Degnan. “Everything’s on fire all the time, and obviously in security, there’s an infinite amount of work. Part of the role of the CISO, or any leader, is deciding what not to do. So, I try to give very clear priorities. Automate the work you know machines should be doing in this day and age. Reserve humans for judgment and creativity in the problems where context really matters. And I’ve always been a big advocate for telling people to take time off. If you feel you need an extra day, take the day, especially now when we have the ability to build redundancy into workloads with AI. AI is pretty exciting. It gives you greater flexibility in reducing burnout.”



She goes further in time off. “My philosophy has always been that everybody should work wherever they’re going to do their best work. If someone is having a rough week and can’t come in to work, they shouldn’t. I would be happy to take the heat for it in that scenario.”



To be a successful CISO, she believes the two most important character traits are personal resiliency and communication skills. For the first, “In the face of the constantly evolving and changing concerns that are always there, you must be able to pick up the stick and keep walking every day.” That’s not just for yourself; it’s the image of strength you need to convey to your team.



Communication skills include the ability to receive information from one source and translate and deliver that information to a different destination. “You’re sitting between different levels of technical understanding among teams, executives, customers, and in some cases regulators. The board, if you report to it, is a prime example. You must have the communication skills able to translate the potential effect on the business of subtle and technical security issues into language the non- or less-technical business managers can really understand.”



For the CISOs with a planned career path, advice from mentors along the journey is common and important. Degnan had no visible path and received no specific advice. Instead, she learned from her own experiences. “The best lesson I ever learned was to never underestimate yourself,” she explained. “There will always be someone who can go deeper or be more technical in any domain, but you can still understand the strategy and the programs better than you credit yourself.”



But while she had no mentors, she is now a mentor for her security team. So, what advice would she give them in their career path? In specifics, it varies between individuals. “More generically, I have two pieces of advice. Firstly, always dive as deep as you can and know your domain, so you are comfortable when talking about it with other people. Secondly, understand the importance of communicating and partnering across the aisle. We’re not here just to look busy; we have to partner with other teams in the business to add the value of security understanding to what they are doing.” 



This is perhaps the single greatest lesson to be learned from Patti Degnan’s philosophy. Security is not an island – it is a partnership with the entirety of the business. Deep understanding of the domain, and constant cooperation within the security team, and from the team to the entirety of the business, is what leads to successful cybersecurity.

Related: CISO Conversations: Chris Wheeler – Trust Is the Job, From the Navy to the C-Suite
Related: CISO Conversations: Nico Waisman – From Self-Taught Hacker to AI-Driven Offensive Security at XBOW
Related: CISO Conversations: Russ Kirby – Passion Is the Antidote to Burnout

During this 20-year journey she evolved from a security victim to a security leader. Noticeably, however, it wasn’t a career plan to become a CISO – cybersecurity leadership simply grew from increasing interest and concern over personal privacy. Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Leadership is a complex subject. Not everyone can be a leader, but you must be a leader to be a CISO. Where does that ability come from? Are you born with it or without it, or is leadership a quality you can learn?



The most common suggestion is that you learn as you journey. Nobody leaves high school and jumps straight into being a CISO. The journey begins with excelling in a position within a small team, being promoted to lead that team, and progressing to lead a larger team. As time passes, you learn more leadership skills by leading teams. But this still doesn’t explain why some people can be excellent at their job, but don’t seem able to become a leader.



“Philosophically, I don’t think anyone cannot learn to be a leader. But I think you must want it. I think inherently everyone has the capability to become a leader in their own way,” says Degnan. The implication is that everyone is born with the potential to be a leader, but the potential can only be activated through individual desire to do so. If, for whatever reason, you want or need to be a leader, you can learn the skills necessary to be one.



Since Degnan’s early stolen identity, adversarial activity has grown from ‘simple’ identity theft to any credential theft that can be used as an entry point into enterprise networks. The threat is more sophisticated, and the risk is more damaging. It is no longer a simple threat to individuals but a threat to entire businesses. As this threat has widened, so have CISO responsibilities also widened – CISOs must now have increased business understanding to protect the business. Cybersecurity has become a business enabler rather than simply a threat preventer. The modern CISO needs to be a businessperson as well as a technical security expert; and the former is slowly increasing in importance over the latter. So, today, can a CISO be purely a businessperson without a background of technical expertise?



“I think you can,” she says, “although you must at least have some understanding of how technology works. But ultimately, it’s entirely dependent on the business you’re in. If you’re in a tech company, you should understand how technology works because you have to translate across different stakeholder groups. I’ve worked with a guy who has primarily worked in manufacturing, and his landscape of what he would have to secure is entirely different from what I’ve had to secure in my career; so yes, I do think you have to have some level of technical understanding. Do I think you need to be a hands-on keyboard developer to understand it? No.”



CISOs do not work alone. They either inherit an existing security team, add personnel to that team, or recruit a new one. So, what does Degnan look for when recruiting her own security team members? Are, for example, security certifications important? 




“To me, no. I know that’s controversial because some people spend their whole life getting a million certifications. I believe some people are great at applying theory to how things work in reality, but you don’t need to have a certification to do that. I’ve had multiple certifications over the years that I haven’t maintained, but they are a good foundation and a way to understand how things work in different areas of security, you know, in the different departments. But I don’t think it necessarily makes you a better practitioner.”

What she does look for, however, is proof of ownership and understanding of any claimed achievements. If a candidate’s resume claims to have built a specific process, she might ask for details. “Walk me through the thought process that got you there, and how did you do it?” she said. The purpose is not directly to challenge the candidate’s claim, but to discover how well they understand the cause of the need and the solution they created for it. 



This approach could but does not exclude keen newcomers who haven’t yet had any opportunity to build anything. “I would never exclude that type of person. But it’s not always possible to include them. It largely depends on the size and maturity of the team you’re building. Large teams can advocate for more junior people that you can grow and shape. Small teams tend to be super lean, and you need to concentrate on acquiring a candidate that can immediately shore up one or more of the domains you need to cover.”



Regardless of the method and reasoning behind recruiting individuals, she keeps one eye on the end product: a single team with a single culture of cooperation. “It is important,” she explained, “to have the right people on the team that are not simply curious and qualified for their own position but will also work together for the team. That’s very important because we’re all in the trenches together, pushing the same rock up the same hill.”



In this process, she doesn’t specifically seek diversity in team member selection. Diversity is likely to come naturally within a team built for functionality. Gender, background, and whether someone is neurodivergent or neurotypical are irrelevant to the purpose of building the best possible cooperative team, and she neither seeks nor rejects any candidate on diversity reasoning.

She goes further in time off. “My philosophy has always been that everybody should work wherever they’re going to do their best work. If someone is having a rough week and can’t come in to work, they shouldn’t. I would be happy to take the heat for it in that scenario.”



To be a successful CISO, she believes the two most important character traits are personal resiliency and communication skills. For the first, “In the face of the constantly evolving and changing concerns that are always there, you must be able to pick up the stick and keep walking every day.” That’s not just for yourself; it’s the image of strength you need to convey to your team.



Communication skills include the ability to receive information from one source and translate and deliver that information to a different destination. “You’re sitting between different levels of technical understanding among teams, executives, customers, and in some cases regulators. The board, if you report to it, is a prime example. You must have the communication skills able to translate the potential effect on the business of subtle and technical security issues into language the non- or less-technical business managers can really understand.”



For the CISOs with a planned career path, advice from mentors along the journey is common and important. Degnan had no visible path and received no specific advice. Instead, she learned from her own experiences. “The best lesson I ever learned was to never underestimate yourself,” she explained. “There will always be someone who can go deeper or be more technical in any domain, but you can still understand the strategy and the programs better than you credit yourself.”



But while she had no mentors, she is now a mentor for her security team. So, what advice would she give them in their career path? In specifics, it varies between individuals. “More generically, I have two pieces of advice. Firstly, always dive as deep as you can and know your domain, so you are comfortable when talking about it with other people. Secondly, understand the importance of communicating and partnering across the aisle. We’re not here just to look busy; we have to partner with other teams in the business to add the value of security understanding to what they are doing.” 



This is perhaps the single greatest lesson to be learned from Patti Degnan’s philosophy. Security is not an island – it is a partnership with the entirety of the business. Deep understanding of the domain, and constant cooperation within the security team, and from the team to the entirety of the business, is what leads to successful cybersecurity.



Related: CISO Conversations: Noopur Davis – The Accidental Global CISO at Comcast
Related: CISO Conversations: Chris Wheeler – Trust Is the Job, From the Navy to the C-Suite
Related: CISO Conversations: Nico Waisman – From Self-Taught Hacker to AI-Driven Offensive Security at XBOW
Related: CISO Conversations: Russ Kirby – Passion Is the Antidote to Burnout

“Philosophically, I don’t think anyone cannot learn to be a leader. But I think you must want it. I think inherently everyone has the capability to become a leader in their own way,” says Degnan. The implication is that everyone is born with the potential to be a leader, but the potential can only be activated through individual desire to do so. If, for whatever reason, you want or need to be a leader, you can learn the skills necessary to be one.



Since Degnan’s early stolen identity, adversarial activity has grown from ‘simple’ identity theft to any credential theft that can be used as an entry point into enterprise networks. The threat is more sophisticated, and the risk is more damaging. It is no longer a simple threat to individuals but a threat to entire businesses. As this threat has widened, so have CISO responsibilities also widened – CISOs must now have increased business understanding to protect the business. Cybersecurity has become a business enabler rather than simply a threat preventer. The modern CISO needs to be a businessperson as well as a technical security expert; and the former is slowly increasing in importance over the latter. So, today, can a CISO be purely a businessperson without a background of technical expertise?



“I think you can,” she says, “although you must at least have some understanding of how technology works. But ultimately, it’s entirely dependent on the business you’re in. If you’re in a tech company, you should understand how technology works because you have to translate across different stakeholder groups. I’ve worked with a guy who has primarily worked in manufacturing, and his landscape of what he would have to secure is entirely different from what I’ve had to secure in my career; so yes, I do think you have to have some level of technical understanding. Do I think you need to be a hands-on keyboard developer to understand it? No.”



CISOs do not work alone. They either inherit an existing security team, add personnel to that team, or recruit a new one. So, what does Degnan look for when recruiting her own security team members? Are, for example, security certifications important? 




“To me, no. I know that’s controversial because some people spend their whole life getting a million certifications. I believe some people are great at applying theory to how things work in reality, but you don’t need to have a certification to do that. I’ve had multiple certifications over the years that I haven’t maintained, but they are a good foundation and a way to understand how things work in different areas of security, you know, in the different departments. But I don’t think it necessarily makes you a better practitioner.”

What she does look for, however, is proof of ownership and understanding of any claimed achievements. If a candidate’s resume claims to have built a specific process, she might ask for details. “Walk me through the thought process that got you there, and how did you do it?” she said. The purpose is not directly to challenge the candidate’s claim, but to discover how well they understand the cause of the need and the solution they created for it. 



This approach could but does not exclude keen newcomers who haven’t yet had any opportunity to build anything. “I would never exclude that type of person. But it’s not always possible to include them. It largely depends on the size and maturity of the team you’re building. Large teams can advocate for more junior people that you can grow and shape. Small teams tend to be super lean, and you need to concentrate on acquiring a candidate that can immediately shore up one or more of the domains you need to cover.”



Regardless of the method and reasoning behind recruiting individuals, she keeps one eye on the end product: a single team with a single culture of cooperation. “It is important,” she explained, “to have the right people on the team that are not simply curious and qualified for their own position but will also work together for the team. That’s very important because we’re all in the trenches together, pushing the same rock up the same hill.”



In this process, she doesn’t specifically seek diversity in team member selection. Diversity is likely to come naturally within a team built for functionality. Gender, background, and whether someone is neurodivergent or neurotypical are irrelevant to the purpose of building the best possible cooperative team, and she neither seeks nor rejects any candidate on diversity reasoning.



One issue that faces every security practitioner is ‘burnout’. The dictionary definition is ‘mental or physical collapse caused by overwork or stress’. Working in security embraces both causes: the work is always-on, crises occur at any hour of any day, and failure to remediate any crisis could adversely affect the company and everyone working for it. Burnout is often described as rampant within cybersecurity, with some surveys suggesting it affects between 50% to 70% of practitioners.



Recovery is possible, but it takes time. But then, what? A return to the same conditions that caused the burnout in the first place: overwork and stress? Preventing future or recurring burnout can only be achieved by improving the working environment of overwork and stress. Stress cannot be eliminated because it is caused by adversarial third parties over which we have no control. Overwork could be reduced by increasing the staffing levels; but it is rare for any CISO to have the budget to adequately do so. And, ironically, the more successful the CISO, the less likely they are to receive funding for more staff.





The cause of burnout is cybersecurity itself. The CISO cannot eliminate it. All a CISO can do is attempt to manage the symptoms as they become, or before they become, apparent. Today, AI can help. 



“I think burnout comes from everything being urgent,” explains Degnan. “Everything’s on fire all the time, and obviously in security, there’s an infinite amount of work. Part of the role of the CISO, or any leader, is deciding what not to do. So, I try to give very clear priorities. Automate the work you know machines should be doing in this day and age. Reserve humans for judgment and creativity in the problems where context really matters. And I’ve always been a big advocate for telling people to take time off. If you feel you need an extra day, take the day, especially now when we have the ability to build redundancy into workloads with AI. AI is pretty exciting. It gives you greater flexibility in reducing burnout.”



She goes further in time off. “My philosophy has always been that everybody should work wherever they’re going to do their best work. If someone is having a rough week and can’t come in to work, they shouldn’t. I would be happy to take the heat for it in that scenario.”



To be a successful CISO, she believes the two most important character traits are personal resiliency and communication skills. For the first, “In the face of the constantly evolving and changing concerns that are always there, you must be able to pick up the stick and keep walking every day.” That’s not just for yourself; it’s the image of strength you need to convey to your team.



Communication skills include the ability to receive information from one source and translate and deliver that information to a different destination. “You’re sitting between different levels of technical understanding among teams, executives, customers, and in some cases regulators. The board, if you report to it, is a prime example. You must have the communication skills able to translate the potential effect on the business of subtle and technical security issues into language the non- or less-technical business managers can really understand.”



For the CISOs with a planned career path, advice from mentors along the journey is common and important. Degnan had no visible path and received no specific advice. Instead, she learned from her own experiences. “The best lesson I ever learned was to never underestimate yourself,” she explained. “There will always be someone who can go deeper or be more technical in any domain, but you can still understand the strategy and the programs better than you credit yourself.”



But while she had no mentors, she is now a mentor for her security team. So, what advice would she give them in their career path? In specifics, it varies between individuals. “More generically, I have two pieces of advice. Firstly, always dive as deep as you can and know your domain, so you are comfortable when talking about it with other people. Secondly, understand the importance of communicating and partnering across the aisle. We’re not here just to look busy; we have to partner with other teams in the business to add the value of security understanding to what they are doing.” 



This is perhaps the single greatest lesson to be learned from Patti Degnan’s philosophy. Security is not an island – it is a partnership with the entirety of the business. Deep understanding of the domain, and constant cooperation within the security team, and from the team to the entirety of the business, is what leads to successful cybersecurity.

Secondly, understand the importance of communicating and partnering across the aisle. We’re not here just to look busy; we have to partner with other teams in the business to add the value of security understanding to what they are doing.” 



This is perhaps the single greatest lesson to be learned from Patti Degnan’s philosophy. Security is not an island – it is a partnership with the entirety of the business. Deep understanding of the domain, and constant cooperation within the security team, and from the team to the entirety of the business, is what leads to successful cybersecurity.



Related: CISO Conversations: Noopur Davis – The Accidental Global CISO at Comcast
Related: CISO Conversations: Chris Wheeler – Trust Is the Job, From the Navy to the C-Suite
Related: CISO Conversations: Nico Waisman – From Self-Taught Hacker to AI-Driven Offensive Security at XBOW
Related: CISO Conversations: Russ Kirby – Passion Is the Antidote to Burnout

“To me, no. I know that’s controversial because some people spend their whole life getting a million certifications. I believe some people are great at applying theory to how things work in reality, but you don’t need to have a certification to do that. I’ve had multiple certifications over the years that I haven’t maintained, but they are a good foundation and a way to understand how things work in different areas of security, you know, in the different departments. But I don’t think it necessarily makes you a better practitioner.”

What she does look for, however, is proof of ownership and understanding of any claimed achievements. If a candidate’s resume claims to have built a specific process, she might ask for details. “Walk me through the thought process that got you there, and how did you do it?” she said. The purpose is not directly to challenge the candidate’s claim, but to discover how well they understand the cause of the need and the solution they created for it. 



This approach could but does not exclude keen newcomers who haven’t yet had any opportunity to build anything. “I would never exclude that type of person. But it’s not always possible to include them. It largely depends on the size and maturity of the team you’re building. Large teams can advocate for more junior people that you can grow and shape. Small teams tend to be super lean, and you need to concentrate on acquiring a candidate that can immediately shore up one or more of the domains you need to cover.”



Regardless of the method and reasoning behind recruiting individuals, she keeps one eye on the end product: a single team with a single culture of cooperation. “It is important,” she explained, “to have the right people on the team that are not simply curious and qualified for their own position but will also work together for the team. That’s very important because we’re all in the trenches together, pushing the same rock up the same hill.”



In this process, she doesn’t specifically seek diversity in team member selection. Diversity is likely to come naturally within a team built for functionality. Gender, background, and whether someone is neurodivergent or neurotypical are irrelevant to the purpose of building the best possible cooperative team, and she neither seeks nor rejects any candidate on diversity reasoning.



One issue that faces every security practitioner is ‘burnout’. The dictionary definition is ‘mental or physical collapse caused by overwork or stress’. Working in security embraces both causes: the work is always-on, crises occur at any hour of any day, and failure to remediate any crisis could adversely affect the company and everyone working for it. Burnout is often described as rampant within cybersecurity, with some surveys suggesting it affects between 50% to 70% of practitioners.



Recovery is possible, but it takes time. But then, what? A return to the same conditions that caused the burnout in the first place: overwork and stress? Preventing future or recurring burnout can only be achieved by improving the working environment of overwork and stress. Stress cannot be eliminated because it is caused by adversarial third parties over which we have no control. Overwork could be reduced by increasing the staffing levels; but it is rare for any CISO to have the budget to adequately do so. And, ironically, the more successful the CISO, the less likely they are to receive funding for more staff.





The cause of burnout is cybersecurity itself. The CISO cannot eliminate it. All a CISO can do is attempt to manage the symptoms as they become, or before they become, apparent. Today, AI can help. 



“I think burnout comes from everything being urgent,” explains Degnan. “Everything’s on fire all the time, and obviously in security, there’s an infinite amount of work. Part of the role of the CISO, or any leader, is deciding what not to do. So, I try to give very clear priorities. Automate the work you know machines should be doing in this day and age. Reserve humans for judgment and creativity in the problems where context really matters. And I’ve always been a big advocate for telling people to take time off. If you feel you need an extra day, take the day, especially now when we have the ability to build redundancy into workloads with AI. AI is pretty exciting. It gives you greater flexibility in reducing burnout.”



She goes further in time off. “My philosophy has always been that everybody should work wherever they’re going to do their best work. If someone is having a rough week and can’t come in to work, they shouldn’t. I would be happy to take the heat for it in that scenario.”



To be a successful CISO, she believes the two most important character traits are personal resiliency and communication skills. For the first, “In the face of the constantly evolving and changing concerns that are always there, you must be able to pick up the stick and keep walking every day.” That’s not just for yourself; it’s the image of strength you need to convey to your team.



Communication skills include the ability to receive information from one source and translate and deliver that information to a different destination. “You’re sitting between different levels of technical understanding among teams, executives, customers, and in some cases regulators. The board, if you report to it, is a prime example. You must have the communication skills able to translate the potential effect on the business of subtle and technical security issues into language the non- or less-technical business managers can really understand.”



For the CISOs with a planned career path, advice from mentors along the journey is common and important. Degnan had no visible path and received no specific advice. Instead, she learned from her own experiences. “The best lesson I ever learned was to never underestimate yourself,” she explained. “There will always be someone who can go deeper or be more technical in any domain, but you can still understand the strategy and the programs better than you credit yourself.”



But while she had no mentors, she is now a mentor for her security team. So, what advice would she give them in their career path? In specifics, it varies between individuals. “More generically, I have two pieces of advice. Firstly, always dive as deep as you can and know your domain, so you are comfortable when talking about it with other people. Secondly, understand the importance of communicating and partnering across the aisle. We’re not here just to look busy; we have to partner with other teams in the business to add the value of security understanding to what they are doing.” 



This is perhaps the single greatest lesson to be learned from Patti Degnan’s philosophy. Security is not an island – it is a partnership with the entirety of the business. Deep understanding of the domain, and constant cooperation within the security team, and from the team to the entirety of the business, is what leads to successful cybersecurity.

It gives you greater flexibility in reducing burnout.”



She goes further in time off. “My philosophy has always been that everybody should work wherever they’re going to do their best work. If someone is having a rough week and can’t come in to work, they shouldn’t. I would be happy to take the heat for it in that scenario.”



To be a successful CISO, she believes the two most important character traits are personal resiliency and communication skills. For the first, “In the face of the constantly evolving and changing concerns that are always there, you must be able to pick up the stick and keep walking every day.” That’s not just for yourself; it’s the image of strength you need to convey to your team.



Communication skills include the ability to receive information from one source and translate and deliver that information to a different destination. “You’re sitting between different levels of technical understanding among teams, executives, customers, and in some cases regulators. The board, if you report to it, is a prime example. You must have the communication skills able to translate the potential effect on the business of subtle and technical security issues into language the non- or less-technical business managers can really understand.”



For the CISOs with a planned career path, advice from mentors along the journey is common and important. Degnan had no visible path and received no specific advice. Instead, she learned from her own experiences. “The best lesson I ever learned was to never underestimate yourself,” she explained. “There will always be someone who can go deeper or be more technical in any domain, but you can still understand the strategy and the programs better than you credit yourself.”



But while she had no mentors, she is now a mentor for her security team. So, what advice would she give them in their career path? In specifics, it varies between individuals. “More generically, I have two pieces of advice. Firstly, always dive as deep as you can and know your domain, so you are comfortable when talking about it with other people. Secondly, understand the importance of communicating and partnering across the aisle. We’re not here just to look busy; we have to partner with other teams in the business to add the value of security understanding to what they are doing.” 



This is perhaps the single greatest lesson to be learned from Patti Degnan’s philosophy. Security is not an island – it is a partnership with the entirety of the business. Deep understanding of the domain, and constant cooperation within the security team, and from the team to the entirety of the business, is what leads to successful cybersecurity.



Related: CISO Conversations: Noopur Davis – The Accidental Global CISO at Comcast
Related: CISO Conversations: Chris Wheeler – Trust Is the Job, From the Navy to the C-Suite
Related: CISO Conversations: Nico Waisman – From Self-Taught Hacker to AI-Driven Offensive Security at XBOW
Related: CISO Conversations: Russ Kirby – Passion Is the Antidote to Burnout

“I think burnout comes from everything being urgent,” explains Degnan. “Everything’s on fire all the time, and obviously in security, there’s an infinite amount of work. Part of the role of the CISO, or any leader, is deciding what not to do. So, I try to give very clear priorities. Automate the work you know machines should be doing in this day and age. Reserve humans for judgment and creativity in the problems where context really matters. And I’ve always been a big advocate for telling people to take time off. If you feel you need an extra day, take the day, especially now when we have the ability to build redundancy into workloads with AI. AI is pretty exciting. It gives you greater flexibility in reducing burnout.”



She goes further in time off. “My philosophy has always been that everybody should work wherever they’re going to do their best work. If someone is having a rough week and can’t come in to work, they shouldn’t. I would be happy to take the heat for it in that scenario.”

[60] SecurityWeek - Critical Perforce Puppet RCE Exploited in Attacks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 10:37:08 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/critical-perforce-puppet-rce-exploited-in-attacks/

[TAM METİN - 2825 kelime]
Critical Perforce Puppet RCE Exploited in Attacks

Tracked as CVE-2026-115731, it allows a client that holds any CA-signed agent certificate to execute code on the server.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Threat actors have been exploiting a critical-severity vulnerability in Perforce Puppet to execute code on vulnerable servers.
Puppet is an infrastructure-as-code (IaC) and configuration management platform that uses a client-server architecture for device management.



Tracked as CVE-2026-115731 (CVSS score of 9.4), the exploited security defect is described as a deserialization of untrusted data issue in the report processing of Puppet Enterprise and Puppet Core, leading to remote code execution (RCE).



It allows clients that have a certificate signed by the Puppet certificate authority (CA) to send reports containing malicious payloads that would be executed on the server with the service user permissions, putting managed systems at risk.



“A successful attack could expose the server’s certificate-signing key, allow an attacker to issue trusted certificates, and enable changes to software and configuration delivered to managed systems on their next Puppet run,” Perforce explains in an advisory.



Unauthenticated attackers could obtain a certificate if the CA auto-signs requests without performing strong verification. Administrators are advised to turn off auto-signing and enforce strong validation to ensure only trusted systems and authorized users can receive certificates.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


The security hole was patched on November 3 alongside CVE-2026-115732, a critical issue where an attacker could use a certificate with a name that extends an approved name to bypass authorization and impersonate a managed machine.



Puppet Enterprise and Puppet Enterprise Advanced versions 2026.0.1, 2025.12.1, and 2023.8.13, and Puppet Core versions 9.1.3 and 8.22.2 contain patches for both flaws.



CVE-2026-115731 was flagged as exploited on November 4 and the US cybersecurity agency CISA added it to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog on November 17, urging federal agencies to patch it within two weeks, in line with BOD 26-04’s recommendations.



The vulnerability also affects the OpenVox configuration management platform. A proof-of-concept (PoC) exploit targeting OpenVox was published shortly after CVE-2026-115731 was patched.



Related: Oracle Patches 950 Vulnerabilities With November 2026 CSPU
Related: Java4Shell Vulnerability Puts Many Enterprise Applications at Risk of Takeover
Related: AI Framework Autonomously Weaponized Flaws in Real Attacks, Google Says
Related: Google Project Zero Sets Course for 30-Day Disclosure Deadline

CVE-2026-115731 was flagged as exploited on November 4 and the US cybersecurity agency CISA added it to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog on November 17, urging federal agencies to patch it within two weeks, in line with BOD 26-04’s recommendations.



The vulnerability also affects the OpenVox configuration management platform. A proof-of-concept (PoC) exploit targeting OpenVox was published shortly after CVE-2026-115731 was patched.

[61] Help Net Security - US enlists private firms to cut scammers off from victims’ cash
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 08:21:15 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/19/us-threat-disruption-center-scam-sextortion-funds/

[TAM METİN - 481 kelime]
US enlists private firms to cut scammers off from victims’ cash

Stopping victims’ money before it reaches foreign scam, cybercrime and sextortion networks is the goal of a new US unit launched by the Homeland Security Task Force (HSTF).

The Threat Disruption Center is part of the HSTF’s National Coordination Center (NCC) in Fairfax, Virginia. The task force calls it “a shared technology resource and collaboration hub.”

Investigators will get tools to sort incoming victim complaints and map the criminal groups behind them, along with dedicated technical support and a direct line to private-sector organizations that can help reach victims and disrupt criminal operations.

“Every hour between a victim’s report and a response gives criminals more time to launder stolen funds,” said Heith Janke, Operations Director of the Criminal Branch at the FBI.

The private sector’s part

The setup depends on a standing arrangement for passing intelligence quickly between law enforcement and participating companies, which can then step in on their own.

“Participating private-sector companies can take these actions independently under their terms of service,” the HSTF says. Those actions include pausing victim payments, taking down malicious web domains and banning scammers’ social media accounts.

The legal side stays with the government. Agencies will “proceed with investigations, prosecutions, and court-authorized seizures.”

TRM Labs is the center’s primary private-sector partner, and the company built and maintains the investigations platform the unit runs on.

The task force says the criminal groups depend on social media, web hosting and domain services, banking, payments and cryptocurrency, and that they use AI to scale their operations.

Losses in the billions

Victims reported $20.9 billion in losses to the FBI’s Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3) in 2025, and the HSTF notes that most victims never file a report.

The new unit builds on Operation Level Up, an FBI program launched in early 2024 that has agents contact cryptocurrency investment fraud victims directly to stop further losses.

“Through Operation Level Up, HSTF partners have already warned thousands of victims before they suffered additional losses,” Janke noted, adding that the center brings tools for faster action at greater scale.

“Transnational criminal organizations are relentlessly seeking new ways to exploit Americans,” said John A. Condon, Acting Executive Associate Director of Homeland Security Investigations (HSI). “Through the HSTF, we are fundamentally changing how we confront these threats.”

The task force behind it

The HSTF was set up by executive order in January 2025 as a permanent interagency law enforcement network tasked with eliminating transnational criminal organizations, cartels and foreign terrorist organizations operating in the US.

It runs 30 regional task forces and 29 satellite offices covering all 50 states, the District of Columbia and US territories. More than 30 federal agencies and over 440 state and local partners take part.

The Threat Disruption Center implements Executive Order 14390, “Combating Cybercrime, Fraud, and Predatory Schemes Against American Citizens,” signed in March 2026.
