[62] Help Net Security - Game consoles and Teslas are slipping past OT network segmentation
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 05:30:09 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/19/slipping-past-ot-network-segmentation/

[TAM METİN - 422 kelime]
Game consoles and Teslas are slipping past OT network segmentation

Claroty went looking for consumer gadgets on hospital, factory and commercial networks and found tens of thousands of them, from game consoles and Roku streaming sticks to Teslas.

Roku sticks and smart watches mostly sat on guest networks, the separate segment meant for visitors’ devices. Sonos speakers and cars mostly stayed off guest networks, and game consoles mostly sat on production networks, next to the equipment that keeps patients cared for and production lines running.

Researchers analyzed more than 45 million devices at more than 30,000 critical infrastructure sites. From a compromised streaming stick or car, an attacker can look for reachable controllers, medical devices and building systems, steal credentials, and work toward critical equipment.

Roku did better than Sonos

Most Roku sticks and smart watches ended up on guest networks, while only a small share of Sonos speakers did.

If a device has little business reason to be there, people tend to put it on the guest network, and that fits the Rokus. Sonos is a different story, and setup is probably why. People usually control Sonos speakers from a phone or tablet on the same network, so the speakers end up wherever the staff’s phones and tablets are.

Cars and hospital consoles

Cars need Wi-Fi for software updates, so employees connect them to the company network after their commute. The researchers counted 3,425 Teslas, 984 Fords, 390 Hondas and 79 Hyundais. It also listed 775 devices from Texas Instruments, which makes chips. Claroty says almost none of the vehicles were isolated on guest networks. It reports six critical CVEs on Tesla Model 3s, including remote code execution flaws, and 121 security issues tied to Ford F-150 trucks, but it doesn’t list the CVE numbers.

Hospitals had the biggest count: 14,624 game consoles, concentrated in children’s hospitals, where patients ask to play. Claroty calls that a legitimate use. Even so, 13,939 of those consoles were on production networks, and 691 were rated high risk. These consoles most likely came through the hospitals’ own purchasing. Roku sticks, which have no business purpose on these networks, were better segmented than hospital consoles that do have one.

Industrial sites had far fewer consoles, 963, and 89.5% were on production networks. Thirty were rated high risk.

“Finding consumer technology like Apple Watches and Nintendo Switches residing on the same networks as OT and medical equipment points directly to ongoing deficiencies in network governance and segmentation within operational settings,” said Sean Tufts, Field CTO at Claroty.

[63] Help Net Security - AI agents inherit employee access, and nobody’s watching how they use it
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 05:00:37 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/19/ai-agents-employee-credentials-nudge-security-report/

[TAM METİN - 865 kelime]
AI agents inherit employee access, and nobody’s watching how they use it

AI agents that employees build inside project trackers, CRMs and automation tools often run on the credentials of the person who created them and carry that person’s access to company data, according to Nudge Security.

Source: Nudge Security

The AI Adoption in Practice 2026 report draws on anonymized, aggregated telemetry from Nudge Security customer environments. It tracks which AI tools show up in organizations, what those tools connect to, which agents are running and how employees use AI.

Agents that borrow their creator’s access

Among organizations with at least one AI agent, the median organization had seven, and about 44% had more than 10. The agents ran on 15 different platforms, and more than half of the organizations with agents had them on two or more. That means security teams have several admin consoles and permission models to check.

The agents commonly turned up in Atlassian’s Rovo Agents, Zapier Agents, Claude Managed Agents, Cursor Automations and ChatGPT Agents. Some platforms give an agent a dedicated bot or integration account. Often, though, the agent works off the OAuth grant or API key of the employee who set it up, or off a connection a team shares.

“In our view, that makes many agents an extension of the people who build them, carrying their access to company data,” the report states.

An agent a sales manager builds in a CRM can reach whatever that manager can reach. These agents run inside the platforms themselves and off company devices, so endpoint tools miss them, and when they don’t get an identity of their own, the identity provider has nothing new to manage.

That also muddies the audit trail. “Your logs show the employee, not the agent, so it’s hard to tell which actions a person took and which their agent took,” the company noted, adding that an agent’s access may stay the same when its creator changes roles.

“The platforms your teams use every day are becoming agent platforms, whether or not anyone decided they should be,” said Jaime Blasco, CTO of Nudge Security. “The project tracker, the CRM, the chat tool: each one now lets an employee build something that acts on their behalf, with their access, continuously. The question for security teams is no longer whether that’s allowed. It’s whether they know it’s happening.”

OAuth connections that bypass inspection

The company saw at least one Anthropic integration in 74% of organizations and an OpenAI integration in 67%. Google Workspace was the most common endpoint for both vendors. Anthropic’s integrations reached further into business systems, even though its adoption was slightly lower than OpenAI’s.

Most of these links are OAuth grants, created when an employee clicks “allow” so an AI tool can read their email, files, code or messages. Data then travels straight between cloud services and never crosses the corporate network, out of sight of AI gateways and data loss prevention tools.

Those grants stay in place until someone revokes them, often long after the employee stopped using the tool. The report recalls the 2025 Salesloft Drift breach, in which attackers stole OAuth tokens that customers had granted to Drift’s AI chat tool and used them to pull data from hundreds of companies’ Salesforce environments.

Two model providers in almost every company

OpenAI was present in 96.9% of the organizations Nudge Security observed and Anthropic in 94.5%, so at least 91% of organizations run both. “The core models have become infrastructure,” the report says, likening them to cloud providers that organizations mix and pick by use case.

Beyond the two big vendors, twelve different AI tools appeared in more than half of organizations. In total, the company identified 2,927 distinct AI vendors in the environments it monitors.

Company data goes into prompts every day

Pasted content or uploaded files showed up in 18% of all observed prompt activity. OpenAI accounted for 55% of observed prompts, and the rest went to other vendors with their own retention and training terms.

API keys and access tokens pose the most direct risk. “Once a working key leaves your environment, anyone who can see it can use it,” Nudge Security warned, advising organizations to rotate any key or token that ends up in a prompt.

Most of this sharing comes from routine work such as debugging code or summarizing a document, and the report doesn’t blame employees for it. “The data entering AI tools usually isn’t the result of someone breaking the rules. It’s a side effect of AI being useful,” it explains.

The company advises security teams to inventory agents inside the business platforms where they are built and find out whose access each one uses. It also recommends reviewing the OAuth grants employees have given AI tools and revoking the ones nobody needs anymore.

Blasco expects the problem to grow. “We expect this to get harder, not easier. Every platform your teams use is adding agent-building features, and each one makes it simpler to hand an agent a person’s access,” he said. “That means the gap between what an organization’s identity provider knows about and what’s actually acting on its data is going to keep widening.”

[64] Help Net Security - Quantum position verification could lock remote hackers out, NIST shows
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 00:07:39 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/19/nist-quantum-position-verification/

[TAM METİN - 561 kelime]
Quantum position verification could lock remote hackers out, NIST shows

Physicists at the National Institute of Standards and Technology have used entangled photons to confirm that a party was physically where it claimed to be. NIST physicist Krister Shalm and an international team ran a quantum position verification protocol inside a research building in Boulder, Colorado, and closed loopholes that had left earlier quantum location schemes open to cheating.

NIST researchers Krister Shalm (left) and Marty Stevens use optical components to guide entangled photons into an optical fiber. Credit: Riley Wilson/NIST

A stolen password works from any laptop on the planet. A location check would tie access to a room. If the method scales, a system could refuse a command, such as a $50 million wire transfer, unless it came from a terminal inside the right building. Scott Glancy, a NIST physicist on the study, put real-world use at more than a decade out, because researchers are still building the tools to share quantum information across large networks.

How the test catches an impostor

The protocol uses two trusted stations, called verifiers, and a third party, the prover, whose location is in question. The team placed the verifiers 195 meters apart, linked by electrical cables and optical fiber run through the building’s corridors, with the prover’s claimed position between them.

One verifier makes a pair of entangled photons, particles linked so that measuring one reveals the matching property of the other. It keeps one and sends the other to the prover. Both verifiers then send random numbers timed to reach the claimed spot at the same moment, and the prover uses them to decide how to measure the photon’s polarization, the direction its electric field oscillates.

Two rules of physics catch the cheater. The first is the speed of light: nothing beats it, so a prover who’s actually somewhere else can’t get an answer back in time. The second is the no-cloning theorem, which says you can’t copy quantum information without disturbing it. If a cheater copies the photon’s information to share with accomplices, the correlations the verifiers check for disappear, and the copying gives the cheat away.

The team repeated the exchange millions of times over a few minutes, then ran a loophole-free Bell test, a statistical check that the photons are more closely correlated than classical physics allows. Because the instructions are random, a cheater cannot guess them and send an early answer. Any device error or tampering makes the test fail, and a pass confirms the quantum equipment worked as intended, so the verifiers do not have to take the prover’s hardware on faith.

“Our results show how digital security can instead be anchored to the physical world,” said Shalm. “Gaining access to a system can only be done by someone who can prove they are in a particular location — such as a bank — thwarting remote would-be hackers.”

The 195-meter span sits inside one building. Glancy said a working system will need high-fidelity quantum networks that share entangled photons between the two ends of a transaction. The experiment also leaned on NIST’s superconducting nanowire single-photon detectors, which captured nearly every photon. Miss too many, and even an honest prover cannot pass the Bell test.

Scientists have pursued quantum position verification for decades. Shalm called the experiment “the first complete demonstration of such a protocol.”

identity verification

[65] Help Net Security - Ransomware access brokers are moving their deals off public forums
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 23:28:20 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/19/ransomware-access-brokers-cybercrime/

[TAM METİN - 638 kelime]
Ransomware access brokers are moving their deals off public forums

Ransomware and extortion gangs claimed about 718 victims a month through October 2026, according to threat intelligence firm ZeroFox, up from roughly 573 a month in 2025. Between early 2025 and early 2026, public dark web listings selling access to hacked corporate networks fell from about 620 a quarter to about 370.

Those listings worked as an early warning. A broker advertising a foothold in a company’s network could signal that a ransomware crew was next. The researchers say the drop very likely does not reflect less crime. The brokers took those sales off public forums and into private channels, where they’re much harder to watch.

The counts keep climbing

The researchers logged at least 2,381 incidents in the third quarter of 2026, the highest quarter in their records and about 14 percent above the previous high of 2,091, set in late 2025. October alone added 852. By the end of October, the 2026 total had already passed all of 2025, which was itself a record year.

These are victims the gangs claimed, usually on leak sites built to pressure companies into paying. The researchers expect unverified or exaggerated leak claims to become routine in 2027. A company name on a leak site is a lead that still needs confirming.

Qilin was the most active group, with at least 1,104 of the 7,239 incidents logged through November 2, or about 15 percent. The Gentlemen followed with 935. The researchers expect growth in 2027 to come from more groups entering the business. North America stayed the most targeted region, but its share slipped from about 59 percent in the second half of 2025 to 46 to 48 percent each month since July 2026, while Asia-Pacific and South America hit their highest shares since at least early 2024.

Where the brokers went

Initial access brokers are the middlemen of the ransomware economy. They break in, then sell the foothold to whoever will run the attack. Over the past year and a half they lost their main storefronts: authorities seized the XSS forum in July 2025 and RAMP in January 2026, and LeakBase went down in March 2026. Brokers moved deals into private channels, with the encrypted messenger Tox the most common choice, and some ransomware groups appear to be handling access themselves.

Prices split along the way. Basic access that averaged about $1,427 in early 2023 sold for under $500 by the third quarter of 2026. Domain administrator credentials, the keys to a company’s Windows network, averaged about $8,000. The cheap end now trades like bulk goods. The expensive end moves through relationships the researchers expect to stay private.

The goods are changing too. The researchers expect more listings for session tokens, the pass a browser holds after a user has logged in and cleared multifactor authentication. A stolen token lets an attacker walk in without logging in at all. They also expect access to vendors and integration providers to sell at a premium, since one compromise can reach many customers.

That pattern has already played out. Between August 2025 and June 2026, actors using the ShinyHunters name compromised OAuth tokens at Salesloft Drift, Gainsight and Klue, then reached customers’ Salesforce data through authorized sessions that sign-in monitoring did not flag.

If your program leans on dark web monitoring to catch a breach early, expect it to see less in 2027. The useful checks are closer to home. Which vendors have standing tokens into your SaaS tools? Would you notice a session that looks off? And when someone calls the help desk asking for a password reset, how does your team know who’s really on the line? That last one is getting tested. Malicious voice phishing attempts over Microsoft Teams reached nearly 10 times their mid-2025 weekly level by mid-2026.

[66] Help Net Security - Azure Linux 4 puts the OS behind Microsoft’s cloud services on your VMs at no extra license fee
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 23:01:14 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/19/microsoft-azure-linux-4/

[TAM METİN - 333 kelime]
Azure Linux 4 puts the OS behind Microsoft’s cloud services on your VMs at no extra license fee

Microsoft made Azure Linux 4 generally available. The company is offering its in-house Linux distribution as a general-purpose server OS for customer virtual machines and scale sets. Customers pay for the Azure compute underneath and no additional OS licensing fee.

The same distribution already runs on millions of cores across Azure services, including Azure Kubernetes Service, App Service and Azure Cosmos DB. A team that runs one distro on VMs, another on Kubernetes nodes and a third in container images, each patched on a different calendar, can put all three on one.

Azure Linux is derived from Fedora and uses RPM packages. Azure Linux is not designed for desktop use or GUI applications.

Version 4 ships Linux kernel 6.18 LTS, the dnf5 package manager, systemd 258, glibc 2.42, RPM 6.0, Python 3.14 and OpenSSL 3.5. OpenSSL 3.5 adds support for post-quantum cryptography, which is encryption meant to hold up against future quantum computers. SELinux, a kernel feature that limits what each process can touch, is on by default, and drivers are signed.

Containers with no shell

Container images come in three types. All are built from the same supply chain as the VMs and hosted on Microsoft Artifact Registry. Core images let teams install any package. Runtime images come set up for Python, Node.js, Java or .NET. Distroless images strip out the shell and the package manager. That shrinks what an attacker can use after getting in, and also what an engineer can use to debug a live container.

Several security vendors support the release. “Palo Alto Networks and AquaSec provide workload and container protection capabilities; Qualys helps teams identify vulnerabilities and monitor compliance; and Upwind and Wiz bring runtime visibility and cloud context to help prioritize security risks. NVIDIA broadens options for GPU-accelerated AI and compute-intensive workloads,” said Jim Perrin, Azure Linux Program Lead for Microsoft, wrote.

Azure Linux 4 is available on GitHub.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/19/microsoft-azure-linux-4/, helpnetsecurity.com, 19.11.2026)

[67] Help Net Security - Java4Shell OpenJDK flaw: Exploitation is tricky and hinges on specific CPUs (CVE-2026-114938)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 11:12:12 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/18/java4shell-openjdk-vulnerability-cve-2026-114938/

[TAM METİN - 735 kelime]
Java4Shell OpenJDK flaw: Exploitation is tricky and hinges on specific CPUs (CVE-2026-114938)

CVE-2026-114938, a memory corruption flaw in Open Java Development Kit’s Base64 decoder, can allow unauthenticated attackers to run code on servers hosting widely used Java applications.

Nicknamed “Java4Shell” by Calif, the company whose researchers found it, CVE-2026-114938 was fixed by Oracle and the OpenJDK project on Tuesday.

There is currently no evidence of successful attacks leveraging the flaw, but the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) “strongly urges” organizations to patch immediately.

About CVE-2026-114938

“To decode Base64 faster, OpenJDK uses specialized x86-64 CPU instructions to process many bytes at once. We discovered a bug in that optimized machine-level code that lets malformed input overwrite neighboring memory,” Calif researchers explained.

“With careful exploitation, the corruption can reveal sensitive memory, as Heartbleed did, or lead to remote code execution. The corruption itself can be triggered by just 64 A characters followed by a single =. In older OpenJDK 17, the trigger input is 128 of A followed by =.”

SentinelOne noted that exploit traffic is specific to each application. “Any Base64 value an application passes to java.util.Base64.Decoder can carry the payload, including HTTP Basic credentials, SAML artifacts, CSRF tokens, and MIME encoded words.”

Calif researchers say they achieved unauthenticated remote code execution (RCE) in default configurations of Keycloak, Atlassian Jira, Atlassian Confluence, JFrog Artifactory, Jenkins, and PingFederate. In Apache Kafka Broker, Apache Solr, Elasticsearch, Gerrit, and Spinnaker, they managed RCE after authentication.

“We also demonstrated RCE in sample applications built on widely used frameworks, including Apache Struts and Spring Boot. Ordinary features such as authentication and file uploads can expose the vulnerable Base64 decoder, extending the potential impact far beyond the products we tested,” they added, and said that security researcher samczsun also shared a client-side exploit with them.

Oracle has rated the flaw 8.1 on CVSS 3.1, citing high attack complexity, while Calif gave it a 9.2 (Critical) under CVSS 4.0.

Despite Java4Shell’s current high profile, many security researchers pointed out that exploiting it for RCE is not easy and hinges on specific CPU, JDK, and Base64 prerequisites.

Caitlin Condon, VP of Security Research at VulnCheck, said that the company’s research team determined that exploitation is hardware-specific: “The vulnerable code runs only on CPUs that provide the AVX-512 VBMI (and BW) instruction features; CPUs without them fall back to a different decode path that does not contain the defect, so the vulnerable instructions don’t execute there.”

Crashing an affected JVM is trivial, VulnCheck found, and repeated requests can keep a service down. Achieving RCE, though, requires heap grooming and a separate memory leak to bypass address space layout randomization (ASLR).

ARM64 CPUs are not affected, Calif researchers noted.

According to Oracle, CVE-2026-114938 affects Oracle Java SE versions 8u511b33-perf, 17.0.21.0.3, 21.0.13.0.3, 25.0.5.0.3, 27.0.1.0.3 (and likely earlier), as well as Oracle GraalVM for JDK (versions 17.0.21.0.3 and 21.0.13.0.3) and Oracle GraalVM Enterprise Edition (v21.3.20.0.3).

Calif says that a number of Java distributions built on vulnerable OpenJDK versions are also affected: Amazon Corretto, Eclipse Temurin, Microsoft Build of OpenJDK, Azul Zulu, SAP SapMachine, BellSoft Liberica, and Red Hat OpenJDK. Fixed builds are getting released by the vendors and should be used.

VulnCheck doesn’t expect widespread exploitation, but expects narrow paths to RCE in specific applications. watchTowr likewise doesn’t expect the exploitation to be indiscriminate.

Calif has published a proof-of-concept that only checks whether a given JDK and CPU combination reproduces the memory corruption, and is holding back its product-specific exploit chains to give organizations time to upgrade.

Still, they said that if took them just two hours and some AI help to create the first working exploit, and attackers can do the same (albeit more slowly).

But there’s no need to panic, according to security researcher Kevin Beaumont.

“[CVE-2026-114938] is not world ending. Just patch as you normally do later on today – as you should have last month as there was a different unauth RCE vuln then,” he advised.

CISA advises organizations to review their software inventory for JDKs and Java runtimes, pinpoint affected installations, and apply relevant Java and vendor patches when they become available.

“If immediate patching is not feasible, implement temporary compensating controls, such as restricting and validating Base64 input at the application boundary, enforcing input-size limits, and limiting unauthenticated access,” the agency said, and urged organizations to monitor for suspicious activity (e.g., rogue runtimes, unexpected JVM crashes, and suspicious Java processes).

[68] The Record - Judge signs off on sale of Spirit Airlines’ data to Google
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 20:39:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/judge-signs-off-google-purchase-spirit-airlines-data

[TAM METİN - 397 kelime]
Judge signs off on sale of Spirit Airlines’ data to Google

A New York federal bankruptcy judge on Tuesday greenlit the sale of Spirit Airlines’ employees’ data to Google, a decision that follows the tech giant’s agreement to abide by restrictions on how it uses and deidentifies the information.

After shutting down in May, Spirit announced it would sell Google its former employees’ data for $10 million, prompting a lawsuit from the flight attendants’ union and criticism from 121 federal lawmakers.

Soon after the outcry began, Google agreed to new terms designed to better protect sensitive data.

On November 9, the union withdrew its objection to the sale, citing Google’s pledge to exclude employees’ health and medical data, alcohol and drug testing results, safety and disciplinary records and identification documents. The tech giant also agreed to what the union called a “rigorous de-identification process” and a mandatory review of the deidentified data by an independent assessor chosen in collaboration with the union.

Google also agreed not to attempt to reidentify the data.

In signing off on the sale, U.S. Bankruptcy Judge Sean Lane said the “procedures for the deidentification, transformation and preparation of the data” comply with applicable data protection laws, according to a court filing.

Google did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

The company has said it intends to use the data for AI model training. The agreement is the first of this scale to emerge in the AI era. The dataset includes an estimated 100 million emails and half-billion Microsoft Teams messages, which experts say will be useful for training agents handling professional tasks.

While the flight attendants’ union has celebrated the protections it won, Arjun Bhatnagar, the CEO of the consumer privacy startup Cloaked, said the data sale still poses a threat to employees.

“Google said it won't try to reidentify anyone, and that promise is supposed to follow the data, but every time a dataset like this gets copied, licensed or breached, someone always finds an opening to connect all the data points,” he said via email.

[69] The Record - Trump administration officials size-up a bad stretch for tech
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 20:00:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/tech-disruptions-house-homeland-committee-testimony

[TAM METİN - 720 kelime]
Trump administration officials size-up a bad stretch for tech

Senior Trump administration officials on Wednesday insisted that recent service outages underpinning much of everyday life — and U.S. national security — are not the result of a coordinated digital attack on the country.

Over the last week, a global blackout of common Microsoft products, a nationwide ground stop by the FAA, glitches in the banking system and National Security Agency, the country’s largest spy agency, have rattled the public and policymakers on both sides of the aisle who fear the high-profile disruptions are secretly the work of foreign adversaries.

“With the processes that we put in place, what we have seen over the last ten days is not an orchestrated attack by a foreign malign actor,” Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton testified before the House Homeland Security Committee.

“We do not have any indication that it would be.”

Throughout the panel’s annual worldwide threats hearing, Clayton, along with Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin and FBI Deputy Director Christopher Raia, sought to address bipartisan concerns, chalking up the interruptions to emerging AI capabilities, aging infrastructure, and increased system patching and upgrades.

Despite the bureau’s cyber teams being involved in the various investigations “from day one,” agents have found no evidence that malicious cyber activity caused the recent failures, according to Raia.

“At this point, there is no indication whatsoever that there is any cyber malicious activity related to any of those incidents.” He later added that the FBI would continue to work with DHS and ODNI “to be to be 120 percent sure.”

The administration’s past efforts to assuage the public, especially around the four-hour-long groundstop, have been undercut by Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy and Mullin himself. Each has offered varying explanations for the extraordinary action, including when the DHS chief went on television and said that, based on conversation with unnamed tech executives, AI may have been responsible.

Mullin struck a different note before Congress.

“I'm 98 percent confident that this has nothing to do with our adversaries hacking or going after our system.”

Mullin added that the administration would hold a phone call with representatives from the private sector about informing their customers when an upgrade is coming so they can adequately prepare.

“We're not pressuring the industry to do it. We just want to have a conversation and say maybe we should start communicating because what happens online is a tremendous amount of fear,” he said. “I think all of us can understand it but there's no cause for it.”

Clayton, who on Tuesday explained the outage of an internal NSA network was the result of maintenance related PHP, went the furthest of the trio in offering possible reasons for the rush of technical failures.

“We are in an environment where the rate of patching, the rate of upgrading of systems, is increasing,” he told the panel. “That is the result of advancements in compute and in applications, we should expect that as people are patching, and as people are upgrading systems.”

He noted malicious actors are using such advances to augment their capabilities, including scanning to identify vulnerabilities in systems.

“We need to use the increased compute and the increased capabilities to combat that scanning,” Clayton said. “That has been going on inside a number of our sophisticated financial institutions and others. So we are meeting this threat but it's something that we need to continue to do.”

The spy chief later urged the American people to “understand that we are in the process of upgrading our systems.”

“I'm looking around this room and I'm seeing different systems from many different decades. Some of them are actually a combination of decades,” he said. When you have combinations of old and new equipment on a particular network, that makes upgrades and things like this challenging. We're going to go through this challenge but I think we're going to come out the other side stronger than any country in the world.”

is the senior cybersecurity reporter for The Record. Prior to joining Recorded Future News in 2021, he spent more than five years at Politico, where he covered digital and national security developments across Capitol Hill, the Pentagon and the U.S. intelligence community. He previously was a reporter at The Hill, National Journal Group and Inside Washington Publishers.

[70] The Record - Lawmakers call on White House to develop defensive AI cyber systems
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 19:00:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/ai-cyber-white-house

[TAM METİN - 751 kelime]
Lawmakers call on White House to develop defensive AI cyber systems

Two leading members of the Senate urged the White House on Tuesday to launch a national effort to more robustly confront AI-enabled cyber threats in light of recent security incidents involving artificial intelligence.

In a letter to Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton, Sens. Maria Cantwell (D-Wash.) and Mike Rounds (R-S.D.) warned that AI is “rapidly increasing the ability to discover and exploit weaknesses in software” and that the country’s defenses “are not keeping pace.”

“The fundamental problem is that AI can discover software weaknesses and help attackers exploit them faster than many organizations can respond,” the senators said. “Even when fixes are available, testing and installing patches across interconnected systems can take weeks or months, potentially disrupting essential services.”

The letter cites recent incidents where AI was used to discover obscure bugs and power cyber intrusions in Japan. The senators also raised concerns from banks about AI agents, which are increasingly accessing bank accounts. Several U.S. banks have said they are unable to tell whether AI agents are rogue or are accessing data on behalf of customers.

Chinese-developed AI models are also being used widely for a variety of nefarious purposes, the lawmakers said.

Rounds is the chairman of the Senate Armed Services subcommittee on cybersecurity and has repeatedly called on the White House to do more to confront risks posed by AI.

The senators said the White House task force on AI, which Clayton chairs, needs to speed up the development and deployment of defensive AI-powered tools while also establishing security standards for autonomous AI agents.

They called on the task force to convene a meeting with AI developers, cybersecurity companies, critical infrastructure operators, the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST), the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) and other agencies.

The defense tools should be able to “continuously identify vulnerabilities, develop and test fixes and and block attacks before they spread,” they wrote, and CISA should be in charge of accelerating the adoption of the tools across banks, hospitals, utilities and government agencies.

“The United States has the technological expertise to lead this effort,” they said. “What is missing is a coordinated national strategy to bring these capabilities together, establish common security standards, and put effective defenses into widespread use.”

Clayton was also urged to start a “national cyber defense grand challenge” that can show how AI defenses can “cut the time from discovery of a critical vulnerability to effective protection.”

The letter comes days after the FBI released an urgent warning about a Chinese tool able to automate significant parts of the cyber intrusion process. The system was used in dozens of real-world attacks in Japan, South Korea and the U.S.

A newly released Government Accountability Office investigation conducted in the spring also found that the four most-used AI platforms — ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, and Grok — all provided terrorist attack plans in a real-world test to determine AI safety risks.

Clayton has not commented on the letter but has spoken publicly in recent days about the need to use AI to discover and patch vulnerabilities.

He said a recent spate of technology outages impacting major banks, tech companies and airlines are tied to efforts to patch vulnerabilities discovered at a faster pace than ever before by defenders using AI scanners.

“Lots of institutions, because these vulnerabilities have been identified, are patching. They’re taking their systems down, they’re upgrading them, they’re putting patches in place… This is being done on a much larger scale because everybody recognizes it’s time to upgrade their systems,” Clayton told Fox Business on Tuesday.

“And as they upgrade them, they’re not coming back as expected in all cases. And in some cases, systems have to hand off to other systems, and those handoffs are not coming back as expected. But this is just an upgrade in patching, an upgrade in systems.”

is a Breaking News Reporter at Recorded Future News. Jonathan has worked across the globe as a journalist since 2014. Before moving back to New York City, he worked for news outlets in South Africa, Jordan and Cambodia. He previously covered cybersecurity at ZDNet and TechRepublic.

[71] The Record - NSA continues response to interruptions in classified internal network
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

NSA continues response to interruptions in classified internal network

Disruptions at the National Security Agency entered their second day on Wednesday as the spy organization worked to get its internal communications network back up and fully running.

The system, known as NSA Net, experienced intermittent outages on Tuesday, leading the agency to tell its work force to stagger arrivals at its headquarters at Fort Meade, Maryland. It eventually closed its campus to most employees by the evening, according to sources.

Non-essential personnel were sent a "Code Gray" notification late Tuesday night, informing them to arrive at 10 a.m. Wednesday. However, that time has already been moved to the afternoon and could change again, said a source familiar with the ongoing situation.

Meanwhile, the agency’s cafeteria, and a Starbucks located on its campus, remain closed.

The same person said the network is working but NSA leaders are still staggering personnel because the login process is the most taxing process on the system.

They also said comments made Tuesday by Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton — that the outage was due to maintenance affecting PHP, a common programming language — were correct.

It remains unknown if the vulnerability was AI-derived or human-driven.

The source speculated that it might take another day or two for full restoration of the classified network, but insisted that the spy agency’s classified national security work remains unimpeded.

The NSA did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

The outage is the latest in a series of high-profile technical disruptions in the last week, including a global outage of Microsoft products, a nationwide flight stoppage and problems with bank services.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer (D-NY) on Wednesday called on President Donald Trump and Clayton to “form a dedicated red team of trained intelligence professionals to challenge the assumption that these outages are just business as usual.”

Schumer, who is lobbying his colleagues to become Majority Leader following the outcome of the midterm elections, said it is “too coincidental that all these outages happened within a few days for us to think it's business as usual. We have to get to the bottom of this.”

He urged the administration to form the team and report its findings to Congress by December 1.

Testifying on Wednesday before the House Homeland Security Committee, Clayton said he understands that the “timing of these incidents can raise questions and it should.”

“The American people should ask us these questions, but, based on what we know today, after a great deal of diligence, these are separate service outages associated with maintenance and technical changes,” he added. “They are not a coordinated or state-sponsored attack.”

[72] The Record - Russian cyber-espionage group used stolen cloud data to hack into organizations, Google says
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 15:15:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/russia-cyber-espionage-google

[TAM METİN - 492 kelime]
Russian cyber-espionage group used stolen cloud data to hack into organizations, Google says

Russian state-backed hackers are using information stolen from cloud accounts to impersonate trusted contacts and launch new espionage attacks against people in their victims' professional networks, according to new research.

The hacking group, known as Cold Relic or ColdRiver, has previously targeted government officials, defense contractors and nonprofit organizations across Europe and North America. U.S. authorities have linked the group to Russia's Federal Security Service (FSB).

In a report published Wednesday, Google's cybersecurity unit Mandiant described how the hackers used data stolen from one U.S. organization in late 2025 to help compromise a second entity.

During the first attack, the hackers took control of an administrator account and used it to download the organization's entire cloud drive and employee directories. They also searched for emails, passwords and personnel records, apparently looking for information they could use in future operations.

The hackers remained inside the organization's systems for several months. They continued reading emails through third-party applications and found ways to regain access after the organization changed passwords and reset user sessions.

Even after losing access to the network, the hackers continued targeting the organization and its contacts by sending emails that appeared to come from employees.

In a separate attack this year, Cold Relic used information stolen in the earlier breach to pose as an employee and approach someone at another U.S. organization through an encrypted messaging app.

The hackers first struck up a conversation with the target before sending malicious links designed to steal Microsoft account login details and authentication tokens. To make the messages appear more convincing, the links led to documents related to their conversation, making the phishing attempt harder to spot.

The hackers ultimately gained access to an account at the second organization and downloaded large amounts of data from the user's cloud storage, Google said.

The researchers did not identify either U.S. organization or disclose what information was stolen.

Google said Cold Relic frequently uses compromised websites to redirect targets to phishing pages, concealing the actual destination and allowing the attackers to assess visitors before displaying malicious content.

The approach reflects Cold Relic's broader strategy of exploiting relationships between victims. Rather than relying solely on phishing emails, the hackers use stolen correspondence, employee information and familiar identities to make subsequent attacks more convincing, according to the report.

Google has tracked the group since 2015 and observed evidence of new phishing operations as recently as this month. Although Cold Relic has attracted less public attention than some other Russian state-backed hacking groups, Google said it has maintained a steady pace of operations.

She previously was a tech reporter for Forbes Ukraine. Her work has also been published at Sifted, The Kyiv Independent and The Kyiv Post.

[73] Talos Intelligence - The challenge of defending against bulletproof dead drops on public blockchains
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 11:00:00 GMT
Link: https://blog.talosintelligence.com/the-challenge-of-defending-against-bulletproof-dead-drops-on-public-blockchains/

[TAM METİN - 2661 kelime]
The challenge of defending against bulletproof dead drops on public blockchains

The evolution of malware command-and-control (C2) infrastructure has shifted away from fragile, centralized servers and hardcoded domains toward resilient, decentralized blockchain networks that eliminate single points of failure.

Threat actors are leveraging immutable public ledgers such as Bitcoin transactions, EVM smart contracts via techniques like EtherHiding, TRON wallets, and Solana memo programs to store, mask, and persistently update their operational endpoints.

By executing read-only remote procedure call (RPC) queries to retrieve encrypted C2 configurations directly into compromised endpoint memory, adversaries can continuously bypass traditional perimeter defenses and domain seizures.

Modern cybersecurity requires a fundamental shift away from static domain blocklists toward monitoring anomalous outbound JSON-RPC traffic, analyzing endpoint memory, and treating blockchain infrastructure as an active threat communication channel.

The command-and-control (C2) channel is the operational backbone of any persistent malware campaign. It is also the most fragile component. The moment an actor's C2 infrastructure is identified and reported to the hosting provider, the domain registrar, or law enforcement, the campaign's operational capability collapses. This fundamental asymmetry, where defenders need to identify only a single infrastructure link to disrupt an operation, has driven a multi-decade arms race in resilience techniques, spanning from fast flux DNS evasion to decentralized and peer-to-peer architectures.

Traditional C2 infrastructure where malware beacons to a hardcoded domain or IP address is volatile by design. Security researchers or incident responders who identify the C2 address can trigger a response. The hosting provider can null-route the IP addresses and the domain registrars can suspend malicious domains under abuse policies, law enforcement can obtain court orders compelling infrastructure seizure, and threat intelligence vendors push blocklist updates that cut off the malware from its C2 links across several compromised endpoints simultaneously.

This single point of failure model renders sophisticated malware families inactive once their C2 is identified and sinkholed. By redirecting malware traffic to researcher-controlled servers, researchers can not only disrupt the botnet but map the full victim community, gather forensic telemetry, and notify affected organizations.

Threat actors have progressively hardened their C2 infrastructure through several evasion techniques to outpace defensive capabilities. Amongst these techniques, some of the popular approaches include the employment of domain generation algorithms (DGA), content delivery network (CDN) domain fronting, peer-to-peer (P2P) architecture, and dead drop resolvers.

DGAs replace static, hardcoded server domains with dynamically generated domains. Malware programmed with a shared mathematical seed computes hundreds or thousands of pseudorandom domain names on a scheduled basis, systematically attempting to resolve each one until it establishes contact with an address registered by the threat actor. While this technique prevents defenders from neutralizing an entire operation by simply seizing or blocklisting a single domain, defenders counteracted DGAs by reverse engineering the generation algorithms, predicting future domains for proactive sinkholing, and applying machine learning models to detect algorithmically generated strings within enterprise DNS queries.

The CDN domain fronting technique evolved to bypass perimeter inspection mechanisms by exploiting discrepancies between the transport and application layers of encrypted web traffic. By initiating a TLS handshake with a legitimate, reputable domain hosted on a major CDN and specifying a covert, malicious host inside the encrypted HTTP Host header, threat actors route communications through benign enterprise traffic profiles. Enterprise security tools inspecting the outer connection perceive only standard communication with a trusted global platform, effectively masking the true destination behind high reputation infrastructure. However, major cloud providers and network carriers have largely dismantled this evasion vector by standardizing routing policies and enforcing strict matching between TLS Server Name indication values and internal request headers.

P2P architectures eliminate the single point of failure in a centralized server altogether by structuring infected hosts into a decentralized, self-healing mesh network. Instead of querying a primary server for instructions, compromised nodes propagate operational commands, binary updates, and collected data directly between adjacent peers using custom overlay routing protocols. This distributed topology ensures that severing dozens or even hundreds of individual endpoints leave the broader network intact and functional. Nevertheless, peer-to-peer networks suffer from inherent architectural exposure, because defenders and security researchers can enumerate the entire topology by acting as benign nodes, map peer lists, and progressively isolate the infected nodes.

Dead drop resolvers establish an indirect channel by abusing established third-party web services as covert repositories for downstream infrastructure coordinates. Rather than communicating with an adversary's operational network directly, deployed malware retrieves encoded or encrypted IP addresses and domain names embedded inside public social media bios, code repository commits, or image metadata. By masking this initial discovery phase behind legitimate user activity on high-traffic consumer platforms, attackers blind simple reputation-based network defenses. Defenders eventually contain dead drop tactics by working directly with service providers to enforce aggressive account suspension, remove flagged content, and monitor automated outbound API requests from internal enterprise endpoints.

Each of the above techniques still relies on some degree of centralized infrastructure that can, under the right conditions, be neutralized.

The evolution of the blockchain as bulletproof hosting technique

C2 configuration hosting on the blockchain eliminates the operational dependencies through embedding encrypted server addresses and URLs directly into confirmed transactions that cannot be deleted or altered by any party, preventing any single authority from taking down the records.

A public blockchain is a distributed append-only ledger. Once a transaction is confirmed by the validators, it becomes a permanent, globally replicated record. The inherent architectural properties that make public blockchains attractive for financial systems translate directly into ideal characteristics for a resilient C2 configuration hosting channel. When configuration parameters such as active endpoint IP addresses and domain names or operational instructions are embedded into blockchain transactions, no single entity, cloud provider, or law enforcement agency possesses the administrative authority to delete, overwrite, or withhold that data. The ledgers are distributed globally, guaranteeing high availability and persistent access regardless of regional takedowns.

Furthermore, leveraging public blockchains enables operational advantages regarding attribution and survivability. Traditional C2 mechanisms often expose the operator through payment trails, registrar records, or network traffic directed at centralized hosts. By contrast, transactions on distributed ledgers can be broadcast pseudo-anonymously through cheaper wallets and retrieving the embedded configuration data which mimics the benign blockchain queries such as public RPC API lookups or block explorer scraping. Combined with nominal transaction fees, the blockchain functions as an indestructible, globally accessible record where deployed malicious agents can reliably synchronize updates without ever exposing the core infrastructure behind them.

Bitcoin blockchain adoption

The adoption of the Bitcoin blockchain as a C2 configuration hosting channel began around 2016 – 2019, conceived by threat actors seeking to overcome the operational fragility of centralized hosting and vulnerable DNS registrars. Because Bitcoin transactions are permanently recorded and broadcast across nodes globally, configuration details written to the ledger become immutable, preventing law enforcement and infrastructure providers from seizing, altering, or sinkholing the underlying records.

The most prominent implementation of this tradecraft emerged in September 2019 when security researchers at Trend Micro uncovered a new variant of the Glupteba botnet that used Bitcoin transactions to update its active C2 servers. Glupteba embedded a function that queried public Electrum servers for transactions linked to hardcoded script hashes controlled by the threat actors. By parsing the script, the malware extracted AES-encrypted payloads containing operational domains and fallback addresses, allowing the botnet to automatically regain connectivity whenever its active infrastructure faced defensive takedowns.

Prior to Glupteba’s abuse, threat actors explored Bitcoin’s public visibility through auxiliary resolver mechanisms, notably observed in certain iterations of the Cerber ransomware and early proof-of-concept crimeware throughout 2016 and 2017. Rather than embedding direct payloads in transaction scripts, these actors monitored output transaction values, addresses, and block explorer APIs to derive seeds for pseudorandom domain generation. While these early experiments exposed the defensive blind spots of relying on public ledger lookups, the high transaction fees, block confirmation latencies, and 80-byte payload constraints of Bitcoin eventually prompted modern campaigns to migrate toward high throughput smart contract ecosystems and memo programs.

Weaponization of smart contracts on public Ethereum Virtual Machine blockchains

An Ethereum smart contract is an autonomous, self-executing software application deployed directly to an address on the Ethereum network. Compiled into bytecode that runs on the decentralized Ethereum Virtual Machine (EVM), it is distributed across several independent nodes rather than hosted on a central server. Smart contracts operate transparently, resist blocking restrictions, and cannot be shut down or modified by any single hosting provider or authority.

Smart contract architecture is more appealing to threat actors when compared to a standard transaction of a block in the blockchain. A block serves as the structural foundation of the blockchain ledger which builds verified transactions into immutable, time-stamped entries.  A standard transaction represents an ephemeral event where a message is broadcasted to move funds or call an address, which may include an arbitrary payload inside the input field. Once mined, this transaction data is written permanently into blockchain history. A smart contract, by contrast, acts as a persistent entity capable of storing data that can be modified over time through subsequent transactions.

Threat actors misuse this unique capability through a technique known as EtherHiding: transforming smart contracts into resilient dead drop resolvers. In the attacks, the actors deploy a smart contract holding encoded C2 domains or IP address within a contract variable. The malicious programs deployed on the compromised machines query the contract via public legitimate blockchain RPC endpoints using JSON-RPC calls such as eth_call over HTTPS and decrypt the encrypted payload received in response in the compromised machine memory and connect to the C2 infrastructure. Because eth_call simulates a contract read operation without creating an on-chain transaction, implants and scripts could retrieve live C2 addresses, leaving no transactional footprints on the ledger.

The EtherHiding technique was surfaced in October 2023, when security researchers documented how threat actors bypassed conventional domain takedowns by using Binance Smart Chain (now BNB Smart Chain) contracts as anonymous, indestructible hosting platforms.

The primary cluster initially responsible for pioneering EtherHiding is the financially motivated threat actor group tracked as UNC5142 and reported by researchers in October 2025, widely associated with the ClearFake distribution campaign. ClearFake initially leveraged Cloudflare workers to deliver drive-by download lures and fake browser updates but shifted to blockchain storage when cloud vendors disrupted their operational infrastructure.

Following the tactical success demonstrated by cybercriminals, state-sponsored advanced persistent threat (APT) groups also integrated EtherHiding into targeted operations. Security researchers revealed in October 2025 that the North Korean state-sponsored threat cluster UNC5342 (linked to social engineering campaigns known as Contagious Interview) adopted EtherHiding to target developers in the cryptocurrency ecosystem. In the report, researchers disclosed that the actor UNC5342 deployed custom smart contracts on BNB Smart Chain to store second-stage payloads and active C2 endpoints for their JADESNOW and INVISIBLEFERRET malware families. They updated the configuration data more than 20 times within a few months, demonstrating how both state-sponsored adversaries and cybercriminal enterprises exploit EVM-compatible ledgers.

Threat actors have increasingly embraced the TRON blockchain to construct multi-chain fallback systems that resist traditional domain seizures and infrastructure takedowns, initially reported in September 2026. APT groups, most notably UNC5342 and Iranian state-sponsored actors, incorporate TRON as a front-line communication channel by embedding encoded routing details directly within wallet transactions. Deployed implants issue routine queries to public TRON application programming interfaces (APIs) to retrieve these transaction records, extract cryptographic keys or location pointers, and connect to secondary payload contracts hosted on platforms like BNB Smart Chain. By capitalizing on TRON's nominal transaction overhead, high processing throughput, and massive legitimate transaction volume, adversaries create an unseizable setup that forces defenders to attempt simultaneous disruptions across distinct, decentralized ecosystems to break command access.

Adoption of the Solana Memo transaction

On the Solana network, transactions and the Solana Program Library (SPL) memo program operate under a distinct execution model compared to EVM-based smart contracts. A Solana transaction is an atomic package containing one or more instructions executed in sequence, capable of invoking multiple independent on-chain programs within a single block confirmation. The SPL Memo Program is a native, pre-deployed utility program residing directly within the Solana runtime. Rather than managing complex state machines or maintaining custom variables, the Memo Program simply validates an arbitrary string passed to it such as UTF-8 text or Base64-encoded binary data and records it directly into the transaction’s verified execution log.

Threat actors repurpose this architecture by treating the Memo Program as a lightweight, transaction-level dead drop resolver. In this technique, the actor controls standard Solana wallets and periodically broadcasts micro-value transactions that execute a call to the Memo Program. These transactions contain encrypted C2 IP addresses or staging domains. The implanted malware on the compromised endpoints is, configured with the Solana memo program ID, query public Solana JSON-RPC endpoints using standard calls like getSignaturesForAddress and getTransaction. The malware pulls the newest confirmed transaction associated with that wallet, extracts the payload from the memo instruction logs, decrypts the destination in memory, and initiates network beacons. When an operational IP or domain is sinkholed by security teams, the attacker simply broadcasts a new transaction with an updated memo payload, and the malware automatically retrieves the latest transaction on its next polling cycle without requiring binary updates on the victim machine.

Solana’s technical profile presents distinct characteristics compared to earlier public blockchains when evaluated as a low-latency, resilient data distribution layer. Processing over 65,000 transactions per second with sub-second block confirmation times around 0.4 seconds, the network enables near-instant propagation of broadcast data globally, avoiding the prolonged mempool delays characteristic of Bitcoin’s seven transactions per second (TPS) or Ethereum’s 15 – 30 TPS baseline. Economically, execution fees averaging approximately U.S. $0.000025 per transaction allow several updates to be published.

Threat actors are leveraging Solana memo transactions for hosting the C2 configurations that were first seen employed by the Lazarus APT group reported in March 2026. In September 2026, Cisco Talos discovered malicious activity conducted by a suspicious North Korea-aligned threat actor UAT-12643, including the use of the Overlord RAT and implementation of a resilient C2 server discovery mechanism by using the Solana blockchain. The actor embeds encrypted C2 URLs into the Solana blockchain using the Solana Memo Program, a native Solana program that validates and permanently records arbitrary text strings in the blockchain ledger as part of a transaction.

Looking ahead at defensive approach to counter bulletproof blockchain dead drops

The weaponization of public blockchains ranging from legacy Bitcoin script hashes to EVM smart contracts, TRON accounts, and Solana memo fields fundamentally inverts how we counter adversary infrastructure. By turning decentralized ledgers into resilient dead drop resolvers, threat actors have removed the single points of failure that security teams traditionally rely on to dismantle operations. Standard approaches like registrar-level domain seizures, hosting takedowns, and DNS sinkholing simply don't work when the staging record lives across many immutable nodes.

Enterprise defense needs to move past static indicators and focus on how endpoints interact with Web3 infrastructure. Because, resolving these dead drops relies on standard read-only Remote Procedure Call (RPC) queries, monitoring or blocking outbound traffic to public node providers like Infura, QuickNode, Tatum, and native chain endpoints could help reduce the attack surface significantly, if they aren’t required for business operations. Security teams should baseline normal developer or business activity and flag automated, high-frequency calls using methods like eth_call, getTransaction, and getSignaturesForAddress. Network defenders should inspect JSON-RPC payloads for anomalous base-encoded data and unexpected payload lengths. Also, endpoint telemetry should prioritize detecting lightweight in-memory decrypters before they can unpack and beacon out to the resolved C2 destinations.

As defenders mature these detection capabilities, adversary tradecraft will inevitably shift. Talos researchers are anticipating that on-chain C2 pipelines will move towards multi-chain fallback architectures, chaining lookups across multiple cryptocurrency networks to evade single-ecosystem blocks. As commercial RPC gateways implement stricter abuse detections, attackers will likely route the RPC lookups through decentralized RPC aggregators, private transaction relayers, or zero-knowledge proof (ZKP) verification systems that obscure the querying host. Eventually, tracking this activity requires treating public ledgers not just as financial tracks, but as an active adversary communications layer.
