[74] Talos Intelligence - UAT-12643: Suspected North Korea-aligned actor blends blockchain resilience with targeted infostealing
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 11:00:00 GMT
Link: https://blog.talosintelligence.com/uat-12643-suspected-north-korea-aligned-actor-blends-blockchain-resilience-with-targeted-infostealing/

[TAM METİN - 3438 kelime]
UAT-12643: Suspected North Korea-aligned actor blends blockchain resilience with targeted infostealing

Cisco Talos discovered malicious infrastructure associated with a financially motivated threat group we are tracking as UAT-12643.

The actor’s arsenal has several payloads including the Go-based Overlord remote access trojan (RAT), a Python-based infostealer internally labelled “Good Game,” and a multi-cryptocurrency clipboard hijacker.

The threat actor is likely operating a campaign targeting cryptocurrency holders, gaming platform account owners, and VoIP software users based on the lure file names of the initial vectors and the payloads.

The Overlord RAT implements a command-and-control (C2) resilience technique in which encrypted server URLs are embedded in Solana blockchain transaction memos.

The threat actor is potentially targeting victims in Europe, according to the payload’s keyword-based file harvesting capability, which specifically targeted documents that had file names in English, French, Spanish, and Portuguese.

Based on the lure themes and payload file names, the potential victim pool spans across multiple verticals. A lure financial statement decoy PDF — launched when the Good Game stealer is executed — indicates targeting finance and accounting staff. The Viber trojanized installer lure targets messaging platform users, while additional payload variants of files named microsip indicate targeting VoIP softphone operators, consistent with call-center or business telephone environments. Cryptocurrency and gaming users are also targeted by both the clipboard hijacker and the infostealer.

Potential links to North Korea-aligned actors

Talos assesses with low confidence that UAT-12643 is a North Korea-aligned advanced persistent threat (APT) actor based on overlaps in tactics, techniques, and procedures (TTPs) with those of the other known North Korean APT actors.

In the current campaign, Talos observed that the actor is using the Overlord RAT as one of their payloads. The threat cluster called UNK_DeadDrop, likely a North Korean threat actor, had employed the Overlord RAT in their earlier campaigns as reported by the security researchers in June 2026.

The Overlord RAT variant observed in this campaign implements a blockchain C2 resilience technique with Solana blockchain transactions. This technique was first reported in March 2026, employed by the Lazarus APT group. Although the implementation methodology differs (earlier, it was through Node.js), the technique of leveraging Solana transaction memo stayed consistent.

Talos is aware that Overlord RAT and Solana blockchain hosting technique is also leveraged by other threat actors in their campaigns, as reported by external security researchers in October 2026.

Threat actor infrastructure

Talos observed that the actor hosted the malicious files on a server with IP address “41[.]216[.]188[.]11”, running a Flask-based file delivery service on port 8000 with resumable byte-range download support, and serving the payload staging directory. Due to the actor’s operational security failure, the server’s root folder was exposed to the internet, and Talos’ analysis of the exposed folder showed us actor’s tool sets, and the port numbers configured on the C2 server for different services.

Flask file-drop (HTTP)

Payload.exe, MyApp2.exe, HTA files

Overlord RAT WebSocket C2

Python stealer TCP C2

Python stealer TCP C2

Microsip-config.exe, microsip-config1.exe

The actor does not use the traditional data exfiltration approach of transferring the data to the C2 server; instead, the stolen data is exfiltrated using two different services. The actor used the discord webhook, which receives an immediate notification that contains a victim’s fingerprint and a single click download link. The stolen data archive is uploaded to “gofile[.]io” and is later accessed by the actor through the download link.

We also observed the actor’s LNK generation script among the samples of the staging directory, indicating the actor’s lure creation workflow. The script accepts a payload URL and icon type (PDF, Word, or Windows Update) and generates a COM-based LNK with the spoofed and hidden execution.

Initial access vectors

Talos observed that the actor uses three different methods to gain initial access to victims’ environments. They have instrumented Windows shortcuts, HTA files, and XML files, so the embedded malicious instructions execute and grant initial access. In this campaign, we have not observed how the actor delivers these malicious initial vector files to victims’ machines.

One of the initial access vectors is the usage of the weaponized LNK files paired with the icons spoofed from legitimate system binaries to appear as a legitimate file in the Windows Explorer.

The shortcut file impersonates a PDF using the standard Windows document icon and abuses MpCmdRun.exe, Windows Defender's command line utility and the argument -DownloadFile to get the payload.  Since MpCmdRun.exe is a signed Microsoft binary, the download passes application allowlisting controls transparently. The downloaded payload will be saved as RuntimeBroker.exe to blend into process inventories, and the console window is minimized with no visible trace.

Another shortcut file applies the same PDF disguise through a double-extension file name and the Microsoft Edge PDF icon and uses certutil -urlcache –f, a one-liner download command that operates under the guise of a certificate cache update. Both shortcuts were built on the same machine (win-8oa3ccqae4d).

Talos observed that the actor has weaponized the JavaScript HTML Application (HTA) file to gain initial access. The weaponized HTA is executed by mshta.exe and the JavaScript in the HTA uses “MSXML2.ServerXMLHTTP” to fetch the payload as raw binary and “ADODB.Stream” to write it to %TEMP%\s.exe, then executes it stealthily by hiding the execution window.

MSBuild living-off-the-land binary (LOLBin)

The actor has also weaponized a standard MSBuild XML project file “dl.xml” by including an inline C# CodeTaskFactory task. When the XML file is executed via msbuild.exe, it compiles and executes the C# code in memory, and no intermediate executable is written to disk for the downloader stage. The download uses a spoofed Microsoft BITS/7.8 User-Agent string, impersonating the Windows Background Intelligent Transfer Service (BITS) used by Windows Update, Microsoft Defender and many third-party updaters, so the request blends in with routine background download traffic.

Analysis of Overlord RAT

The Overlord RAT variant utilized by UAT-12643 in this campaign is a modular Windows RAT compiled from Go source code.

The defense evasion and anti-analysis techniques implemented include the detection of in-memory execution to suppress forensic artifacts when running in sandboxes and a “kill flag” mechanism that triggers a kernel-level Blue Screen of Death (BSoD), making the process effectively unremovable without triggering a full system crash.

The RAT first verifies that the current process is running with administrative privilege. It then acquires SeDebugPrivilege for the current process. With that privilege held, it calls a native NT kernel API (NtSetInformationProcess) directly, bypassing the documented Win32 API layer entirely, to set the ProcessBreakOnTermination information class on the current process handle to value 1, which indicates the kernel will handle the process as critical to system operation. With these settings, the operating system treats the process the same way as the core Windows system processes. When a user or a security tool attempt to forcibly terminate the process, the kernel does not allow it to exit cleanly. Instead, it immediately halts the entire operating system and triggers a BSoD with the stop code CRITICAL_PROCESS_DIED.

For persistence, the RAT employs two approaches. The primary approach is through a registry autorun entry under the current user's hive, with a randomized value name pointing to the RAT binary located in the Network folder of the local user profile application data folder. The secondary approach is a pre-boot persistence via the Windows Recovery Environment OEM script path. It first removes any previous version of itself and then sets up the OEM environment directly under %TARGETOSDRIVE%\Recovery\OEM\. This folder is the location that the Windows Recovery Environment accesses when performing a "Reset this PC" operation. By placing files here, anything registered in this directory gets executed inside WinRE before the operating system is restored.

Once the directory is set, the RAT generates a random 20-character file name for its payload file. Each character is picked individually at random from a fixed character set, meaning every installation produces a uniquely named file. With a random name chosen, it writes the RAT's own executable binary into the OEM directory as that payload file.  It then constructs the shell command that WinRE will use to launch the payload and wraps it into Windows batch script with the .bat extension.

The use of %TARGETOSDRIVE% is significant, as this environment variable only exists and resolves correctly inside the WinRE pre-boot environment, not in normal Windows. This means the command is specifically crafted to work at recovery time, not during a regular session.

Before modifying any system configuration, it backs up the existing WinRE reset configuration file. Finally, it modifies the ResetConfig.xml file that governs what WinRE does during a reset. If no config file exists yet, it creates one from scratch; if one already exists, it patches it to append the malicious batch scripts as registered OEM tasks. From this point forward, whenever the victim triggers "Reset this PC" whether from within Windows Settings or from the recovery boot menu, Windows Recovery Environment will execute the RAT's payload before restoring the operating system, ensuring the implant survives a full system reset.

Analysis of the RAT’s binary showed a complete command surface with 35 commands, each corresponding to a distinct capability delivered over the C2 channel. These commands belong to several remote administration capability categories as described in the table below:

Captures keystroke using GetAsyncKeyState, Webcam streaming, microphone and desktop audio capture and desktop screen capture.

Directory listing and creation, file read, write, delete, copy, move, perform recursive search, upload, download, and compression tasks.

Process enumeration and termination.

Execute arbitrary command and script with real-time console output streaming back to C2.

Establishes Reverse SOCKS proxy tunnel that routes operator TCP traffic through the victim network, enabling access to internal hosts unreachable from the public internet.

HTTP-based plugin downloads with reflective DLL injection at runtime where new capabilities are loaded entirely in memory with no binary update or written to the disk.

Overlord’s Solana blockchain C2 URL hosting

The RAT implements a resilient C2 server discovery mechanism by using the Solana blockchain. The actor embeds encrypted C2 URLs into the Solana blockchain using the Solana Memo Program, a native Solana program that validates and permanently records arbitrary text strings in the blockchain ledger as part of a transaction. Any Solana wallet can include a memo instruction in a transaction of up to 566 bytes of arbitrary data to the permanent, globally replicated transaction record.

A threat actor can post one such transaction from a controlled wallet address whenever the C2 server address needs to be updated. Every deployed RAT picks up the update on its next discovery cycle without any redeployment or reconfiguration.

This is achieved by first retrieving the transaction history for the actor-controlled wallet using the Solana getSignatures JSON-RPC method which receives a chronologically ordered list of transaction signatures with the newest transaction first. For each signature it fetches the full transaction via getTransaction with jsonParsed encoding, which instructs the RPC node to decode every instruction data field into structured JSON response. The RAT module then walks every instruction in the parsed transaction looking for one whose data string is exactly 43 characters in length. The 43-character length is the fixed string length produced when a Base58-encoded WebSocket URL is AES-256 encrypted. Any instruction whose data field is a different length will be skipped, including legitimate Memo transactions from other users and all other Solana program instructions. Only instructions matching this precise length signature are treated as C2 memos.

The Good Game stealer payload Talos observed in the arsenal of UAT-12643 is a publicly available Python-based infostealer developed under the label "Good Game (version 1.1.2)” by CirqueiraDev. The actor of this campaign is not the developer, and the malware is distributed as a self-contained Windows executable produced by packaging a Python 3.12.0 code with PyInstaller. Its architecture is split between two cooperating bytecode modules. The “final_build.pyc” acts as the launcher, decoy interface, and reverse-shell client, while “kora.pyc” contains the full data-harvesting stealer logic.

Good Game’s anti-analysis and evasion

The malware employs evasion techniques at every stage of execution, beginning the moment the process starts. The launcher immediately retrieves the console window handle via GetConsoleWindow() and calls ShowWindow() to hide the terminal window. A fully functional Notepad-style graphical user interface (GUI) is rendered in the foreground via tkinter, a Python’s standard, built-in library used for creating GUIs while all malicious threads run as daemon threads behind it.

Before the stealer module begins with any data collection, it runs three sequential execution checks. First, it confirms the host operating system is Windows by checking platform.system(); the stealer aborts silently on Linux or macOS. Second, it verifies network connectivity by performing an HTTP GET request to Google, and if the request fails, the stealer exits and records the failure reason in a file named “skipped" in the temporary directory. Third, and most significantly, it runs sandbox detection checks in five steps.

It checks for the presence of libraries including sbiedll.dll, api_log.dll, vmcheck.dll, wpespy.dll, cmdvrt64.dll, avghookx.dll, snxhk.dll, and guard32.dll.

The MAC address check extracts the host's network adapter hardware address via the getmac module and compares it against known OUI prefixes assigned to virtualization vendors: VMware (00:05:69, 00:0c:29, 00:50:56), VirtualBox (08:00:27), Hyper-V (00:15:5d), Xen (00:16:3e), and Parallels (00:1c:42).

The hardware memory check queries GlobalMemoryStatusEx through a ctypes MEMORYSTATUS structure and flags machines with less than two gigabytes of physical RAM as sandbox environments.

The boot-time check uses psutil.boot_time() to compute system uptime and treats an uptime of fewer than five minutes as indicative of a freshly provisioned analysis VM.

Finally, the Wine emulation check probes for the presence of “C:\windows\system32\wineboot.exe” to detect Windows API compatibility layers.

If any single check triggers, the stealer exits silently, leaving no harvested data and minimal trace.

The decoy URL mechanism of the stealer adds a further layer of camouflage. It opens a PDF-hosted document in the victim's default browser exactly once per machine and computes an MD5 hash of the URL and stores a flag file named “url_opened_<hash>.flag” in the temporary directory of the victim machine. On all subsequent executions, the lure URL is not re-opened, preventing repeated suspicious browser launches that would alert the victim.

Persistence established by Good Game

The malware establishes persistence through two independent mechanisms on Windows, ensuring the implant survives reboots regardless of which pathway is disrupted. The primary method is a registry autorun by opening the key HKCU\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run using winreg.KEY_SET_VALUE” and writes the absolute filesystem path of the running executable under the value name Zoipers, causing the binary to be launched automatically at every subsequent user logon without requiring elevated privileges. The secondary persistence mechanism is executed from within the stealer module of kora.pyc. The stealer copies the running executable into the Windows Startup folder at %APPDATA%\Microsoft\Windows\Start Menu\Programs\Startup\ using shutil.copy().

Beyond simple re-execution persistence, the stealer also temporarily disables the Windows Task Manager during its active operation. It calls advapi32.RegCreateKeyExW directly through ctypes against the key HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Policies\System and sets a DWORD value DisableTaskMgr=1 via RegSetValueExW.  After the data exfiltration completes successfully, the stealer restores the value to zero (DisableTaskMgr=0), reducing the probability that the victim will notice the restriction.

Good Game stealer functionalities

The stealer's most extensive capability targets 23 Chromium-family and Firefox-based browser variants, covering Google Chrome across all distribution channels, Microsoft Edge, Opera, Opera GX, Brave, Vivaldi, Yandex Browser and all its regional variants, Amigo, Torch, Kometa, Orbitum, CentBrowser, 7Star, Sputnik, Epic Privacy Browser, Uran, and Iridium.

The stealer scans LevelDB stores across Discord clients and over 19 Chromium-based browsers for authentication tokens using two regex patterns one for plain-text tokens and one for tokens encrypted with the “dQw4w9WgXcQ:” prefix introduced in newer Discord versions. It decrypts the stolen token via the browser's extracted master key, then validates each recovered token against the Discord API to build a complete account profile covering username, display name, user ID, email, phone number, Nitro subscription tier, linked payment methods, gift codes, and multi-factor authentication (MFA) status.

The stealer also captures a full-monitor screenshot and a single webcam frame, both saved as PNGs and added to the archive before the temporary files are deleted and terminated. The stealer then harvests session data from 21 cryptocurrency wallets including Exodus, Electrum, Ledger Live, Trezor Suite, Atomic Wallet, Trust Wallet, Coinbase, and Binance, as well as four gaming platform launchers (Steam, Riot Games, Epic Games, and Rockstar Games).

Beyond structured credential databases, the stealer conducts a targeted sweep of the victim's personal file system across the folders Desktop, Downloads, Documents, Pictures, Videos, OneDrive root, and the Windows Recent Items folder at %APPDATA%\Microsoft\Windows\Recent. The sweep applies to two independent filters that a file must satisfy simultaneously before it is collected.

The first filter is an extension allowlist covering 45 file types spanning different categories mentioned in the table below:

Plain text and configuration formats

.txt, .log, .ini, .json, .xml, .csv, .md, .rtf, .cfg, .conf

.png, .jpg, .jpeg, .bmp, .gif, .tiff, .svg, .webp

.pdf, .doc, .docx, .xls, .xlsx, .ppt, .pptx, .odt, .ods, .odp

.zip, .rar, .7z, .tar, .gz, .bz2

video and audio formats

.mp4, .avi, .mkv, .mov, .wmv, .flv,.mp3, .wav, .aac, .flac, .ogg

The second filter is a keyword allowlist of four languages English, French, Spanish, and Portuguese that must match the file's base name exactly. The keyword list is designed to maximize overlap with the types of sensitive documents that cryptocurrency users, online gamers, and financially active individuals are likely to store on their desktops or in their documents folders. It covers two-factor authentication backup codes, mnemonic seed phrases, cryptocurrency wallet application names, generic password file naming conventions, banking and payment terms, private key and recovery phrase labels, Discord authentication tokens, Telegram session identifiers, and generic high-value labels such as secret, vault, backup, admin, and root.

Files that satisfy both the extension and keyword filters are written into the archive in the temporary directory, with each file name suffixed by a randomly generated four-digit number before the extension.

Data exfiltration mechanism of Good Game stealer

All harvested data is written progressively into a single ZIP file. The ZIP file object is opened with ZIP_DEFLATED compression in the system temporary directory. The archive file name uses the prefix “SK_”, followed by eight randomly generated characters, creating a unique identifier per victim and making the archive harder to identify by name alone.

The ZIP is structured into named subdirectories such as Screenshot/, Webcam/, Session Files/Wallets/, Session Files/Game Launchers/, Session Files/Apps/Telegram/, and Interesting Files/ alongside flat files in the root for system information and antivirus enumeration results. Browser-derived outputs are labelled with a count suffix such as Passwords (N).txt, where N reflects the number of credentials recovered from that browser, enabling the actor to prioritize.

Once all stealer modules have been completed, the closed archive is uploaded to hxxps[:]//upload[.]gofile[.]io/uploadFile using an HTTP POST request. The server returns a JSON response containing a direct download link to the uploaded archive.

With the GoFile download URL in hand, the stealer constructs a Discord webhook notification and POSTs it to the hardcoded webhook endpoint. The notification takes the form of a Discord message containing the victim's hostname, username, machine architecture, operating system version, and OS release label providing the operator with an immediate preview of the victim's machine profile without needing to download the archive first. After the webhook POST completes, the stealer calls its cleanup routine to delete the local ZIP archive and all temporary intermediate files including the screenshot PNG and webcam PNG from the temporary directory.

Clipboard hijacker targets multi-cryptocurrency wallet addresses

The clipboard hijacker Talos identified in the actor’s toolset is a compact Windows x64 executable written in C++. It has no network component or C2 communication protocol; instead, the actor configuration is compiled directly into the binary as XOR-encrypted wallet addresses in the binary data section.

Upon execution, the binary dynamically resolves the API function addresses and creates a global mutex “UuuRjinBZPBMIjnc”. Then it retrieves the machine name and searches for the substrings including VBOX, VMWARE, QEMU, and SANDBOX. Persistence is established by copying the binary to %APPDATA%\MsgEdgeUpdate.exe with FILE_ATTRIBUTE_HIDDEN and FILE_ATTRIBUTE_SYSTEM attributes applied, then adding it under user hive’s Run registry key with value named “MsgEdgeUpdate”, spoofing the appearance of a legitimate Microsoft Edge updater process.

The hijacker polls the Windows clipboard every 500 milliseconds in an infinite loop, applying address-format fingerprinting to detect the copied cryptocurrency. All clipboard APIs are resolved dynamically through user32.dll. On detection, the corresponding attacker wallet address is XOR-decrypted from the data section and overwrites the original wallet address in the victim machine clipboard.

The following ClamAV signatures detect and block this threat:

Win.Trojan.OverlordRAT-10060615-0

Win.Trojan.OverlordRAT2-10060616-0

Win.Infostealer.PyStealerDeps-10060618-0

Py.Infostealer.GoodGame-10060619-0

Py.Trojan.GoodGameLoader-10060620-0

Win.Trojan.WalletClipper-10060621-0

Win.Downloader.LNKMpCmdRun-10060622-0

Win.Trojan.LNKVideosExe-10060624-0

The following Snort2 and Snort3 Snort Rules (SIDs) detect and block this threat:

[75] Unit 42 - Borrowed Code, Real Consequences: What OPTIMJACK Teaches Us
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 20:43:14 +0000
Link: https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/optimjack-supply-chain-attack/

[TAM METİN - 819 kelime]
Threat Research Center

Borrowed Code, Real Consequences: What OPTIMJACK Teaches Us

Published:November 17, 2026

Categories:GeneralInsights

Tags:JavaScriptMalicious injection attackPayloadSoftware supply-chain attack

Malicious injection attack

Software supply-chain attack

On Nov. 16, 2026, a threat actor exploited a vulnerability in Optimizely's Web Experimentation platform to inject malicious JavaScript into customer accounts. Nicknamed OPTIMJACK, this campaign delivered that code to visitors of at least 178 organizations' websites.

The tampered code was delivered directly from the vendor's legitimate content delivery network (CDN), under the vendor's own security certificate.

Enterprises invest heavily in hardening their core web infrastructure. A single compromised third-party tool can undermine that work. OPTIMJACK is a reminder that any vendor script with dynamic execution privileges on your site is part of your direct production code.

Threat actors know that third-party apps are the path of least resistance into lucrative environments. Defenders can respond with a fundamental principle that security engineering has relied on from the beginning: segmentation.

What Happened in OPTIMJACK

On Nov. 16th, 2026, the attacker exploited the vulnerability to place malicious JavaScript in the custom Project JavaScript field of customer accounts.

Because the tampered snippets were served from the vendor's legitimate CDN under its own certificate, at least 178 organizations, including well-known media, technology and financial services brands, unknowingly served the code to their visitors.

Once the code loaded in a visitor's browser, it did three things:

Cryptocurrency wallet swapping: It replaced wallet addresses that visitors copied with the attacker's own

Payment card skimming: It collected card details from checkout forms

Mobile exploit delivery: It attempted to deliver the Coruna and DarkSword exploit kits to iPhones and iPads

Optimizely patched the flaw, removed the injected code and secured affected accounts early on November 17.

Trusted Vendors Are the Path of Least Resistance

Websites are not a single monolith. They are dynamic assemblies of dozens of third-party scripts, analytics tags and optimization tools.

That diversity drives speed, but it also introduces new vectors of attack through the software supply chain. When attackers compromise a single trusted vendor upstream, they bypass the perimeter defenses that vendor's customers have built.

The choice of malicious payload also matters. Swapping valid cryptocurrency wallet addresses on a page is a quick and persistent way to direct ill-gotten payments to a threat actor.

Segmentation Is the Strongest Step Available Today

If a third-party script executes on your page, it effectively has the same privileges as your own developers. Organizations need to architect their sites with that assumption in mind.

The best thing security teams can do right now is apply strict segmentation. The goal is simple— a compromised vendor script should never be able to touch sensitive user inputs. Methods of accomplishing this goal include:

Remove non-essential tags from sensitive pages: Strip all non-essential marketing, experimentation and analytics tags from authentication and checkout pages

Isolate payment collection: Place payment collection inside hardened, cross-origin iframes

Segmentation has been a cornerstone of good security engineering since the beginning.

What Affected Organizations Should Know

Even with the flaw patched, affected websites still need to act. Security teams should take these steps now:

Audit all project modifications made while the malicious code was available

Double-check all code that may have been altered during that period

Treat exposed payment data as potentially compromised, including payment card details entered into checkout forms on affected pages

OPTIMJACK is a reminder that any vendor script with dynamic execution privileges on your site is part of your direct production code. One vulnerability at a single vendor allowed an attacker to reach visitors of at least 178 organizations through a trusted, legitimate delivery channel.

Third-party scripts drive speed, but when they execute on your page, they effectively have the same privileges as your own developers.

Administrators of affected websites should start with the cleanup steps above. These segmentation steps will help keep a compromised vendor script away from sensitive user inputs. Isolating and segmenting as much as possible is key to software supply chain security management.

The Unit 42 Compromise Assessment can help you proactively search for historical and ongoing indicators of compromise to discover if, how, and why previously undetected malicious activity is occurring so that you can respond before further damage occurs.

If you are experiencing an active breach or think you may have been impacted by a cybersecurity incident, contact Unit 42 to connect with a team member. The Unit 42 incident response team is available 24/7/365. If you have cyber insurance or legal counsel, you can request Unit 42 by name. You can also take preventative steps by requesting a Proactive Assessment and putting our team on speed dial with a Unit 42 Retainer.

[76] Microsoft Security - SCKit abuses ScreenConnect to deploy remote access and target technician sessions
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 13 Nov 2026 21:31:44 +0000
Link: https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/11/13/sckit-abuses-screenconnect-deploy-remote-access-target-technician-sessions/

[TAM METİN - 2554 kelime]
ScreenConnect architecture and attack surfaces

Attack chain overview

Mitigation and protection guidance

Indicators of compromise and supporting artifacts

Beginning on November 11, 2026, ConnectWise reported an active campaign of a threat actor gaining unauthorized access to cloud-hosted ConnectWise ScreenConnect instances belonging to managed service providers (MSPs). Microsoft Threat Intelligence has confirmed ConnectWise’s reported activity and we have also observed threat actors abusing ScreenConnect, a legitimate remote-support application, to install unauthorized remote-access clients and deploy a malicious worm, SCKit, on ConnectWise customer managed endpoints.

Additionally, Microsoft analyzed the malicious worm’s behavior: deployment starts with commands issued through ScreenConnect to silently install an identical client connected to threat actor-controlled infrastructure. A batch launcher retrieves a PowerShell decoder that decrypts and extracts SCKit using device-specific key material. The toolkit establishes persistence through scheduled tasks and registry Run entries and deploys RelayTrap, a component designed to deliver a malicious payload to a connecting technician.

We have identified that the worm targets IT support technician workstations using the hijacked authenticated ScreenConnect Session and uses the victim’s management console to distribute payloads to other managed devices. The resulting access to either target enables threat actors to remotely administer affected endpoints, execute commands, and deploy further tools independently of the legitimate support instance.

Defenders should investigate technician workstations and downstream affected endpoints. Removing an unauthorized client alone does not remove the toolkit’s persistence or invalidate exposed web sessions. In this blog, we examine SCKit’s attack chain and payload components and provide mitigation guidance, indicators of compromise, and Microsoft Defender XDR advanced hunting queries to help organizations identify and disrupt this activity.

ScreenConnect architecture and attack surfaces

ScreenConnect, formerly ConnectWise Control, is a remote-support and access platform used by MSPs to administer customer devices, often alongside remote monitoring and management (RMM) software. Its operating model has three parts:

Management instance. A cloud-hosted or on-premises ScreenConnect server provides the web console and manages sessions. Its relay service carries communication between the technician and endpoint clients.

Technician workstation. Support staff sign in to the console through a browser and launch a local host client for interactive support.

Customer endpoint. An installed guest, or access, client maintains unattended access and executes commands delivered through ScreenConnect.

An Access session provides persistent, unattended access through the installed client. A Support session is a temporary connection that an end user joins for assistance. ScreenConnect also supports remote commands from the web console without an interactive technician session. The endpoint client receives the command and runs it on the customer device; the technician workstation is not an intermediary for that execution.

Reported server-side entry through extensions

Extensions are add-ons installed on the ScreenConnect server to add features or integrate with other products, including ticketing and RMM tools. ConnectWise Automate was formerly named LabTech; Labtech Plus is a third-party extension, not the Automate product itself. The RMM+ extension family exposes remote-command APIs protected by a separately configured passphrase rather than the technician’s normal console sign-in.

ConnectWise’s advisory describes a reported authentication flaw in RMM+ versions 1.0.31 and 1.0.32: when no access key was configured, the extension accepted an empty key. In that configuration, an unauthorized caller could submit commands to the server’s API for delivery to managed endpoints without a normal ScreenConnect user sign-in. ConnectWise is investigating extension abuse as an initial-access route; the entry method for the reported campaign remains under investigation.

A separate path from customer endpoints to technicians

SCKit is a malware toolkit that includes RelayTrap, a malicious replacement for the ScreenConnect client on an affected customer endpoint. RelayTrap is designed to deliver a payload to a technician’s workstation when they connect for support. ConnectWise states that the fix for CVE-2026-84869 blocks the toolkit’s attempted endpoint-to-technician spread. This client-side attack path is separate from the server-side access used to deploy SCKit.

Attack chain overview

The deployment sequence below runs on the customer endpoint. It establishes a separate remote-access connection and prepares the scripts and RelayTrap component that target a later technician

1. ScreenConnect commands install an additional client

The threat actor uses the MSP’s ScreenConnect management instance to direct the installed endpoint client to start cmd.exe. That local process invokes curl.exe to download an MSI over HTTP from an attacker-controlled ScreenConnect server. The command requests /Bin/ScreenConnect.ClientSetup.msi with access-session parameters and a c=Exploited<N> marker (c=Exploited9 in the illustrated command), and saves the installer to C:\Windows\Temp\sc.msi.

Windows Installer then installs the package silently on that endpoint, without restarting it, using /i, /qn, and /norestart. The added client connects to an attacker-controlled ScreenConnect instance, creating a remote-access channel separate from the MSP’s approved instance. Securing access to the legitimate console does not remove this additional client or its connection.

2. A batch launcher retrieves the PowerShell decoder

Further commands on the customer endpoint stage the toolkit. A PowerShell command decodes a URL-safe Base64 string and writes the result to a batch file in Windows Temp. The batch sets BATPATH to its own location, downloads the decoder from ns[.]mo7sen[.]dev/EadF.php?p=4, and starts it with -Mode full.

The decoder reads BATPATH, or falls back to SCKIT_SELF, to locate the launcher. Its hidden-relaunch branch creates a VBScript wrapper and runs it through wscript.exe to start the same batch without a visible window.

The scripts use the following p values at the same server path to request individual components:

3. The decoder decrypts and extracts the SCKit bundle

The decoder reads MachineGuid from HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Cryptography and includes it as the m request parameter. The response provides a Base64-encoded body and two headers, X-Lab-Half and X-Lab-Salt.

To derive the decryption key, the decoder hashes the UTF-8 machine identifier with SHA-256, XORs that hash with 32 bytes decoded from X-Lab-Half, and appends the machine-identifier bytes to the recovered secret material. PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 then derives a 32-byte key using the response salt and 4,096 iterations.

The decoder uses the first 16 bytes of the decoded response body as the initialization vector (IV). It decrypts the remaining bytes using AES-256-CBC with PKCS7 padding, decompresses the plaintext with GZip, and writes the resulting ZIP archive. The archive is then extracted to %TEMP%\SCKitBundle.

In full mode, the decoder prepares %TEMP%\SCKit\trapkit\payload and stages the watcher, installer, alert script, and RelayTrap.cs. It also copies its batch launcher into the payload directory for RelayTrap to distribute. The copied launcher retrieves the decoder and starts toolkit deployment.

4. Additional component: PowerShell script loader

Alongside the bundle decoder, SCKit includes a separate loader for encrypted PowerShell scripts. Unlike the decoder, which retrieves and expands an archive, this component requests a script using p=1 with the machine identifier. It applies the same secret-recovery, PBKDF2, AES-CBC, and GZip operations, but uses a salt embedded in its source rather than one supplied in a response header.

After decompression, the loader converts the bytes to UTF-8 text and writes %TEMP%\k<32-hex>.ps1, using a newly generated random GUID for the filename. It starts a hidden PowerShell process to run the file with the forwarded arguments, then attempts to remove the script after the child process exits.

5. RelayTrap adds persistence and targets connecting technicians

On the customer endpoint, persistence keeps the toolkit available for a later support connection. The deployment places scripts under C:\ProgramData\RelayTrap. The RelayTrapLiteAlert value in the machine-wide CurrentVersion\Run key launches an alert script at user logon. A scheduled task starts the watcher with a payload filename and lists of client identifiers and relay hosts to exclude. The -PollSeconds 10 argument configures the watcher to poll every 10 seconds.

The watcher enumerates ScreenConnect client services and reads their executable paths and connection parameters. For each client, its build routine creates a directory under trapbins, copies libraries from that client’s installation, and uses the .NET C# compiler to build RelayTrap.exe. It checks for changes to the source or client libraries before rebuilding.

RelayTrap is designed to reuse the customer endpoint’s ScreenConnect connection details in place of its normal client. When a technician connects with a vulnerable host client, RelayTrap attempts to deliver the staged batch launcher through that session. The launcher retrieves the same decoder, extending the toolkit’s deployment design from the customer device to the technician workstation. Configured skip lists exclude specified client identifiers and relay hosts from processing.

ScreenConnect services are also configured for automatic startup. Registry writes target Microsoft Defender Antivirus path exclusions for staged MSI copies and CWInstall_…\Installer.ps1. The installer configuration lists sc[.]payproofkit[.]com and cdn007-digitaldownloads[.]com as additional ScreenConnect MSI sources. A subsequent curl.exe command downloads another client installer from sc[.]payproofkit[.]com.

6. Browser access targets authenticated ScreenConnect sessions

This component targets the technician’s management-console access, rather than only the original customer device. The account’s permissions determine which additional sessions and devices the distribution workflow can reach.

7. Cleanup and redeployment

The toolkit’s cleanup script places a stop marker in the trap directory. It removes trap-related tasks, services, processes, Run entries, and files, and restores ScreenConnect client services unless client removal was requested. It then removes any remaining stop marker to allow a newly deployed watcher to run.

After running the cleanup script, the decoder’s cleanup mode starts the bundled dropper again unless -NoReArm is set. Cleanup therefore also serves as a path to redeploy the toolkit.

Mitigation and protection guidance

Address the server-side extension APIs and technician clients separately. Extension authentication and client-side file transfer are different attack surfaces; securing one does not remove the other or an existing endpoint installation.

Keep the identified extensions disabled. Follow the vendor advisory for Labtech Plus, RMM Plus, RMM+, RMM Plus Password Link, and RMM+ Password Link. Review their installation and configuration history alongside administrative access.

Update technician clients separately from the console. ConnectWise states that ScreenConnect client 26.6.5 and later block the toolkit’s attempted endpoint-to-technician transfer technique. The fix for CVE-2026-84869 was released on September 8, 2026. Verify each technician client’s installed version; a cloud-console update does not automatically update those clients. The vendor bulletin also describes temporary file-transfer restrictions while updates are pending.

Investigate through a separate channel. Use Microsoft Defender for Endpoint live response or another approved out-of-band method instead of ScreenConnect on a suspected affected device. Isolate affected endpoints and include connecting technician workstations in scope.

Preserve console and endpoint evidence. Retain extension, authentication, Run Command, and Toolbox records together with process, file, task, and registry history. Review Toolbox history for tools removed after use, and inspect queued commands and tools before reconnecting devices.

Revoke exposed sessions from a clean device. Invalidate affected ScreenConnect web sessions and associated identity-provider sessions, rotate exposed credentials, and review accounts and permissions. Password changes alone do not address every existing session.

Remove persistence and unapproved clients. Validate each client’s enrolled instance and relay destination before removing it. Remove malicious tasks, Run values, trap files, and unauthorized exclusions through approved response tooling. Do not use a cleanup script recovered from the kit as a remediation tool.

Verify the complete state before restoring connectivity. Confirm approved services and destinations, effective antivirus settings, and the absence of re-created tasks or scripts. Review tamper-protection events when investigating exclusion changes. Rebuild devices when integrity cannot be established.

Keep Microsoft Defender Antivirus, cloud-delivered protection, and tamper protection enabled. Verify that devices meet the requirements for protecting antivirus exclusions. Maintain an inventory of authorized remote-management instances and use application control to restrict unapproved software.

Microsoft Defender XDR detections

Microsoft Defender XDR customers can use the following Microsoft Defender for Endpoint alert titles to investigate activity associated with this threat. Review related alerts together with device timelines and ScreenConnect audit records to trace command execution, persistence, browser-data access, and network connections.

Threat intelligence reports

Microsoft Defender XDR customers can use the following threat analytics report to get the latest intelligence on SCKit and the abuse of ScreenConnect, including protection status, affected assets, and recommended actions for their environment.

Microsoft Defender XDR Threat analytics and Secure Now

Customers can access the threat analytics report: SCKit abuses ScreenConnect to deploy remote access and target technician sessions

Customers can also access recommended controls and guidance on Secure Now.

Advanced hunting queries

Each query below runs independently and uses a 14-day lookback. Adjust the time range to the investigation window and available retention, and correlate results with approved administrative activity.

Connections to campaign infrastructure

Find connections to the listed domains and IP addresses. Review the full destination, initiating process, and approved remote-support infrastructure for each match.

Known installer, archive, and uploader files

Find file events for the sc.msi package, staged ZIP, and uploader using their SHA-1 hashes. The query uses SHA-1 because SHA256 is usually unpopulated in DeviceFileEvents. Combine hash matches with command-line and persistence findings.

Registry values referencing RelayTrap

Find writes to the RelayTrapLiteAlert value or values containing RelayTrap. Review the registry key, value data, and initiating process.

RelayTrap scheduled-task events

Find scheduled-task events containing RelayTrap or TrapWatch. Inspect the task definition to identify its triggers, executable, and arguments.

Microsoft Defender Antivirus path-exclusion writes

Find Microsoft Defender Antivirus path-exclusion registry writes. Review changes naming Windows Temp, ConnectWiseControl_, CWInstall_, or RelayTrap, and verify their authorization and effective policy.

SCKit – Generated PowerShell Loader Artifacts

Identifies temporary PowerShell files matching the k<32-hex>.ps1 naming pattern generated by the SCKit loader, potentially revealing staged payloads independently of known file hashes or infrastructure.

Indicators of compromise and supporting artifacts

Hashes identify individual files. The watcher compiles RelayTrap against local client libraries, and embedded configuration changes script hashes. Use file and process behavior to investigate other builds. The table includes configuration, cleanup, and proof artifacts to support the investigation.

[77] Mandiant (Google Cloud) - COLD RELIC: A Case Study in Impersonation and Compromise
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 14:00:00 +0000
Link: https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/cold-relic-a-case-study-in-impersonation-and-compromise/

[TAM METİN - 3067 kelime]
COLD RELIC: A Case Study in Impersonation and Compromise

Visibility and context on the threats that matter most.

Written by: Josh Murchie, John Wolfram, Joseph Spero, Peter Ukhanov, Fernando Tomlinson, Jr., Wesley Shields

Over the past year, Mandiant has responded to a series of linked intrusions demonstrating how Russian cyber espionage group COLD RELIC (formerly tracked as COLDRIVER and UNC4057; also known as Star Blizzard, Callisto, and Gossamer Bear) turns a single cloud compromise into a springboard for ongoing third party compromise and espionage. Most recently, Mandiant investigated a 2026 compromise at a US-based organization where COLD RELIC directly weaponized data, the corporate staff directory, and identities stolen during a late-2025 compromise, which Mandiant also investigated, at a separate US-based organization.

COLD RELIC is a Russian state-sponsored threat group that the US Department of Justice (DOJ) has assessed operates within Russia's Federal Security Service (FSB). The group is known for implementing sophisticated phishing and malware campaigns with the aim to gain access to information of strategic or political relevance to the Russian government, as well as to leverage compromised access to target compromised individuals’ broader social and professional networks. Once COLD RELIC compromises a target, it uses stolen emails and data to establish authentic pretexts and impersonate contacts when reaching out to subsequent targets. While COLD RELIC has largely maintained a low profile, yielding minimal public reporting relative to other Russia-nexus threat actors, it has been highly successful at compromising high-value targets (including government officials, defense contractors, and non-governmental organizations (NGOs) across Europe and North America) while maintaining a consistent operational tempo.

While Google Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) has tracked and disrupted COLD RELIC phishing campaigns since 2015, recent Mandiant incident response investigations have uncovered critical new insights into the group’s social engineering and post-compromise tradecraft. We are sharing this end-to-end analysis—along with actionable detection and hardening guidance—to help organizations defend against COLD RELIC’s ongoing use of stolen pretexts, localized residential proxies, and cross-cloud identity abuse. As part of our efforts to thwart this threat actor, we issue Government-backed attack alerts when we detect COLD RELIC targeting Google accounts. COLD RELIC remains an active threat; GTIG has observed evidence of new phishing operations as recently as November 2026.

Over more than a decade of tracking COLD RELIC alongside Mandiant incident response investigations, we have observed the group continually refine how it leverages data harvested from one intrusion to enable follow-on operations against downstream targets. In late 2025, COLD RELIC downloaded a large volume of data from a US-based organization, searching for names, email addresses, and email archives, likely to fulfill broader intelligence requirements. In 2026, Mandiant investigated a subsequent COLD RELIC intrusion at a second US-based organization where phishing URL parameters and impersonation pretexts indicated that COLD RELIC likely leveraged contact and relationship context from the first organization to target the second.

One of the hallmarks of COLD RELIC phishing campaigns is the use of a compromised website as the initial link that will redirect the target to the actual phishing domain. This provides COLD RELIC with a layer of misdirection and hides their phishing domains, while also giving them a chance to fingerprint anyone visiting the link to determine if they should be redirected or not. A COLD RELIC phishing link usually contains a parameter on the end of it with a value that is set by the attacker and often indicates a relationship to a previous victim or what the lure is. For example, a URL might look like these (though this is not always the case):

Figure 1: Sample URLs incorporating targeted individuals’ names or lure topics

During Mandiant’s incident response investigation at the second US-based organization, responders identified that COLD RELIC initiated contact over an encrypted messaging app while impersonating an employee from the first victim organization. In parallel, GTIG correlated a COLD RELIC redirector URL from the intrusion that had been submitted to VirusTotal in June 2026, whose query parameter referenced the first victim organization.

Case Study 1 - Stolen Credentials to Data Exfiltration

During the late-2025 intrusion, GTIG observed COLD RELIC operators compromise the cloud productivity environment of a US-based organization. To gain initial access, COLD RELIC leveraged a residential proxy network in the US located in close geographical proximity to the victim. COLD RELIC authenticated using the stolen credentials of a Super Admin account, in combination with a multi-factor authentication (MFA) push notification fatigue attack. After the victim user accepted an MFA request, COLD RELIC registered a device model previously unseen within the tenant of the victim organization. COLD RELIC leveraged passkeys, MFA backup codes, and rogue MFA devices to retain access throughout the intrusion.

COLD RELIC used the privileges of the compromised Super Admin account to download the contents of the entire organization’s cloud storage drive by both mass sharing files to a COLD RELIC-operated email account and downloading files directly. While operating inside the tenant of the victim organization, COLD RELIC accessed the Admin Console, queried Admin security investigation and email audit logs to check defensive visibility, and viewed, modified, and deleted Alert Center notifications to remove forensic evidence of the initial intrusion. COLD RELIC also exported full employee lists and initiated a domain-wide data export via Google Workspace Customer Takeout using Super Admin privileges. Unlike individual end-user Google Takeout, Customer Takeout is an administrative tenant-wide export tool; these requests are logged in Google Workspace Admin audit logs (CUSTOMER_TAKEOUT_CREATED), and administrators can also disable Google Takeout for users at the organizational level.

Following mass data theft and antiforensics activities, COLD RELIC maintained access to the victim organization for several months and performed additional activities including:

Email inbox sync via OAuth to external email clients

Email inbox sync via OAuth to external email clients

Reauthentication using unrevoked attacker-enrolled passkeys that persisted across password and session resets and bypassed device registration controls

Reauthentication using unrevoked attacker-enrolled passkeys that persisted across password and session resets and bypassed device registration controls

Targeted cloud drive document access related to credentials, email archives, personnel information, and other documents relevant to COLD RELIC’s intelligence collection requirements

Targeted cloud drive document access related to credentials, email archives, personnel information, and other documents relevant to COLD RELIC’s intelligence collection requirements

When the threat actor was removed from the victim organization’s infrastructure, GTIG observed COLD RELIC leveraging email accounts impersonating employees of the victim organization. These impersonated accounts were used to target both internal employees and associates within the same professional circles with renewed social engineering attempts.

Case Study 2 - Leveraging Previous Compromise - Initial Approach

During the second intrusion, COLD RELIC demonstrated competency in navigating multi-cloud environments and federated authentication mechanisms. GTIG observed COLD RELIC operators compromise a Microsoft Entra ID user account of a US-based organization that used Entra ID as its federated single sign-on (SSO) identity provider for Google Workspace. To gain initial access, COLD RELIC impersonated an employee of the organization impacted in Case Study 1 to establish a pretext for communications over a secure messaging app. The threat actor ultimately shared phishing links during the course of the conversation. These phishing links, designed to steal credentials and authentication tokens for Entra ID, redirected the victim to document lures relevant to the ongoing conversation in order to avoid raising suspicion. Upon successful authentication using the stolen credentials, COLD RELIC registered and used a new MFA device in the SSO solution to retain access to the user account throughout the intrusion.

A redacted and abbreviated version of the social engineering conversation is included below (image generated with LLMs):

Figure 2: Redacted, LLM-generated depiction of observed threat actor social engineering engagement

By impersonating someone known in the same professional circles as the new target, COLD RELIC increased their chance of phishing success. Ultimately, in this case, COLD RELIC was able to obtain credentials to the organization and perform bulk download of files accessible in the compromised user’s cloud drive.

Post-Compromise Activity

After gaining initial access to an organization, COLD RELIC registers rogue MFA devices, passkeys, and MFA backup codes to establish long-term persistence. Once persistence is secured, COLD RELIC conducts aggressive data collection and exfiltration operations, accessing email, chat, and cloud storage repositories. While COLD RELIC's primary objective post-compromise is mass collection and download of files and email archives, it simultaneously performs targeted queries for specific terms of interest. In particular, COLD RELIC searches for credentials, personnel information, email archives (.pst files), and documents related to their collection requirements. Additionally, COLD RELIC seeks out data that can support subsequent spear-phishing operations, including organizational rosters, contact lists, resumes, and event schedules to build convincing pretexts for future targets.

Beyond seeking out data to support subsequent operations, COLD RELIC also appeared to repurpose the account used in Case Study 2 for follow on targeting. After the compromise, the encrypted messaging application used for the initial approach recorded that the threat actor changed their profile name to a completely new person. GTIG believes this new persona was likely used to continue to target other organizations in the same professional circles in a similar manner to both of the cases documented here.

Outlook and Implications

COLD RELIC’s campaigns demonstrate the convergence of human-centric social engineering and cloud identity abuse. By obtaining access to strategic personnel rosters and operational documents, Russian intelligence services turn legitimate business communications into attack vectors against trusted partners. Their ability to navigate across multiple cloud ecosystems highlights that organizations must defend their entire identity estate holistically. GTIG will continue to track, disrupt, and expose COLD RELIC infrastructure to protect global cloud users.

Remediation and Hardening

As previously mentioned, GTIG has observed COLD RELIC heavily rely on residential proxy networks in the course of their intrusions. Moreover, the group goes one step further in obfuscating their activities by selecting proxies that geolocate to the same approximate location as their victim users, thereby avoiding impossible travel or other heuristic-based alerting mechanisms. GTIG recommends enriching authentication logs with residential proxy and VPN IP reputation feeds, alerting on sudden residential ASN shifts for privileged accounts, and applying conditional access policies to block or detect logons from known proxy networks.

In some cases, the severity and scope of COLD RELIC intrusions were amplified due to users storing credentials and other secrets within their organization’s cloud storage platforms. This enabled COLD RELIC to pivot to additional accounts or services that it would not have otherwise been able to access, including users’ personal accounts which are typically unmonitored by their employers. Organizations can implement cloud storage Data Loss Prevention (DLP) policies, such as Google Workspace’s Security & Data Protection, to warn or block users attempting to create, upload, or externally share files matching sensitive patterns.

In at least one case, GTIG observed that a victim organization identified the intrusion and attempted to remediate COLD RELIC’s access to their cloud tenant via password resets for impacted accounts. However, because the threat actor hijacked sessions and the victim organization failed to also revoke all existing sessions for the accounts in question, COLD RELIC maintained access. In similar circumstances, such as Business Email Compromise (BEC) intrusions, GTIG recommends organizations revoke all user sessions in addition to forcing password resets for impacted accounts. Organizations should implement the following recommendations:

Identity & Multi-Factor Authentication

Identity & Multi-Factor Authentication

Enforce Phishing-Resistant MFA (FIDO2 / WebAuthn): Replace SMS, voice, and push notifications with hardware security keys (e.g., Titan Security Keys). Hardware keys are bound to the origin URL and cannot be intercepted by AiTM reverse proxies or approved via fatigue attacks.

Enforce Phishing-Resistant MFA (FIDO2 / WebAuthn): Replace SMS, voice, and push notifications with hardware security keys (e.g., Titan Security Keys). Hardware keys are bound to the origin URL and cannot be intercepted by AiTM reverse proxies or approved via fatigue attacks.

Restrict and Audit Passkey Registrations: Monitor identity logs for passkey registrations and rogue device enrollments, particularly on administrative accounts.

Restrict and Audit Passkey Registrations: Monitor identity logs for passkey registrations and rogue device enrollments, particularly on administrative accounts.

Harden MFA Registration: Require existing administrators or out-of-band identity verification to approve new MFA devices or secondary authenticators (such as Microsoft Authenticator or passkeys).

Harden MFA Registration: Require existing administrators or out-of-band identity verification to approve new MFA devices or secondary authenticators (such as Microsoft Authenticator or passkeys).

Organizations and individuals may consider enrolling in the Advanced Protection Program.

Organizations and individuals may consider enrolling in the Advanced Protection Program.

Session Management & Incident Response

Session Management & Incident Response

Mandate Session & OAuth Revocation in BEC Playbooks: When remediating suspected account compromise, password changes alone are insufficient. Administrators must immediately revoke all active browser sessions, refresh tokens, and authorized third-party OAuth app permissions across both Google Workspace and Microsoft Entra ID, and remove any unauthorized passkeys, backup codes, or MFA devices.

Mandate Session & OAuth Revocation in BEC Playbooks: When remediating suspected account compromise, password changes alone are insufficient. Administrators must immediately revoke all active browser sessions, refresh tokens, and authorized third-party OAuth app permissions across both Google Workspace and Microsoft Entra ID, and remove any unauthorized passkeys, backup codes, or MFA devices.

Audit Third-Party OAuth Grants: Continuously review authorized third-party applications in Google Workspace and Microsoft 365, flagging unexpected native desktop email clients (e.g., Thunderbird) or external mail services (e.g., Proton Mail).

Audit Third-Party OAuth Grants: Continuously review authorized third-party applications in Google Workspace and Microsoft 365, flagging unexpected native desktop email clients (e.g., Thunderbird) or external mail services (e.g., Proton Mail).

Network & Infrastructure Controls

Network & Infrastructure Controls

Detect & Block Residential Proxy Logons: Restrict administrative access from consumer ISPs, VPNs, and known residential proxy ASN ranges (e.g., AS5650, AS20115).

Detect & Block Residential Proxy Logons: Restrict administrative access from consumer ISPs, VPNs, and known residential proxy ASN ranges (e.g., AS5650, AS20115).

Cross-Cloud DLP Policies: Deploy Data Loss Prevention (DLP) rules across cloud storage (Google Drive, OneDrive, SharePoint) to alert on documents containing sensitive terms (e.g., passwords, roster, .pst) or to restrict mass external file-sharing.

Cross-Cloud DLP Policies: Deploy Data Loss Prevention (DLP) rules across cloud storage (Google Drive, OneDrive, SharePoint) to alert on documents containing sensitive terms (e.g., passwords, roster, .pst) or to restrict mass external file-sharing.

Detection Opportunity

T1621: Multi-Factor Authentication Request Generation

Monitor for MFA Spam: Alert on multiple failed login or MFA challenge events for a single user in a short timeframe, followed closely by a successful authentication from the same IP address.

OAuth Abuse & Mail Sync

T1114.002: Email Collection: Remote Email Collection

T1528: Steal Application Access Token

Unapproved OAuth Scopes: Alert on new OAuth authorizations (Authorize event in GWS OAuth logs) granting broad scopes (e.g., https://mail.google.com/) to non-standard or unapproved email clients like Proton or Thunderbird.

Automated Data Exfiltration Attempt

T1119: Automated Collection

Customer Takeout Initiation: Generate alerts for the creation of any tenant-wide collection or export (e.g., Customer Takeout Created in GWS Admin logs), as this is a technique that can result in data exfiltration of an entire tenant.

Data Theft via Bulk Download

T1530: Data from Cloud Storage

Anomalous Download Volume: Monitor logs for spikes in file downloads (e.g. Download events in GWS Drive logs) by a single user within a short period of time, particularly originating from non-corporate ASNs/IPs or known residential proxy networks.

Data Theft via External Sharing

T1567.002: Exfiltration to Cloud Storage

External File Sharing Anomalies: Monitor logs for a spike in documents being shared externally (e.g., GWS Drive User Sharing Permissions Change or Visibility changed to shared_externally), particularly to free email providers (e.g., @gmail.com).

Device & Passkey Enrollment

T1098.005: Account Manipulation: Device Registration

New MFA/Device Enrollment: Alert on new passkey enrollments (e.g., GWS Passkey enrolled events) or device registrations (GWS Device Registered events), particularly when originating from unexpected geographic locations or immediately following an MFA fatigue pattern.

Use of MFA Backup Codes

T1078.004: Valid Accounts: Cloud Accounts

Backup Code Usage: Monitor logs for authentications with MFA backup codes (e.g., GWS User logs where the Login Challenge Type is Backup code), particularly if it originates from a non-corporate ASN or known residential proxy networks.

Impairing Defenses via Alert Deletion

T1562.008: Impair Defenses: Disable or Modify Cloud Logs / Alerts

Alert Center Tampering: Alert on any user modifying or deleting security alerts (e.g., Batch Delete Operation Performed events in GWS Admin logs), particularly if the user is deleting alerts related to their own account or critical severity alerts (e.g., GWS Government-backed attack alerts).

Internal Reconnaissance via User List

T1087.004: Account Discovery: Cloud Account

Directory Export: Alert on domain user exports (e.g., User List Download events in GWS Admin logs). While admins may do this legitimately, it should be heavily scrutinized, particularly when performed from unknown devices.

Forensic Counter-Investigation

T1082: System Information Discovery

Admin Log Querying: Monitor logs for users performing audit log queries (e.g., Investigation Query events in GWS Admin logs). Unexpected querying of security, access, or audit logs may indicate a threat actor attempting to perform counter-forensics.

Searching for Passwords & Archives

T1083: File and Directory Discovery

T1552.001: Credentials In Files

Sensitive File Search: Monitor logs for file directory keyword searches (e.g., Item search performed events in GWS Drive logs) containing terms related to archives or credentials such as .pst, .bak, password, cred, or repeated accesses/downloads of documents explicitly titled with credential-related keywords.

[78] CrowdStrike - CrowdStrike Is Building the Engine of the Agentic SOC with Falcon Next-Gen SIEM
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Nov 18, 2026 00:00:00-0600
Link: https://www.crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/crowdstrike-named-leader-2026-gartner-mq-siem/

[TAM METİN - 1279 kelime]
CrowdStrike has been named a Leader in the 2026 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Security Information and Event Management (SIEM). We believe this recognition reflects a fundamental truth about the future of security operations: The agentic SOC starts with SIEM, and CrowdStrike Falcon® Next-Gen SIEM was built to be its foundation.

The Agentic SOC Starts with SIEM

Security analysts spend hours piecing together context across dozens of point tools, while every new data source drives SIEM costs higher. Now, AI agents operate across the enterprise with elevated privileges, adding a new attack surface the SOC has to defend. Keeping pace requires a new operating model: the agentic SOC, where expert AI agents investigate and respond to every detection and analysts direct the work.

An agent can only reason on the data and context it can see, which makes the SIEM the most important decision in the agentic SOC. A legacy SIEM built as a system of record forces teams to choose between ingesting everything at rising cost or dropping data and accepting blind spots.

The SIEM also decides what the SOC sees first. Legacy SIEMs lean on static rules that fire on single events in isolation, burying real attacks under low-fidelity alerts. Agents that receive this queue spend their time investigating noise. The agentic SOC needs a SIEM that delivers fewer, better detections that are correlated across domains and informed by how adversaries operate.

We built Falcon Next-Gen SIEM for this shift. Below we share five ways a SIEM built for the agentic SOC breaks from the legacy model, and why we believe only CrowdStrike delivers all of them natively on one platform.

Uses Detection-Ready Data

Legacy SIEM pricing increased the cost of visibility. Every new data source created another integration to maintain and another line on the bill; as a result, data was dropped. For human analysts and AI agents, working with partial data leads to wrong decisions

Falcon Next-Gen SIEM ends the trade-off. Customers’ highest-value security telemetry is already in the CrowdStrike Falcon® platform, and native Falcon data across endpoint, cloud, identity, exposure management, and AI agents flows into Falcon Next-Gen SIEM at no added ingest cost. Certified data pipelines, built on CrowdStrike Falcon® Onum and validated by CrowdStrike Falcon® Complete, bring in detection-ready third-party data and filter out noise at ingestion. Customers can reduce data storage costs by up to 50%1, and federated search reaches data wherever it lives, so teams see everything without ingesting everything.

This same data now covers AI agents, a source that legacy SIEMs weren’t built to handle. CrowdStrike® Falcon Guardian™ shows what an AI agent did on the endpoint, including prompts, tool calls, MCP servers, skills, and subagents. The telemetry lands in Falcon Next-Gen SIEM alongside endpoint, identity, cloud, and third-party data, so a compromised agent surfaces already connected to its owner and host.

Delivers Adversary-Informed Detections

A rule that fires on a single event can't see a campaign. Legacy correlation forces analysts to manually connect the dots; agents handed those alerts must guess which ones belong to the same attack.

Falcon Next-Gen SIEM correlates signals across domains and enriches them with CrowdStrike adversary intelligence. Agents can recognize a coordinated campaign and pull related alerts into a single investigation, prioritized by risk to the business. No other SIEM is natively integrated with intelligence from CrowdStrike Counter Adversary Operations, which tracks more than 290 named adversaries globally. As adversaries change their tradecraft, the detections change with them.

Supports Agentic Investigations

Better detections don't clear the queue. Even a well-tuned SIEM produces more high-fidelity detections than analysts can investigate. Bolting automation onto a legacy SIEM doesn't fix it. Linear, serial workflows produce agents that make fast but wrong decisions. AI SOC tools built on general-purpose frontier AI models lack the precision to close false positives and find real threats.

The difference is where the agents live. When agents and the SIEM share one data layer, every detection can be investigated with full context, around the clock. CrowdStrike's agents encode the expertise of the elite Falcon Complete team, which is trained on real casework from tens of thousands of customers for autonomous triage and investigation, including knowing when to escalate for human oversight. Agents’ accuracy improves the longer they learn from and adapt to each organization's unique environment. Inside Falcon Next-Gen SIEM, agents pass context to one another as the investigation unfolds, and a central coordinator reaches a verdict no single agent could on its own. Every investigation shows its work, so analysts stay in control.

Enables Teams to Build Custom Agents

Legacy SIEMs ship with content libraries that aren’t tailored to each customer. Every SOC has its own playbooks, escalation paths, and risk tolerances that can’t be pre-packaged.

CrowdStrike Charlotte AI™ AgentWorks lets teams go further. It's a no-code workspace where teams build custom agents on the same Falcon Next-Gen SIEM data and shared context as every out-of-the-box agent. The most advanced companies already have agents in production delivering real outcomes. For example, one customer who participated in the AgentWorks Hackathon used Charlotte AI AgentWorks to build an agent that catches multifactor authentication (MFA) reset abuse in about 70 seconds instead of their usual 20 to 30 minutes. Another customer’s custom agent found 1,710 hosts running unapproved MCP servers in a single day.

Powers Machine-Speed Response

In a legacy SIEM, the alert is the last step. Response happens in another tool after someone picks it up. CrowdStrike® Charlotte Agentic SOAR, built into Falcon Next-Gen SIEM, connects every verdict to governed response across the Falcon platform and third-party tools, from containing a host to revoking a session. Humans approve the actions that matter, and every step is traced and auditable, so the platform can act in seconds.

A SIEM Built to Power the Agentic SOC

Legacy SIEM was built to store logs. The agentic SOC needs a SIEM built to stop breaches. We believe being named a Leader in the 2026 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for SIEM reflects this shift, and customers see it too. CrowdStrike is one of only two vendors named a 2026 Gartner® Peer Insights™ Customers' Choice for SIEM, with 92% of reviewers Willing to Recommend, based on 278 reviews as of 31 January 2026. By bringing detection-ready data, adversary intelligence, agentic investigations, custom agents, and machine-speed response together natively on one platform, Falcon Next-Gen SIEM gives security teams the engine to stay ahead of modern adversaries.

Visit the Falcon Next-Gen SIEM product page.

Learn more about agentic SOC transformation.

Learn how CrowdStrike is extending the agentic SOC in this blog.
