[79] CrowdStrike - CrowdStrike Brings the Human Advantage to AI Detection and Response
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Nov 18, 2026 00:00:00-0600
Link: https://www.crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/crowdstrike-brings-human-advantage-aidr/

[TAM METİN - 933 kelime]
As organizations deploy more autonomous agents, security teams face a growing volume of complex AI activity, which makes malicious behavior harder to distinguish from legitimate workflows.

The threat is already taking shape. According to the CrowdStrike 2026 Threat Hunting Report, AI agent-triggered detection leads surfaced at 2.5 times the rate of human-triggered leads. CrowdStrike also observed DPRK-nexus adversaries poisoning trusted AI framework packages, highlighting risks to AI development supply chains.

Investigating these emerging threats requires specialized expertise that many security teams are still developing, from recognizing how agents can be manipulated to tracing malicious activity across the wider environment.

Stopping AI Threats Starts with Understanding Agent Intent

Consider a developer using an AI coding assistant to merge a pull request. The agent invokes a skill to complete the task, but the skill also accesses sensitive data and sends it to a threat actor-controlled IP address.

The developer intended to merge code, not expose sensitive information. Yet the agent operates under the developer's trusted identity, using familiar tools and permissions. Without understanding the instructions driving its behavior, distinguishing legitimate activity from malicious actions becomes much harder.

This is where CrowdStrike® Falcon Guardian™ provides critical context. By capturing human prompts, AI system instructions, and agent activity at the operating system level where agents execute (e.g., reading local files), Falcon Guardian helps analysts understand what the developer asked the agent to do, what instructions guided its behavior, and what actions it ultimately took. CrowdStrike® Agent Graph connects upstream AI activity to downstream endpoint execution so analysts can trace the sequence of events and identify suspicious deviations from the original task.

With this visibility, security analysts can infer the developer's intended outcome and investigate what drove the agent's behavior. Was it following legitimate instructions, executing a malicious skill, or independently pursuing a goal that went beyond the original request? Understanding the distinction helps analysts determine whether the activity was malicious and identify the appropriate response.

Determining the cause of suspicious actions is only the beginning. Analysts still need to establish what data was exposed, whether credentials were compromised, and whether the agent used that access elsewhere.

Answering those questions requires looking beyond the AI session. Drawing on frontline threat intelligence and experience investigating real-world intrusions, CrowdStrike Falcon® Complete analysts and CrowdStrike Falcon® Adversary OverWatch™ threat hunters can recognize suspicious patterns that may not be obvious from an isolated AI detection. By correlating AI activity with endpoint, identity, cloud, and SaaS telemetry, they can investigate suspicious activity, determine whether it indicates malicious intent, and uncover the broader scope and potential impact of a compromise.

Customer context is equally important in identifying potentially malicious activity. An agent accessing a repository or invoking an administrative tool might be expected in one workflow but suspicious in another. Falcon Complete considers the customer's environment, sensitive assets, and established security policies to determine whether activity is legitimate and what response is appropriate.

If the activity is deemed malicious, this understanding directly informs remediation. Removing a malicious skill or deregistering a compromised MCP server may disrupt the initial attack, but it won't necessarily address credentials already stolen or malicious processes running on an endpoint. Depending on the scope of the intrusion, analysts may need to contain affected hosts, remove malicious artifacts, and address compromised access. The goal is to stop the attack across the environment, not simply eliminate the initial AI threat.

Connecting AI Detection to Expert-led Response

CrowdStrike brings together AI runtime visibility, adversary intelligence, proactive threat hunting, and expert-led response to help organizations identify and stop emerging threats targeting AI agents.

CrowdStrike Falcon® Guardian discovers, governs, and secures AI adoption and development by detecting threats such as prompt injection and capturing runtime context across prompts, MCP connections, tools, skills, and downstream execution. CrowdStrike Agent Graph connects agent instructions to endpoint activity, tracing behavior from prompt to impact.

CrowdStrike Falcon Adversary OverWatch extends proactive threat hunting to AI applications and agents monitored by Falcon Guardian. Elite threat hunters combine runtime context with adversary intelligence and broader cross-domain telemetry to uncover emerging threats and malicious activity that automated detections may miss.

CrowdStrike Falcon Complete for Falcon Guardian delivers 24/7 managed detection and response (MDR) to combat threats targeting AI. CrowdStrike's expert analysts use Falcon Guardian's runtime context and CrowdStrike Agent Graph to investigate suspicious behavior, reconstruct attacks, and correlate activity across endpoints, identities, cloud, and SaaS. Falcon Complete combines this visibility with adversary intelligence and customer context to assess malicious intent and determine the appropriate response. Using established MDR workflows, analysts can take actions such as removing malicious skills and containing affected endpoints to help stop threats before they cause further damage.

This unified approach helps ensure that responding to an AI threat doesn't end with the initial detection. It extends to understanding and addressing the consequences across the customer's environment.

How Businesses Can Embrace AI with Confidence

Falcon Complete pairs specialized AI agents and advanced automation with expert analysts. AI agents accelerate alert triage, investigate suspicious activity, and automate routine response workflows at machine speed, while human analysts focus on complex investigations, interpreting adversary behavior, and making critical response decisions informed by customer context.

As AI agents become more autonomous, security becomes an interpretation and response problem as much as a visibility problem. Seeing what an agent does is the starting point. Understanding the attack, determining its impact, and stopping it requires the combination of machine-speed operations and frontline expertise.

The mission remains the same: Stop breaches. With the right expertise and response capabilities behind them, organizations can embrace AI with confidence.

Learn more: Falcon Complete for Falcon Guardian is now generally available.

[83] BSI - BSI-Jahreslagebericht: Cybersicherheitslage in Deutschland ist angespannt bis kritisch
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 10:00:00 +0100
Link: https://www.bsi.bund.de/DE/Service-Navi/Presse/Pressemitteilungen/Presse2026/261119_Lagebericht.html

[TAM METİN - 428 kelime]
BSI-Jahreslagebericht: Cybersicherheitslage in Deutschland ist angespannt bis kritisch

Ort Berlin | Bonn
Datum 19.11.2026

Das Bundesamt für Sicherheit in der Informationstechnik (BSI) stellt in seinem aktuellen Jahresbericht eine weitere Verschärfung der IT-Sicherheitslage in Deutschland fest: Die Situation im Cyberraum ist angespannt bis kritisch. Anlass der Neubewertung ist neben der aktuellen geopolitischen Lage die rasante Entwicklung im Bereich der Künstlichen Intelligenz (KI).

Zur Quantifizierung der IT-Sicherheitslage legt das BSI mit dem aktuellen Jahreslagebericht erstmals den BSI Cyber Index (BSI CX) vor. Durch den Index werden Risikofaktoren im Cyberraum als Kennzahl zusammengefasst. Mit einem Stand von durchschnittlich 170 Punkten verzeichnet der BSI CX einen Anstieg um 50 Prozent im Vergleich zum vergangenen Berichtszeitraum.

Bundesinnenminister Alexander Dobrindt: „Deutschland ist tägliches Ziel professioneller Cyberangriffe. Und die Bedrohung wächst. Mit der zunehmenden Nutzung von künstlicher Intelligenz nehmen Einfallstore zu und unsere Verwundbarkeit steigt. Dieser zunehmend kritischen Bedrohungslage begegnen wir mit einem massiven Cybersicherheitspaket mit neuen Fähigkeiten und neuen Befugnissen.“

BSI-Präsidentin Claudia Plattner: „Die Disruption, die insbesondere von hochperformanter KI ausgeht, hat neue Maßstäbe gesetzt: Wir haben es mit Werkzeugen zu tun, die Cyberangriffe in bisher unbekannten Mengen und Geschwindigkeiten ermöglichen. Cyberaggression hat sich verändert – sie ist nicht nur schneller, umfassender und schlagkräftiger, sondern auch um ein Vielfaches komplexer geworden. Das bedeutet mitnichten, dass wir ihr schutzlos ausgeliefert sind. Wir müssen uns aber klarmachen, dass Sicherheit im digitalen Raum kein Zustand ist, sondern in einem hochdynamischen Umfeld wachsender Gefährdungen ständig neu hergestellt werden muss.“

Im Berichtszeitraum wurden täglich 160 neue Sicherheitslücken in IT-Systemen bekannt – 35 Prozent mehr als im Vorjahr. Besonders besorgniserregend ist dabei die immer kürzer werdende Zeitspanne zwischen Bekanntwerden einer Schwachstelle und deren Ausnutzung (Time-to-Exploit): Im letzten Berichtszeitraum vergingen bis zur ersten bekannten Ausnutzung durchschnittlich 53 Tage, im aktuellen Berichtszeitraum waren es 25 Tage und im zweiten Quartal 2026 nur noch 8 Tage – ein Rückgang um 85 Prozent. Durch fortschrittliche KI-Modelle wird sich diese Entwicklung fortsetzen. Angreifer kennen Schwachstellen heute oft bereits, bevor sie öffentlich gemacht werden oder ein Patch zum Schließen der Lücke überhaupt verfügbar ist.

Angegriffen wird, wer ein leichtes Ziel ist. Wie in den Vorjahren sind insbesondere kleine und mittlere Unternehmen (KMU) sowie Kommunen besonders verwundbare Angriffsziele. Zudem nutzten weniger als die Hälfte der Nutzer digitaler Anwendungen grundlegende Sicherheitsmaßnahmen. Auch dies zeigt, dass die gesamtgesellschaftliche Resilienz gegen Bedrohungen im und aus dem Cyberraum weiterwachsen muss.

[84] NIST - Quantum Physics Can Verify a Person’s Location, NIST Researchers Show
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 12:00:00 +0000
Link: https://www.nist.gov/news-events/news/2026/11/quantum-physics-can-verify-persons-location-nist-researchers-show

[TAM METİN - 1235 kelime]
Quantum Physics Can Verify a Person’s Location, NIST Researchers Show

Technique offers the potential to protect infrastructure, safeguard financial institutions and prevent phishing.

The quirks of quantum mechanics can definitively determine whether a person is really at a specified location, such as a bank or government building, or is an imposter residing elsewhere.

Using exquisitely sensitive quantum sensors and quantum technologies developed at NIST, researchers eliminated loopholes that had left other quantum-based verification strategies vulnerable to fraudsters.

Although applying the technique to real-life situations is a decade or more away, it may one day help authenticate stock market transactions, ensure that only people inside a secure building can retrieve sensitive data, and thwart phishing attacks on a future quantum network.

Imagine a corporate treasurer directing her company’s financial network to make a $50 million wire transfer. How can the network be certain the command is coming from a secure terminal in the company’s headquarters, and not a hacker’s laptop across the globe?

In a first-of-its-kind experiment, scientists at the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) and their colleagues have demonstrated that the quirks of quantum mechanics can definitively confirm the location of a person. Using exquisitely sensitive quantum sensors and quantum technologies developed at NIST, physicist Krister Shalm and an international team of collaborators eliminated loopholes that had left other quantum-based verification strategies vulnerable to fraudsters.

The researchers reported their findings this week in Nature.

Applying the technique to a real-life situation may be more than a decade away, noted NIST physicist Scott Glancy, a study collaborator. That’s because researchers are still developing the tools that will allow quantum computers and sensors to accurately share quantum information in large-scale networks.

By preventing people outside a specific location from accessing data, the technique has the potential to:

Safeguard healthcare records and other private information.

Prevent remote adversaries from issuing commands to critical infrastructure such as nuclear plants, power grids and water treatment facilities.

Stop hackers from making unauthorized stock transactions in a firm’s trading account.

Thwart phishing attacks based on video or phone calls from unknown locations.

Cybercrimes cost U.S. citizens more than $16 billion in 2024. Far fewer than 1% of cybercriminals are caught, in part because many of the thieves operate from outside the country.

“Our results show how digital security can instead be anchored to the physical world,” said Shalm. “Gaining access to a system can only be done by someone who can prove they are in a particular location — such as a bank — thwarting remote would-be hackers.”

Shalm and his collaborators employed a procedure known as quantum position verification (QPV), in which two trustworthy parties, called the verifiers, seek to confirm the location of a third person, dubbed the prover. To make that determination, the verifiers task the prover with measuring a particular property of a photon — a particle of light – and then reporting back the results.

On the NIST campus in Boulder, Colorado, the team built two verifying stations 195 meters apart, connected by electrical cables and ultrathin optical fibers threaded through the corridors of a research building. The purported location of the prover resided somewhere in between them.

In the first step of the experiment, one of the verifiers creates a pair of photons that possess a uniquely quantum property known as entanglement. Entangled particles are so inextricably linked that measuring a property of one particle automatically reveals the property of the other — even if the two particles lie on opposite sides of the galaxy.

In the team’s plan, the verifier keeps one of the entangled photons and sends the other to the prover. The verifiers each send random numbers timed to arrive simultaneously at the prover’s assumed location. The prover uses the random numbers to compute how to measure a particular property of the entangled photon — its polarization, or the direction in which the electric field of the particle wiggles as it travels through space.

The prover then sends their measurement to the verifiers. Based on how much time elapsed from the moment the verifiers sent the random bits to the prover to when they received the response back, the verifiers can work together to triangulate where the prover is located.

This procedure is repeated millions of times over the course of a few minutes. The verifiers then look at how often the polarization measurements they made on their entangled photon match those of the entangled photon sent to the prover. A quantum principle known as the no-cloning theorem dictates that these correlations are destroyed by anyone attempting to copy and distribute the information to colluding partners.

By passing a test known as a loophole-free Bell test, which proves that the entangled photons are more closely correlated than classical physics allows, the verifiers can be certain that the prover is really in the targeted location and not just pretending to be in that place.

The Bell test can be passed only if the prover consistently measures the entangled photon according to the instructions specified by the verifiers’ random numbers. Because those instructions are random, fraudsters cannot predict them and use the predictions to send early responses back to the verifiers, confounding their ability to determine the true location of the prover.

Any experimental error or manipulation of the quantum devices by adversaries will cause the Bell test to fail, exposing the cheater. If the Bell test succeeds, the verifiers can be certain that the prover is in the correct location and that the quantum devices functioned correctly.

The cosmic speed limit — that nothing can travel faster than the speed of light — also played a role in the experiment. If the prover were located outside the region in which he professes to reside, the person would take longer than expected to report his measurements to the verifiers. That delay would expose the prover as an imposter.

Although scientists have been trying to demonstrate QPV for decades, “our experiment is the first complete demonstration of such a protocol,” Shalm said.

Using QPV to assure a customer that they are truly communicating with someone in their bank’s building will require high-fidelity quantum communication networks, added Glancy. Such networks will share entangled photons between the customer and their bank.

“NIST and many other research organizations and companies are developing the technologies that will support such a quantum network,” he said.

Quantum Advances at NIST

Several technologies pioneered at NIST enabled the researchers to successfully conduct their experiment. These include superconducting nanowire single-photon detectors, which were crucial for capturing nearly every photon in the experiment. If too many photons are missed, it would be impossible to pass the Bell test, and even an honest prover would not be able to convince the verifiers that they are in the target location.

In addition, the team relied on a fast, highly efficient method to generate a source of entangled photons, which NIST scientists created a decade ago.

“All of these technologies combined, along with the expertise of our research team, provided us with the tools we needed,” said NIST physicist Krister Shalm.

Paper: Gautam Kavuri et al. Quantum position verification with remote untrusted devices. Nature. Published online Nov. 18, 2026. DOI: 10.1038/s41586-026-11247-3.

[85] SentinelOne Labs - LABScon26 Replay | Governing Just-in-Time Intelligence: AI, Dual-Use Capabilities, and the Limits of Guardrails
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 14:00:00 +0000
Link: https://www.sentinelone.com/labs/labscon26-replay-governing-just-in-time-intelligence-ai-dual-use-capabilities-and-the-limits-of-guardrails/

[TAM METİN - 412 kelime | KAYNAK: feed özeti]
In his LABScon 26 talk, Mauro Vignati, Advisor on Digital Technologies of Warfare at the International Committee of the Red Cross (ICRC), argues that guardrails cannot address the deeper problem with frontier AI. Advanced cognitive capabilities are now distributed through the same on-demand, as-a-service model that has shaped industry, and later cybercrime, for seventy years. That model was designed for industrial efficiency, not for technology this powerful.

Vignati traces the model back to Toyota’s post-war just-in-time production system, built on the principle that customers get only what they need and pay only for what they get. The IT needed to coordinate that system helped give rise to application service providers and eventually to software-as-a-service. Cybercriminals later adopted the same logic for crimeware and ransomware, and hacktivists for DDoS campaigns. In each case the people taking part needed no technical skill of their own.

Model-as-a-service differs from its predecessors because the capability is dual-use at the source, supplied by private technology firms largely independent of who the end users are or what they intend. Vignati explains why criminals who tried to build their own AI systems have largely turned to wrapping commercial ones instead, leaving the legitimate economy to carry the cost of training and infrastructure. He then draws on cybernetics’ law of requisite variety, the principle that any system regulating another must match its complexity, to argue that AI governance has no simple fix. In its place he proposes a layered framework for deciding what a model can do, for whom and under what conditions.

His warning is that as long as the industry keeps offering powerful general-purpose models, criminals and activists will continue to parasitise commercial services.

For policymakers, AI providers and threat researchers, this talk offers a historical lens on how offensive capability spreads and a starting point for thinking about how to govern it.

Mauro Vignati is Advisor on Digital Technologies of Warfare at the International Committee of the Red Cross (ICRC). His work focuses on the militarisation of digital space and AI, the exploitation of the cognitive domain as a means of confrontation, and the impact of digital technologies in armed conflict on civilians, critical infrastructure and society. He has more than 20 years of experience in cybersecurity and previously spoke at LABScon 2022.

This presentation was featured live at LABScon 2026, an immersive 3-day conference bringing together the world’s top cybersecurity minds, hosted by SentinelOne’s research arm, SentinelLABS.

Keep up with all the latest on LABScon here.

[86] Schneier on Security - France Is Blaming Iran for the Fridge Cyberattack
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: 2026-11-18T12:05:35Z
Link: https://www.schneier.com/blog/archives/2026/11/france-is-blaming-iran-for-the-fridge-cyberattack.html

[TAM METİN - 146 kelime]
France Is Blaming Iran for the Fridge Cyberattack

U.S. intelligence agencies have recently reached a “low confidence” assessment that Iran was responsible for the attacks in France, according to three U.S. officials who spoke on the condition of anonymity to discuss sensitive intelligence. A “low confidence” assessment generally means that there is some intelligence that points in a certain direction but is fragmented, and that the assessment could change as more information is collected.

The agencies’ judgment on the French incidents linked those attacks to Iran generally and not to a specific group such as the Revolutionary Guards, two of the officials said. They added that Iran was also seen as probably responsible for other similar attacks around Europe, but that those investigations were in their early stages.
